# *The Seminar, Book I: Freud's Papers on Technique* (1953–1954)

## **Session of 18 November 1953**

*\[No stenotypy available for the November and December 1953 sessions; what follows reconstructs the lesson from extant notes.\]*

### **The Sense of the Study of Texts and of Its Teaching**

Lacan opens by aligning his pedagogical method with a model drawn from Zen Buddhism. A Zen master, he notes, does not deliver doctrine *ex cathedra*; instead, he ruptures the disciple's silence with whatever is to hand — a sarcasm, even a kick — so that the student is thrown back on the task of seeking, in the texts themselves, the answer to a question that is properly his own. The master intervenes not to hand over a finished science but to confirm an answer the student is on the verge of finding for himself. This style of teaching refuses systematicity in principle even as it is condemned, in practice, to wear a dogmatic face, since any thought in motion must nonetheless present some surface, some articulable form, in order to be transmitted. Lacan thereby announces from the outset that his teaching will be one of thought-in-movement rather than the recitation of established doctrine.

He then asserts what for him is a guiding methodological conviction: that Freud's thought is the most perpetually open to revision of any in the analytic tradition. To reduce Freud's discoveries to a stock of worn-out terms — *unconscious*, *super-ego*, and so on — is precisely to betray them. Each concept in Freud is alive; each has, as Lacan puts it, *its own life*, which is to say its dialectic: it stands in relation to its opposite, develops, and is reformulated. The Freudian concepts had, at the moment of their elaboration, a necessity bound to questions previously posed; to receive them as inert lexicon is to lose their character as responses.

It is therefore insufficient, Lacan continues, to do the history of Freud's thought in the manner of a doxography that would situate him as a thinker of the scientistic nineteenth century. Something genuinely heterogeneous enters with *The Interpretation of Dreams*: not a continuation of the program of physicalist psychology but the reintroduction of *meaning* (*sens*), of a psychological density that is concrete. From the scientistic vantage Freud was seen as regressing to a near-archaic posture — *reading something in dreams*, as ancient diviners had — and Freud himself, embarrassed, tried to recover the project under the rubric of causal explanation. Yet in the act of interpreting a dream one is operating in the register of sense: in the subject's subjectivity, his desires, his relation to his milieu, to others, to life itself.

The task Lacan now sets is the reintroduction of the register of sense, and the reintegration of that register at its own proper level. He invokes the physiological school of Brücke, Ludwig, Helmholtz, and Du Bois-Reymond, who had formed a kind of sworn faith: that everything would reduce to physical forces of attraction and repulsion. Once one accepts these premises, there is no exit from them. That Freud did exit is for Lacan the decisive event: he exited because he gave himself other premises — he dared to attach importance to what was happening to him: the antinomies of his childhood, his neurotic troubles, his own dreams. Freud thus stands, and stands for us, as a man situated amid the most human of contingencies: death, woman, the father.

This return to the sources, Lacan suggests, scarcely deserves the dignified title of "science." He recalls Plato's image from the *Phaedrus* of the good cook who carves the animal at its natural joints, finding the place of least resistance. Each structure calls for its own mode of conceptualization. To prefer instead the simpler monistic deduction of the world from physical forces is to abandon the genuine work of articulation. One must recognize that the instrument of dissection is not the knife but the concept — and that the concept has its own original order of reality. Concepts do not arise neatly from human experience; were that the case, they would be well-made. Our first denominations are made out of *words*, which are clumsy instruments for delineating things. Every science, accordingly, dwells for a long time in night, entangled in language.

Lavoisier offers the paradigm of how a science extricates itself: at the moment he proposes the false concept of *phlogiston*, he also delivers the right concept, *oxygen*. There is, first, a fully formed human language given to us, which we use as a poor instrument. From time to time, reversals occur — the move from phlogiston to oxygen — and the bad instrument is exchanged for a better one. The implication is that analysis, too, must perform this kind of conceptual surgery on its inherited language.

In every science, mathematical or other symbols must be introduced through ordinary language; their use must be explained. We thus find ourselves always at the level of a certain human exchange, the very level at which the therapist also stands, and at which Freud stood despite his disavowal. As Jones showed, Freud imposed on himself the ascetic discipline of not yielding to the speculative bent of his nature; he submitted to the discipline of facts, of the laboratory, distancing himself from "bad language." But once one introduces the notion of the subject, one introduces oneself: the man who speaks to you is a man like the others, who makes use of that same bad language.

From the very origin, Freud knew that he could not make progress in analyzing the neuroses unless he analyzed himself. The growing importance accorded to counter-transference signifies the recognition that there are two in the analysis — and not two merely phenomenologically: this two-ness is a structure. It is by virtue of this structure alone that certain phenomena become isolable and separable. The structure of subjectivity is what gives human beings the idea that they are intelligible to themselves at all.

Being neurotic, then, can serve as a path to becoming a good psychoanalyst; in the beginning, this was what served Freud himself. Like Monsieur Jourdain with his prose, we are constantly making sense, contresense, and nonsense — but the task is to discover within this welter the lines of structure.

Lacan acknowledges Jung's own rediscovery of certain archetypes proper to the human species in dream and religious symbolism — this too is a structure. What Freud introduced beyond this, however, is the *determinism* proper to that structural level. From this stems the ambiguity that one finds everywhere in his work: is the dream desire, or is it recognition of desire? Both readings are licensed by the text, and the ambiguity belongs to the structure of the thing itself.

Likewise the ego: it is at once, as Freud says, like an empty egg, differentiated at its surface by contact with the world of perception — and also, every time we encounter it in analytic practice, the one who says "no," who says "*moi je*," who speaks impersonally as "*on*," who speaks of others, and who expresses itself across these different registers. The ego is, in short, a complex of functions held together under a single name.

We will, Lacan announces, follow the techniques of an art of dialogue — and, like the good cook, we know which joints we will encounter, which resistances. The super-ego, he adds, is also a law devoid of sense, but a law bound to problems of language. When I say "you will take a right," I orient my language toward yours; I think about what is happening in your head at the moment I am speaking — this effort of attunement is the communication proper to language.

This "you" (*tu*) is so fundamental that it intervenes before consciousness. Censorship, for example, is intentional; it operates before consciousness, with vigilance. The "you" is not a signal but a reference to the other — it is at once order and love. Similarly, the ego-ideal is an organism of defense, perpetuated by the ego to prolong the satisfaction of the subject. It is also, Lacan adds, the most depressing function, in the psychiatric sense.

The id is likewise not reducible to a pure objective given, to a roster of the subject's drives: no analysis ever terminated in some measurable quantum of aggressivity or eroticism. The id is rather a certain point in the dialectic of analytic progress, the extreme point of existential recognition: "you are this" — an ideal never actually attained at the end of analysis.

Nor is the goal complete self-mastery, the absence of passion. The ideal is rather to render the subject capable of sustaining the analytic dialogue: of speaking neither too soon nor too late. This is the aim of a training analysis. The introduction of an order of determinations into human existence, into the domain of sense, is what is called *reason*.

Freud's discovery, Lacan concludes the opening session, is the rediscovery, on a fallow terrain, of reason itself.

---

## **Session of 13 January 1954**

Lacan opens the new year and the new trimester with a pointed announcement: the time for one-sided listening is over. The first trimester saw the audience confined essentially to receiving his lecture; in this new trimester he expects something else — namely, that he himself will be able to hear them. The law and tradition of the *séminaire*, he reminds his audience, demands more than personal effort: it demands effective collaboration in the form of *communications*. This is not a rhetorical gesture but the structural condition of the genre he is practicing.

That collaboration, he stipulates, can come only from those most directly engaged with the seminars, that is, from those who are already involved at some level in analytic practice. He will of course continue to offer responses, but he wants this trimester to inaugurate what he calls a *new stage* of the seminar's functioning, to which each participant must contribute their maximum.

He defines "your maximum" by way of negation: when he calls on someone to take up a precise section of the common task, they should not respond with the bored air familiar to all of them, citing exceptionally heavy obligations that week. He is speaking above all to those who belong to the autonomous group he represents, and he wants them to understand why the group has constituted itself separately: precisely for a task that engages, for each of them, nothing less than the future and the meaning of everything they are doing and will do in their lives.

If members do not come to the group in this full sense — putting their entire activity into question — Lacan cannot see why they would have constituted themselves in this form. Those who do not feel within themselves the sense of this task should join some other form of bureaucracy. The implicit reference is to the split from the Société Psychanalytique de Paris that had just produced the new Société Française de Psychanalyse; Lacan is reminding his auditors that the institutional separation only makes sense if it is animated by a genuine theoretical and clinical demand.

These reflections, he says, are particularly pertinent because they are about to take up what is commonly called Freud's *Papers on Technique*. The term is already fixed by tradition: during Freud's own lifetime, a *Sammlung kleiner Schriften* — a collection of small writings on the neuroses — had appeared, isolating a series of papers from 1904 to 1919 that, by title, presentation, and content, indicate as a whole what the psychoanalytic method is.

What motivates this editorial grouping, he explains, is the practical injunction to warn the inexperienced practitioner against certain confusions concerning method and practice. Across these papers one also sees the gradual elaboration of fundamental notions for understanding the mode of action of analytic therapy — in particular, the notion of resistance, the function of transference, the mode of intervention in transference, and ultimately the notion of the essential function of the *transference neurosis*. These four headings will structure the seminar's trajectory.

The little group of writings is of particular importance, yet the grouping itself, Lacan warns, is not entirely satisfactory. Perhaps the title "Papers on Technique" is not what gives it its real unity. The unity is rather a *stage* in Freud's thought, and it is from this angle that they will study them.

This stage is intermediate. On one side stands what an analyst — Paul Bergman, Lacan implies, with his apt though not always elegant pen — has called Freud's "germinal experience." This is the early discovery, the matrix from which everything springs. The papers on technique stand between this germinal phase and the later metapsychological theorization of 1920 and after.

Lacan situates the early period as running up to roughly 1904 or 1906, with 1904 marking the publication of the paper on the psychoanalytic method (where the term *psychoanalysis* appears in a formal way in the title, though it had been used earlier). 1909 is the year of the Clark University conferences — Freud's American voyage accompanied by his "son," Jung.

This early-period landmark thus represents the first development of the germinal experience. At the other end stands 1920, the elaboration of the theory of the instances, the structural or metapsychological theory of the Freudian experience. Between these two endpoints the *Papers on Technique* are arranged. This intermediate position is what gives them their sense.

To imagine the *Papers on Technique* form a unity simply because Freud is "talking about technique" would be entirely mistaken. In a certain sense, Lacan notes, Freud never *stopped* speaking of technique. The *Studies on Hysteria* are nothing but a long exposition of the discovery of analytic technique; one sees the technique forming there. If one wanted a complete systematic exposition of how Freudian technique developed, one would have to begin from the *Studies* and refer to them constantly.

He has not chosen the *Studies* simply because they are not easily accessible — not everyone reads German or even English. There is an edition in the Nervous and Mental Disease Monograph Series, but he could not reasonably demand the effort of obtaining it from everyone. Beyond such practical reasons, however, there are deeper grounds for the choice he has made.

To extend the point: even in *The Interpretation of Dreams* it is perpetually and continuously a question of technique. There is, in fact, scarcely a work of Freud — even on the mythological, ethnographic, or properly cultural themes — that does not contribute something on technique.

To press this still further, Lacan notes that an article like *Analysis Terminable and Interminable*, published in volume V of the *Collected Papers* around 1934 \[actually 1937\], is one of the most important articles on technique. Technique, in other words, is co-extensive with the Freudian corpus; what is specific to the so-called *Papers on Technique* is rather their *position* in the development of the thought.

The question of the *spirit* in which they should approach these writings is, then, decisive to fix at the outset. He has called his preliminary remarks "Introduction to the Commentary on Freud's Papers on Technique."

There are several ways of viewing the matter. If one were content to bend admiringly over Freud's texts and marvel at them, one would have full satisfaction. The writings are of a freshness and vivacity that never fail to produce the effect characteristic of Freud — the personality discovers itself directly in them. The simplicity, the motivations, the dreams Freud reports, the frankness of tone — these alone constitute a lesson. The ease with which Freud treats the questions of practical rule shows that for him this was an *instrument*, in the sense in which one says a hammer is "well in hand." Freud effectively says: well in hand *for me*; another might prefer a slightly different instrument, more to their hand. This metaphor is concretized in the texts themselves.

The whole question of the formalization of technical rules is treated with a liberty that itself amounts to a teaching that might already suffice. Nothing is more salubrious, more liberating, than the direct reading of these papers in which, for the first time, a set of practical rules is given with a thoroughly instrumental character — and nothing more powerfully shows that the question lies *elsewhere* than in the rules themselves.

There is more to add to the way Freud transmits the path of his thought. A second face, secondary perhaps but very sensible, shows itself in certain passages: the suffering character of the personality, the sense Freud has of the necessity of authority, which goes hand in hand with a certain fundamental depreciation of what someone with something to transmit can expect from those who follow him. And one finds, too, a profound mistrust of the way things are applied and understood, with even a depreciation, not difficult to find, of the human material offered to him in the contemporary world.

This permits us to glimpse why Freud, beyond his literary style, exercised the weight of his authority so concretely and practically: he was at once exclusive with regard to all sorts of deviations and imperative in the way he allowed the transmission of his teaching to be organized around him. There is a *political* face of Freud, in other words, that is legible from the writings themselves.

But this is only an aperçu of what such a reading can reveal. The question is whether one will limit oneself to this historical aspect of Freud's action and presence with respect to the transmission of technique. Lacan answers, no — one cannot. However stimulating, agreeable, and relaxing this might be, it would be inoperative. One must take up the writings under the pressure of the contemporary question: what are we doing when we do analysis?

This question, of what analysis is, has been his commentary throughout, and he sees no reason not to pursue it now. Starting from the actuality of technique — from what is being said, written, and practiced as analytic technique — he wonders whether his audience is aware of the most striking fact about contemporary practice, in this fresh year 1954: among the analysts of the world the situation is one of *the most radical confusion*.

He maintains that among analysts who think — already a narrow set — there is perhaps not a single one whose idea of what one does, what one aims at, what one obtains, and what is at stake, coincides with that of any contemporary or neighbor. He invites his auditor Michèle Cahen to confirm this. If one were to play at juxtaposing the most extreme formulated conceptions, one would find them issuing in rigorously contradictory formulations — and this without searching far for paradoxers. The matter is grave enough that even partisans of paradox abstain.

Humor is generally absent from these elucubrations on therapeutic results, on their forms and processes. Practitioners cling to the railing of some piece of theoretical elaboration done by Freud himself, and this gives each of them the guarantee of being still in communication with the colleagues. It is through the *Freudian language* — and not through any agreement on conceptions — that communication is maintained between practitioners who very evidently hold rather different conceptions of their therapeutic action, and of the general form of that interhuman relation called psychoanalysis.

When he says "interhuman relation," Lacan notes, he is already locating things at the point they have reached. For it is evident that the notion of the relation between analyst and analysand is the direction in which modern doctrines have moved in trying to find a footing, an elaboration corresponding to the concrete of experience. This is certainly the most fecund direction in which things have been engaged since Freud's death.

He cites Balint's notion of a "two bodies psychology," a term Balint borrows from the late Rickman — one of the rare figures with a little theoretical originality in the analytic milieu after Freud. This way of framing things permits one to gather together easily all the studies done on the object relation, the importance of counter-transference, and a series of connected terms, with the role of *fantasy* — that is, the imaginary interaction between analysand and analyst — at the front. This will be a frame to reckon with.

Does this direction allow us to situate the problems well? Yes and no. There is great interest in promoting research of this kind, insofar as it marks the originality of the analytic relation in contrast to a "one body psychology," the usual constructive psychology. One must mark from the outset that it is *elsewhere* than within a single body that everything we will elaborate in analytic expression is constituted — namely, in a certain determinate relation.

But is it enough to say that this is a relation between two individuals? Here, Lacan suggests, lies a part of the problem that has been insufficiently deepened. Here one can see the impasses into which the theoretical formulation of technique has been forced.

He cannot say more at this point, but for the regulars of the seminar, the implication should be clear: there is no "two bodies psychology" unless one introduces a third element. He has already presented one phase of this third in the form of the symbolic relation of *speech* taken as such — and taken as the central perspective of analytic experience. It is in a *three-term* relation, not in a two-term relation, that analytic experience is fully formulable.

This does not mean that fragments or important sections of analytic experience cannot be expressed in another register. But what he wants to lay down as a premise is precisely this: a triadic foundation. From this triadic foundation one can begin to grasp the difficulties of the different formulations of inter-analytic relations.

If the foundation of the inter-analytic relation is indeed triadic, there will be several ways of choosing among the three elements of the triad. Each accentuation of one of the three relations internal to a triad will give a way of classifying theoretical elaborations of technique. This may seem abstract now, but it will be operative as a classificatory schema later.

To bring this closer to concrete ground, Lacan returns to the "germinal experience" in Freud. Last trimester's lessons had centered on the notion that *the complete reconstitution of the history of the subject* is the essential, constitutive, structural element of analytic progress. He believes he has shown that this was Freud's starting point: each analysis is a matter of apprehending a singular case, and what makes the value of each of the five great analyses (three of which they have already studied) is the way Freud takes a case in its singularity.

To take a case in its singularity: what does this mean? It means essentially that for Freud the interest, the essence, the proper dimension of analysis is the *reintegration by the subject of his history* — and history pushed to its sensible limits, which is to say to a point that exceeds the merely individual.

This central thesis presents itself in Freud's emphases on certain points to be conquered in technique. It first appears as a "situation of history" — an emphasis on the past. But, Lacan recalls, history is not the past pure and simple: history is the past *insofar as it is historized in the present* — and historized in the present *because it has been lived in the past*. The dialectical reversal here is essential to Lacan's reading.

The technical pathways to this reintegration of the subject's history will therefore take the form of a search for the *restitution of the past*. This restitution, considered as the goal and the material result, is pursued by certain technical routes; and one must see — and one will see, throughout the technical writings — that for Freud this has remained, even to the end, in the foreground of his preoccupations.

Around this accent on "restitution of the past" a series of questions arises: properly speaking, the questions opened by the Freudian discovery itself, and in fact the questions hitherto *avoided* in analysis — namely, the questions of the *functions of time* in the realization of the human subject. Time, here, is not a mere parameter; it is a constitutive function.

The more one returns to the source of Freudian experience — Lacan emphasizes that he means the source, not the historical origin — the more one realizes that this is what perpetually animates analysis, beneath profoundly different vestments. One must pose the question of what the *restitution of the past*, in the past-oriented sense, means for the human subject.

Even when, with the doctrine of the three instances — or four, as Lacan hints — Freud gave the structural point of view a considerable development, and even when this favored an orientation of analysis toward detecting the *present* relation within the analytic session, the singular session and the series of sessions between the four walls of the analysis, even then the accent on past restitution persisted.

To sustain this claim, Lacan needs only to invoke an article Freud published in 1937, *Constructions in Analysis*, where it is still and always a matter of the reconstruction of the subject's history. No example is more characteristic of the persistence of this central pivot throughout Freud's work. There is almost a final re-insistence on this theme in the fact that Freud presses the article. One can take it as the pointed extract, the last word, of what was at stake throughout a work as central as the *Wolf Man*: namely, what is the value of what is reconstructed of the subject's past?

At this moment, Freud arrives — one feels it elsewhere in his work too — at a notion that emerged in last trimester's discussions: that the subject's reliving or intuitive remembering of the formative events of his existence is not, in itself, the decisive thing. Freud's striking formulae include the remark that dreaming is itself a form of remembering (*Träumen ist ja auch ein Erinnern*, in the *Wolf Man* case). Indeed Freud goes so far as to say that even screen memories, properly elaborated, give us the equivalent of what we are seeking.

Do you see, Lacan asks, what point we have come to? In Freud's own conception, then, what is at issue is the *qualified, experienced reading of the cryptogram* that the subject currently possesses in his consciousness — not only of himself, but of himself and of everything, that is, of the totality of his system suitably translated. This is what is at stake.

And this is what we read in the restitution of the subject's totality. Although Freud never abandoned the ideal of *reconstruction* — the very term he uses to the end — the accent falls more on the *reconstructive* face than on the face of *reliving*, of an affective revival in the common psychological sense. The ideal of reintegration is not that the subject feels something as truly his own through reviviscence, but rather that something be reconstructed.

Freud's most formal admission, therefore, is that affective reliving is not the essential. This is paradoxical only if one lacks the register Lacan wants to promote — the register of *speech as such* — as essential to understanding analytic experience. In the end, what is at issue is even less remembering than *rewriting history*.

He is describing what is in Freud, which is important if only to distinguish things. This does not mean Freud is right; but the woof is permanent, subjacent, continuous in the development of his thought. Freud never abandoned what can only be formulated as: *rewriting history*. This formula allows us to situate the various small details Freud gives of analytic practice.

He could confront this with completely different conceptions of analytic experience — without needing to search for extremists. For instance, those who treat analysis as a kind of *homeopathic discharge*, within the actual experience of the analytic field — within the analyst's office, the consulting room — discharge of a certain fantasmatic apprehension of the world, which is supposed to gradually reduce, transform, equilibrate itself into a certain relation to the real. There the accent is placed entirely elsewhere: a passage from a fantasmatic relation to a so-called "real" one, with "real" prudently put in scare quotes. Lacan could cite countless examples from a writer he has already named, whose technical formulations are not rigid but nuanced — and whose work nonetheless comes down to this.

What we will encounter as the fundamental question, in the course of our reading, is the bias by which a fundamental institution of practice — the one given by Freud — came to be transformed into a *technique*, a certain handling of the analyst–analysand relation. We must see how this transformation took place.

A notion absolutely central to this transformation is the way Freud's notions from the period *just after* the technical papers were taken up: the three instances, and especially the *ego*. Around the conception of the ego pivots both the entire subsequent development of analytic technique and the difficulties of its theoretical elaboration.

There is, certainly, a world between what we actually do — in that lair where a patient speaks to us and we occasionally speak to him — and the theoretical elaboration we give of it. Even in Freud, where the gap is infinitely more reduced, there remains a distance.

Lacan is not the only one to have asked the question, what did Freud actually do? Others have asked it explicitly. Bergler asks it in black and white and says that we know little except what Freud has himself let us see in the five great analyses. These give us our best opening onto how Freud comported himself.

But the traits of Freud's experience cannot, in their concrete reality, properly be reproduced. The reason is the singularity of the experience with Freud himself: Freud was the one who had really opened this experience. This alone gives a wholly particular optic. It can be demonstrated by the dialogue between patient and Freud, by Freud's behavior with respect to the patient who is, one always feels, for Freud only a kind of support, question, or check in the path along which he, Freud, advances alone.

This solitude gives the case histories their absolutely dramatic — in the proper sense of the word — character, including what issues from human drama: failure, in each case Freud presented.

The situation is altogether different for those who are positioned to follow the paths Freud opened across his life, up to something one might call the entrance to a kind of "promised land." But one cannot say Freud entered it.

It suffices to read his testament — *Analysis Terminable and Interminable* — to see that if there was something of which Freud was conscious, it was that he had not entered the promised land. This article is not, Lacan adds, a reading for just anyone who can read; fortunately not many can; but for those who can, and who are analysts, it is difficult to assimilate. (For non-analysts, he quips, it does not matter.)

So the situation differs for those who follow Freud's path. And it is precisely on the question of how these paths are taken up, adopted, re-understood, rethought, that any critique of analytic technique must be centered. The smallest piece of technique — or the whole — is only valuable in proportion to our understanding of where the fundamental question lies for each analyst who adopts it.

To take an example: when we hear talk of the ego as both the *ally* of the analyst and as the sole source of access — *we know only the ego*, as one commonly writes (it is written by Anna Freud, with one sense; by Fenichel, with another) — and across nearly all post-1920 analytic writing the formula is: we address only the ego, we communicate only with the ego, everything must pass through the ego.

On the other hand, all the development on this ego-psychology can be summed up in roughly this formula: *the ego is structured exactly like a symptom*. Within the subject, it is only a privileged symptom — the human symptom *par excellence*, the mental illness of man. To translate the analytic ego in this rapid, abridged way captures, Lacan thinks, the essence of what emerges from a straightforward reading of Anna Freud's *The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence*.

One cannot fail to be struck by how the ego, in this account, is constructed and situated within the subject's totality *as a symptom*. Nothing distinguishes it from one. The demonstration is dazzling, and no less dazzling is the resulting confusion: the catalog of defense mechanisms constituting the ego in this singular position is one of the most heterogeneous catalogs imaginable, as Anna Freud herself notes — bringing repression together with the turning of an instinct against its object or the reversal of its aims places side by side elements that are not homogeneous.

At the current point we may not be able to do better; this is a parenthesis. What is important is to perceive the profound ambiguity that the analyst makes of the ego: the ego that is everything one accesses, and the ego that is a kind of stumbling, *Fehlleistung*, slip.

Fenichel, at the very beginning of his chapters on analytic interpretation, speaks of the ego as everyone does, and feels the need to say that the ego has the essential function of being a function by which the subject *learns the sense of words* — that is, from the first line, Fenichel is at the heart of the subject. The whole question is whether the sense of the ego *exceeds* the ego, or is in effect a function of the ego. If it is a function of the ego, then all the development that Fenichel gives afterward is absolutely incomprehensible. But he does not insist.

Lacan calls this a slip, because Fenichel does not develop it, and the rest of his text says the contrary, ending with the claim that the id and the ego are in the end exactly the same thing — which is hardly clarifying. Either the rest of the development is unthinkable, or what he begins with is not true.

And one must know: what *is* the ego? In what is the subject caught? This includes, besides the sense of words, the fundamental formative role of language in his history. This brings Lacan to the question they will have to pose throughout the seminar: what was contained, implied, in what Freud was bringing forth at this moment? What was already oriented toward the formulations of present-day practice? And what perhaps narrowed in the way we now see things — or, on the contrary, what has been realized since that goes in the direction of a more rigorous, more adequate systematization, of an *enlargement*? It is in this register, and in no other, that the commentary can have its sense.

To make even more precise the way he envisages this examination: at the end of the last lessons he had broached a certain readability of something one might call *the psychoanalytic myth*. This readability is not so much a critique as a measure of the breadth of the reality with which analysis confronts itself — insofar as it can only respond mythically.

That is to say, an apprehension as wide as possible of the positive side of the theoretical conquest realized in relation to that *x*, which is not at all given to be an *x* — nor a closed *x*; that *x* is something quite open called *man*. The problem is more limited, and more urgent for us, when it is a question of technique. For there, Lacan says, the examination falls under the discipline of analysis itself: as distant as the patient's acts and behaviors are from what he brings into the session, just so distant are our concrete behaviors in the session from the theoretical elaboration we give of them.

But this distance, while a first truth, only has its sense and reach insofar as it reverses: it also means *as close*. Just as the patient's concrete acts are only intelligible — even the basic absurdity of interhuman conduct only intelligible — as a function of that "system" (as Melanie Klein happily named it, without knowing what she was saying, as usual), the system called the human ego: the series of defenses, negations, dams, inhibitions, fundamental fantasies that orient and direct him — exactly so, our theoretical conception of our technique, even if it does not coincide exactly with what we do with our patients, deeply structures and motivates the slightest of our interventions with them.

And this is what is grave. It is not enough to "know": it is not enough to have a certain conception of the ego for that conception to enter our work with the patient like a rhinoceros in a china shop. That does not suffice. But there is a certain relation, a certain way of conceiving the function of the patient's ego in analysis — Lacan only opens the question for now — such that the inverted mode in which we permit ourselves to bring in our own ego in the analysis is itself in play.

Naturally, as analysis has revealed, we permit ourselves things without knowing it. But we do, in fact, permit ourselves to bring in our ego. This is interesting because, since analysis is so much a matter of *readaptation to the real*, one must know: is it the measure of the analyst's ego that gives the measure of the real?

The question of the theory of technique is just as interesting. The analyst's action, whatever they make of the totality of their world-system — the concrete system, not the explicitly formulated one, not the unconscious one, but the system at work in our daily expression — is something that will or will not serve as a measure in the analysis.

He thinks he has opened the question enough for today. He asks the audience to see now the interest of what they can do together. He asks Mannoni — telling him not to leave — to associate himself with a neighbor, say Anzieu, to study the notion of *resistance* in Freud's writings, those collected under the title *Technique psychanalytique* at PUF, with the lectures of *Introduction to Psychoanalysis* not to be neglected.

He also asks whether Perrier and Granoff would associate themselves on the same theme. They will see how to proceed; they will let themselves be guided by experience itself.

---

## **Session of 20 January 1954**

Lacan opens by inviting those who have interested themselves in the notion of resistance — as it is implicated in the *Papers on Technique* and the later writings — to take the floor. Mannoni acknowledges that he should be the one to begin. He explains that he has collaborated with Anzieu only by telephone; they had wondered who should speak first. Anzieu would begin from the beginning (the *Studies on Hysteria*), but Mannoni has done the *geography* rather than the history of resistance, and so it makes sense for him to lay out the territory first.

Mannoni's framing is announced as a *study of the country of resistance*. The expository order will therefore be: first the geographical aspect, then the historical development. He has worked principally on texts running from 1904 to 1918 — the articles gathered in *La Technique psychanalytique* and chapter XIX of the *Introductory Lectures*, which is explicitly devoted to resistance. The other articles are not specifically devoted to it, but they take it up constantly: roughly sixty significant occurrences.

Freud encountered resistance, Mannoni continues, as an obstacle to the treatment as he previously conceived it — a treatment founded on the fundamental rule of free association. In this guise resistance presents itself as an interpersonal phenomenon within the analytic relation, and this point of view will never be abandoned. The interpersonal aspect is the durable kernel under all subsequent theoretical refinement.

Lacan interrupts to summon Granoff — telling him to take notes and to speak afterwards if there are points of disagreement. The seminar's collaborative structure is being enforced as Mannoni proceeds.

Mannoni resumes: the first aspect Freud mentions is the interpersonal — resistance appears between two persons as an obstacle to communication. Immediately afterward, other aspects emerge. Freud discovers that resistance is not merely an obstacle but an *object of analytic study* — something that can be studied in itself, since it harbors the secret of the neurosis. From 1904 to 1918 the analysis of resistances will form the center of technical preoccupation. One can say that here begins a development of considerable interest, since the analysis of resistances will become, twenty years later, the *analysis of the ego*.

He will stop at 1910, but notes that an important contribution comes in 1920 through Karl Abraham, and that the very end of Freud's writings — *Analysis Terminable and Interminable* (1937), the penultimate of the *Collected Papers* on the *Splitting of the Ego* (unfinished) — sees Freud poised to draw the decisive conclusions without having drawn them.

He calls it a development because everything that arrives in 1938 and after is already perfectly indicated, in germ, in 1904 and perhaps even before. Clinically, resistance is conceived as an obstacle between two persons. Freud counts as resistance everything that hinders the treatment.

Mannoni indicates page references (p. 119 of *La Technique psychanalytique*, p. 310 of the *Introductory Lectures*, chapter XIX) and gives Freud's clinical formula: the subject exploits every occasion to escape the analyst; these escape-attempts reveal an intention unrecognized by the subject; the analyst is occupied above all in struggling against it. The struggle imagery is faithful to Freud's vocabulary.

Anzieu, Mannoni notes, will have something to say about this. At the origin Freud's attitude is one of *combat* between the subject and resistance. Yet Freud also knows that it is the *analyst* who provokes the resistance: "the first effect of the fundamental rule is to render the subject mute." Freud insists that this does not create resistance but only renders manifest resistances that were latent. From this it will be possible to conceive of resistances as a *structure*.

Lacan affirms Mannoni is right and asks for the precise reference. Mannoni cites pages 46 and 314\. He notes the impression that Freud must have had a provocative attitude vis-à-vis the resistances, that he sought them out cheerfully. Our attitude today, though apparently different, is the same. The essential has not changed: the analyst's presence — even silent and discreet — has the immediate effect of producing not the same muteness but the manifestation of resistances.

Even so, the resistance must be overcome for the work to be possible. It must be analyzed for two reasons: this is the best means of overcoming it, and it contains the secret of the neurosis. As early as 1904 Freud writes that the phenomenon of resistance alone allows one to understand the subject's behavior.

Mannoni proposes to set the question of *transference* provisionally aside, even though transference is already implicated in the clinical aspect. Setting transference aside, the idea of resistance at first sight blends with the idea of *repression*. He cites Freud: repression belongs fundamentally to the topographical register; the goal of therapy is to transport the repressed from one locus to another — from the unconscious to the conscious — and resistances hinder this transport. One might think it advantageous to bypass the obstacle. Freud did try this, but it failed: the repressing forces are momentarily circumvented but they subsist, and one must take them as obstacles.

At this point one leaves the topographical register and enters the *dynamic* register. Simultaneously (p. 111), Freud exhorts the patient to consider his illness not as something contemptible but as an *adversary worthy of him*, the source of precious data for his subsequent life. Resistance is recognized as worthy of attention and as a source of precious information.

To see things in this way, Mannoni continues, Freud needs no new theory: repression is the result of antagonistic forces, and the analysis of resistances informs us about the state of these forces, hence about the subject. The dynamic perspective is already sufficient.

\[A passage on the patient's "splintered" psyche follows. Mannoni quotes Freud: the neurotic brings us a psyche shattered and fissured by the resistances. He notes that such fissures are difficult to imagine between psychic agencies and ego-levels. Freud continues: when we eliminate the resistances, we see this psyche coordinate itself, and the great unity called the Ego aggregate to itself all the instinctual stirrings.\]

The words "instinctual stirrings" (*Triebregungen*) indicate a labor of *reclamation* over the id — Freud's *Zuiderzee* image, anticipating the later formula "where it was, I shall come to be." We are obliged to consider this labor and these fissures from the standpoint not of the actual ego but of a *virtual* ego that will be formed by aggregating to itself the future egos that are the pieces of the id. Here, in germ, is what will later be called (in Balint) the *nuclei of the ego* — the place that the structural conception will come to occupy. In Anna Freud's book, defenses of the ego are still always against the id.

In *Analysis Terminable and Interminable*, Mannoni notes, Freud will say that these are not obstacles isolating parts of the Ego from other parts of the Ego. Before one could quite say — what Mannoni does not himself dare say — that *resistance is the ego*, Freud had already said: *resistance is the transference*.

Reintroducing transference, Mannoni feels he is reintroducing it into the china shop of metapsychology. He must step back into the interpersonal terrain of clinical considerations. The question can be posed thus: if it is the patient as "natural person" who resists the analyst — by all his means, like Proteus before Menelaus, simulating cure or simulating complete imbecility — then the analyst has, it seems, the choice between only two tactics.

Either act as Menelaus did, applying the nymph's counsel — *hold firm*. Freud (p. 97\) advocates this: accept the challenge, hold one's ground, affirm without rest that this is resistance, encourage oneself. Or, alternatively, see to it that the resistance is replaced in the subject by *goodwill* — that is, one might think, exploit the positive transference.

Such goodwill is so useful that Freud counsels never to touch the transference so long as it has not changed into resistance. Mannoni cites Freud's warning (p. 99\) that this change of transference into resistance cannot fail to occur, and that transference furnishes the most formidable of resistances.

But, Mannoni cautions, one must not think that transference becomes resistance only by turning negative; a positive transference can equally retard the end of analysis. Freud's genius seems to have developed above all in the clinical domain.

This was a domain Freud could not explore directly: he had to forge the instruments of theory step by step. Without metapsychology — Freud confesses, almost calling it the *fantasy* of metapsychology — we would not advance a single step. So Freud constructs the theory that explains how the transference *is* the resistance.

Mannoni declines to enter into all the details. He will set aside the economic aspect — where libido divides into two, makes a compromise, and appears at the transferential end like a birth, adopted by the subject because it satisfies the resistance — and take the essential: the *transference of repetition* that plays itself out in conduct, while the aim of analysis is the *evocation of memory*, "without leaving the psychic limits." By this, Mannoni thinks, Freud means: without leaving that part of the ego opposed to the part that is perhaps not ego, the part that is behavior and action.

Mannoni is not sure that Freud has not abandoned the first goal of analysis — to vanquish resistances, to vanquish hysteria. Transference is one of these resistances. So in a theory of this kind transference-as-resistance would be a kind of *secondary effect* of the primary resistances. The transference is a reminiscence that is not the memory expected of the patient — for memories cure the symptom. It would be resistance if it were not transformed into memory: it is resistance because it is *stopped on the path* that goes from the unconscious to the conscious.

Freud thereby returns to his blessed metapsychology. The obstacle is once again between the less conscious and the more conscious. He can install there *Ampère's little man* — the imp who does or does not open the door to the repressed representations. Freud adds that this is only a fiction, but immediately notes that it gives an idea quite close to the real state of things.

Mannoni asks whether he has explained well, or whether he should return to the passage from clinical fact (transference between two persons) to metapsychological transference where there are no longer persons but Ampère's little man — and the resistance has become a kind of valve of the repressing mechanism.

But it seems to him that the great metapsychological discovery commanding all subsequent developments is the thesis that transference is the resistance — and not merely that the *negative* transference is. This is the capital discovery.

If one examines what happened next, one sees a pressure exerted that invites Freud to consider *persons* rather than Ampère's little man. This is what Mannoni alluded to in mentioning Abraham, Jung, and Melanie Klein: the *personal* reintroduced.

This is already in germ in Freud: in pages 27 to 314, Freud interrupts a development to say suddenly, "But the resistance — that's the father\!" And it enters the metapsychology like something from another planet, another piece of clinic. There was a real metapsychological continuity between the topography and dynamics of resistance — the structural-defense thread — but as soon as one reintroduces persons, or lets oneself be influenced by Abraham, Klein, or even Freud himself in these impersonal contexts, one is tempted to speak in ways that do not accord well with the metapsychology. This, Mannoni suggests, is a critical point.

His exposition has not been strictly chronological: the same notions recur at different epochs; the clinical aspect does not come before the analysis of the ego — it is in germ from the start. But there is a chronology — not of Freud's discoveries but of his *teaching*, that is, of the different ways Freud was understood by his commentators and disciples. And Freud knew it.

The penultimate article of the *Collected Papers*, on the *Splitting of the Ego* (volume V), begins with a remarkable sentence. Lacan interjects that it was published after Freud's death. Mannoni quotes the famous opening, in which Freud confesses uncertainty about whether what he has to say is something long familiar and obvious, or something entirely new and embarrassing.

Mannoni proposes that *Splitting of the Ego* is in germ in the 1894 conceptions — as a lack of inner freedom, an inner resistance in the consciousness of the subject. And yet in 1938 the expression could still appear entirely new. The structural thought emerged so early that even Freud could not be sure how new his final formulation was.

He takes the liberty of stating a personal impression: resistance remains, do what one may, an obstacle to communication in an *interpersonal* context — and it is in the same context that, at the end, it is understood, that is, communicated, which is the same as saying *limited*. But the passage from the first situation (where it is obstacle) to the second (where it is known and becomes communication) requires a *dialectical detour* that makes resistance a dialectical mechanism of repetition of the ego's structure. At this point it becomes a nonsense: we understand well enough that the subject does not want to or cannot obey the rule, but we do not understand that he both wants to and cannot. It is in this void that the entire metapsychological labor takes up lodging.

In other words, what slightly bothers Mannoni is Freud's perpetual effort to envelop *personal* relations in an *interpersonal* theory, while Mannoni sees the interpersonal theory itself enveloped in an *interpersonal dialectic* which is the phenomenology Freud has opened for us. Freud died in the middle of the penultimate article where one has the impression he was about to say so. Phenomenology, Mannoni concludes, looks to him like a clinical proposition rather than a metapsychological one.

Lacan responds: Mannoni's tendency is clearly phenomenological. He thanks him for the most felicitous opening for the seminar's renewed dialogue. There is an enormous amount in what he has said, on the whole well situated. And Mannoni has at the end posed the question they will have to confront throughout the commentary.

Lacan must say at once that he does not think the solution is quite of the form Mannoni lets us glimpse — Mannoni himself sensed this. Lacan will give a more complex formulation that will put them more at the heart of the actual question of the orientation of technique and the meaning of analysis.

But it is good to have posed the question as Mannoni did — moving from an intra-personal mechanism to an inter-personal one. There the word *mechanism* is itself approximative, but that is not the whole: the interpersonal "mechanism" takes its sense from a further point Lacan has yet to specify. He does not want to interrupt the development resulting from the couple's collaboration, however relative it has been: he asks Anzieu to say where his own week's research has led him.

Anzieu announces his plan. He has sought the notion of resistance at its source — what he believes is its source — the *Studies on Hysteria*. He should have read carefully everything that came before, but that question remains in suspense. His plan: an Introduction (before the *Studies on Hysteria*) and a Body of the Exposé (the *Studies* themselves).

In the introduction, he asks: what did hypnosis teach at the end of the nineteenth century? And what was the *positive* aspect of hypnosis, the only aspect to which attention had been given until then? He lists three discoveries: (1) amnesias can be filled in under hypnosis — Charcot's discovery; (2) doing this work, the symptoms disappear — Breuer's discovery; (3) why do the symptoms disappear when the amnesia is filled in? Because the subject takes consciousness of his own tendencies, or because he has fought intensely against his own sexual impulses. This third point is properly Freud's discovery — and it is not just a completion of the Charcot-Breuer series: the essential Freudian discovery is the *negative* aspect of hypnosis.

There is, Anzieu says, a kind of dialectical *reversal* that will permit the passage from hypnosis to psychoanalysis. The discovery is this: hypnosis is fine, but there are people who are *not hypnotizable*. One returns to the most concrete reality. Anzieu quotes Freud: in such-and-such a case it worked very well, but I have never encountered a case where it worked exactly that way.

Freud devotes articles to the question of whether to distinguish a *hypnotic hysteria of retention* from a *hysteria of defense*. Some patients cannot be hypnotized. Freud aligns this fact — known but neglected — with another, equally known and judged unimportant: other patients *could* be hypnotized but do *not want* to be. Freud's idea: these two cases might be identical; they could mean only a *counter-will*. Whoever has doubts about hypnotism is not hypnotizable, and it makes no difference whether he expresses his opposition or not. Whether Freud can fully adhere to this conception is, as Anzieu notes, not yet entirely clarified — but the first intuition is in place.

Here, then, in its extremely vivid aspect, is Freud's first intuition: *not being able* and *not willing* are exactly the same thing, whether or not one knows why one does not will. But knowing *why* it is the same thing is still very confused.

On the model and as a consequence of this first intuition, Freud will discover a second. To do so, he draws out the consequence of his first intuition: he renounces the application of hypnosis. He proceeds with his patients by the *cathartic method* — asking them the memories that come to mind when they think of their symptoms and the first origin of their symptoms, without hypnotizing them. The discovery: one obtains exactly the same results with or without hypnosis.

Therefore, Anzieu continues, it is not hypnosis as such that had favored the efficacy Charcot and Breuer had demonstrated; it was the way one used it. Anzieu returns to Freud: by such experiences I gained the impression that by mere *insistence* — it suffices to insist with the subjects, and they always end up finding the memories — and without hypnosis, it was really possible to bring to light the series of pathogenic ideas. But since this insistence demanded much effort on my part, it showed me that I had overcome a *resistance*. This led Freud to formulate the whole thing theoretically: by my psychic labor, I have been able to overcome a psychic force which, in the patient, prevented the pathogenic idea from becoming conscious — that is, from being remembered.

This is Freud's second intuition: if I am forced to insist with the patient that he give me this memory which is right there nearby; if I have to insist, to tire myself, if it is really a painful labor for me — then it is that he is *resisting* me. The chain being thus opened, the third intuition arrives immediately: without this having been revealed to me, it came to my mind that it must be exactly the same psychic force that had acted against the hysterical symptom at the start, and that now prevented the pathogenic idea from becoming conscious.

So, just as the people who cannot and the people who do not want to be hypnotized are the same, this force at the symptom's origin is exactly the force that, in the *hic et nunc* of the analytic rapport, prevents the pathogenic idea from being remembered. The intuitions are concluded; now to the explanations.

What is this force, Freud asks, that has acted, continues to act, that I revive? One can judge the nature of this force from the nature of the *pathogenic ideas* — forgotten and repressed — which thanks to the method one has now succeeded in bringing to light. These ideas are always painful: linked to affects of reproach, moral suffering, personal wound. They are such that one would have preferred not to have had the experience, that one would rather forget. All this — Freud says — culminates in the notion of *defense*, as though it had come to him spontaneously.

Anzieu explains that it did not in fact come to Freud spontaneously: given his psychological learning, the notion was already well known. But Freud rediscovers it, reinvents it, to account for the experience in which he finds himself. So at the origin the subject had a painful experience. Its painful character entailed a reaction on the part of the *ego* — for it is only at this moment that the word "ego" appears in the text — a reaction by which the ego defends itself against the painful impression.

Defending oneself against the painful impression, Freud says, is not enough to guard against its return. To guard against its return, the ego constructs a system: this is the formation of the symptom. The defense takes a new form, called *repression*. Freud hesitates at first to use the word *repression*; he uses *rejection*, *expulsion*; only later is *repression* definitively adopted.

When the physician strives to make the subject remember this painful idea — the idea that provoked the ego's defense, then provoked its repression and was erected as symptom — the same force is again at work. To me, the experimenter, this force appears as *resistance*. Three names, then, can be given to a single reality: this force is *defense* when it reacts and struggles against the instinctual impulse; it is *repression* when it abolishes the memory; it is *resistance* when it opposes remembrance.

Here are inscribed, in this "germinal cell" of which Bergman spoke and which Mannoni indicated in advance, three planes: the *structural* plane; in the background the *dynamic* plane; and the actualized, striking *topical* plane — the resistance as it appears to me, the analyst.

That is the first point: the discovery of resistance. The second point is the *handling* of resistance. It is very curious that there are two ways of handling resistance in this passage of the *Studies on Hysteria*: an explicit, recommended way, and a half-avowed way.

The explicit way is to *insist* that the subject overcome the resistance. One can insist in two ways: by giving the subject *assurances* — "I don't know what the origin of my symptom is" / "Yes you do, say it\!" — or, more conciliatingly: "You do know, it will come to your mind, wait." Freud uses a metaphor that one encounters in the English word *struggle*. Anzieu notes he could not consult the German text often; all citations should be revised.

Lacan interjects: what is irritating is that for *Anna O*. we do not have the German text. It is unnameable absurdity that we have the Anna O. case only in the American edition.

Anzieu continues: if the assurances given in this combat-metaphor do not suffice, Freud has recourse to a technique — the *imposition of hands* on the subject's forehead. What is its meaning?

Lacan: exactly — this is the intermediate stage between hypnosis and dialogue.

Anzieu raises a chronological question: was the pressure-of-hands the intermediate phase, or did Freud first use only the assurances and only later add the pressure?

Lacan recalls the case of Lucie R.: it was in cases where Freud obtained only an incomplete hypnosis that he stopped worrying about whether the patient was or was not hypnotized. Even when, asking the subject classically "are you sleeping?", he had the unpleasantness of being told "no, I am not asleep at all," Freud was driven into a most embarrassing situation, which he describes with a naïve charm that shows the ambiguities of practice: he had to persuade the subject (a) that it was not the same sleep as the one about which the subject answered, (b) that he must still be somewhat asleep. At the edge of the most perfect ambiguity, Freud says quite clearly that this puts him in great embarrassment, and that he was only freed from it from the day he stopped worrying about it. But he maintained the pressure of hands — on the forehead or on each side of the head. He would explain to the subject that he must fix his mind on the idea, on the cause of the symptom. The symptoms were at that moment treated one by one, in themselves, confronted directly as problems posed.

Under Freud's hands the patient was assured that without doubt the thoughts, the memories of elements of the subject's situation about to present themselves, were the relevant ones; he had only to trust them. Freud added the technical detail that at the moment he lifted his hands, by a kind of mime of the lifting of a barrier, the patient would be perfectly conscious and would only have to take what presented itself to his mind to be sure of holding the right end of the thread.

It is remarkable, Lacan notes, that this method proved, for the cases Freud reports, perfectly effective. For the case of Lucie R. — so pretty — was entirely resolved, with an ease that has the beauty of primitive works.

In every newly discovered case, there is a happy chance, a happy conjunction of the gods that permits a happy solution, a privileged case. In a case like *Anna O*. we are in the presence of that long *working through*, a labor on the case, of the themes coming up: in some respects we already see the full thickness of the most modern analytic cases, despite the method employed — the whole series of events, the whole history, is relived and re-elaborated several times. We see that a long-range work is at issue, lasting nearly a year.

In Lucie R.'s case things go much faster. There is a kind of elegance that makes the case something essential and striking. But things are even too compressed to let us really see where the levers are; nonetheless it is quite usable.

This woman who had had olfactory hallucinations — one can call them olfactory hallucinations, hysterical symptoms whose meaning is detected, in places and dates, in a quite felicitous way by Freud — gives Freud the occasion to give us all the details of his way of operating.

\[To Anzieu\] Continue.

Anzieu: I think the first technique, that of assurances, is a technique of *force*, while the technique of pressure of hands is a technique of *ruse*. Freud explains it himself: by placing my hands on the subject's forehead, I draw his attention to that gesture, and by that very fact the resistance finds itself *displaced* — from what it was resisting against, to that gesture; and by that very fact the memory that was beneath can come to light. This is why Anzieu finds it important that Freud begins with assurance before passing to pressure on the forehead, because his reaction to the subject's resistance is first to *force* him, to be the stronger; whereas the second is to *ruse* with him.

Lacan asks for the textual basis. Anzieu admits it is an interpretation: in the chapter on psychotherapy in the *Studies on Hysteria* Freud says, "first give assurances; if that does not work, the pressure of hands." Lacan: this is a method of *exposition*; it will have to be reviewed.

Anzieu maintains: even if it is not chronological, it is important that Freud's first reaction is to be the stronger and force the resistance, and his second to *ruse* with it, to deflect it. His third reaction is indicated only after several pages of descriptions of various forms of resistance, and the best means of overcoming resistance in certain cases is to find the *patient's secret* and tell it to him, so that he is forced to abandon the resistance.

We thus arrive at something typically analytic: *interpretation*. This is not interpretation of the resistance but of the content. It is the most radical means of lifting the resistance. From force to ruse, from ruse to interpretation, there is a considerable progress. Freud also explains that it is important to give the subject access to the marvelous world of psychological process: we obtain his collaboration and lead him to consider his own case with the objective interest of the scientific observer. And there too we make the resistance recede. Anzieu would relate this to what Freud was discovering simultaneously regarding theory.

Lacan interrupts, apologizing. He recalls insisting on the wholly privileged character of the cases Freud treated — owing to the special character of Freud's technique. We can only presume, by a certain number of rules he gave us — rules faithfully applied — and by the avowal of the best authors, even those who knew Freud personally, that we cannot fully form an idea of how Freud actually practiced. We have not enough actual documents to know how Freud applied the technique, what his technique was.

If Lacan insists on the character of Freud's advance — a research not marked by any ordinary style, not a research like other scientific research, but a research in the domain of the *truth of the subject*, a dimension of truth placed at a level of accent and presence that makes this research something not entirely reducible to the objective, even objectivizing research of common scientific method — it is because this *something*, the realization of the truth of the subject, is a proper dimension that must be detached in its originality from the very notion of *reality*. This is what Lacan has been emphasizing throughout: lessons that, having summarized the work of past years, are introductory to this year.

Freud at that moment was on the same path of a research after a truth that interested him himself totally, even in his person — hence in his very presence to the patient, in his activity as therapist (though *therapist* is insufficient to characterize Freud's attitude). And by Freud's own admission, this could only have given his relations with his patients an absolutely singular character.

It is, if you will, the singularity carried to the second power — the maximal singularity of analytic treatment, that of the treatment done by the very one who *discovered* analysis. Lacan has already said: analysis must always be considered as resting on this character of singularity in the realization of the experience. Analysis as a science is a science of the *particular*. The realization of an analysis is always a singular case, among singular cases that nonetheless lend themselves to some generality since there is now more than one analyst.

But the experience *with Freud* presents this singularity at its extreme, by the very fact that Freud was constructing and verifying analysis itself. The notion of the singularity of this experience cannot be eliminated from the position we must give to the Freudian experiences as such. We cannot efface the true fact that it was the first time anyone was doing an analysis.

Lacan does not say one was applying a method: the method is *deduced* from this; it is a method for others. But the absolutely unique, *inaugural* character of Freud's procedure is something we would fail to register only at the cost of all sorts of obscurities, which we will see later — it is not for nothing.

Lacan insists on this not merely to bring out the subject, but because to understand everything that will follow, the questions of analytic technique, we presently could not — and Lacan notes that he is making some effort to drive this in for the moment, since one must momentarily suspend one's attention on something to give it its full value.

He insists that the auditor pose this to themselves as a problem to be sustained in attention — it really is essential. Above all, once one has learned to consider analysis as Lacan has taught: a kind of experience of the *particular* in this singular particular of the original — something that takes on even greater particular value.

If one does not underline the difference between this and what followed — for those who interested themselves not so much in the truth as in the constitution of access-paths — one will never see clearly the difference of accent and sense to give to certain phrases and texts in Freud, which later take on, in other contexts, a quite different value, even if they could be considered as copied from one another.

This is the interest of the commentaries on Freud's texts: they let us see, in detail, on each point, the questions which — as one sees already today — are of considerable importance and are numerous, insidious. Properly speaking the very type of question whose concern is everyone's — the natural tendency of the human mind being to *avoid* them and to rely on a refrain, a schematic, abridged, imaged formula on which one can rest. It is easier to rest than to call things back into question.

Anzieu resumes the list: a further necessity is to act as explainer, professor, and confessor who imposes absolution after confession. The fourth element is the influence of the affective aspect — personal influence, in short, an opening of transference and counter-transference. That was the second point. There was also a third he will skip: the sketch of the diverse forms of resistance.

Lacan: this is very important.

Anzieu lists the four or five forms Freud indicates: (1) the patient says "I thought an idea would come to me, I had confidence in you, but I am only anxious, and nothing comes"; (2) the violation of the fundamental rule: something would have come to mind, but he does not say it because he judges it unimportant or unpleasant; (3) he uses a subterfuge that hides the resistance: "I am distracted by the piano in the next room, I can only think of that"; (4) he begins to speak at length, but speaks without emotion, in an artificial way — the most subtle resistance; or, still more difficult, the patient does remember the famous pathogenic memory but disavows it: "it is not I who remembers it, it is as though it were you, Doctor, who said it"; (5) or "you expected me to say it, and I say it because you expected it. You must have thought I was going to think of it"; (6) finally, the ultimate resistance Freud indicates in another passage: that the memory is not at all recognized as memory — a vague, diffuse, imprecise impression that cannot be attached to a scene. This is the maximum of resistance. One understands why Freud always sought to reconstruct resistance in its most subtle form.

The last point of his exposé, again following the *Studies on Hysteria*, is the theoretical explanation of resistance. Freud applies to it the conceptual model in which he had been formed in the school of dynamic neurology of the time. He proposes representing a *triple stratification* around a central nucleus — the pathogenic nucleus in which the pathogenic, traumatic experience is registered. Three arrangements: linear, concentric, and dynamic-zigzag.

Anzieu will speak only of the second arrangement: similar memories are grouped in stratifications Freud compares to *bundles of documents in archives*.

Lacan responds: what is striking is that we take off completely from the pseudo-anatomical metaphor of the time — verbal images more or less ambulating along nervous conductors — while here the images evoke exactly what Anzieu says: that of a *bundle of documents*.

Anzieu: in the English text — *Table of Contents*, *well-ordered archives*.

Lacan: *scores with several registers* — all metaphors that themselves tend invincibly toward the *materialization of speech*, and not the mythic materialization of the neurologists but the concrete materializations in which speech begins to flow on the manuscript or printed leaf. There is something here that cannot fail to strike one. Even the metaphor of the blank page — the palimpsest — has come to the pen of more than one analyst.

In the passage Anzieu evokes, the notion of several longitudinal strata — several threads of discourse, imagined in the text that materializes them as a *bundle* — is treated as something literally concrete. A bundle, a current of parallel words which at a certain moment widens to surround the famous pathogenic nucleus, itself a history, deviates to include it, and rejoins a little further on.

The phenomenon of resistance, Lacan stresses, is *literally constituted* by something said in the text as follows: there are two senses, a *longitudinal* sense and a *radial* sense. It is in the radial sense that resistance is exerted, when one tries to approach the threads at the center of the bundle. Resistance is the consequence — when one tries to pass from the outer registers toward the center — of a positive force of repulsion exerted by the repressed nucleus; resistance is felt in the effort to penetrate toward the threads closest to the center.

Freud even goes so far — not in the *Studies* but in a later text, the *Metapsychology* writings — as to write: the force of resistance is *inversely proportional to the distance from the repressed nucleus*. Lacan is not certain that is the exact phrase, and will retrieve the citation, but it is striking. It is the very materialization of resistance in its first apprehension — at once clinical, as Mannoni said, and, to designate the subject's discourse at the level of this clinical experience, the most felicitous and the least satisfactory metaphor for the neurophysiologist.

To say where it happens, what the material biological support is, Freud takes *discourse itself* as a reality as such — a reality that is there, that he expresses by evoking the bundle, the *fascicle of proofs*, as it is also called: juxtaposed discourses that cover one another, follow one another, form a certain dimension, a certain thickness, the mass of a dossier.

Since Freud does not yet have the notion of the *material support of speech in isolation* — today there would be the succession of phonemes composing a part of the subject's discourse, which he would have taken as the element of his metaphor — he is forced into more diffuse imagery. One encounters a resistance all the greater as the subject's discourse approaches a certain form of discourse, the last and the good one — which the subject absolutely refuses.

Lacan thinks that what is perhaps not put in relief in the synthesizing effort his speakers have made — well done as it is — concerns certain notions. One that has not been brought to the foreground is, in sum, the *relations of unconscious and conscious*.

This is in the foreground of the questions to be posed about resistance. The question is not so much to know more precisely — before knowing what this resistance represents, whether inside the subject or in the subject's relation with the analyst — what it means in the end. It comes to a question:

Is resistance a phenomenon that happens *in the analysis*? Is it in the analysis only? Or is it something we can speak of *simpliciter* and at any time, even when the subject is walking outside analysis, even when the subject has nothing to do with analysis — before he comes to it and after he has left? What is resistance? Does resistance continue to have its sense in the subject outside the analysis?

This is another way of posing — a way that brings many problems — the problem Mannoni broached so felicitously. The general idea of his theme is how he took the question, but other questions arise. With little time left today, is it indeed all that opposes itself to this *reconquest of the unconscious* — a word, Lacan notes, that has not been pronounced?

Anzieu: it has not yet been pronounced.

Lacan: Yes. But Mannoni has taken other texts. There is in particular a text on resistance in the analysis of dreams, extremely important, that neither of them referred to — one that gives the assumption to certain of the problems they raised.

It is a matter of knowing whether the character of *inaccessibility* of the unconscious is something that, in a perspective that is — they will see — not simply the actual perspective of analysis (the metapsychological perspective seen today with Mannoni's and Anzieu's exposés) but a different one, has the meaning often given it.

Lacan thinks it is perhaps not a discovery, but something that fixes ideas, to notice that these notions of resistance are extremely *ancient*, present from the very origin, from the very birth and first researches of Freud, and that this notion of resistance is also very early bound to the notion of the *ego*.

When one reads, in the text of the *Studies*, certain striking sentences — when Freud speaks not only of the ego, as Anzieu remarked (the ego appearing there as such in the text), but speaks of the ego as representing *a certain ideational mass* — that *Vorstellungsmasse*, the content, the mass (Lacan no longer has the German term to mind) — the ego is then already something sensed as approaching, as letting one foresee, all the problems posed today by the notion of ego.
