Lacan would almost say these are notions with *retroactive effect*. Reading these first formulations in the light of everything that has since developed in the problem of the ego — perhaps even the most recent formulations *mask* them rather than bring them out.

One cannot fail to see, for instance, in the formula "ideational mass," something that singularly neighbors a certain formula Lacan has given of one of the elements of *counter-transference*, which is precisely nothing other than the function of the ego-functions of the analyst, what Lacan has called the *sum of the analyst's prejudices*. The ego is something that represents, in the patient too, the whole organization of certitudes, beliefs, coordinates, references constituting properly speaking what Freud called from the start an "ideational system" — what one can in abbreviated form call here "the system."

Does resistance come uniquely from there? Or is it something further? What Lacan called in one of his last lessons — representing the auditor at the limit of a certain domain of speech, precisely this *ideational mass*, that of the subject's ego — is there or is there not the structuration he called, in the same turn of his discourse, the *sum of silence* after which *another* speech reappears, the one to be reconquered in the domain of the unconscious, that part of the subject which is that part of his history separated by something that separates him from his history?

Is it, yes or no, uniquely this something that is the organization of the ego? Is it purely and simply the ego's organization that, as such, constitutes the resistance and makes the difficulty of access in the radial sense — to use the very term Freud uses — of which it is a question when we approach the phenomena of consciousness, the content of consciousness?

Here is a question very simple, too simple, and as such *insoluble*. Happily, the progress of analytic theory in Freud and his disciples is entirely bound to the *avatars*, in the course of the first thirty years of the century, where analytic technique encountered enough experimental phases to differentiate its questions in a way that allows us — by the good schema analysis itself has shown us, which lets us show analysis by *turning around it*, exactly as analysis is a *detour* to accede to the unconscious — to differentiate the question.

This brings us back, Lacan says, to what he has told them will be the model of their research: to search, in the avatars and metaphors and phases of the evolution of analytic experience itself, for something that informs us about its own nature — the nature of this experience insofar as the analytic experience is *itself* a human experience, and one masked to itself.

This is to bring the problem to the *second power*: the problem posed by neurosis — which matters because it imposes itself; Lacan here only affirms this, it will be demonstrated as the examination unfolds. He emerges with evidence from this veritable mental and practical impasse where analysis currently ends up. One sees that Lacan goes far in formulating what he says.

It is important to *submit to analysis* this operational schema. Lacan can say that analysis itself has taught us to try to read, in the different phases of the theoretico-technical elaboration of analysis, something that allows us to read further into the authentic reality at stake: the *reconquest of the unconscious by the subject*.

This method, Lacan continues, will lead to precisions that go much further than the merely formal domain of a catalog of procedures and conceptual categories. This taking-up of analysis in an itself-analytic examination is a procedure that, as their work has already shown in their first access to Freud's clinical texts, will reveal its fecundity especially on questions of technique.

On this he will leave them today. He congratulates Mannoni and Anzieu for what they have done. He will try next time, approaching by a new biais, another door of entry to the problem. He does not think it will be exactly from their work that the most fecund exposition can be given. He will not fail to underline what is fecund in each of their exposés, and what they will already be able to answer next time. The title of next session has been communicated and published: *Resistance and the defenses — its sense and their functions — in the analytic experience*.

A first aperçu: the notion of resistance in its relations with the notions of defense, which were at the origin the *neuro-psychoses of defense* (1894), and at the end the symptoms. Freud says one must return to the notion of defense. They will see things that allow them to take one further step.

---

## **Session of 27 January 1954**

Lacan opens by giving the floor again to Anzieu, whose contribution he feels he may have somewhat minimized last time. Anzieu picks up the final paragraph of the *Studies on Hysteria* concerning resistance — Freud's effort at theoretical explanation of the resistance described and discovered in the preceding pages. He had begun explaining, and Lacan had continued, the *triple stratification* of psychic material that Freud describes around a central nucleus of memories — the pathogenic nucleus to which one must accede.

There is a linear, chronological arrangement on the one hand — kinds of stratifications of similar memories linked together — and on the other a kind of dynamic, zigzag procedure following the content of thought, by which one arrives from the surface to the center. It is the description of the stratifications of similar memories that is interesting here from the standpoint of resistance.

Freud represents them as concentrically stratified around the pathogenic nucleus. What determines these concentric layers? Their degree of remoteness from the nucleus. They are, Freud says, layers of *equal resistance*. Anzieu is tempted — a personal idea — to see in this formulation the influence of a mode of thought beginning to be important at the time: thinking in terms of *fields* (electric, magnetic, dynamic fields). Just as one approaches the center of a field and the lines of force grow stronger, so Freud probably conceives the superposition of memories on the same model.

It is no accident, Anzieu adds (as Mannoni had remarked), that one speaks of *Ampère's little man* — the imp Freud places as barrier between unconscious and conscious. It is no accident that one is dealing with an *electrical* metaphor. The relations of electrical and magnetic order intervene in the theorization of resistance.

Mannoni interjects with a quibble: Freud should have taken *Maxwell's demon* rather than Ampère's little man, since the latter does nothing. Lacan agrees but had not wanted to enter into the theory of electricity: Ampère's little man does not have the power to open or close. Mannoni: that role belongs to Maxwell's demon.

Hyppolite intervenes: Maxwell's demon cannot be alerted to the passage of a molecule. Signalization is impossible. The demon cannot be informed when a molecule passes. The intervention serves to underline that the metaphor of the imp at the gate already implies a problematic of *information* and *signal* — a problematic that the physics of the period had only just begun to make rigorous.

Lacan: we are entering into a quite scabrous ambiguity, since "the resistance" is indeed the formula. These questions are all the more opportune since analytic texts evidently teem with such methodic improprieties. These subjects are indeed difficult to treat and verbalize without giving the verb a subject.

Evidently we hear all the time that "the ego sets off the signal of anxiety," handles the "death drive" and the "life drive." One no longer knows where the central exchange, the switchman, the switch is. As all this is difficult to realize prudently and rigorously, in the end we see all the time little Maxwell's demons appearing in the analytic text, full of foresight and intelligence\! The trouble is that we do not have a precise enough idea of the nature of demons in analysis.

Anzieu suggests one would have to return to the history of electricity and magnetism. He is not sure whether the notion of *nerve impulse* — its electrical nature — had already been discovered by then. In one of Freud's early works, applying the psychological method with a number of refinements, Freud had succeeded in discovering a certain continuity of cells composing a nerve. The very question — what is a nerve? — was still under discussion.

Lacan: Freud's neurological works are at the origin of the theory of the *neuron*.

Anzieu: the conclusion of one of his works stands at the very edge of discovering this theory. It is rather curious that Freud remained on the edge of discovering theories — and that it was to *psychoanalysis* that he turned.

Hyppolite asks whether Freud had not considered himself to have failed in the matter of electricity. He thinks he has read this somewhere. Anzieu: in zoology. Mannoni clarifies: in *clinical* electricity. Freud renounced applying electrical apparatuses to the neurotic patients after a painful trial. Hyppolite: exactly — the *compensation* for what he considered a failure. Anzieu: yes, indeed; he had tried electrotherapy in private practice.

Lacan elucidates: Hyppolite is alluding to the fact that in the works preceding Freud's psychoanalytic period, Freud's anatomical works can be considered successes and were so sanctioned. When Freud moved to the physiological plane, he seems to have manifested a certain disinterest — and this is one reason he did not push to the end the import of the discovery of cocaine. Even there his physiological investigation was soft: it remained close to the therapeutic use as analgesic, and Freud left aside the more rigorous matter, the anesthetic value of cocaine, by insufficient physiological curiosity.

This is a trait of Freud's personality. One can ask whether — as Anzieu said — Freud was reserving himself for a better destiny. He made certain *returns* to domains in which he seemed to have some leaning.

But to go so far as to say it was a compensation is, Lacan thinks, excessive. If one reads the works published as *The Origins of Psychoanalysis* — the first recovered manuscript, the theory of the psychic apparatus — Freud is fully in the line of the theoretical elaboration of his time on the mechanistic functioning of the nervous apparatus. One should not be surprised that electrical metaphors enter; nor should one forget that electricity itself began as a physiological experimentation, and was returned to the nervous influx. It was in the domain of *nervous conduction* that the electrical current was first experimented with. We do not know how far that import might reach.

Anzieu thinks the important point is conceptual. Freud was formed in neurology by physiologists who brought a quite new conception into that domain — and one has rediscovered their kind of *oath*.

Lacan: the three great conspirators of psychophysiology. (Brücke, Helmholtz, Du Bois-Reymond, with Ludwig — alluded to in Session 1.)

Anzieu: the oath stipulated that no forces exist other than those analogous to physico-chemical forces. There are no occult or mysterious great forces: all forces reduce to attraction and repulsion. It is interesting to return to the text of this oath of 1840 — modeled on astronomy.

Anzieu thought, hearing Lacan speak of the ego as a *mass of ideas*, that he was alluding to this. Brentano gave the volume of Stuart Mill's complete works containing these data of empiricist psychology. What did Jung try to do when he enounced the law of the association of ideas — that ideas attract one another? He was taking up Newton's great law from physics — that bodies attract one another — and transposing it to the psyche. The great law of the psychic world was analogous to that of the physical world. The notion of *force* emerged here, and electricity was one of the privileged domains.

This was probably going to substitute itself for the *lever*, the model of antique mechanics. Now there is this notion of attraction and repulsion to explain fundamental phenomena. What is experienced *counter-transferentially* as resistance, Freud theorized in terms of force. And force supposes something that opposes itself to it. Force is force only in relation to a resistance — fundamental notions in electricity.

Anzieu thinks it was above all as conceptual model that Freud was led to put the accent. It is properly counter-transferential: "He is resisting, and this makes me furious." Clinically, the notion of resistance represents an experience we are all led to have at one moment or another with almost all our patients in our practice.

Lacan: what? what is this? Anzieu describes the extremely disagreeable experience in which one says to oneself, "He was just there, he could find it himself, he knows it without knowing he knows it, he need only take the trouble to look — and this fool, this idiot" — all the aggressive and hostile terms that come to mind — "won't do it." And the temptation to *force* him, to constrain him.

Lacan: do not titillate too much there. Hyppolite: it is the only thing that lets the analyst be intelligent — when the resistance makes the analysand look like an idiot. It gives one a high consciousness of self.

Lacan: the trap of counter-transference, since one must call it that, is nonetheless more insidious than this first plane.

Anzieu: it is the prime *gros plan*. This is what struck Freud. When he later strove to account for it, he elaborated it as *counter-transference*, according to the conceptions he had at hand from his earlier works.

One arrives, Anzieu continues, at the representation Lacan himself sketched: the pathogenic nucleus is not a passive nucleus but an eminently *active* one, and from this nucleus there is a whole *infiltration* that branches out toward the entire psychic apparatus. Resistance is, on the contrary, a *symmetrical infiltration* coming from the subject's *ego*, striving precisely to stop these ramifications of the pathogenic infiltration at the point where they try to pass into more and more superficial layers.

Lacan: to what text are you referring? Anzieu: still in the *Studies on Hysteria*. Lacan: what you have just said is that resistance is characterized as — you did not say the word, you arranged not to — as in sum the *defense* of the ego against the infiltrations of the pathogenic nucleus. What is your text?

Anzieu: the *Studies on Hysteria*, after the exposition of the three schemas (linear, concentric, dynamic). He cites Freud: the pathogenic organization does not behave like a foreign body but rather like an *infiltration*. He asks Lacan the meaning of *infiltrate*. Lacan: what is infiltrated — the *infiltrate*. Anzieu reads on: the *infiltrate* must in this comparison be taken to be the resistance.

Lacan: very well — but this does not mean it is the *reaction of the ego*; there is no idea here of a reactive formation of resistance. Anzieu: that is exact.

Lacan: despite yourself, you are already soliciting later texts in the direction of identifying resistance at the expense of something already named in the *Studien*: this *ego* one speaks of in the *Studien*, this *ideational mass*, this content of ideations that constitutes the ego in the *Studien* — it is to be seen.

Lacan will help him by saying what he thinks. This is why they are there: to see what the evocation of the notion of ego signifies across the whole of Freud's work. It is utterly impossible to understand what the notion of ego represents in psychology as it began to emerge with the 1920 works (on *Group Psychology*, on *The Ego and the Id*) if one drowns it all in a general apprehension of a certain slope of the psyche. This is not at all what the ego is in Freud's work. It has a functional role, bound to certain technical necessities.

To say at once what Lacan means: take the triumvirate functioning in New York — Hartmann, Loewenstein, Kris — in their present elaboration of an *ego psychology*. They are constantly — one need only refer to their texts — around this problem: what did Freud's last theory of the ego mean? Have its technical implications truly been drawn yet? It is written this way; Lacan is not translating, only repeating what is in the last three or four articles of Hartmann, accessible in *Psychoanalytic Quarterly* 1951, three articles by Loewenstein, Kris, and Hartmann grouped there and worth reading. One cannot say they arrive at a fully satisfactory formulation, but they evidently search in this direction and pose principles and theoretical precisions that have very important technical applications.

They themselves formulate that these implications have *not* been fully drawn. This does not mean they have not been drawn at all. Hartmann says so: the question is there. It is very curious to see how this work elaborates itself across a succession of articles in recent years, especially since the war. Lacan thinks this gives every appearance of a significant *failure* that should be instructive for them. In any case there is a whole distance, a world traversed between (a) the starting point — the ego as spoken of in the *Studien* — and (b) this last, still problematic theory of the ego forged by Freud from 1920 on. Between them lies something: the central field they are studying.

How did this theory of the ego come to light, presenting itself today under the entirely problematic aspect of being the last point of Freud's theoretical elaboration — a theory of Freud either never before seen or never properly recognized, an extraordinarily original and new theory? And at the same time emerging in Hartmann's pen as something which, with all its force, tends to rejoin the classical psychological current.

Both things are true. Kris too writes: the *entry of psychoanalysis into general psychology*. There appears at the same time something completely new and original in the theory of the ego — even *inconceivable* without all the elaboration between 1910 and 1920\. Here is something paradoxical that will have to be brought out, whether they reach the holidays with these *Papers on Technique* or with the *Schreber* writings. It is important to be quite prudent. Despite himself, Anzieu has let slip something not in the text.

\[To Anzieu\] Continue.

Anzieu has, in conclusion, a number of remarks. First: this notion of resistance appears in Freud, is discovered, in the course of a *lived experience*. As Freud says in the *Studies*, the great surprise is to see that when one asks the subject to let himself associate freely, one notices that the subject has everything in him, in good order: the memories are arranged in good order, everything is in good order in him.

If one succeeds in acceding to this sum, one finds it. In other words, the etiology of the symptom and of hysteria is in the subject himself. It is arranged in good order and is waiting to be sought. The subject ought, then, to see and find it by himself, since it is in him and in good order. But then resistance intervenes. The subject *can* know but does not *will* to — exactly like the subjects who could be hypnotized, who would be greatly benefited, but who do not will.

So resistance is born from the psychotherapist's reaction — one does not yet speak of psychoanalysis — a kind of reaction, counter-reaction, of *rage* against the subject's refusal to surrender to the evidences within him, to *cure*; and consequently the psychotherapist's first reaction is to *force* — openly or insidiously, by insistence — the subject.

Freud will thus be led to see in resistance — and Anzieu rejoins Mannoni's conclusions — a phenomenon of *two-relation*, an interindividual phenomenon.

Lacan: in 1896 there is already talk of psychoanalysis. The term *psychoanalysis* exists already in an article of the *Revue neurologique* — accessible to everyone, written in French. Perhaps even in French the word *psychoanalysis* appeared for the first time.

Anzieu: instead of seeing that resistance is transference and that the analyst's resistance is counter-transference, Freud refers resistance back to the subject and says that if the subject resists the analyst, it is because the patient resists *himself* — because he has defended himself against pulsions awakened in him by a certain experience at a certain moment of his history. By this very fact resistance is referred back to the subject — that is, the three forms Freud strives to classify: resistance, repression, defense.

Lacan: where does Freud strive to classify them? Anzieu: he tries to classify them in time. Defense is the ego's reaction against the sexual impulsion; repression is the abolition of the memory; resistance is what the subject opposes to the actual remembering, where he is exposed to the discovery of this notion.

Lacan: this is how *we* summarize notions that are not so harmonious. They slide imperceptibly across the development of thought toward ever-more-differentiated acceptations that later certainly rejoin. This is indeed what is at stake — but this harmonious classification (resistance, defense, repression) is nowhere presented this way under Freud's pen.

Anzieu: more or less, yes, in the *Studies on Hysteria*. For this element of *reaction to the subject's reaction*, one would have to bring in personal factors in Freud — and this would lead very far. But it is known, from what Freud said in *The Interpretation of Dreams* (whose unity Bernfeld retrieved), that there was an extremely strong tendency to *domination* in Freud — he identified with Masséna and Hannibal. He had considered law and politics — exercising power over persons — and his vocation for medicine, he attributes to having heard lectures on Goethe, a text of Goethe on nature. This seems to explain itself thus: to direct power over human beings, Freud substitutes the indirect and acceptable exercise of power that *science* gives over nature. And this power reducing in the end (one sees here the mechanism of *intellectualization*) to understanding nature and thereby submitting it — the classical formula of determinism. This authoritarian character marks all of Freud's history, particularly his relations with heretics and disciples.

Lacan: I must say that if I have spoken in this direction, I have not gone so far as to make it the *key* to the Freudian discovery.

Anzieu: I do not think it the key either, but an interesting element to bring out. The hypersensitivity of Freud to the subject's resistance is not without relation to his own character.

Lacan: what permits you to speak of Freud's *hypersensitivity*? Anzieu: the fact that *he* discovered it — and not Breuer, not Charcot — that it was he to whom it happened, because he felt it more vividly and elucidated what he had felt.

Lacan: yes, but do you believe one can give value to such a function as resistance, as something that signifies in the subject a particular sensitivity to what resists him? Or is it not, on the contrary, *having known how to dominate it*, to go further and elsewhere, that allowed Freud to make it a factor one can objectify, maneuver, name, handle, and turn into a spring of therapy?

And do you really believe Freud is more authoritarian than Charcot, when Freud renounces as much as he can the use of *suggestion* in order to let the subject integrate that something from which resistances separate him? In other words: is it not those who *misrecognize* resistance who exhibit more authoritarianism in their handling of the subject?

Lacan would rather be inclined to think that someone who seeks by all means to make the subject his object, his thing — in hypnotism, or someone who seeks a subject who becomes pliable as a glove, to give him the form one wants or extract what one can — is someone driven more by a need of domination, of exercise of his power, than Freud, who in this matter appears finely respectful of what is also commonly called the resistance of the *object*, of the *material*.

Anzieu: assuredly.

Lacan: he believes one must be extremely prudent here. We cannot so easily handle the whole technique. When Lacan speaks of the analysis of Freud's work, it is precisely to proceed with full analytic prudence and not to make of a *trait of character* something that is a constant of personality, much less a characteristic of *the subject*. There are things very imprudently formulated under Jones's pen — though even Jones is more nuanced than what Anzieu has said.

To think that Freud's career was a compensation for his desire for power, even for a frank megalomania (of which traces remain in his sayings, still to be interpreted) — Lacan thinks the question of Freud's drama at the beginning when he discovered his path cannot be summarized in such a way that all he brought into contact with the subject is characterized as a *continuation of the desire for power*.

We have learned enough in analysis about revolutions and conversions in personality not to feel obligated to equate Freud dreaming of dominating the world by will-to-power-and-command with Freud the initiator of a new truth. This does not seem to fall under the same *cupido*, even if under the same *libido*.

Hyppolite intervenes: in the domination of Charcot — without accepting Anzieu's formulas fully — one is dealing with domination over a being reduced to *object*. Lacan: perhaps we are going a bit far in reducing. Hyppolite: it is possession of a being no longer master of himself. Whereas, by contrast, Freudian domination is to *vanquish a subject*, a being still possessing self-consciousness. There is thus in some sense a *stronger* domination in dominating a resistance to be overcome than in the pure suppression of that resistance — without wanting to draw the conclusion that Freud wanted to dominate the world.

Lacan: yes, there would be that, if there were what... Hyppolite: it is easy domination, Charcot's.

Lacan: it is a question of knowing whether Freud's was *domination simply*. After what we have seen of his way of proceeding in the clinical cases he reports — and Lacan still reserves much not indicated in his way of proceeding — we have seen things that surprise us, but that surprise us *in relation to certain technical principles to which we accord importance*. Things that surprise us in his interventionism.

In some sense they are perhaps far less the kind of victory over the subject's consciousness Hyppolite spoke of than are the modern techniques that always put the entire accent on resistance. Far from it: in Freud we see an attitude that one cannot call more complex but perhaps more *undifferentiated* — that is, more *human*.

Freud does not always define what is today called "interpretation of the defense" versus interpretation of the content — which is perhaps not always the best path. In the end, more subtly, we find it is the same thing. But one must be a bit subtle to see this. And we see that ultimately, in Freud, the interpretation of the content plays the role of an interpretation of the defense.

It is certainly not the kind of technique whose shadow Hyppolite is right to evoke. Lacan will try to show by what angle precisely the danger of such a *forcing* of the subject by the analyst's interventions presents itself. These are far more current in so-called "modern" techniques — as one speaks of "modern" chess — than they ever were manifest in Freud.

Lacan does not believe that the theoretical emergence of the notion of resistance can serve as a pretext for formulating against Freud this kind of accusation, which runs radically counter to the manifestly liberating effect of his whole work and therapeutic action. It is not a tendency-trial Lacan is making against Anzieu. But it is a tendency Anzieu manifests in his conclusion, and one that can serve only to view things in a critical form. One must have a spirit of examination, of critique, even toward the original work; but in this form it can only thicken the mystery rather than bring it to light.

Anzieu adds one last point on the psychoanalytic movement in this same privileged experience whose explanation Freud sought. Freud discovers three notions: defense, resistance, repression. After a moment of hesitation, in the same year, he tries to synthesize, to extend beyond hysteria to obsessional neurosis and paranoia the notion of defense, seeking the specific type of defense at work in these other neuroses.

Freud subsequently centers psychoanalytic psychology on the notion of *repression*, then later on the notion of *resistance*. In 1920 he returns to the notion of *defense* sketched here. In this sense one is indeed dealing with the *germinal cell* of Freudian thought, whose aspects he will across his work successively develop — those that chronologically are the most essential.

Lacan: when you say "germinal cell," to whom are you referring? Anzieu: to Bergman — *germinal cell*.

Lacan: in this article, whose name had escaped him but which he well remembers, what is at issue throughout — what is given as the germinal cell of analytic observation, and this matters because it touches on the question of the *meaning* of the Freudian discovery — is the notion of *retrouvailles* and of *restitution of the past*, of which Bergman shows our experience set out. He refers to the work with Breuer (*Studien über Hysterie*) and shows that to the end of Freud's work and the last expressions of his thought, the notion of *restitution of the past*, in a thousand forms and finally in the form of *reconstruction*, is always maintained in the foreground for him. This is what is at issue in that article: the accent is not at all put on the grouping around the fundamental experience of resistance.

Anzieu: he had not spoken of the germinal cell himself; he had striven to attach the development of resistance to the whole development.

Lacan: he wishes to say some words. He thinks the expositions of Mannoni and Anzieu have the interest of showing them the burning sides of the whole affair. There has been in their exposés — as befits minds well formed, if relatively recently introduced to the practice and technique of analysis — something quite *acerbic*, even *polemical*, which always has its interest as an introduction to the vividness of the problem.

There is here a very delicate question — all the more delicate because, as Lacan indicated in his interruptions, it is quite *current* among certain of those present. The reproach implicitly formulated as inaugural to Freud's method is itself entirely paradoxical: for if something makes the originality of analytic treatment, it is precisely having perceived at the very origin and at once something *original* in the subject — what puts him in this truly problematic relation with himself, what makes it not all simple to cure him — and having put this in conjunction with the *finding* itself, the discovery (in the sense Lacan set out at the start of the year): the *sense* of the symptoms.

The refusal of this sense poses a problem. The necessity that this sense be more than revealed, that it be *accepted* by the subject — this is something that places in the first rank the techniques (those for which the human person, in the modern sense whose value we have recognized, is essential, of which psychoanalysis is part) that not only *respect* the person but *function in this dimension*, and cannot function otherwise.

It would be paradoxical to put in the first plane that the notion of resistance is in principle something *forced* by the technique. This seems evident — which does not mean the problem does not pose itself. The style of intervention of analytic technique today: it is clear that some analyst literally does not take a step in treatment without teaching his students to pose the question "what defense has he again invented?" This is a properly *inquisitorial* phase — not police-work in the sense of finding something hidden, but the inquisitorial phase, which is different: knowing what posture the subject has taken, what attitude, what *finding* has he made to put himself in a position such that everything we say to him will be inoperative.

To put it bluntly — though "bad faith" is too tied to implications of the order of *knowledge*, foreign to this state of mind, too subtle still — there is nonetheless the idea of a kind of fundamental *bad will*, a properly voluntarist implication: the subject not only does not want to know anything, but is capable... \[pages are missing in the stenotypy here, marked "//"\].

When the missing pages resume: half a lifetime, even a human life, can elapse. One should not be too surprised to find the subject at the end with attitudes and thoughts, a content quite different in the same word he used at the start. In between he may have married, procreated, and this suffices to give an exactly opposite sense to a dialogue that at the end of the journey could be considered as reproducing word for word the dialogue sketched at the start. The words will have a new sense, since the persons will be totally different.

Lacan takes an example — an article by Annie Reich, published in 1951, on *counter-transference*. This article takes its value, its coordinates, with a certain way of orienting technique that goes very far in a certain school — let us say in a certain part of the English school.

One arrives, as one knows, at the proposition that all of analysis must take place in the *hic et nunc* — that everything happens in a kind of ever-present grappling with the subject's intentions, here and there, in the session, through which we glimpse fragments, rags, sketches more or less well-related of his past, but where in the end this kind of *psychological strength-trial* within the treatment is where all the activity of analysis resides.

After all, this is the question: the *activity* of analysis. How does it act? What carries it? For those at stake — for Annie Reich — nothing is important except this kind of recognition by the subject, *hic et nunc*, of the intentions of his discourse. And these intentions have value only in their *hic et nunc* bearing, in the present interlocution.

When the subject is with his grocer or his barber, implicitly he is haranguing the figure he addresses. Anyone, Lacan notes, who has the slightest practice of conjugal life knows there is a part of *implicit demand* in the slightest fact that a spouse reports rather what annoyed him in the day than the contrary. But sometimes there is also something else: the care of informing the other of some important event. Both are true. The question is on what point one puts the light, what one considers important.

Things sometimes go further. Annie Reich reports the case of an analyst in this situation: he has had — one feels she is blurring certain traits, but everything indicates this must be a *training analysis* with someone very close, whose field of activity is very close to the field of psychoanalysis — this subject (the analysand) is led to make a radio communication on a subject that interests the analyst himself very much. Such things happen.

It happens that this radio communication is made just after the subject — the analysand — has lost his mother. Everything indicates that the mother in question plays an important role in what are called the fixations, even the subjectivities of the patient. The mourning strikes a few days before the broadcast. He is certainly very affected by it. Nonetheless he holds his engagements in a particularly brilliant manner. At the next session, the subject arrives in a kind of stupor, near to confusion: nothing can be drawn from him; what is drawn is surprising in its incoherence. The analyst boldly interprets: "You are in this state because you think I hold against you the success you had on the radio, on this subject which, as you know, interests me at the highest level."

Good — and Lacan skips over the rest. The continuation shows it takes no less than a year for the subject to recover his wits regarding this *shock-interpretation*, which did have an effect — he had recovered his wits instantly. The fact that the subject emerges from a state of blur and fog following an analyst's intervention so direct does not at all prove that the intervention was *therapeutically structuring* in the proper sense — that is, that it was *true*. No. It returned the subject to the sense of the *unity of his ego*. He was in confusion. He emerged from it abruptly by saying to himself: "Here is someone who reminds me that indeed all is wolf-to-wolf — we are in real life."

The instant effect is no proof of the rightness of the interpretation. In analysis one rightly considers that when the subject brings *confirmatory material*, this proves the rightness of the interpretation — and even this deserves nuance. Here, at the end of a year, the subject realizes that what was at stake in his confusional state was bound to a *counter-blow* of his mourning reactions, reactions he could only overcome by literally *inverting* them.

This evidently supposes entering into the psychology of mourning. Some of his audience know enough of mourning, with its depressive aspect, to conceive that this communication made in a quite particular mode of relation — speech on the radio, addressed to a crowd of invisible auditors, an invisibility that, for the subject's imagination, addresses itself not only to those who listen but to all, to the living *as to the dead* — could have a particular weight.

The subject was evidently in an extremely conflictual relation: he could regret that his mother could not witness his success, while something in his discourse might be addressed to her — in this discourse addressed to invisible auditors.

In any case, the clearly *inverted, pseudo-manic* character of the subject's attitude, and its tight relation to the recent loss of this mother who represented for him the loss of a privileged object in his love-bonds, is manifestly the spring of the critical state in which he had arrived at the following session, immediately after his exploit: that he had realized, in spite of contrary circumstances, brilliantly, what he had pledged to do. The important thing is *not* this.

The important thing — quite manifest under the pen of someone far from a critical attitude toward a certain style of intervention — is that *the mode of interpretation on the basis of the intentional signification of the act of discourse in the present moment of the session* is subjected to all the relativities that the engagement of *the analyst's own ego* in the situation may entail.

What is important is not that the analyst himself was mistaken. Nothing even indicates that one can say it is counter-transference itself that is guilty in this interpretation manifestly refuted by the rest of the treatment. That the analyst himself experienced the sentiments he imputed his analysand felt toward him — not only can we admit this, it is exceedingly probable.

That he was *guided by these sentiments* in the interpretation he gave — this is not in itself something to be considered dangerous. It is the analyst's affair, since he is the only one being analysing in his own functioning, to *take account* of these sentiments opportunely, to enlighten himself by them as by another indicator-needle in his technique. It has never been said that the analyst must never experience sentiments toward his patient. One must say he must know how to *place* them, not give way to them, and use them in a technically well-situated manner.

It is because the analyst here believed he should seek first, *in the hic et nunc*, the reason of the patient's attitude — and believed he should find it in something effectively existing in the *intersubjective field* of the two persons — that the error occurred. He was well placed to know it, because indeed it was thus that he himself experienced the sentiment of hostility, or at least irritation, toward his patient's success.

What is grave is that he believed himself authorized by a certain technique to *use it directly, at once*. What is Lacan opposing here? He says that the analyst believed himself authorized to make what Lacan will call an interpretation *from ego to ego* — or *égal à égal* (equal to equal), permit the pun. An interpretation whose foundation and mechanism cannot in any way be distinguished from the mechanism of *projection*. When Lacan says projection, he does not mean *erroneous* projection.

There is a formula that, before becoming an analyst, Lacan with his slight psychological gifts had put at the base of the little compass with which he evaluated certain situations. He readily said to himself: *sentiments are always reciprocal*. This is absolutely true, despite appearances.

As soon as one puts in the field *two* subjects — Lacan says two, not three — the sentiments are always reciprocal. Therefore the analyst was *founded* in thinking that, given his having these sentiments, the corresponding sentiments could virtually be evoked in the other. Proof: the patient perfectly accepted the interpretation. When told "you are hostile because you think I am irritated against you," telling him sufficed to establish the sentiment. The sentiment was already validly there, since it sufficed to set the little spark to make it exist.

What is important, then, is that the subject's acceptance of the interpretation is *quite founded*, for the simple reason that, in a relation as intimate as that between analysand and analyst, the patient is sufficiently aware of the analyst's sentiments to be induced into something symmetrical. The question is whether a certain way of understanding the analysis of defenses does not lead us to a technique almost obligatorily generative of a certain *kind of error* — an error that is not one. An error, that is, of something that lies *before the true and the false*, something so obligatorily right and true that one cannot say whether it answers to truth: in any case it will be *verified*.

It is thus a matter of knowing why such a danger exists. Lacan thinks he can tell them. It is that in this kind of interpretation of defense, *from ego to ego*, however eventual its value, one must abstain. Even in such defense-interpretations there must always be at least — and this means not even sufficient — a *third term*. In reality one needs more, as he hopes to demonstrate. But today he is only opening the problem with some words that are important — concerning precisely these reciprocal functions of the egos of the subjects.

It is late. He cannot enter as far as he had wished into the problem of the relations of resistance and defense. He wishes nonetheless to give some indications. After showing them the problems and dangers of a certain technique of the analysis of defenses, he believes it necessary, after Mannoni's and Anzieu's expositions, to set down certain *principles*.

There is one quite clear thing that deserves reference as the starting point of a coordinated definition, in function of analysis, of the notion of resistance. Freud gave the first definition, Lacan thinks, in *The Interpretation of Dreams*. Those who can read German and have the Imago edition (and the English) will find it on page 521\. He flags that it is in Chapter VII, "Psychology of the Dream-Processes," first section, "Forgetting of Dreams." We have a decisive sentence: *Was immer die Fortsetzung der Arbeit stört, ist ein Widerstand* — whatever disturbs the continuation of the work is a resistance.

The "work" here is analytic work. In dream-analysis, it is not a question of symptoms — it is a question of *treatment*, of *Behandlung*: a treatment of an object, a treatment of something that passes through certain processes.

The French translation has rendered this as: every obstacle to interpretation comes from psychic resistance. Lacan signals this point, since it does not make things easy for those who have only Meyerson's sympathetic but courageous translation. It should inspire a salutary mistrust regarding a number of translations of Freud. The whole preceding paragraph is translated in this style.

The footnote in the German edition, immediately discussing the same point — asking whether, if the patient's father dies, this is a "resistance" (Freud will conclude in his characteristic way) — this footnote is *suppressed* in the French edition. Freud's German footnote points out that the proposed thesis would be easily misunderstood; it has, of course, only the meaning of a *technical rule*, an admonition to the analyst. It is not to be denied that various incidents may occur during an analysis that cannot be charged to the analysand's intention — his father may die without his having killed him; war may break out and put an end to the analysis. But behind the open exaggeration of the thesis there is a new and good sense. Even if the disturbing event is real and independent of the patient, it often depends on him *how much disturbing effect is granted to it*, and resistance shows itself unmistakably in the eager and excessive exploitation of such an occasion.

Indeed, this is what is at stake: everything that suspends or destroys the continuity of the treatment is resistance. Lacan thinks one must start from texts like this, suspend them in the mind, sift them, and see what they yield. What is at issue? The pursuit of the *Arbeit*, the work. Freud did not put *Behandlung* (which could be rendered as "the cure") but *Arbeit*. The work consists of a number of things: it can be defined by its form, by what happens in it — the verbal association determined by the rule, the *fundamental rule* of *free association*.

All this leads to the famous question: there is still this work; and indeed it is *only* this, since they are in the analysis of dreams, where it is evidently a question of the *revelation of the unconscious* and of nothing else at the level of dream-elaboration. Here they stand: at the revelation of the unconscious.

This already allows them to evoke a number of problems — in particular, the one Anzieu evoked earlier: this resistance, where does it come from? There is much to say. We have seen that there is no text in the *Studien* allowing us to consider it to come *as such* from the ego. And nothing in the elaboration of *The Interpretation of Dreams* indicates that it comes — in any way, much less exclusively — from what is called the *secondary process*, an important stage of Freud's thought.

Even in the years around 1916, when Freud publishes his first properly metapsychological article on *Repression* (*die Verdrängung*), we see *repression* appear — first indication — existing as: *Der Widerstand* \[is inversely proportional to die Entfernung\]. That is, at that moment resistance is conceived as something that occurs on the side of the conscious, but whose identity is essentially regulated by its *distance* (*Entfernung*) from what was originally repressed. The link between resistance and the content of the unconscious itself is still extremely sensible here. This article is from the *intermediate* period.

What was originally repressed, in the end? At this stage and up to this intermediate period, it is still and always the *past*. A past that must be *restituted* — and we cannot help reevoking the problems and ambiguities it raises regarding its definition, nature, and function, if we want to start from something solid in conceiving what Freud calls a "resistance."

Let us say that everything during this period — which is the *Wolf Man* period — when Freud asks what *trauma* is, the whole problem is bound for him to the realization that: (a) trauma is an extremely ambiguous notion; (b) the *eventual* notion of trauma is something in any case that cannot be put in question, since it appears according to clinical evidence that the *fantasmatic* face of trauma is infinitely more important — and from that moment the event passes to the second plane in the order of subjective references; but (c) the *dating* of trauma is something one must conserve *mordicus*, hold to firmly.

Those who followed Lacan's teaching on the *Wolf Man* must know it is only of this that it is a question in the *Wolf Man*. Who will ever know what he saw? But it is certain that what we do not know — whether he saw it or not — he cannot have seen except at such-and-such a date, and could not have seen it even a year later. It is only of this that it is a question. Lacan does not think he is betraying Freud's thought: it suffices to know how to read; it is written in black and white. He wants to show that the important thing can only be defined in the *perspective of history and recognition*.

For those unfamiliar with the dialectic Lacan has abundantly developed, he wishes to give some notions. One must always be at the level of the alphabet. He apologizes to those for whom this will be repetition. He will give an example to make clearly understood the problem of *recognition* — and how one cannot drown it in notions as confused as those of memory or remembering: *Erlebnis* (which can still have a sense in German), the French *souvenir* (lived or not lived) lends itself to all ambiguities.

He gives a little story: I wake one morning in my curtain, like *Semiramis*. I open my eye — this is a curtain I do not see every day, since it is the curtain of my country house, and I see it only every week or two — and I notice in the lines formed by the curtain's fringe, *once again* (I have only seen it once before like this), the profile, say, of a kind of face: sharp, caricatural and antiquated, what to me vaguely represents the style of an eighteenth-century marquis. (The silly fabulations the mind makes on waking.)

It is *because of this* — this gestaltist crystallization, as one would say today, this *recognition of a figure long known* (it would have been the same had it been a stain on the wall) — that he can say the curtain has not moved an inch: for exactly eight days before, on waking, he had seen the same thing. He had of course completely forgotten it. But it is because of this that he knows the curtain has not moved. It is always there, in exactly the same place.

This is an apologue: it takes place on the *imaginary* plane, though it would not be hard — and all the symbolic coordinates that around it represent silly things ("eighteenth-century marquis," etc.) play an important role; for if Lacan had not certain fantasies about what the profile represents, he would not have recognized it in the curtain's fringe.

But leave that aside. What this implies on the plane of *recognition* — namely that *this was indeed the case eight days ago* — is bound to a phenomenon of *recognition in the present*. This is exactly what Freud uses in the *Studien*, where he says there were some studies on memory at the time, and refers to evoked memories and to *recognition* — the actual present force that gives memory not necessarily its weight and density but simply its *possibility*. Freud proceeds thus: when he no longer knows to which saint to commend himself for the subject's reconstruction, he turns to the pressure of hands on the forehead, and enumerates to the subject all the years, months, weeks, even days — naming them one by one, "Tuesday the 17th, Wednesday the 18th, etc."

He trusts enough in what has since been defined regarding what memory is, what is called *socialized time*, the implicit structuration of the subject by this socialized time, to think that when he arrives at the point where the clock's hand will effectively cross (across this symbolization Freud makes of it) the subject's critical moment, the subject will say: oh yes — precisely that day, I remember something.

Lacan is not confirming whether it worked. Freud says it did. Do you grasp, Lacan asks, the import of what he is saying? In other words, the *center of gravity of the subject* is supposed by analytic technique from the origin. There is no occasion to demonstrate this is refuted at the end — for in truth, if it is not so, one does not see at all what analysis brings as new. The center of gravity of the subject *is* this present synthesis of the past called *history*.

It is to this we trust when we want to make the work progress. This is the first phase of things. Does it suffice? No, of course not. The subject's resistance is exerted on this plane. But this resistance, as you will see, manifests itself in a curious way that deserves to be defined and explored by particular cases.

Lacan recalls one: Freud had the whole story (the mother had told it to him). He communicated it to the subject, saying "here is what happened — here is what was done to you." Each time, the patient — hysterical — replied with a small hysterical crisis, a reproduction of her characteristic crisis. She listened and replied in her form of reply — by symptom. This poses small problems if we call this "resistance." It is a question Lacan opens for today.

What he wishes to end on: when Freud at the end of the *Studien* defines resistance as that inflection the discourse takes as it approaches the pathogenic nucleus — the something that bears what is sought and that repels the discourse, the something the discourse flees — what is it?

We cannot resolve these problems except by deepening the sense of this discourse. We have already said it is a *historical discourse*. But what we have not resolved is: *what is the link*? Let us not forget the starting point of this technique: a hypnotic technique. In hypnotism the subject holds this entire discourse. He holds it in a particularly striking way, dramatized — implying the presence of the auditor, essential. And this discourse, on coming out of hypnotism, he no longer remembers.

Nonetheless, this is the entry into the technique, since what was perceived was that *reviviscence of the trauma* was in itself and immediately, if not permanently, therapeutic. So this concerns the subject — this discourse held without further reflection by someone who can say "I" (*moi*). The least one can say (which does not escape you) is that the apparently *lived-again* character of the trauma in the hysterical second-state is an absolutely ambiguous way of speaking. It is not because it is dramatized, because it presents itself with pathos, that the word "relived" can satisfy us.

What does it mean — the subject's *assumption* of his own lived experience? If Lacan brings the problem to its most ambiguous point — namely the subject's second hypnotic state — it is because there it is evident that the question is posed. And it is exactly the same at all levels of analytic experience, exactly insofar as the question is posed: *what does a discourse signify*?

That we *force* the subject to establish himself in a certain parenthesis — that of the fundamental rule, which in the end tells him "your discourse has no importance" and even further implies that, from the moment he gives himself to this exercise, he only half-believes his own discourse, since he knows that at every instant he is under the cross-fire of our interpretation.

He expects this, then. The question is precisely: *who is the subject of the discourse?* On this they will resume next time and try to discuss, in relation to these fundamental problems, the signification and import of resistance.

---

## **Session of 3 February 1954**

Lacan opens by recalling that they had arrived last time at a point where they were asking: what is the nature of resistance? Today he wishes to make some remarks, to induce his audience into a certain mode of apprehending the phenomenon at the level of experience — at the moment when something happens in relation to a certain way of treating their vocabulary, which has several faces (without thereby being ambiguous). He wants to show how to recognize *at its source* what appears, in experience oriented toward analysis, to be resistance.

The audience has felt the ambiguity, and not merely the complexity, of the approach to the phenomenon one calls resistance. From several testimonies and formulations in Freud, it seems that resistance emanates from what is to be revealed — what is otherwise called the repressed, the *verdrängt*, or the *unterdrückt*. The first translators rendered *unterdrückt* as "stifled," which is rather soft. Are *verdrängt* and *unterdrückt* the same thing? Lacan will not enter into such details today. They will see them only once they begin to grasp the perspectives — to see the distinctions between these phenomena establish themselves.

He wishes to lead his audience today to a point that seems, in the very texts they have been commenting on — these little *Papers on Technique* — to be one of those points where the perspective is established. Before manipulating vocabulary, as always, it is a matter of trying to *understand*, of being in a place where things order themselves.

At Friday's case presentation he announced something, and he will try to keep his promise. There is something, right in the middle of this collection, called *The Dynamics of Transference*. Like all the articles translated in the collection, one cannot say there is reason to be entirely satisfied with the translation. There are singular inexactitudes, going to the limit of impropriety. There are astonishing ones, and they all go in the same direction: to *efface the edges* of the text. To those who know German, Lacan strongly recommends turning to the German text — they will see many things in this article on the dynamics of transference.

There is much to say about the translation, and in particular a *cut*: a period inserted at the penultimate line, which isolates a tiny phrase that appears to come out of nowhere — "Let us recall that no one can be killed *in absentia* or *in effigie*." Whereas in the German text it is articulated to the last sentence: "for one must recall that no one can be killed in absentia or in effigie." The phrase is fully articulated, not isolated.

This passage, which Lacan has announced as particularly significant, he wishes to read. It seems to articulate directly with what he was trying to introduce them to by recalling the important passage in the *Studien*, in the psychotherapy article, about resistance encountered "in the radial sense" (as Freud says) of the subject's discourse when it approaches the deep nucleus — what Freud calls the *pathogenic nucleus*.

It is irritating, Lacan says, to have to read it in French. He reads the French translation of Freud's passage: "Let us study a pathogenic complex in its sometimes very apparent and sometimes almost imperceptible manifestation..." Lacan notes the German wording: *Verfolgt man nun einen pathogenen Komplex von seiner (entweder als Symptom auffälligen oder auch ganz unscheinbaren)*. If one knows the German text, one can render this as "its manifestation," but the parenthetical phrase that the French translation places outside the parentheses changes the emphasis.

What is at stake is the way the complex *translates itself*, and it is of this translation that one speaks when calling it apparent or imperceptible. This is not the same thing as saying the complex itself is so. There is a displacement that suffices to introduce a kind of floating.

Lacan continues quoting Freud: from its manifestation in consciousness down to its roots in the unconscious, we soon arrive at a region where resistance makes itself felt so clearly that the association which then surges forth bears its mark — the mark of this resistance — and appears to us as a *compromise* between the demands of the resistance and those of the work of investigation. Lacan corrects: it is not "the association that surges forth" but *nächste Einfall* — the nearest, the next, association. The sense is preserved.

He continues: experience — and this is the capital point — shows that *it is here that the transference arises*. When something in the elements of the complex (its content) is susceptible of being carried over onto the person of the physician, the transference takes place, furnishes the next idea, and manifests itself as a resistance, by an arrest of associations for example. Such experience teaches us that the transference-idea has succeeded in gliding through to consciousness in preference to all other association-possibilities *precisely because it satisfies the resistance*. Freud emphasizes this italicized point.

Freud continues: a fact of this kind reproduces itself an *incalculable number of times* in the course of an analysis. Each time one approaches a pathogenic complex, it is first the part of the complex capable of becoming a transference that finds itself pushed toward consciousness — and the patient persists in defending it with the greatest tenacity.

The two elements of this paragraph to highlight, Lacan says, are: first — we arrive soon at a region where resistance makes itself clearly felt, so we are in the register where resistance properly emanates from the very process — the *approximation* of the discourse. Second — experience shows that *it is here that the transference arises*. Third — the transference is produced *precisely because it satisfies the resistance*. Fourth — such a phenomenon reproduces itself an incalculable number of times in psychoanalysis.

It is indeed an observable, sensible phenomenon in analysis. This part of the complex that has manifested itself in the form of transference finds itself, at this moment, pushed toward consciousness, and the patient defends it with the greatest tenacity. Here a note is appended that brings the phenomenon into relief — a phenomenon that is indeed observable, sometimes with an extraordinary purity, marked by the order of interventions suggested by practice and the recipes that may have been transmitted to us, directly emanating from another Freudian text: when the patient falls silent, there is every chance that the tarrying of his discourse is due to some thought relating to the analyst.

This often translates, in technical handling, into the question: "you doubtless have some idea more or less relating to me, or something not far from it?" — an automatism Lacan has had to teach his students to *moderate* and *restrain*. Freud's German footnote here cautions, in turn, that one should not generally infer a particular pathogenic importance from the element chosen for transference-resistance. If in a battle the combatants struggle bitterly for a small church or a particular farm, one need not assume that the church is a national sanctuary or that the house shelters the army's treasure: the value of the objects may be merely *tactical*, of significance only for this one battle.

This solicitation, Lacan continues, will in certain cases crystallize the patient's discourse in remarks concerning the analyst's bearing, face, furniture, or the way he received the patient that day. But this is not without foundation: something of that order may indeed inhabit the patient's mind at the moment. There is a great diversity of relations established in what one can thus extract by inciting the patient to direct his associations and focus them on a certain orientation.

But one observes, among the "incalculable number of times," sometimes something infinitely more *pure*. At the moment when the subject seems on the verge of manifesting, of formulating something closer, more authentic, more burning than has ever been reached in the truth of the subject, the subject *interrupts himself* — and is capable, in certain cases, of manifesting, of formulating in words, something like: "I realize, suddenly, at this moment, the *fact of your presence*."

This is something that has happened to Lacan more than once in his own experience, and to which analysts can easily testify. There is something here that establishes itself in connection with the sensible, concrete manifestation of resistance, which intervenes among all these facts in function of transference, at the level of the very tissue of analytic experience.

It takes on a quite elective value because the subject himself feels it as a kind of *sudden swerve* of the discourse. He is not capable, owing to the characteristic subjective aspect of the phenomenon, of giving testimony of it as such, but he simultaneously manifests this testimony as the expression of something else — a sudden turn that makes him pass from one slope to the other of the discourse, almost from one accent to another of the function of *speech*.

Lacan will resume. He simply wanted to put the phenomenon clearly before them, centered, focalized, as he considers it illuminating for today's concern. Before continuing, he wants to stop again at Freud's text, to show how, where Freud himself signals it, what Lacan is speaking of is the same thing Freud speaks of. They must momentarily disengage from the idea that resistance is something *coherent* with the whole construction that makes the unconscious — in a given subject at a given moment — contained and (as is said) "repressed."

It is a phenomenon Freud *localizes, focalizes* in analytic experience, whatever extension may later be given to the term "resistance" in its relations and connections with the ensemble of defenses. This is why the little note Lacan is going to add is important. There, Freud puts the dots on the *i*'s: it should not be concluded that the element chosen for the transference-resistance has any particular pathogenic importance. The interest of the objects may be merely *tactical*, existing only for this one combat.

You see clearly the phenomenon at stake, Lacan says: it is something connected with that movement by which the subject *avows himself*. In this movement, Freud says, something appears that *is* resistance. When this resistance becomes too strong, that is the moment when *the transference arises*. It is a *fact*, Lacan emphasizes — Freud does not say "phenomenon"; the text is precise. If Freud had said "a phenomenon of transference appears," he would have put it; but he did not. The proof that he *would* have put it is at the end of this text, in the last sentence — which in French has been mistranslated as "let us avow that nothing is more difficult in analysis than to *vanquish the resistances*," whereas the text says: *die Bezwingung der Übertragungsphänomene* — "the forcing of the phenomena of transference."

Lacan does not know why "phenomena of transference" was translated as "resistance." *Übertragungsphänomene* is Freud's own vocabulary. Why was it translated as resistance? It is not a sign of great culture — or of great understanding.

What Freud wrote is that it is precisely there that there arises, not the *phenomenon of transference* (he ought to know what he says), but rather *something* in essential relation with transference. As for the rest, the entire article is about the dynamics of transference. This is the central point of all the questions Freud poses in this article — questions concerning the *specificity of the function of transference* in analysis: the fact that transference is there not as it is everywhere else, but plays a quite particular function in analysis. Here is the heart, the pivot of this article, which Lacan recommends one read.

He brings it in support of a certain central question about resistance. From a certain moment on: *who is it that speaks?* What does this reconquest, this *retrouvaille* of the unconscious, mean? They have posed the question of what memory, remembrance, the technique of this remembrance signifies — and what free association signifies, insofar as it permits us by a certain path to accede to a certain formulation of something that is the *history of the subject*. But what becomes of the subject? Is it always the same subject in question across this progress? Here they are before a phenomenon in which they grasp something — a *knot*, a connection, an original constriction, or rather, properly speaking, a resistance in this progress.

And they see, at a certain point of this resistance, something produced that Freud calls *the transference* — that is, at that moment, the actualization in a certain sense of the *person of the analyst* — and of that something Lacan extracted from his own experience: at the most sensible and significant point of the phenomenon, the subject feels it as the brusque perception of something not easy to define — the lived phenomenon, the *sentiment of presence*.

This is something we do not have all the time, it must be said. We are influenced by all sorts of presences; our world truly has its consistency, its density, its lived stability only because in a certain manner we take account of these presences. But to *realize them as such* is, Lacan says, something whose life we constantly tend to efface. Things would not even be easy to conduct if at every instant we felt presence in all that it implies, and in the deepest depth of what it implies — *mystery* — a mystery we tend rather to put aside, and to which, all said, we are *accustomed*.

This, Lacan thinks, is something we cannot dwell on too long; we will try to take it up, to recognize it, from other ends. What Freud teaches us — the proper analytic method — consists always in *retrieving the same rapport, the same relation, the same schema* in different lived forms: occasional behaviors, and everything that happens within the analytic relation, at "different levels."

Retaining this point, we must try to establish what is called a *perspective* — a kind of perception of a depth of separation among several planes — and to see that what we are accustomed, by certain handling and notions (our "labels"), to posit in massive and rigorous fashion (as the Id and the Ego, for instance) is perhaps not simply a contrasted pair.

A more complex *stereoscopy* spreads itself out. For those who attended Lacan's commentary on the *Wolf Man* — already a year and a half ago — he wishes to recall certain very striking things from that text.

When they arrive at the moment when Freud addresses the question of the castration complex in this subject — something that emerges at various places in the observation but is in an extremely particular *functional* relation to the subject's structuration — Freud poses certain questions both to himself and to us.

Notably: at the moment when castration-fear comes into question in this subject, we see appear a whole series of *anal-plane* symptoms — all sorts of intestinal manifestations — and the question Freud comes to pose is this: we interpret all these symptoms in the register of the so-called *anal conception of sexual relations*, a certain stage of the infantile theory of sexuality.

How does this happen, since by the very fact that castration has entered into play, the subject has at that moment elevated himself to a level of *genital* structure? This is his theory of sexuality. Freud explains something quite singular here.

He explains: when the subject has arrived, by way of various elements (chief among them *maturation*), at an initial infantile or prematurational stage where he is ripe to realize at least partially a more specifically *genital* structuration of the interpersonal relations with his parents — the mechanisms that intervene to make this subject *refuse* the homosexual position which is his in this relation, this realization of the Oedipal situation, the subject *refuses*, *rejects* — the German word is *verwirft* — everything from this plane, precisely the plane of genital realization.

He returns to his anterior verification of this affective relation; he falls back on the positions of the anal theory of sexuality. In other words, what is at issue is something that is *not even a repression* in the sense of something that would have been realized on a certain plane and then pushed back. Repression, Freud says, is something else: *Eine Verdrängung ist etwas anderes als eine Verwerfung*.

The French translation — by persons whose intimacy with Freud should perhaps have illuminated them a little more, but it is doubtless not enough to have borne a relic of an eminent personality to be authorized to make oneself its keeper — renders this as: "a repression is something other than a *judgment* that rejects and chooses."

Why translate *Verwerfung* by "judgment"? Lacan concedes it is hard to translate, but still — the French language. Hyppolite suggests: *rejet* (rejection). Lacan: yes, *rejet*, or on occasion *refus*. Why introduce *judgment* here? Is this the theory of judgment? Nowhere in this paragraph is there a trace of *Urteil*. Three pages further on Freud says *kein Urteil über* — "no judgment about." This is the *first* time *Urteil* comes in, and it is in conclusion. But here there is none. No judgment has been brought to bear on the *existence* of the problem of castration. *Aber es war so* — but things were so — *als ob sie nicht existierte* — as if it did not exist.

In the order of the question we are posing — of what resistance is, of what repression is — this important articulation shows us at the origin a *final* something that must exist for repression even to be possible: namely, *something other*, a *beyond* of this history in which already, from the origin, something has been primitively constituted that not only does not avow itself but, by not formulating itself, is literally as if it did not exist — yet is nonetheless, in a certain sense, *somewhere*.

For what Freud tells us everywhere is that it is this *first nucleus of the repressed* that is the *center of attraction* — that calls to itself all subsequent repressions. If this is not said apropos of resistance, it is said under every form. It is the very essence of his discovery: in the end there is no need to invoke any innate predisposition (though he admits one occasionally as a general framework); he never uses such predisposition to explain how a repression of such-and-such a type — hysterical or obsessional — comes about.

Read *Bemerkungen über die Neuropsychosen*, the second 1898 article on the neuro-psychoses of defense. If repression sometimes takes certain forms, it is by reason of the *attraction of the first nucleus of the repressed*, which is due, at that moment, to a certain experience Freud calls "the originary traumatic experience."

A question to be taken up later: what does "trauma" mean? It has had to be relativized in a particular way — and the question of the imaginary... all this is interesting.

But this primitive nucleus is something situated *elsewhere* than in the stages and avatars of repression. It is somehow its ground and support. Lacan suspends for a moment the theme of the *Wolf Man* — he will return to it — for in the structure of what happens to the *Wolf Man*, the quite singular moment of the *Verwerfung*, the realization of experience *as genital*, has a quite particular fate, which Freud himself differentiates from all others.

Now, singularly, this something somehow *excluded* from all that belongs to the subject's history, from all the subject is able to *say*, is the spring of this observation on this subject. It required Freud's forcing — the technique employed — to come to terms with it, that is, for the repeated experience of the infantile dream to take on its sense and permit not the reliving but the *reconstruction* of the subject's history directly. They shall see whether — and what — has appeared in the subject's history. He suspends this for now.

Let us take things at another end, of what Freud has taught us to see — let us take the *Traumdeutung*. And let us take it at the beginning of the part on dream-processes, *Traumvorgänge* (chapter VII), the first part where Freud consents to relate everything that emerges from all he has elaborated in this fundamental book — the chapter that begins with this magnificent phrase: it is very difficult to render through the description of a *succession* the *simultaneity* of a complicated process, and at the same time to appear to broach each new exposition without preconceived ideas.

This phrase, Lacan notes, represents the very difficulties he himself has, here, in taking up ceaselessly this problem always present in their experience. He must, under various forms, manage each time to create it under a new and seemingly isolated angle.

What does Freud tell us in the first part of the study of dream-processes — that is, at the level of the chapter on the phenomenon of forgetting? One must read these texts; one must each time make oneself the *innocent*: there is here truly something, a progress, where one feels — touches with one's finger — something quite singular.

Apropos of the forgetting of dreams and its sense, Freud approaches this phenomenon by way of all the objections one can raise about the validity of dream-remembrance: what is this dream? Is the subject's reconstitution of it exact? We have no guarantee that something else — call it ulterior verbalization — is not mixed in. Is not every dream a kind of instantaneous thing to which the subject's speech reconstitutes an entire history?

Freud sets all these objections aside, showing they are not well-founded and that this is not the point. He shows, with increasing emphasis, the quite singular fact that *the more uncertain the text the subject gives, the more significant it is*. It is precisely on the doubt the subject bears toward certain parts of the dream that he, the analyst, awaiting and listening, there to reveal its sense, will see that *this is the important part* — because the subject doubts it, one must be sure of it. As the chapter advances, the procedure thins to the point where, at the limit, almost, the most significant dream would be the dream *completely forgotten* and of which the subject could say nothing. It goes that far — for in the end this is more or less what Freud says: one can often retrieve through analysis everything forgetting has lost; in a whole series of cases, a few scraps allow one to retrieve not the dream (which is accessory) but the thoughts at its base.

"A few scraps" — that is what Lacan is telling them; nothing remains. But what interests Freud? Here we evidently fall on these *thoughts at the base*. Each time we speak of the term "thought," there is nothing more difficult to handle for people who have learned psychology. As we have learned psychology, "thoughts" will become something like what we roll over and over as people accustomed to thinking.

But perhaps these *base-thoughts* are not what one means in studies on the phenomenology of thought — thought without images, with images, etc. — the things we currently call thought. For what is at issue throughout is a *desire*.

And God knows that this desire, we have learned to notice, is in the course of this research like a singular *ferret* we see disappear and reappear through a kind of game of pass-the-thimble. In the end, we do not always know whether it is on the side of the unconscious or of the conscious, as in the chapter on *regression*.

This desire must still conceal some questions, and after all some mystery. For when one looks closely at the desire at issue, it poses nothing less than the question Lacan had posed at the end of last session: *whose desire*? And of *what lack*, especially? But the important thing is what we see here. And what we see is at once illustrated by an example. Lacan takes only this one, from a little note Freud extracts from the *Vorlesungen* (the *Introductory Lectures*).
