Freud speaks of a patient at once skeptical of and very interested in him, who after a rather long dream — during which (she says) certain persons speak to her of his book on the *Witz*, the joke, and say good things about it — and all this, as is manifest, does not seem very rich. Then it is a question of something, and all that remains of the dream is: "canal." Perhaps another book where the word "canal" appears, something about a canal — she does not know, it is quite obscure. Freud takes this as the example of a dream analysis. There remains "canal," and one does not know what it refers to, where it comes from, or where it goes — perhaps from a book or from something else, but one does not know what.

Well, this is what is *most interesting*, Freud says — when one has not just some scrap, but a tiny scrap with an aura of *uncertainty* around it. And what comes of it? Not the most interesting thing Lacan will tell them, but it gives the whole story.

The next day, she reports she has an idea attaching itself to this: it is precisely a *joke*. A crossing from Dover to Calais, an Englishman and a Frenchman, in the course of conversation the Englishman cites the famous saying: *Du sublime au ridicule, il n'y a qu'un pas* — from the sublime to the ridiculous there is but a step. And the Frenchman, gallantly: "Oui, *le pas de Calais*" — yes, the Strait of Calais.

This is particularly nice for the interlocutor. The *pas de Calais* is the English Channel — the canal. We find the canal again. And what does this give us? Lacan says it must be seen: this has exactly the same function as that *surge at the moment of resistances*.

It is evidently this: the skeptical patient had at length debated Freud's merit on the *Witz*. After her discussion, at the moment when her conviction, her discourse hesitates — she does not know where to go — exactly the same phenomenon appears, like what Mannoni said the other day (which Lacan found very felicitous), speaking as an obstetrician: *the resistance presents itself by the transferential end*.

"From the sublime to the ridiculous, there is but a step" — this is the point where the dream *hooks onto the auditor*, who is, for Freud, the analyst. Obviously "canal" is not much, but after associations it is, somehow, indisputable.

After this little example Lacan would like to take others. If they extended their investigation, they would see quite singular things — in particular the close connection manifested by this whole chapter (Freud, God knows, is sensitive in his grouping of facts). It is no accident that things come to be grouped in certain chapters. Note, for example, how at the moment when the dream takes a certain orientation, there occur in the dream phenomena specifically of the *linguistic* order: a linguistic *fault* made by the subject (consciously), the subject knowing in the dream that it is a linguistic fault, a personage intervening to correct him. This puts in accord and harmony with that moment the phenomenon of *adaptation to something of the discourse* and an adaptation at a critical point — an adaptation that realizes itself not only badly but *doubles itself* under our eyes.

Lacan sets this aside for the moment. Let us take something he picked this morning a bit at random — a famous thing Freud published in 1898\. In the first chapter of *Psychopathology of Everyday Life*, Freud refers, apropos of the forgetting of names, to the trouble he once had on a journey to evoke the name of the author of the famous Orvieto fresco — that vast composition in the cathedral manifesting the phenomena awaited for the end of the world and what turns around the appearance of the Antichrist.

What Freud wants to retrieve is *Signorelli*. He cannot. Others come instead: *Botticelli*, *Boltraffio* — not it. He arrives at it by an analytic procedure. He then takes it as an example. It does not surge from the void: it is inserted in a text — Freud is in relation with a gentleman, they are speaking. The antecedents are quite interesting.

They are at that moment going from Ragusa toward the interior of Dalmatia — at the limit of the Austrian Empire, in *Bosnia-Herzegovina*. The word "Bosnia" comes up in connection with anecdotes, and "Herzegovina" too. Then come remarks on the particularly sympathetic disposition of a certain Muslim clientele toward a certain primitive perspective — the extraordinarily decent way these people, fully integrated into Islamic culture, react at the doctor's announcement of very bad news (that the disease is incurable). Freud's interlocutor seems to be a physician practicing in the region. The people show no hostility but turn to him saying: *Herr\! If something could have been done, you would certainly have been able to do it.* And in the presence then of something one must accept, the courteous, measured, respectful attitude toward the physician — called *Herr* in German.

All this forms the background on which the conversation continues, with the *significant forgetting* that will punctuate and propose its problem to Freud. Freud shows us that he himself began to take part in part of this conversation, and the fact is that from a certain moment his attention was turned elsewhere — while telling the story, he was thinking of something else. And this other thing had been brought to him by this medical story, by the attitude of these sympathetic clients, and by something that had come back to mind on two themes.

First: he knew the price these patients (especially Muslims) attached to everything sexual — he had literally heard one say "if one has lost this, life is no longer worth living" — a patient who had consulted for sexual potency problems.

Second: in one of the places he had stayed, he had learned of the death of one of his patients, whom he had treated for a long time — something one does not learn without a shock.

He had not wanted to express these things, since he was not very sure of his interlocutor regarding the valorization of sexual processes, and he had not willingly let his thought stop on the subject of this patient's death. He says he withdrew all his attention from what he was saying.

Freud makes a small diagram (one can find it in the Imago edition). He writes all the names: *Botticelli*, *Boltraffio*, *Herzegovina*, *Signorelli* — and below, the repressed thoughts: the syllable *Herr*, the question. The result is, in some sense, what is *left*: the word "Signor" called up by "Herr" (in the polite Muslim address); "Traffio" called up by the shock of the bad news about his patient (the place-name *Trafoi*). And if Freud could retrieve, at the moment when his discourse went to retrieve the painter of the Orvieto fresco, *what was left available* — given that certain radical elements had been called by what Freud calls the repressed (the ideas about Muslims' sexual histories, and the theme of death) — this is what is salvaged.

What is to be said? The repressed was not so repressed, since Freud gives it to us at once in his discourse — though he had not spoken of it to his traveling companion. Everything happens as if these words — and one can indeed speak of *words*, even if they are parts of words, since these *vocables* have a life as individual words — these words are *the part of the discourse Freud truly had to hold*. Freud tells us so explicitly: this is what I did *not* say.

But what he did not say was, at bottom, what he himself was beginning to say; that is what interested him, what he was *prepared* to say to his interlocutor. By having not said it, what remained for the continuation of his connection with this same interlocutor? Only debris, fragments — the *fall-offs*, so to speak, of this speech.

Do you see how *complementary* the phenomenon at the level of reality is to what happens at the level of the dream? What we are witnessing is, with respect to a *veridical speech* — and God knows how far this veridical speech could resound — for what is at issue with it is the *absolute* of which it speaks: namely *death*, present here, exactly the thing before which (Freud tells us) it was not simply because of his interlocutor that he had preferred not to confront it too directly.

And God knows the problem of *death for the physician* is lived also as a problem of mastery: he has, after all, in this affair *lost*. It is thus we always feel the loss of the patient, especially when we have treated him long. Well, what *decapitates* the name *Signor-elli* — for everything concentrates around the first part of this name, with all its semantic resonance — is this: *to the extent that speech is not said, speech can reveal the deepest secret of Freud's being*; to the extent that it is not said, *he can no longer hold onto the other except by the fall-offs of this speech*. There was something of which only the debris remain.

The phenomenon of forgetting is here manifested in what is literally a *degradation of speech in its relation to the other*. And it is here Lacan wishes to arrive, through all these examples, at this ambiguous signification — and the word *ambiguous* is valid — that *it is precisely insofar as the subject's avowal of his being does not reach its term that something is produced by which speech literally falls wholly onto the side where it hooks onto the other*.

Lacan says it is ambiguous, because it is not foreign to the *essence* of speech, so to speak, to hook onto the other. Speech is exactly that: it is *mediation* — and this is above all what Lacan has been teaching them — it is mediation between the subject and the other. And this mediation implies, of course, the *realization of the other in the mediation itself*: that speech can unite us to him is essential. This is the side Lacan has always insisted on, because it is within this we constantly move.

But, on the other side: this speech — and Lacan emphasizes, in Freud's perspective, that we cannot say *expression*. He wrote everything in *Function and Field of Speech* without — intentionally — using the term "expression." It is impossible not to see that all of Freud's work unfolds in the direction of *revelation*, not expression. The unconscious is not *expressed*; if at all, then by *Entstellung* — distortion, transposition. In the whole sense of the Freudian discovery, there is something to *reveal*.

This other side of speech — which is revelation and the final spring of what we seek in analytic experience — produces precisely this: at the moment something we call resistance (and which is just what we are seeking the sense of) intervenes, *it is to the extent that speech does not say itself*...

As is curiously written at the end of an article that is among the worst things ever — but so innocent and candid — Sterba's article *The Fate of the Ego in the Therapeutic Process*, which centers all analytic experience around this *doubling of the ego*, one half coming to our aid against the other going in the contrary direction. At the end, Sterba can no longer get out. Everything is in *what comes to speech, what is pushed toward speech*.

This *coming-to-speech*, insofar as something perhaps makes it fundamentally impossible — this is the essential pivot where, in analysis, speech, so to speak, *tilts wholly* onto its function of *relation to the other*; everything is at the level where this *hooking-onto-the-other* is produced.

For, to be as obtused as one can be by certain ways of theorizing and dogmatizing and regimenting in analytic technique — as if anyone, by his prior formation, could be more open than another to valorizing this existential rapport of the subject to the analyst — to have told us one day that one of the preconditions of analytic treatment is that the subject have a certain *realization of the other as such*. Of course, smart aleck\! But it is simply a question of knowing at *what level* this other is realized, *how*, in what function, in what circle of his subjectivity, at what *distance* this other lies. And we know that across analytic experience this distance varies ceaselessly — and pretending to consider it as a certain stage, a certain phase of the subject...

It is this same spirit that makes Mr. Piaget speak of the supposed *egocentric* notion of the child's world — as if adults on this subject had something to teach kids\! Lacan would like to know what weighs more in the balance of the Eternal as a better apprehension of the other: Piaget's, in his position of professor and at his age, or the child's? This child whom we see so prodigiously open to everything an adult brings him about the sense of the world.

This child, when one reflects on it, what does this sense of the other signify with respect to this perspective — this prodigious permeability of the child to all myths, legends, fairy tales, stories — this way of letting himself *literally be invaded*. Does one think this is compatible with the little cube-games by which Piaget shows us he accedes to a fully Copernican knowledge of the world? This is what is at stake.

It is a matter of knowing how, at that moment, there points toward this other what can be summed up as this most mysterious and essential *sentiment of presence* — which can also be integrated into all the prior structurations Freud speaks of in this text: not only the love-life but the organization of the subject's *world*. The first inflection of this speech — as soon as the realization of the subject's truth inflects itself in its curve — this first reinflection, Lacan would take from a formula given him by one of his analysands, whose analysis he supervises. He had asked the supervisee: "where is your patient regarding you this week?" And the formula given coincided exactly with what Lacan had tried to situate: "He took me as witness."

This is indeed one of the highest, but already deflected, functions of speech: *the taking-as-witness*. A little further along, *seduction*. Further still, the attempt to *capture* the other in a game where speech passes (as analytic experience has shown) to another function, more symbolic, a deeper instinctual satisfaction. Not to mention the final term: the complete *disorganization* of the function of speech in the transference-phenomena Freud stops at as a point where the subject liberates himself entirely and does exactly as he pleases.

In the end, what this consideration brings us back to is what Lacan began from in his *Report* on *Function and Field of Speech*: the opposition and entire gamut of realizations between *full speech* and *empty speech*. Full speech: speech insofar as it realizes the truth of the subject. Empty speech: speech in which the subject loses himself among the machinations of the system of language and all the reference systems given him by the cultural state in which he has more or less partaken — relative to what he has to do *hic et nunc* with his analyst.

The question directly introduced by today's stopping-point on this phenomenon leads exactly here: this *resistance* projects itself indeed — in its fruits, in its results — onto the *system of the ego*, insofar as the system of the ego is not even conceivable without the system, so to speak, of *the other*. This ego is exactly *referential to the other*; this ego constitutes itself by relation to the other. It is exactly correlative, and the level at which the other is *lived* situates exactly the level at which the ego, literally, exists for the subject.

Resistance indeed incarnates itself in this system of the ego-and-the-other. It realizes itself there at this or that moment of analysis — but it *starts* from elsewhere: from the *impotence* of the subject to come to terms within this domain of the realization of his *truth*. It is at every instant, in a way doubtless more or less already defined for a determinate subject by reason of the fixations of his character and structure, that at a certain level this act of speech comes to project itself, in a certain relation of ego to other, at a certain level, in a certain style of relation to the other.

What does this mean? You see: from this moment, what is the paradox? See the paradox of the analyst's position. It is precisely at the moment when the subject's speech is the *fullest* that I as analyst could intervene. But intervene on what? On his *discourse*. And the more it is *his*, the more I center myself on his discourse. But the inverse is equally true: the more his discourse is *empty*, the more I too am led to *catch hold of him* — that is, to do what is done all the time in this famous "analysis of the resistances": to seek the *beyond* of the subject's discourse — a beyond (reflect well) that is *nowhere*, that is *not there*, that the subject *has to* realize but precisely has not realized — that is, this beyond which is in sum *made of my own projections* at the level on which the subject is realized.

Lacan showed last time the *dangers* of making these intentional interpretations or imputations, verified or not — or not even susceptible to verification — that are no more verifiable than any system of projections that always more or less partakes of it.

This is the difficulty of analysis: when we say we are doing the *interpretation of the resistances*, we are precisely facing this difficulty. How to operate at a certain level of *lesser density* of the speech-relation? How to operate in this inter-psychology, ego and *alter ego*, into which we are placed by the very degradation of the speech-process? In other words: what possible relations are there between a certain function of *intervention by speech* — *interpretation*, to call it by its name — and the level of *ego* insofar as this level always implies correlatively *both analysand and analyst*?

This is the question. From a certain moment, at a certain level, the function of speech has tilted so wholly onto the side of the other that it is no longer mediation but only *implicit violence*, *reduction of the other to his function in relation to the subject's ego* — what can we still do to validly handle speech in analytic experience?

You feel the absolutely oscillating character of the problem and how it brings us back to questions that are, in the end, these: what does it mean for man, this support taken in the other? And why does the other become *less truly other* the more exclusively the support is taken there? It is from this vicious circle that one must emerge in analysis. And why are we, so to speak, the more taken in it the more the history of technique shows an ever-greater accent on this problem, insofar as one accentuates the *ego-side* of resistances?

It is the same problem expressed another way: why does the subject *alienate himself* the more he affirms himself *as ego*? And we return to the question of the previous session: *who is it, then, who beyond the ego seeks to make himself recognized*?

---

## **Session of 10 February 1954**

Lacan opens by recalling that those present last time were able to hear him pursue a development on the central passage of Freud's *Dynamics of Transference*. He reminds those who were absent that his whole development consisted in showing that the *major phenomenon of transference* is something that starts from what one might call *the ground of the movement of resistance*.

That is: the moment when something that remains masked in analytic theory by all its forms and paths — namely resistance *in its most essential ground* — manifests itself in that movement Lacan has called the *tilting of speech toward the presence* of the auditor and witness who is the analyst. We grasp it, so to speak, in its pure state in the moment when the subject *interrupts himself* — and one knows that this moment is most often the most significant of his approach toward truth, in a kind of sentiment, frequently tinged with anxiety, of the analyst's presence.

He has also shown them, or indicated, that the analyst's question — which, because Freud indicated it, has for some become almost automatic ("are you thinking of something concerning me, the analyst?") — is only a kind of *activism* ready to crystallize a discourse more oriented toward the analyst. But it merely manifests this fact: insofar as the discourse does not arrive at the *full speech* in which the unconscious ground of the subject must reveal itself, *already the discourse in itself addresses itself to the analyst*, interests him, is made to interest him — and is manifested in that alienated form of being which is identical to what is called the subject's *ego*.

In other words: the relation of the ego to the other, the rapport of the subject to this other-himself, this *fellow* in relation to whom the subject was first formed — this constitutes an essential structure of human constitution, and is the *imaginary function* from which we can understand, conceive, explain what the ego is in analysis.

Lacan does not say "what the ego is" in the sense of psychology's *function of synthesis*, the form in which it can be followed and seen to manifest itself, but in its *dynamic function in analysis*: the ego insofar as it manifests itself there as *defense*, *refusal*; insofar as it inscribes in some way the whole history of the subject's successive oppositions to the integration of what is afterward called "his tendencies," "his drives" — the deepest and most misrecognized.

In other words: we grasp in these moments so well indicated by Freud how *the very movement of analytic experience rejoins the function of fundamental misrecognition of the ego*. We are thus led, at the end of this demonstration whose spring he has shown, to the sensitive point of Freud's investigation on all sorts of other planes.

He has shown them this apropos of what for Freud manifests itself as the very essence of *dream-analysis*. And he showed it there, graspable under an almost paradoxical form: how for Freud the analysis of the dream is the analysis literally *of something that in his investigation has the function of speech*. And how this is demonstrated by the fact that what Freud grasps as the last trace of a vanishing dream is precisely at the moment when it turns entirely toward him (Freud) — that it is at this point, when it is no more than a trace, a debris of the dream, that we find that *transferential point* by which the dream models itself on an identical movement, that significant interruption manifested elsewhere as the *turning-point of a moment of the analytic session*.

He also showed them the signification of the relation between *unspoken speech* — speech because refused, *verworfen*, properly speaking rejected by the subject — and the *proper weight* of speech in a fact of slip or, more precisely, *forgetting of a word*: the example drawn from the *Psychopathology of Everyday Life*, and how there too the mechanism is sensible — of what the subject's speech *should have formulated* and of what *remains* to address itself to the other. In the present case: what is missing is the subtraction of a word, *Herr*, from the vocable *Signorelli*, which he can no longer evoke an instant later, precisely with the interlocutor before whom potentially this word *Herr* had been called with its full signification.

We are thus led, around this revealing moment of the fundamental relation of resistance and the dynamic of the movement of analytic experience, to a question that can be polarized between two terms: *the ego* and *speech*.

Something so little deepened in this relation — which yet should be the object of essential investigation — that somewhere, under Fenichel's pen, for example, we find: it is by the ego (incontestably, held somehow as acquired, given) that the sense of words comes to the subject. Yet does one need to be an analyst to find such a remark could be — at the least — open to contestation?

Can one even say that in our present discourse, admitting that the ego is what (as is said) *directs* our motor manifestations — consequently the issue of those vocables called words — can one say even that in this act *the ego is master of all that words conceal*?

Is not the symbolic system, formidably *intricate*, criss-crossed, marked by that *Verschlungenheit* (something impossible to translate other than as *property of entanglements* — and that the translator of the *Papers on Technique* renders as "complexity," which is how feeble), something that infinitely exceeds the momentary intention we can put into it?

The translator's "complexity" misses what *Verschlungenheit* designates: linguistic entanglement. Any easily isolated linguistic symbol is *solidary* not only with the ensemble, but cuts across itself, constitutes itself by a whole series of *affluences*, of oppositional overdeterminations that situate it simultaneously in several *registers*. To say it all: precisely, this *system of language* in which our discourse moves — is it not something that infinitely exceeds every momentary intention we may put into it?

And how precisely on this function of *resonance*, of ambiguity, of communications, of richnesses already implied in the symbolic system as constituted by the tradition we are inserted into as individuals (much more than we *spell out* or *learn* it) — how it is on this language that analytic experience precisely plays. For at every instant what this experience does is *show* the subject that he says more than he believes he says — to take the question only under this angle.

If we took it under the genetic angle, we would be carried into the question of how the child learns language, and we would then be carried into a question of psychological investigation that, regarding method, would take us so far that we cannot even broach it.

But it seems incontestable that we cannot judge precisely of the child's acquisition of language by the *motor mastery* he shows of it, by the appearance of the *first words*. And these listings — doubtless very interesting — these catalogs of words observers like to register to know which words appear first in a given child, drawing rigorous significations — leave entire the problem of knowing in what measure what indeed emerges in motor representation should not be considered as emerging from a *prior apprehension of the entire symbolic system as such*, an apprehension that gives these first appearances (as the clinic moreover manifests) a quite contingent signification.

For everyone knows the diversity of these first fragments of language revealed in the child's elocution — how striking it is to hear the child express adverbs, particles, words like *perhaps* or *not yet*, before having expressed a single substantive, the least name of an object. There is manifestly here a question of pre-position of the problem indispensable to situate any valid observation. If we do not manage to grasp the essential function — the *autonomy* of the symbolic function in human realization — it is impossible to start brutally from the facts without making at once the grossest errors of comprehension.

This is not a course in general psychology, and Lacan will doubtless not have occasion to take up the problem of language acquisition. Today he only wants to introduce the essential problem of the *ego and speech*, starting (of course) from the way it reveals itself in our experience — a problem we can only pose at the point where the formulation of the problem stands. That is, we cannot proceed as if the theory of the ego — in all the questions it poses, the theory as Freud formulated it in opposition with the Id, once proffered by Freud, and impregnating a whole part of our theoretical conceptions and consequently our technical ones — were settled.

This is why today he wishes to draw their attention to a text called *Die Verneinung*. *Die Verneinung*, otherwise — as Hyppolite was just remarking to him — *dénégation* (denegation), and not *negation*, as it has been quite insufficiently translated into French. This is always how Lacan himself has evoked it whenever he has had occasion, in explanations, seminars, conferences. The text is from 1925, posterior to the appearance of those papers limit-positioned to the period they are studying — those concerning the *psychology of the ego* (*Das Ich und das Es*). It thus takes up this relation always present and living for Freud — the relation of the *ego to the spoken manifestation of the subject in the session*. As such, it is extremely significant.

It seemed to Lacan, for reasons that will manifest themselves, that Hyppolite — who gives them the great honor of participating in their work by his presence and interventions — could bring a great help in establishing this dialogue (during which one cannot say Lacan reposes, but at least in which he no longer manifests himself motorically): to bring the testimony of an elaborated critique by the reflection of all they know of his anterior works, to bring the elaboration of a problem which, they shall see, interests nothing less than the whole theory if not of *knowledge* at least of *judgment*.

This is why Lacan asked Hyppolite — doubtless with a little insistence — to bring not only to supplement him but what he alone can bring in rigor to a text of the nature of the one on *denegation*. There are difficulties here, and certainly a mind not formed in the philosophical disciplines (which we cannot do without in our function) would have trouble. Our function is not that of some vague *affective rubbing* in which we would provoke in the subject, across a confused experience, returns of more or less evanescent experiences in which all the *magic* of psychoanalysis would consist.

We do not do what we do in an experience pursued at the most sensible level of human activity — that of *reasoning intelligence*. The sole fact is that it is a matter of *discourse*; we do nothing else other than approximate, which has no title to psychoanalysis. We are thus in our full duty in listening, on a text such as the one to be considered, to the qualified opinion of someone exercised in this critique of language and apprehension of theory.

This text of Freud manifests, as so often in its author, that fundamental value by which the slightest moment of a Freudian text permits us a rigorous technical apprehension — that *every word merits being measured at its precise incidence*, its accent, its particular turn, merits being inserted into the most rigorous logical analysis. This is what differentiates it from the same terms vaguely grouped by disciples whose apprehension has been "second-hand" and never fully elaborated — whence the kind of *degradation* in which the development of analytic theory is constantly manifested by its hesitations.

Before giving Hyppolite the floor, Lacan wishes to draw their attention to an intervention Hyppolite once made, conjoined with a kind of debate provoked by a certain way of presenting things on the subject of Freud and his intention toward the patient. Hyppolite had brought Anzieu a momentary help. *Momentary*, Hyppolite confirms.

Yes — a momentary help. It was a matter of seeing what Freud's fundamental, intentional attitude was toward the patient at the moment when Freud pretended to substitute *the analysis of resistances by speech* for that kind of subjugation, of capture, of substitution for the patient's own speech operated by *suggestion* or *hypnosis*. Lacan had shown himself very reserved as to whether there was in Freud a manifestation of combativeness or of domination — characteristic of relics of the ambitious style that might be glimpsed in Freud's youth. Lacan thinks the text he is about to cite is rather decisive: Freud speaks there of suggestion, and this is why Lacan brings it today — because it is also at the heart of their problem.

It is in *Group Psychology and the Analysis of the Ego*. So it is apropos of *group psychology* — that is, of *relations to the other* — that for the first time the ego *as autonomous function* is brought into Freud's work. A simple remark Lacan points to today, because it is rather evident and justifies the angle under which he brings it. In chapter IV of this article, called *Suggestion and Libido*, we have the following text:

Freud writes that one is thus prepared to admit that suggestion is a fundamental phenomenon — the opinion of Bernheim whose extraordinary feats Freud himself was able to witness in 1889\. But, Freud recalls, already then he experienced a kind of *muted revolt* against this *tyranny of suggestion*. When a recalcitrant patient was told "what are you doing? you are counter-suggesting yourself\!", Freud could not help thinking that *violence* was being done. The man certainly had the right \[to counter-suggest\]. Freud's later opinion took the form of a revolt against the \[view that suggestion, which explained everything, should itself be withdrawn from explanation\]. And Freud cited the old joke: *if Saint Christopher carried Christ, and Christ carried the world, where then could Saint Christopher have put his feet?*

Lacan emphasizes: a true revolt that Freud experienced before properly *this violence which can be included in speech* — not seeing precisely this *potential bent* of the analysis of resistances in the direction Anzieu indicated the other day, which is precisely what we are here to show is to be avoided in practical application. The contresense to be avoided in the practical application of what is called "analysis of resistances." This text, Lacan thinks, has its value and merits being cited.

Thanking Hyppolite again for the collaboration he is willing to bring, Lacan asks him — who has been willing to devote prolonged attention to this text — to bring simply his feeling about it.

### **Jean Hyppolite: Die Verneinung**

Hyppolite begins by thanking Dr Lacan for his insistence, which procured him a night of work, the child of which he brings before them. He does not know what it will be worth. Lacan kindly sent him not only the French text but also the German. Just as well — Hyppolite thinks he would have understood absolutely nothing in the French had he not had the German.

He did not know this text. It is of an absolutely extraordinary structure and, at bottom, extraordinarily enigmatic. The construction is not at all the construction of a *professor*; it is — Hyppolite does not want to say *dialectical*, the word is abused — but an extremely *subtle* construction of the text. He had to engage himself, with the German and the French (whose translation is, with respect to other Freud translations, *honest*), in a true *interpretation*. It is this interpretation he will give. He believes it valid, but it is not the only one and certainly merits discussion.

Freud begins by presenting the title *Die Verneinung*. Hyppolite — discovering it after Lacan — realized it would be better translated as *denegation* than *negation*. Similarly one will see employed *Urteil verneinen*, which is not "the negation of judgment" but a kind of *unjudging*. Hyppolite thinks a difference must be marked between *the negation internal to a judgment* and *the attitude of negation*, for otherwise the article seems incomprehensible.

The French text does not bring into relief either how Freud's analysis is something extremely *concrete and almost amusing*, or how by examples (which contain a *projection* one could situate in the analyses done here) — the one where the patient (or the analysand) says to his analyst: *you have doubtless thought that I will say something offensive to you; but it is nothing of the sort*.

Freud's comment, Hyppolite continues: *we understand that the fact of refusing such an incidence by projection — that is, by spontaneously lending this thought to the analyst — is precisely its avowal*.

Hyppolite noticed that in everyday life it is very frequent to say "I certainly do not want to offend you in what I am going to say." One must translate: "I want to offend you." It is a will that is not lacking. Freud continues to a *generalization* of bold reach, leading him to pose the problem of negation as *the very origin, perhaps, of intelligence*. This is how Hyppolite understands the article, which has a certain philosophical density.

Freud recounts another example, that of someone who says: "I saw in my dream a person, but it was certainly not my mother." One must translate: "it was surely her." Freud cites then a procedure the psychoanalyst — or anyone — may employ: *Tell me what in your situation seems the most incredible, the most impossible*. And the patient, the neighbor, the interlocutor finds something that is the most incredible. But it is precisely *that which must be believed*.

Here is an analysis of concrete cases generalized into a mode of *presenting what one is on the mode of not being it*. This is what is fundamental: "I will tell you what I am not — beware, it is precisely what I am."

But Freud remarks here on the *function* belonging to this denegation. He employs a word Hyppolite felt familiar — the word *Aufhebung*. This word, as Hyppolite reminds the audience, has had diverse fortunes; it is not for him to say. Lacan: but yes, precisely for you.

Hyppolite: it is Hegel's word for "dialectic" — which means at once *to negate*, *to suppress*, *to conserve*, and in sum *to lift up*. It can be the *Aufhebung* of a stone, or also the *cessation* of my subscription to a journal.

Freud says: the *denegation* is an *Aufhebung* of repression, but not an *acceptance* of the repressed.

Here is something truly extraordinary in Freud's analysis — there emerges from these concrete examples a prodigious philosophical reach Hyppolite will try to resume. *To present one's being on the mode of not being it*: that is what it is — an *Aufhebung* of repression, but not an acceptance. In other words, the one who says "here is what I am not" — there is no longer repression here, since repression signifies unconsciousness and this is conscious. Denegation is a way of making pass into consciousness what was in the unconscious; everything becomes conscious, but *repression subsists still in the form of non-acceptance*.

Here continues the kind of philosophical subtlety Freud is developing. He says: *here the intellectual separates itself from the affective*. There is truly a kind of profound discovery here. To do an analysis of "the intellectual" — Hyppolite, pushing the hypothesis — would say: not how "the intellectual separates itself from the affective" but how "the intellectual" is this kind of *suspension*, in a certain measure (one would say in a slightly barbarous language) a *sublimation*. That is not exactly it; in any case the intellectual separates itself from the affective. And perhaps it is *born*, as such, *thought*: it is the content affected by a denegation.

To recall a philosophical text — once again Hyppolite apologizes, but Lacan himself does this too — at the end of a chapter of Hegel, it is a matter of substituting *real negativity* for that appetite of destruction that seizes upon desire and has something profoundly mystical (more than psychological): an appetite of destruction in desire such that, when the two combatants confront each other, soon there will no longer be anyone to attest to their victory or defeat — an *ideal* negation.

Here, the denegation of which Freud speaks is exactly — and this is why it introduces in "the intellectual" an *ideal negation*, an *ideal negativity* — for we will see a kind of genesis where Freud employs the word *negativity* about certain psychotics. Hyppolite shows how this negativity is at bottom different, mythically speaking. To understand the article, one must admit something not immediately visible: a *dissymmetry* — translated by two moments in Freud's text, which are translated the same way in French — a dissymmetry between *the passage to affirmation* and *the passage to love*. The veritable role in the *genesis of intelligence* belongs to denegation: denegation is the very position of thought.

But let us proceed more slowly. Freud said: *the intellectual separates itself from the affective* (*Man sieht, wie sich hier die intellektuelle Funktion vom affektiven Vorgang scheidet*). And Freud adds the other modification of analysis: *the acceptance of the repressed* (*Die Verneinung ist eine Art, das Verdrängte zur Kenntnis zu nehmen*).

Yet repression is not suppressed. Let us try to represent the situation: first, here is what I am not — one concludes: that is what I am. Repression exists still under the *ideal form of denegation*. Second, the psychoanalyst obliges me to accept what I just denied. Freud adds, with little dots in the text (he gives no explanation): *and yet repression has not as such disappeared*.

What seems to Hyppolite very profound: if the analysand *accepts*, he returns from his denegation, and *yet repression is still there*. Hyppolite concludes one must give this a philosophical name Freud did not give: *the negation of negation*. Literally, what appears here is the *intellectual affirmation, but only intellectual, as negation of the negation*. The word is not in Freud, but Hyppolite thinks one can prolong it in this form — this is indeed what it means.

Freud at that moment says: we are thus in a position — since we have separated the intellectual from the affective — to formulate *a kind of genesis of judgment* — that is, in sum, a *genesis of thought*.

Hyppolite apologizes to the psychologists present, but he does not much like positive psychology. This genesis could be taken for a positive psychology; it seems to him deeper — a kind of history at once *genetic and mythical*. He thinks that, as this primordial affective will engender in a certain way intelligence, in Freud (as Dr Lacan said) the primary form psychologically called "affective" is itself a *human* form that — if it engenders intelligence — does so because it contains already at its origin a *fundamental historicity*. It is not the *pure affective* on one side and the *pure intellectual* on the other.

In this genesis Hyppolite sees a kind of great myth — behind an appearance of positivity in Freud there is a *great myth*. And what? Behind affirmation, what is there? There is *Vereinigung* (union), which is *Eros*. And behind denegation (*Verneinung*) — careful: intellectual denegation will be something more — *the appearance of a fundamental dissymmetric symbol*. The primordial affirmation is nothing other than affirming, but negating is more than wanting to destroy. This process badly translated as *rejet* — *Verwerfung* — one should use, whereas there is *Ausstossung*, which means *expulsion*. We have, so to speak, the two primary forms: the *force of expulsion* and the *force of attraction*, both, it seems, under the domination of pleasure — both in the text, which is striking.

Judgment thus has a history. Freud shows there are two types — what everyone knows, even the most elementary philosophy: there is the *attributive judgment* and the *existential judgment*. There is saying of a thing that it is or is not *this*, and saying of a thing that it *is* or *is not* (*Die Urteilsfunktion hat im wesentlichen zwei Entscheidungen zu treffen*).

Freud shows what is *behind* attributive and existential judgment. To understand his article, one must consider the negation of attributive judgment and the negation of existential judgment as not yet the negation that appears as the symbol. At bottom, there is not yet judgment in this genesis; there is a first myth of the formation of "outside" and "inside" — there is the whole question. The importance of this myth of the formation of outside and inside — of the *alienation* between the two terms translated by the opposition of the two: alienation and *hostility* of the two.

What makes these three pages so dense is, as one sees, that everything is brought into question, and how one passes from these concrete remarks, so minute in appearance and so profound in their generality, to something that brings into question a whole *philosophy* and a structure of thought.

Behind the attributive judgment, what is there? There is *I want to appropriate, to introject* or *I want to expel* (*das will ich in mich einführen und das aus mir ausschließen*). Freud seems to say there is at the beginning — but "at the beginning" means nothing, it is like a myth: *once upon a time* — there was an ego, a subject, for whom there was nothing foreign. *That* — the foreign and oneself — is an *operation*, an *expulsion*. This makes comprehensible a text that brusquely surfaces and seems a bit contradictory: *Das Schlechte, das dem Ich Fremde, das Außenbefindliche, ist ihm zunächst identisch* — the bad, the foreign-to-the-ego, the outside-existing, are *initially identical* to the ego.

But just before, Freud had said that one expels — that there is an operation of expulsion and an operation of introjection. This form is the *primordial form of what will be the attributive judgment*. What is at the origin of the existential judgment is the relation between *representation* and *perception*. Freud deepens this. What is important is that *at the beginning it is also neutral whether there is or there is not*. There *is*. But the subject reveals his *representation of things* to the primitive perception he had. The question is, when he says it exists, whether this reproduction *still conserves its being-real in reality* — that he can *find again* or not find again. This is the relation between representation and the possibility of finding again its object. *It will have to be found again*.

This proves always that Freud moves in a dimension deeper than Jung's — a kind of dimension of memory — and so does not lose the thread of his analysis. But Hyppolite fears making them lose it, so difficult and minute is the article.

What was at issue in the attributive judgment: to *expel* or *to introject*. In the existential judgment: to attribute to the ego (or rather to the subject, more general) a representation — that is, to define an *interior* by a representation to which no longer corresponds (but did correspond, in a retrospective return) its object.

What is brought into question is the *genesis of interior and exterior*. And, Freud says, one sees the birth of judgment from the primary drives (*Das Studium des Urteils eröffnet uns vielleicht zum erstenmal die Einsicht in die Entstehung einer intellektuellen Funktion aus dem Spiel der primären Triebregungen*). There is thus a kind of *finalized evolution* of this introjection and expulsion, regulated by the *pleasure principle*.

Freud writes: *Die Bejahung* — affirmation — *als Ersatz der Vereinigung, gehört dem Eros an* (affirmation, simply as a substitute for unification, belongs to Eros).

What is at the source of what we call affirmation is *Eros* — that is, in the attributive judgment, for example, the fact of *introjecting*, appropriating to ourselves rather than expelling outside. For negation, Freud does not use *Ersatz*; he uses *Nachfolge*. But the translator renders it the same way as *Ersatz*.

The German: *Die Bejahung — als Ersatz der Vereinigung — gehört dem Eros an, die Verneinung — Nachfolge der Ausstoßung — dem Destruktionstrieb*. Affirmation is the *Ersatz* of *Vereinigung*; negation is the *Nachfolge* (succession, sequel) of *expulsion* — more precisely, of the *destruction drive*. This becomes wholly mythical: two drives interlaced in this myth carrying the subject — one of *union*, the other of *destruction*.

A great myth, repeating others. But the little nuance: affirmation only purely and simply *substitutes itself* for unification — whereas the negation that results from it later seems to Hyppolite alone able to explain Freud's next phrase, when it is simply a matter of *negativity*, that is, of the destruction drive.

Then there may well be a *pleasure of negating*, a *negativism* that simply results from the suppression of libidinal components — that is, what disappeared (= was repressed) in this pleasure of negating is the libidinal components. Consequently the destruction drive depends, too, on pleasure. Hyppolite finds this very important, *capital*, in technique.

Only, Freud says, and here appears the dissymmetry between affirmation and negation: the *functioning of judgment* — and this time the word is *Urteil*, whereas before we were in the primary limits preluding judgment — *is rendered possible only by the creation of the symbol of negation* (*Die Leistung der Urteilsfunktion wird aber erst dadurch ermöglicht, daß die Schöpfung des Verneinungssymbols dem Denken einen ersten Grad von Unabhängigkeit von den Erfolgen der Verdrängung und somit auch vom Zwang des Lustprinzips gestattet hat*).

Why does Freud not say "the functioning of judgment is rendered possible by *affirmation*"? Why does *negation* play a role not as destructive tendency, nor inside a form of judgment, but as *fundamental attitude of symbolicity and of explicitness*?

Creation of the symbol of negation that renders thought *independent of the results of repression* and consequently of the *pleasure principle*. This phrase of Freud would not make sense for Hyppolite had he not already attached the *tendency to destruction* to the pleasure principle.

There is a kind of difficulty. What does this dissymmetry between affirmation and negation signify? It signifies that *all the repressed* can in a certain manner be re-taken up and re-utilized in a kind of *suspension* — and that, in some way, instead of being under the domination of the drives of attraction and expulsion, there can be produced *a margin of thought, of being, in the form of not-being*, which appears with denegation, the very symbol of denegation attached to the concrete attitude of negation.

For one must understand the text thus, if one admits the conclusion that seemed to Hyppolite a bit strange: *to this interpretation of negation, it accords very well that one finds in analysis no "no" stemming from the unconscious* (*Zu dieser Auffassung stimmt es sehr gut, daß man in der Analyse kein "Nein" aus dem Unbewußten auffindet*).

But one finds destruction there. So one must absolutely separate the *destruction drive* from the *form of destruction*, for otherwise one would not understand Freud. One must see in denegation a *concrete attitude at the origin of the explicit symbol of negation* — which explicit symbol *alone* renders possible something like the *use of the unconscious* while maintaining repression. Such is, Hyppolite thinks, the sense of the text: *and that the recognition of the unconscious, on the side of the ego, expresses itself in a negative form* (*und daß die Anerkennung des Unbewußten von seiten des Ichs sich in einer negativen Form ausdrückt*).

This is the summary: one finds in analysis no "no" stemming from the unconscious — but *the recognition of the unconscious from the ego's side* (which is always misrecognition, even in knowledge), one finds always on the ego's side, in a negative form, the possibility of *holding the unconscious while refusing it*.

Freud concludes: *no stronger proof of the successful uncovering of the unconscious than when the analysand reacts with the sentence: "I did not think that," or "I never thought of that"* (*Kein stärkerer Beweis für die gelungene Aufdeckung des Unbewußten, als wenn der Analysierte mit dem Satze: Das habe ich nicht gedacht, oder: Daran habe ich nicht (nie) gedacht, darauf reagiert*).

There is thus in this three-page text of Freud — of which (Hyppolite apologizes) he managed only with difficulty to find what he believes the thread: first, this kind of concrete attitude resulting from observation of denegation; the possibility thereby of dissociating the intellectual from the affective. And on the other hand, a *genesis of all that precedes* in the primary, and consequently the *origin of judgment and of thought itself* — under the form of thought as such, for thought is well *before*, in the primary, but it is not there *as thought* — by way of denegation.

### **Lacan resumes**

Lacan thanks Hyppolite. They cannot be too grateful for having given them, by a kind of movement *co-extensive with Freud's thought*, the chance immediately to rejoin what Hyppolite — Lacan thinks — has remarkably situated as truly *beyond positive psychology*.

He remarks in passing: by insisting as we always do in these seminars on the *trans-psychological* character of the psychoanalytic field, we are only retrieving what is the evidence of our practice — what the thought of the very one who opened its doors manifests ceaselessly in the smallest of his texts. There is much to draw from reflection on this text. He proposes that, since Mlle Guéninchault has the goodness to take notes, the text should benefit from a *favored turn* and be quickly mimeographed for distribution.

This too-brief lesson of Hyppolite's, Lacan thinks, merits a special treatment. The extreme condensation and the bringing of quite precise reference-points is certainly — perhaps in some sense much more didactic than what Lacan himself expresses in his own style. He will have it mimeographed for the use of those who come.

He thinks there could be no better preface to a whole distinction of *levels*, a whole critique of concepts — which is what he is striving to introduce them to, in order to avoid certain confusions. He thinks, for example, that what has emerged from Hyppolite's elaboration shows them *the difference of levels* of the *Bejahung*, of affirmation, and of negativity, insofar as the latter institutes — at an anterior level (Lacan deliberately uses heavy-footed expressions) — the constitution of the *subject-object relation*.

This is, Lacan thinks, what this apparently minor Freud text introduces them to at once — rejoining, without doubt, certain of the most current elaborations of philosophical meditation.

And from this, this allows us to critique in the first place the kind of ambiguity always maintained around the famous opposition *intellectual-affective* — as if affectivity were a kind of *coloration*, an ineffable quality, something to be sought in itself, in some way independent of the kind of *emptied skin* that the purely intellectual realization of a subject-relation would supposedly be.

Lacan thinks this notion of "the affective" — which pushes analysis into paradoxical, singular paths — is properly *puerile*, a kind of *sensational* connotation. The least sentiment indicated by the subject, with a character of singularity or strangeness, in the text of the session, is what flows from this fundamental misunderstanding.

The affective is not a *special density* missing from intellectual elaboration. And on another level — the *production of the symbol*, the *opening* of the subject to symbolic creation — is something in the register that is, as said at the start, *mythical*, in this register *anterior to discursive formulation*. Are you understanding? Only this can permit us — Lacan does not say to situate at once, but to discuss, to apprehend — what he calls this *full realization of speech*.

A little time remains. Lacan wishes to incarnate the question in examples — to point by examples how the question poses itself. He will show by two sides. First, by a phenomenon called *psychopathological*, to which the elaboration of psychopathological thought has brought a first-rank novelty, a total renewal of perspective: the phenomenon of *hallucination*.

Until a certain date, hallucination was properly considered as a kind of *critical phenomenon* around which posed itself the question of the *discriminating value of consciousness*. It could not be consciousness that was hallucinated; it was something else. In fact, it suffices to introduce ourselves to the new *Phenomenology of Perception* of Merleau-Ponty's book to see that hallucination is on the contrary *integrated as essential to the intentionality of the subject*.

This hallucination — we content ourselves with a number of themes and registers, like the *pleasure principle*, to explain its production, considered somehow fundamental, like a first movement in the order of the subject's satisfaction. We cannot content ourselves with something as simple. Recall the example Lacan cited last time, in the *Wolf Man*. It is indicated, by the progress of this subject's analysis, by the contradictions in the traces by which we follow the elaboration he made of his situation in the human world: that *Verwerfung* — that something making it so that the genital plane has been for him literally always *as if it did not exist* — that something we were led to situate precisely at the level of the *non-Bejahung* — that something, you see, we cannot put on the same level as a *denegation* at all.

What is most striking is the sequel — which Lacan promised to indicate, and takes up today. It is the immediate connection that emerges, so much more comprehensible in light of today's explanations around Freud's text: that — although nothing has been manifested on the symbolic plane (for this seems to be precisely the condition for something to exist: that there be this *Bejahung*, a *Bejahung* not of the order of negation-of-negation, which is something else) — what happens when this *Bejahung* does not occur?

It is that the *only trace* we have of this symbolic plane on which it has not been realized for the subject — the genital plane — is a kind of emergence not at all in his history, but really *in the external world*: a small *hallucination*. It is the external world that is manifested to the subject: castration, which is precisely what for him *has not existed*, in the form of his imagining he has *cut his little finger*. Cut it so deeply it now hangs only by a strip of skin. He is submerged with the feeling of so inexpressible a catastrophe that he dares not even speak of it to the person next to him.

What he does not dare speak of is that this person next to him — to whom he refers all his emotions — is literally as if she were at that moment *annulled*. There is no longer any other.

There is a kind of *immediate external world of manifestations perceived in a kind of primitive, unsymbolized real* — despite the symbolic form (in the ordinary sense) that the phenomenon takes, where one can see this: *what is not recognized is seen*.

Lacan thinks that for elucidating — not psychosis (let him be heard: the Wolf Man is not at all psychotic at the moment he has this hallucination; he may be psychotic later, but not at the moment of this absolutely limited, nodal experience, foreign to the experience of his childhood, fully disintegrated — nothing permits classifying him in childhood as schizophrenic).

So it is a *phenomenon* of psychosis — Lacan asks them to hear — that is at issue. To understand this kind of correlation, this balancing: at the level of an experience quite primitive at the origin, at the source, that opens the subject to a certain relation to the world by the *possibility of the symbol*: *what is not recognized erupts into consciousness in the form of the seen*.

If you deepen this particular polarization sufficiently, it will appear much easier to approach the ambiguous phenomenon called *déjà vu*, which is exactly between these two modes of relation, the *recognized* and the *seen*.

Insofar as something — communicable, thinkable in the world's terms (the integrated discourse of daily life) — finds itself, for certain reasons, brought to the *limit-level*, recognized to be at the limit of what surges with a kind of special *pre-signification*, it carries itself over (with retrospective illusion) into the domain of *déjà vu* — that is, of that perceived with original quality which is in the end nothing other than what Freud speaks of when, apropos of *reality-testing*, he says all reality-testing implicitly refers to something already perceived in the past.

But this applies to infinity: in a certain way every perceived requires this reference to perspective. This is why we are brought back to the level of the *imaginary as such* — at the level of the image, model of the original form, that makes us, in another sense than the sense of the *recognized-symbolized-verbalized*, find ourselves there in the problems evoked by the Platonic theory not of remembrance but of *reminiscence*.

Lacan has announced another example, proposed for their reflection on this subject. He takes one that is precisely of the order of what is more or less properly called "the modern way of analyzing." One imagines that "moderns" — but they will see these principles are already exposed in 1925 in this Freud text — make much of analyzing "the surface first." This is the *fin du fin* permitting the subject to progress in a way not given up to that kind of chance represented by intellectualized sterilization of content.

Lacan takes an example given by Kris in one of his articles. One of Kris's subjects in analysis, already analyzed once, has serious obstacles in his profession — an intellectual one, something close to the preoccupations that might be theirs. The subject has all sorts of difficulties in *producing*. His life is impeded by the very efforts necessary to bring out something publishable. This impediment is the feeling he has — let us abbreviate — of *being a plagiarist* of someone very close to him, a brilliant scholar, slightly more than a student, with whom he constantly exchanges ideas. He always feels tempted to take the ideas he furnishes his interlocutor — a perpetual impediment to anything he wants to bring out.

Kris explains the problems of the analysis. Nonetheless, at one moment he has managed to bring forth a text. One day he arrives almost triumphantly declaring that everything in his thesis is *already in a book* in the library, in a published article, presenting its essential manifestations. *Plagiarist in spite of himself*, this time. In what will the supposed "interpretation by the surface" Kris proposes consist?

In this: Kris — manifesting something that a certain way of taking analysis would perhaps lead beginners astray from — actually *takes an interest in what happened*, in what is in this book. Examining it more closely — referring to the actual text — one realizes there is in fact *absolutely nothing* in this book that represents the essential of the theses brought by the subject. Things are sketched that pose the question, but nothing of the *new theses* brought by the subject is already there. In other words, *the thesis is indeed effectively original*.

---

## **Session of 17 February 1954**

Lacan opens by saying he intends today to lead them into a region that may be said to have been delimited by their remarks last time, but which is exactly that toward which they have been advancing from the beginning of this commentary on the *Papers on Technique*: the region more exactly *between* the *formation of the symbol* and the *discourse of the ego*.

He has given today's seminar the title *Analysis of Discourse and Analysis of the Ego*. He cannot say he will fulfill so ambitious a title in a single session; it is rather a way of opening certain problems and viewpoints.

By opposing these two terms, he is doing something that substitutes itself for the classical opposition of the *analysis of material* and the *analysis of resistances*. He recalls the term Hyppolite brought into relief last time: the use of *Aufhebung* in the *Verneinung* text, which Hyppolite kindly commented for them, recalling its very particular, complex, manipulable sense — for in German it signifies at once *to negate*, *to suppress*, and also *to conserve in the suppression*, *to lift up*.

We have here, evidently, the example of a concept that cannot be too deeply pondered in reflecting on what we do in this dialogue, as analysts have remarked for some time. We are dealing with the *ego* of the subject with all its limitations, defenses, all its character; and one can say that the whole literature of analytic writings is as if *embarrassed* to define exactly what mode of rapport, what mode of action, what function this ego plays in the operation, the progress in which we find ourselves proposed by the situation to *lead it forward*.

It is certain that all recent discussions and elaborations around this function of the ego — those that speak of the analysand's ego as having to be the *solid point*, the *ally* of the analyst in the great analytic work — have always manifested in their content and progress *singular contradictions*.

For, as Lacan has been careful to stress at many turns, it is very difficult — except by arriving at the notion of a kind of not only *bipolarity* but properly speaking *splitting*, radical distinction between two egos — to conceive how this ego, which has the great philosophical tradition behind it, presents itself as *master of errors*, *seat of illusions*, *locus of a passion proper to it* that goes essentially to *misrecognition*.

There are terms in Anna Freud's *Ego and the Mechanisms of Defense* — somewhat disconcerting by its *thingifying* character — which Lacan finds in paragraphs of her book where one has the impression both that she speaks of the ego as something in the atmosphere and style of comprehension they are striving to maintain here, and on the other hand that she speaks of the *little man inside the man*, something with a kind of autonomous subjective life within the subject who would be there to defend himself ("Father, watch on your right; Father, watch on your left") against what may assault him from without and within.

But on the other hand, if we take it under the angle of a moralist-style description, she speaks incontestably of the ego as the seat of certain *passions* in a style not unworthy of what La Rochefoucauld can say at every instant about the *ruses of self-love*.

This situation of a *dynamic function* of the ego in analytic dialogue remains, then — and this appears even more whenever they have broached the principles of technique — up to the present, it seems, for want of a rigorous situation, profoundly *contradictory*.

Lacan believes many of his audience have read Anna Freud's well-known book. It is extremely instructive. Certainly one can point out — because it is a fairly rigorous book — the points where the *faults* of her demonstration appear in her own discourse, more sensible still when she gives *examples*. She defines, in significant passages, the function of the ego, saying: in the analysis, the ego manifests itself only by its defenses. That is, only insofar as it opposes itself to the proper *analytic work*. Does that mean that everything opposing the analytic work is *ego-defense*? She recognizes elsewhere this cannot be maintained: there are other elements of resistance besides ego-defenses. This is how Lacan began to broach the problem with them.

Someone close to them in the Society once spoke of Anna Freud with a lyrical élan — calling her "the plumb-line of psychoanalysis" at the 1950 Congress. But the plumb-line is not enough in an architecture: one also needs other instruments — a water-level, for instance. Still, "plumb-line" is not bad; it lets us situate the *vertical* of certain problems.

Lacan now wishes to follow the seminar's tradition. He thinks this is not a bad introduction to what he will ask Mlle Gélinier to present at the heart of the problem: an article by Melanie Klein, *The Importance of Symbol-Formation in the Development of the Ego*. He thinks it is not a bad way to introduce Mlle Gélinier's intervention to first read them a Freud text concerning Anna Freud's understanding of child analysis, especially ego-defenses, in a technical analytic example.

Here, for example, is a small case Anna Freud brings: a young patient who comes for analysis for a grave state of anxiety perturbing her life and studies, who comes to obey her mother. Her behavior with Anna Freud remains amiable and frank, but Anna Freud observes that she carefully avoids any allusion to her symptom and passes over in silence the anxiety crises between sessions. When Anna Freud insists on bringing the symptom into the analysis or interpreting anxiety revealed by associations, the patient's amiable behavior immediately changes. She pours on Anna Freud a torrent of ironic remarks and mockery. Anna Freud's attempts to relate this attitude to her behavior toward her mother fail totally. The conscious and unconscious relations of the girl with her mother offer a quite different picture.

The patient's sarcasm and ridicule disconcert the analyst and for a time make the continuation of treatment seem useless. A more profound analysis shows later that the persiflage and mockery do not constitute, properly speaking, a *transference reaction*, nor are they linked to the analytic situation. The patient has recourse to this maneuver, directed against herself, whenever sentiments of tenderness, desire, or anxiety are about to surge into consciousness. The more powerful the affect, the more vehemence and acrimony the girl puts into ridiculing herself. The analyst attracts these defense reactions only *secondarily*, because she encourages the appearance of anxiety in consciousness. The knowledge of the content of anxiety, even when the patient's other sayings permit its exact interpretation, remains *inoperative* so long as any attempt to approach the affect intensifies the defense. It was possible in analysis to render the content of the anxiety conscious only after succeeding in making conscious — and thereby inoperative — the *defense against affects by ironic depreciation*, a process that until then had been realized automatically in all circumstances of the patient's life. Historically, this defense procedure by ridicule and irony is explained by an *identification with her deceased father*, who had wanted to teach his daughter self-mastery and mocked her whenever she gave way to sentimental manifestations. The defense thus *fixes the memory* of a tenderly loved father.

Lacan asks: does it not seem in this short text that what we see, under the form of this necessity to *analyze the ego's defense*, is in fact nothing other than the correlative of an *error*? The error, as the text itself shows, is that Anna Freud at once took things under the angle of the *dual relation* — herself and the patient. To the extent that Anna Freud herself recognizes she had taken the patient's defense for what it manifested itself as — namely an *irony, an aggression against her* (Anna Freud) — that is, very exactly to the extent that she perceived on the plane of her own ego, of Anna Freud, this defense, and took it as a *defense-of-the-ego in a dual relation* with her, Anna Freud — she wanted immediately to make of it a *manifestation of transference* according to the incomplete (though so often given, classical) formula of the *reproduction of a situation*, without specifying *how that situation is structured*.

In other words, she at once began to interpret in the direction of *seeking to understand the analytic relation on the prototype of the dual relation* — the subject's relation to her mother. She found herself before a position that not only marked time but was perfectly *sterile*.

What does she call "having analyzed the defense against affects"? It does not seem one can see in this text anything other than her own self-comprehension: that this was not the path on which she could progress. We thus find ourselves once again before the problem on which Lacan thinks the distinctive accent is best put by manifesting the difference between (a) the *dual interpretation* where the analyst enters into a rivalry from ego to ego with the analysand, and (b) the interpretation that makes some progress in the direction of *symbolic structuration of the subject* — which is *beyond the actual structure of his ego*.

In other words: we return to the question — *of what Bejahung*, of what assumption by the ego, of *what "yes"* is it a question in analytic progress? What is the Bejahung to be obtained, which constitutes the revelation, the unveiling essential to the progress of an analysis?

Somewhere Freud, in a writing not outside their circle since he himself calls it *Of Psychoanalytic Technique* (in the *Outline of Psychoanalysis*), says something like: a *pact* is concluded. This is one way of presenting things — between the analysand and the analyst. The ailing ego of the patient promises us total *frankness*: the free disposition of everything his auto-perception delivers him. On our side, we assure him the strictest discretion and put at his service our experience in interpretation of the material influenced by the unconscious. Our knowledge compensates his ignorance and permits the ego to recuperate and govern the lost domains of his psyche. This *pact* constitutes the analytic situation.

The question Lacan was posing in his last conference, more or less formulated and implied, was: our knowledge no doubt comes to the aid of his ignorance; but there is also *our* ignorance — not only our ignorance of the situation (the situation in the register of the *symbolic determination* of his subject, the one before us), but also (why not?) a certain part of ignorance in the *structural mapping* of these various symbolic situations.

The originary character determines a certain *symbolic constellation* in the subject's unconscious, a constellation that must always be conceived as *structured*, *organized* according to a certain order — a *complex* order. It is no accident that the word *complex* came to the surface of analytic theory (Lacan notes that it was not Freud himself but Jung who invented it). It indicates by itself that when we go to the discovery of the unconscious, what we encounter are *structured, organized, complex situations*.

Freud has given us what we may call the first model, the *standard*, in the form of the *Oedipus complex*. Those who have followed this seminar for some time know — and this has been particularly drawn out apropos of the commentary on the least-disputable cases (those most richly delineated by Freud, in two or three of his five great analyses) — how, as one deepens the relation of the Oedipus complex, one sees how many problems and ambiguities it poses.

The whole development of analysis has consisted in a successive bringing-into-relief of each of the *tensions* implied in this triangle, which forces us to see something quite other than a massive, triangular block summarized in the classic formulation of *sexual attraction for the mother and rivalry with the father*. One knows that at the very middle of the Oedipus complex, and from the origin, the *profoundly symmetric character* of the structure of each of these dual relations connecting the subject to both father and mother.

In particular, the distinction of the *narcissistic or imaginary* relation with the father, the *symbolic* relation, and a certain *real* (or residual) relation, with respect to the architecture proper that touches and interests us in analysis, already shows the complexity of the structure. It is not inconceivable, briefly, that some other research direction would allow us to elaborate the Oedipal myth as it has been formulated up to now.

Moreover, much has not been left of the Oedipal relation despite all the richness of material approached and included within it. One has hardly broached more than the schema Freud himself gave.

It is not at all unthinkable that we will manage — and Lacan thinks he will manage, over time, to show them — to give of the Oedipus complex, while maintaining its essential of course (for it is, as they shall see, truly *fundamental*), a richer account.

Fundamental not only for any comprehension of the subject, but for any *symbolic realization* by the subject of this *himself* which is the Id, the unconscious — which is not simply a series of disorganized drives (as part of Freud's theoretical elaboration would tend to make one think, going so far as to formulate that only the ego has organization in the psyche), but *speech instituting the subject in a certain complex relation*.

The progress of analysis, they have seen last time, apropos of the two stages Freud brings into relief — *appearance of the repressed in the denegated*, and the fact that the *reduction of this negation* does not yet give us on the subject's side the *Bejahung* — calls for close attention to the *value of the criteria* we require (on which we are moreover in agreement with the subject) to obtain a particularly satisfying *Bejahung*. They would see how complex the problem in fact is, taken under the angle of what could be called the *authentication by the subject* of what Freud himself calls the *analytic reconstruction* — where the source of evidence is, those voids by which memory must be *relived*.

What does that mean? We know "relived" is something of a particular nature, which brings into question the whole signification of what is called the *sentiment of reality*. To say it: Freud quite rightly recalls that we can never integrally trust memory.

What, then, do we demand, or more exactly what satisfies us when the subject tells us that things have arrived at that *click-point* (as Fenichel writes somewhere) where the subject has the sentiment of a veridical \[thrust\]? What is it? This certainly carries us to the heart of the problem of the *sentiment of reality*.

You have seen — apropos of Hyppolite's commentary the other day — that Lacan has pushed in this direction an indication apropos of the very significant example of the *Wolf Man*: namely something manifesting itself almost too transparently, too concretely, sensible under this quasi-algebraic form: *the real* — or what is perceived as real (recall what Lacan pointed out, as in the genesis of the *Wolf Man*'s hallucination) — *the real is, in sum, that something which absolutely resists symbolization*. In the end, the maximum of the sentiment of the real in its burning manifestation — that *unreal reality, hallucinatory* (the term will reappear shortly in Melanie Klein's text) — there is nothing more manifest in the so-called sentiment of the real than when the real corresponds to the real.

Here the most striking thing: it corresponds to a stage of the Wolf Man's life where the symbolization, the realization of the sense of the *genital plane*, was *verworfen*, as Lacan pointed out. So we should not be surprised that certain interpretations called "content interpretations" are in fact not at all *realized* by the subject — neither realized nor symbolized. For precisely they manifest themselves at a stage where they cannot in any degree give the subject the only possible revelation of his situation in this forbidden domain that is his unconscious.

They cannot give it to him as long as it is not complete — precisely because we are still either on the plane of *negation* or on the plane of *negation of negation*. But something has not been crossed which is precisely *beyond discourse*, requiring a certain *leap in discourse*; and in this measure there is only *Aufhebung* of repression, not the *disappearance* of repression.

To conclude on Anna Freud's text: what she calls "analysis of the defenses against affect" is only the stage of *her own comprehension*, where she notices she is going astray. Once she has noticed she is going astray — having considered that the defense against the subject's affect is a defense against *herself*, having substituted herself for the subject's mother to make her understand this — she abandons this first stage and can truly *analyze the transference resistance*.
