And it leads her where? To someone *who is not there* — to a *third*, to something — she does not indicate more — that must much resemble in general structure the position of *Dora*.

That is, insofar as effectively the subject *identified herself with her father*, and in this identification her ego structured itself in a certain way, and this structuration of the ego (designated here as *defense*) is in fact a part — the most superficial part — of this identification by which the deepest plane of the *recognition of the subject's situation in the symbolic order* rejoins. That is, the order of symbolic relation covering the whole field of human relations, whose initial cell is the Oedipus complex, and where the subject situates herself — there, perhaps, in a way she enters into conflict with her sex.

Lacan now gives the floor to Mlle Gélinier, who will show them — in opposition to what is in Anna Freud always *first an approach of the ego*, as if truly the ego were Anna Freud: a person armed with a plumb-line. Anna Freud underlines this: it is first a *median position*, rational at maximum, in the sense heard the other day after Freud's text — an essentially intellectualist position. She says somewhere: everything must be conducted in analysis from this median, moderated position which is the ego's. And it is first by a kind of *education, persuasion, approach to the ego* that everything starts — and where everything must return.

In contrast to Anna Freud's position — and it is no accident that these two ladies who have analogies have *Merovingian rivalries* and have opposed each other — they will see from where Klein starts, and what her viewpoint is for broaching the problems of a particularly difficult subject. The question: how could Anna Freud have used with this subject this kind of position of *prior re-education of an ego* called *strong* or *weak*? What does that mean in analysis: "strong ego," "weak ego"? How does Klein broach the problem?

### **Marie-Cécile Gélinier presents the Klein paper**

Mlle Gélinier introduces the 1930 article. It seemed hard to summarize. She will begin with the clinical case to make the question more concrete, then turn to Klein's theoretical introduction, and finally how Gélinier reacted to it.

The case is *Dick*, a boy of four. He has the level of a child of 15 to 18 months. His vocabulary is very limited and incorrect: he deforms words and uses them ill-aptly, though at other times one realizes he knows their sense. Most striking: this child has no desire to be understood; he does not seek to communicate. He has, Klein finds, *no adaptation to reality* and *no emotional relation*; he is devoid of affect in all daily-life circumstances. He does not react to his mother's or nurse's presence or absence; he shows no anxiety in any circumstance; he does not play. His only ludic activities are emitting senseless sounds and noises. He is insensible to physical pain; he does not react when he hurts himself.

Toward adults — mother, father, nurse — he has two alternating attitudes. Either he systematically opposes (when one wants him to repeat words, he does not repeat them or deforms them), or if he pronounces words correctly he seems to remove their sense and grind them out mechanically. He never seeks any caress from his relatives.

He is physically clumsy, badly coordinated, unable to hold scissors or knives, though he handles his spoon at table well.

The history: at birth the mother's milk was insufficient and not good. She nursed seven weeks during which he wasted away. A wet-nurse was offered, but he refused the breast. Bottle: refused. Solid foods: refused — he will not bite. From the start, severe digestive troubles, later hemorrhoids, anal prolapse. He remains a great anorexic — the most resistant symptom in treatment. He has never been surrounded by true love: anxious, not maternal mother; indifferent father and nurse. At two, an affectionate new nurse and grandmother. From this period he became clean — he managed to control his excrement and even to desire it, to please the nurse.

He made what Klein calls *an attempt at adaptation*. He partially succeeded — learned some words. He became sensitive to one prohibition (the nurse's interdiction of masturbation): she called him a "naughty boy" and from then on he was no longer seen masturbating. Some improvement: increased vocabulary, attempted adaptation to objects. But anorexia continues; he still cannot hold knives or scissors; he refuses anything other than liquid or pap. Despite progress through the nurse's presence, no *emotional contact* with the nurse — superficial progress only.

The problem Klein poses: why so total an absence of contact with human beings and things? What is happening?

Gélinier asks the audience to leave the clinical case for the theoretical considerations opening the chapter. These she found hard to summarize. Klein passes continually from the plane of *contents* (what the child says) to the plane of *structures*, without flagging the transitions.

The theoretical considerations center on: how can a child pass from the *sadistic-oral stage* to the next stage, that of *symbolism*? Klein describes the sadistic-oral stage mainly by content: how the child desires to appropriate the mother's body and its contents; how an *equivalence* is established between body-contents and excrements, possible children, and the father's penis-in-the-mother's-body; how the child, *introjecting* all this, is then invaded by bad things he can only *expel* — only to *re-introject* them again.

She describes a *vicious circle* of introjections and expulsions, of which Gélinier does not see how it can be broken — and where, Gélinier confesses, she does not understand where Klein situates the *child himself*. One sees only the circuit, not the subject.

Lacan: this is precisely what is at issue.

Gélinier continues: Klein asks what is the *subject*, the child, anxious about? About the sadism he experiences and manifests toward the mother, her body, its contents and their equivalents. He is anxious to destroy the mother and (by introjection) himself in return. He is anxious to destroy the love-object and himself.

\[An interjection: it is all very complicated.\]

Klein thinks this anxiety triggers a first mode of defense, fundamentally different from later depressive mechanisms. Lacan: supporting herself on a Freud text saying that *at the origin* there must be something other than what we can insert in the structures around the *ego*\-notion — evidently, since we are *before* ego-formation.

These repression mechanisms are of two kinds: *expulsion* (outward rejection of one's own sadism) and *destruction of the dangerous object*. But Gélinier does not understand how these mechanisms exit the vicious circle, since they reproduce it. The question: how can the child exit this anxiogenic situation whose origin is *his own sadism*?

Here Klein introduces the notion of *symbolism*, referring to Ferenczi and Jones, for whom symbolism is the foundation of all *sublimation* and all action on the external world. This is perhaps the most important point. Gélinier hopes she has understood Klein's notion: symbolism is, for Klein, the fact that the subject makes an *equation*, an equality, between his body (entire or in parts), the mother's body (penis, vagina, children, excrements) and *external objects*. Symbolism is for her an *equality* posited between the body proper and external objects.

Lacan: the term *equation* is moreover in Freud — in the article where he shows equivalences in anal structures, with the great schema showing the equivalence *child \= phallus \= excrement* and so on.

Gélinier: Klein thinks the child exits his anxiety better by realizing the *greatest possible number* of these equations. The situation: the child, to relieve his anxiety, *projects, distributes* it onto the external world, onto objects.

Lacan: are you sure of that? Gélinier: yes. Klein says these multiple equations are the foundations of reality-relations and of the external world. She adds that later the ego will be able — but she does not say how — Lacan: yes, that is right — to make true reality emerge under this reality which is none, since it is only projected anxiety. She does not specify the true reality.

She concludes: for the ego to develop well, for the anxiety of the sadism of the oral phase to be overcome, there must be a certain *minimum quantity of anxiety* (else the subject would not distribute it onto the external world) but *not too much*: an *optimum quantity* the ego can tolerate to master.

Klein then returns to Dick. She thinks (without justification) Dick has a *constitutional capacity of his ego to tolerate anxiety*. She thinks his *genital drives* were particularly *premature*, entailing a lesser tolerance of sadistic drives and increased defenses against them.

So Dick could not — being too genital and too poorly tolerating sadism — distribute his anxiety onto external objects, except for two or three: his only ludic interests — *trains*, *stations*, *door-handles*.

Lacan: you are making an important elision from your clinical description. There was something else: Dick's behavior at Klein's office. Gélinier: I'm coming to that later.

Lacan: because what you just said, the histories of doors, stations and trains — that is mostly *with Klein*. Gélinier: that is what she says *before*. She thinks the only distributions Dick made on the exterior, given his anxiety, were trains, stations, doors — and these three objects symbolically represent: the train is the *penis*; the station is the *mother*; the door-handles are \[...\]. Klein thinks the distribution of anxiety onto objects stopped there. This stop in the distribution constitutes Dick's lack of contact: he is in contact with nothing because he has not distributed his anxiety. His *fear of his own sadism*, the defense against it, also makes him incapable of any aggressive act — biting, handling scissors and knives. The same defense prevents him from *translating into fantasies* what he lives, his sadistic relation to the mother's body.

Klein then describes the first sessions. Dick entered, left his nurse without emotion, wandered aimlessly, took no interest in any object, made disordered movements without signification, fixed mimic, lost absent gaze — *as if Klein were not other than a piece of furniture, none of his attitudes addressed her*.

Klein insists: Dick's attitude differs from severely neurotic children, who put themselves in a corner or hide. With Dick: *as if he were in the void*.

Knowing his interest in trains and stations and that this was his only anxious investment of objects, Klein took a big train and put it next to a little train.

Lacan: are you sure Klein knew this in advance? She didn't know anything of the kind — she just shoved the train into his hands. Voice: she knew it before. Lacan: it doesn't matter much.

Gélinier: Dick calls the big train *Papa-train* and the little train *Dick-train*. He takes the little Dick-train and rolls it to the window. Klein verbalizes: *The station, that's Mommy. Dick is going into Mommy.* At this moment the child drops the train and runs at full speed into the dark space between two doors. He says: "Dark." Klein verbalizes: *It's dark inside Mommy.* The child questions: "Nurse?" — but this time in a voice that *had a sense*. He asks for his nurse, something he never did. She answers: *Nurse will come back soon.* The child repeats these words intelligently — they have a sense corresponding to the situation. Klein remarks that from that day forward he learned these words, remembered them, and reused them correctly.

At the next session he enters as before, but now goes into the dark corner and puts the *Dick-train* there, wanting it to remain. He repeats: "Is nurse coming?" The third time, he goes again into the dark corner behind the chest of drawers, and there he is very anxious, and for the first time *he calls the analyst to him*.

Klein remarks: the fact that he invests more objects (chest, dark corner, plus the trains) correlates with a sentiment of *dependence* vis-à-vis the nurse and herself — he claims their presence — and the first *anxiety expressed, manifested*, the call to the nurse.

Fourth session: he points to a small coal-wagon and says "cut\!" Klein gives him scissors. He tries to maltreat the contents of the coal-wagon but cannot. Klein takes the scissors and *the sadism upon herself*, cuts up the contents herself. When finished, the child takes all of it, throws it in a drawer, and says: "Gone\!" Klein verbalizes: *Dick wanted to take, to cut, the excrement from his mother's body.*

Next session: he sees the coal-wagon again, anxiously hides it under the other toys. Klein repeats his anxiety. He brings it out. She tells him he wanted to take and cut what was in his mother's body. The child again runs into the dark space between rooms — and Klein says this shows the dark space also represents the mother's body, for when she told him he wanted to attack the inside of his mother's body, he scratches with his nails — repeating: he is damaging the inside of his mother's body. Klein gives no other content; the same sadistic games continue. He distributes onto more and more objects: the basin, other toys, the buffet.

Gélinier summarizes Klein's understanding of Dick. The reason he defends more strongly than another against his sadism was his *premature genital development*. Klein's signs of this: when Dick had wanted to eat a little toy-personage representing the father's penis, after doing so he showed *pity* for the wounded personage, sympathized, wanted to *restore* what he had taken. This *precocious capacity for sympathy* is for Klein a sign of premature genital development. By this, he withdrew his interest from the entire external body (which was the body, the penis), and all this became dangerous, aggressive. He cut himself off from reality; fantasy-development stopped. He took refuge in a single fantasy — the *empty, dark mother's body* — and withdrew his interest from external objects representing the body's contents.

How does Klein understand the analytic action for Dick? The interpretations Klein gave — and here she underlines a *technical point*: usually she does not interpret on so unique a material, but here, as there was only one sort of material, she acted at once — having reached the unconscious problem entailed a *sedation of anxiety*. This relaxation permitted a *new influx of anxiety*, distributed (as she had described) on external objects. One sees this in the course of treatment as in everyday life: he contacts and grows interested in an increasing number of beings.

She describes general progress: more aggression possible; interest in more objects; stronger transference with her; vocabulary increase — Klein says he *learns new words* and *remembers more*.

\[Gélinier's critique\]

Gélinier finds it impossible to be satisfied with Klein's clinical description saying Dick has relation with nothing. There seem to be relations, but he negates them because they are dangerous. The passage where Klein says he takes refuge in the fantasy of the empty, dark mother-body and "withdraws interest from objects representing its contents — children, excrement, penis" — Gélinier cannot understand how a fantasy could be *fragmented* this way: how the child could be reassured inside the body without that inside implying all the rest. Can one take a little piece of something? Here she cannot understand.

A third point that struck her: how Klein understands Dick's *vocabulary recovery*. Klein seems to think that from the start of treatment Dick *learns words*, as if he began to have memory and to desire to learn. Gélinier had occasion to investigate a child of Dick's age and identical clinical picture: when this child began to speak, it was very clear — *he was not learning words; he knew them but was no longer negating that he knew*. He had an acquired stock of syntax and vocabulary but *negated* them, as he negated his relations with external objects. Klein thinks the child began to learn words.

Finally, the critique of Klein's notion of symbolism. For Klein, symbolism is a simple, unilinear procedure: an *equation* between body (whole or partial) and external objects. But Klein notes well — and insists — on the *correlation in time* between the advent of symbolism and the moment of ego-reinforcement. But she does not explain the link. The ego's function is not specified or broached.

Klein seems to consider symbolism as a mechanism, among others, of *adaptation*. She says it is fundamental — without it nothing would go (Dick has not acquired it; he is stopped). But for Klein this mechanism is one among others, not changing the subject's structure — it merely makes the ego's task (mastering anxiety, adapting) easier and more possible.

Klein does not account at all for how this *distribution of anxiety onto objects* — this fantasmatic world — *passes* to relations with a *true real*, a *real real*. She says a stronger ego can do it. But *how*? And what *is* this real, she does not say.

Gélinier proposes a reformulation: take the first stage from which Klein starts — the sadistic-oral stage, mainly described by contents. Considering it from the standpoint of *structure*, one can say all there is both *fantasmatic and real* — meaning there is yet *neither real nor fantasmatic proper*, since nothing differentiates them. It is a *pre-real* and *pre-fantasmatic*.

Lacan: Klein moreover says, formulates it: *unreal reality*.

Gélinier: this stage is characterized by an *indifferentiation of the all*, and the subject is nothing other than the double movement of introjection and expulsion — a vicious circle whose every moment is *anxiogenic*: introjection of sadism, expulsion of the good.

Lacan: rather it is the *bad* that is expelled. Gélinier: yes, but he expels everything at once. The anticipation of a new structure always presents itself first under *negativity* before passing to another form. Here the negativity is *sadism*. But as long as this negativity remains in the register of indifferentiation, where real and fantasmatic are confounded, *in what ego does he feel the quasi-real*? One always remains in the vicious circle, because this negativity cannot reach its term where it would be *structuring* and pass to another stage of differentiation, where the differentiation of fantasmatic and real would be effected.

In Dick's case, this passage seems to have been made by the *analyst's intervention*, intervening here as a *third term* — comprehending the fantasy, valorizing it, permitting the sadism, because by *verbalization* she differentiates the *symbolic from the real*: she renders symbolic the mother's body and the sadism.

Lacan resumes: *Klein hits him with symbolism with the utmost brutality*. She begins straightaway by flinging at him the major interpretations — a brutal verbalization, almost as revolting for us as for any reader — of the Oedipal myth: "You are the little train; you want to fuck your mother." That is her method. Of course this lends itself to all sorts of theoretical discussions, inseparable from the diagnosis. But it is certain that as a result of this intervention *something happens*, and everything is there.

Gélinier: Klein seems to think something happens because she...

Lacan: what Gélinier explains — the lack of contact — is the *defect of the ego*. This subject properly speaking has an *ego that is not formed*. The way Klein distinguishes him in his profound indifference, apathy, absence, from other neurotics is already significant.

But what is clear, in sum, is this: it corresponds to what Lacan has been telling them — *what is not symbolized is reality, before any formation of symbol*. One can say that what limits this young subject is precisely this: he is wholly in *reality in a pure state, unconstituted* — what is *not differentiated*.

This is what Klein shows: the interest for objects as *distinct*, as objects of human interest, in great number, as *equivalent* objects — the development of that infinite world of objects which constitutes a *human world*. And her text is precious because she is, so to speak, an experienced therapist — she *senses* these things, expresses them poorly, but one cannot reproach her.

In any case, the theory of the ego is *incomplete*; she is perhaps not decided to give it; that is what is missing.

But what she explains well — Lacan must hurry, will resume next time — is: if the objects of the human world multiply and develop with the richness constituting the originality of this world, it is to the extent that, in a kind of *process of expulsion linked to the primitive destruction-drive*, these objects in their first signification (call it, briefly, *affective* — they have exorcised that word now) — concerning a certain primitive relation to the instinctual root of being — to the extent that these objects are *created*, *appear*, by a process of expulsion and destruction — and equally, as these *ejections* are produced in relation to the still-unorganized subject (not yet organized in the register of properly human, communicable reality) — there surges each time a *type of identification* not tolerable.

*Anxiety* is not a kind of energy the subject distributes to constitute objects; there is no turn of phrase in this sense — anxiety is always defined as *surging up* (*arising*). To each of these object-relations corresponds a *mode of identification* of which anxiety is properly speaking the *signal*.

Each time the subject identifies with whatever — and here it is something preceding properly *egoic* identification (even when ego-identification will be made, any new re-identification of the subject will make anxiety surge: "anxiety" in the sense in which anxiety is *temptation*, *vertigo*, *re-loss* of the subject re-finding himself at these extremely primitive levels). Anxiety is such that effectively the play cannot occur, where the subject introduces himself for each object of anxiety — but this is only a connotation. Anxiety is a *signal*, as Freud always sensed (when he approached the notion of anxiety, he formulated it well): a kind of signal, a quality, a subjective coloration.

This anxiety is precisely what does not occur for Dick, because the subject cannot even arrive at this kind of identification that would be already a sketch of symbolism. He remains *facing reality* — paradoxical as this is, he *lives in reality*: he is in Klein's office; there is for him no *other*; there is for him no *himself*; there is reality pure and simple — the interval between the two doors *is the mother's body*.

The trains and what follows are something, but neither *nameable nor named* — until Klein dares, with that brute instinct of hers (which perforated the sum of knowledge that had been impenetrable), to *speak to him*, to speak to a being who literally had given her the apprehension that, *in the symbolic sense*, this is a being who *does not respond*: he is there as if she did not exist, as if she were a piece of furniture.

She speaks to him — that is, she *literally gives names* to something that *already partakes of the symbol* (since it can be immediately named) but which had been, up to then, properly speaking *for the subject pure and simple reality*.

This is the meaning of the term *prematuration* she uses: the subject has reached, in a sense, already the *genital stage* — for the genital stage, after the whole phase of symbolization of fantasies (the comings-and-goings of the subject's identifications which, as he sketches them and withdraws them, redoes them with other objects), gives the major objects of his primitive identification a *series of equivalents* which *multiply* his world and permit (across the imaginary) giving frames to that infinitely more developed real which is the *human real*.

It is in the measure that the subject cannot make these comings-and-goings — that he is immediately in a reality signifying at his level a reality absolutely *de-humanized*: because there is no development, no series of comings-and-goings substituting itself for a series of objects, because each time *anxiety arrests the definitive identification*, the *fixation of reality*.

It is, one may say, an *already-symbolized reality* — since it can be given sense — but one *before* any movement of coming-and-going. It is an *anticipated, primary, frozen symbolization* — a single primary identification — something with a name: *the void*, *the dark*, which is what responds in the subject's proper structure to the originally *gaping* human, who naturally and simply before and in this *gape* has at his disposal only a very limited number of objects of reality he cannot even name.

What does this mean? Gélinier remarked well: he cannot name them at a certain level, for he has already a certain *apprehension* of vocables — let us say he has not made the *Bejahung* of them, he does not *assume* them. Paradoxically, in the function where there exists in him at this level (so paradoxical as it seems) a possibility of *empathy* much greater than the normal — this is what Klein seems to indicate, for he is so well in rapport with the reality \[...\] rarely, but in a distant, non-anxiogenic way.

When Klein had little pencil-shavings — fragmentation of what is not yet made (one must not forget that it is when one begins to demolish that the little \[...\] articulates itself, and also the unleashing of the mechanism) — when he saw the shavings on Klein's blouse, he said: *Poor Melanie Klein*. Do you see the direction in which the problem poses itself?

Lacan will return next time. They will find themselves at the heart of the problem of the relation of symbolism and the real, taken at its angle of origin, the most difficult genetic point. They see also the connection with the region designated the other day in Hyppolite's commentary: the *function of destructionism in the constitution of human reality*.

---

## **Session of 24 February 1954**

Lacan opens by recalling Freud's famous warning, from Chapter VII of *The Interpretation of Dreams*, against confusing the scaffolding with the building. Freud there asks us to imagine the psychic apparatus as a kind of *compound microscope*, a photographic apparatus, or some similar instrument: the psychic locality corresponds to a place *within* such an apparatus where one of the preliminary stages of the image is produced. In the microscope and the telescope these are, in part, *ideal* localities — regions in which no tangible component of the apparatus is located. Freud begs to be excused for the imperfection of such images: they are intended only to assist an attempt to render the complexity of psychic functioning comprehensible by *decomposing* the function and assigning each component to a part of the apparatus.

Inutile to say, Lacan adds, that since advice is made not to be followed, we have not failed since to take *the scaffolding for the building*. On the other hand, this authorization Freud gives us to take "auxiliary representations to approach an unknown fact, the simplest and most tangible being the best" has incited Lacan himself to take a certain liberty in constructing a schema. An optical apparatus much simpler than a complicated microscope — almost childlike — will serve them today.

Lacan recommends in passing a meditation on *optics*. Curious thing: an entire system of metaphysics has been founded on geometry and mechanics, sought there as models of comprehension. It does not seem that one has yet drawn all the profit one might draw from optics. Optics is a strange thing — a whole science whose goal and function consists in *reproducing by apparatuses* something that, unlike all other sciences (which bring to nature a kind of cutting, dissection, anatomy), strives by apparatuses to *produce* that singular thing called *images*.

Lacan is not asking them to take bladders for lanterns, optical images for the images that interest us. But it is not for nothing they bear the same name. Optical images present singular and instructive diversities: some are *purely subjective images* — those called *virtual*; others are *real images*, which by certain sides behave just like objects, can be taken for objects. And there is something more singular: these *real images* can be re-taken and *virtual images* given of them. The real image can rightly take the name of *virtual object*.

The theoretical foundations of optics rest entirely on a *mathematical theory*, without which it would be absolutely impossible to structure optics. For there to be a possible optics, there must be the possibility of *representation*: to any given point in real space, a single point can correspond in another space — that of the imaginary. This is the fundamental structural hypothesis. Without it, one could not write the slightest equation, symbolize the slightest thing — optics would be absolutely impossible. Imaginary and real spaces both interfuse here, while remaining thinkable as different.

Take, as illustration: when one sees a rainbow, one sees something entirely subjective. One sees it at a certain distance broaching onto the landscape. *It is not there*. It is a subjective phenomenon. Yet thanks to a photographic apparatus one records it quite *objectively*. So what is it? Do we no longer know where the subjective is and where the objective? Or is the photographic apparatus rather a *subjective apparatus* — entirely constructed with the aid of a little *x* and a little *y* that inhabit the domain in which the subject lives, namely the domain of *language*?

Lacan leaves these questions open and goes to the example. In Lacan's place, set a formidable cauldron — something as close as possible to a *hemisphere*, well-polished on the inside: a *spherical mirror*. If it is set forward to about the table, the audience will not see itself in it. But you know a spherical mirror produces what is called a *real image*: every light-ray emanating from any point of an object placed at a certain distance — preferably in the plane of the sphere's center — corresponds (by convergence of reflected rays on the sphere's surface) to another luminous point in the same plane, giving a *real image* of the object.

Lacan begs forgiveness for not having been able to bring the cauldron and apparatus today; the audience must represent it. Suppose this is a box, hollow on one side, placed at the center of the sphere. Here is the hemisphere; here is the box, it has a foot. The classic experiment, at the time when physics was *amusing*, when one made experiments — just as we are at a moment when this is truly *psychoanalysis*: the closer we are to amusing psychoanalysis, the more it was true psychoanalysis. Eventually it will become rounded off, made up only of approximations and tricks: one will no longer understand at all what one is doing. Just as it is no longer necessary to understand anything of optics to make a microscope. But rejoice — we are still doing psychoanalysis, even when doing what we are doing today.

On the box you place a *vase* — section of a vase — a real vase. Below, inside the box, is a *bouquet of flowers*. What happens? Lacan enlarges the cauldron — the hemisphere must be enormous, with a large opening — and explains: by the property of the spherical mirror, every luminous point of the bouquet (which is below) reflects on the sphere's surface and comes to the symmetric luminous point above. *All* rays from a given point return to the same point, by the surface's property; thus a *real image* is formed — the real image of the bouquet forms *inside the neck of the vase*. The rays do not cross exactly well in the schema, but this is also true in reality and in all optical instruments — it is always an approximation.

These rays continue, diverge again — that is, for an eye placed in the right position they are *convergent*. The characteristic of rays striking an eye in convergent form is to give what is called a *real image*. Divergent in coming to the eye, they are convergent in leaving the eye. If the contrary — rays striking the eye in the opposite sense — we have the formation of a *virtual image*. This is what happens when you see an image in the mirror: you see it *where it is not*. Whereas with the real image, you see it where it is — on the sole condition that your eye be *in the field of the rays that have already crossed at the corresponding point*.

That is, at that moment you will see the curious *imaginary bouquet* appear at this place. (You do not see the real bouquet, hidden below.) If your eye is in the right field, you will see appear a curious imaginary bouquet on which the eye must *accommodate* (since the image forms just at the place of the vase), and because the eye must accommodate the same way for the same plane, you will have what is called an *impression of reality*. Yet you will sense that something is a bit off — because the rays do not cross exactly well, there will be something blurred. The farther you are, the more the *parallax* (the small accommodation for lateral displacement of the eye) becomes minimal — the more complete the illusion.

Lacan apologizes for the length of this development — it is an apologue that will serve them much. It is not Lacan who invented it; it is known since long ago under the title *Experiment of the Inverted Bouquet* (Bouasse). Taken as it is, in its innocence and without preconceived idea by its authors (who did not fabricate it for us), it appears — even in its contingent details (vase and bouquet) — particularly seductive.

For if something is to be placed at the base of this *primitive imaginary* dialectic — in connection with the apprehension of *the image of one's own body*, more deeply with the relations of the *Ur-Ich* or *Lust-Ich*, the notion of a primitive ego constituting itself in a kind of *cleavage*, distinction from the external world (the relation of what is included within and what is excluded by all those processes of *Ausstossung*, projection, delimitation of the ego's proper domain) — if there is something to be put in the first plane in all our conceptions, at the primitive genetic stage of ego-formation, it is precisely that of *container and content*. And in this sense the relation of vase to flowers it contains can serve as a metaphor — and a most precious one.

When Lacan insists on the theory of the *mirror stage* — on the fact that this taking-consciousness of the body as totality is something that happens *prematurely* (though correlatively) with respect to the moment when functional development gives the subject integration of his motor functions — this takes its value from the fact that a *virtual apprehension* of an imaginary mastery, given to the subject by sight of the *total form* of the human body (whether his own image or one given him by a fellow), is something that, for him, in this experience, is *detached, disengaged*, does not confuse itself with the maturational process. In other words, the subject *as subject anticipates* on this physiologically incomplete mastery; this anticipation will mark, give its particular style to, every subsequent exercise of motor mastery once effected.

This was nothing other than the original adventure by which man for the first time experiences: that he *sees himself*, *reflects himself*, *conceives himself as other than he is*. This is an absolutely essential dimension of the human, structurally constitutive of his fantasmatic life.

So everything happens as if, at one moment, the imaginary pot containing the bouquet of flowers were unbound from it — the pot in relation to which the subject already makes a first apprehension among all the *Ids* we suppose at the origin: there we are object, instincts, desires, tendencies — pure and simple reality, in the sense that *reality is not yet in any way delimited*, can yet be the object of no definition (qualitative, good or bad), as in the series of judgments Freud referred to in *Die Verneinung*: whether *it is* or *it is not*. Reality is here at once *chaotic and absolute*, originary. Within this first apprehension, the image of the body gives the first form, also to the subject, that allows him to situate what is of the ego and what is not.

Lacan schematizes — every development of a metaphor or *apparatus for thinking* requires that, at the start, one make felt to what it serves and what it means. The schema will allow play of all sorts of reciprocal movements of this container, here supposed *imaginary*.

Reverse the experiment's conditions: the vase below, the flowers on top. Lacan has reversed the schema; we can at our pleasure make imaginary what is real, provided we keep the *relation of signs*: \+ − \+ or − \+ −. Provided the relation is conserved — that we have to deal with a *hidden real* and an *imaginary* that reproduces it, and a *real put into connection with this imaginary*.

This first *imaginary ego* is what the first play of *inclusion* and *exclusion* will situate itself in relation to — everything at issue in the subject before the birth of the ego. What else does this schema, this apologue, show us? For the *illusion* to be produced — that is, for there to be constituted, for the eye that looks, a world where the imaginary can include and thereby form the real, where the real can include and thereby situate the imaginary — there must still be a condition: the eye must be in a certain position. *It must be inside this cone*.

If outside the cone, the eye will no longer see the imaginary — for a simple reason: nothing of the emission-cone there will strike it. It will see things *in their raw real state*: namely, the inside of the mechanism — a poor empty pot (in Bouasse's experiment, where the bouquet is hidden below), or lonely flowers (in Bouasse's experiment as modified by Lacan, where the vase is hidden below).

What does this mean? You will tell me: we are not an *eye*. What is this eye that strolls? And if all this is to mean something?

The box: it means *your own body*. The bouquet: the *instincts and desires*, the *objects of desire* that wander about. And the cauldron? It might well be the *cortex*. Why not? If it were the cortex, that would be amusing. We'll speak of this another day.

In the middle of all this is your eye. It does not stroll: it is fixed there, it is a kind of *little appendix titillating our cortex*. So why tell us this eye is strolling, sometimes things work, sometimes they do not? Evidently: the eye, as very frequently, is the *symbol of the subject*, and all science rests on *reducing the subject to an eye*. That is why all science is projected before you, objectivized.

For us to hold ourselves for a little instant inside our eye, we must put ourselves in the position of the scientist who decrees: *that is not true*. He decrees he is simply an eye and posts at the door "do not disturb the experimenter." In life, of course, things are quite different, precisely because we are *not an eye*.

So what does the eye there mean? What can we make of it in this comparison? It means simply this: in this relation of imaginary and real, in the constitution of the world that must result from it, *everything depends on the position of the subject*. And the position of the subject — you must know, since Lacan repeats it — is essentially characterized by *its place in the symbolic world*, that is, in the world of *speech*. Lacan says no more.

What he has been telling them implies many things — the *relation to the other*, etc. But before being the relation to the other, it is *its place in the symbolic world*. That is, whether or not he has the possibility (or defense) of being called *Pedro*. According as he is or is not called Pedro, he is in the field of the cone or not in it.

Here is what you must put in your heads, as a point of departure. Even if it seems a bit *stiff* to understand all that follows, namely Melanie Klein's text — something we must take for what it is: *an experience*.

What does this case show us? Mlle Gélinier recounts and resumes it. Why not take her text? Lacan reviewed it and it is faithful — which does not prevent referring back to Klein. Here is a boy of about four years, with a *general developmental level called 15 to 18 months*. This is a matter of definition; one never knows what one means in such a case.

What instrument of measure? Often unspecified. What is called an *affective development of 15 to 18 months* remains still more vague than the image of one of the flowers in the experiment just produced. A vocabulary very limited and worse than limited, *incorrect*: he deforms words, uses them inaptly most of the time, while at other moments he plainly knows their sense. The most striking fact, on which Klein insists: this child has *no desire to make himself understood*; he does not seek to communicate. His only more-or-less ludic activities are to emit sounds and to take pleasure in sounds without signification, in noises.

What, then, do we have to deal with? A child who, curious to note, *possesses something of the language* — clearly, since Klein could not make herself understood by him otherwise. So on the one hand certain elements of the symbolic apparatus are there. On the other, his striking attitude.

Klein, from the first contact (which is so important) characterizes the apathy, the indifference. He is not, for all that, *without being in some way oriented*. He does not give the impression of an idiot, far from it. He is in Klein's presence, and Klein distinguishes his attitude from all the neurotic children she has seen before. She marks the distinction: he shows no apparent anxiety, even in its masked or veiled forms (those produced in neurotics) — not always explosive manifestations, but simply certain attitudes of withdrawal, stiffness, timidity, where something is held back and which would not escape an experienced therapist. On the contrary, *he is there as if nothing matters*. He looks at Klein *as he would look at a piece of furniture*.

Lacan underlines these aspects, because he has brought into relief precisely the absolutely uniform, undifferentiated character of a certain viewpoint reality has for this child: everything is *equally real* and *equally indifferent*.

What does Klein tell us? Here begin Mlle Gélinier's perplexities. Klein tells us the *child's world* arises from a *container* (which would be the *mother's body*) and a *content of the body* of this mother. In the course of his instinctual relations with this privileged object, the child is led to a series of *imaginary incorporations*. He can bite, absorb the mother's body. The style of this incorporation is a *style of destruction*. The child understands incorporation is destructive; what he will encounter in the mother's body is also a number of *objects*, themselves endowed with a certain unity (though included), but which may be *dangerous* for him — for the same reason exactly that he is dangerous for them. He invests them with the same capacities of destruction "in mirror" — properly speaking — that he himself feels he carries in this first apprehension of the first objects.

So he will accentuate, in relation to the first limitation of his ego or his being, the *exteriority* of these objects: he will *reject* them as bad, dangerous objects — *caca*. These objects themselves, once externalized, isolated from this first universal container (this first great whole, the fantasmatic image of the mother's body — the total empire of the first infantile reality at that moment), will still appear to him marked with the same *maleficient accent* that marked his first relations with them.

This is why he will *re-introject* them a second time, and direct his interest toward *other less dangerous objects* — making the famous equation "feces \= urine," for example, and various other external-world objects: more neutralized, equivalent to them, linked to the first objects by an *imaginary equation*.

At the origin, what the *symbolic equation* we later rediscover between these different objects *is*, at its emergence, is an alternative mechanism of *expulsion and reintrojection*, of *projection and reabsorption* by the subject. That is, precisely the imaginary play Lacan is trying to symbolize by these *imaginary inclusions of real objects*, or inversely by these *takings of imaginary objects within a real enclosure*.

The subject plays with container and content. He has already *entified* in certain objects (the "little train," for example) the possibility of a certain number of individualizations, of tendencies, even of *persons* — himself as "little train," in relation to his father who is "big train," quite naturally.

The number of objects significant for him is, as a surprising fact, extremely reduced — to the minimal signs allowing him to express the inside/outside, content/container: the *dark space* immediately assimilated to the *interior of the mother's body*, in which he takes refuge. But what does not occur is the *free play, the conjunction* between these different forms (imaginary and real) of objects. And this is why — to Gélinier's astonishment — when he turns and takes refuge in the empty, dark interior of this maternal body, the *objects are not there*.

This for a simple reason: in his case, *the bouquet and the vase cannot be there at the same time*. That is the key. Gélinier's astonishment rests on this: for Klein everything is on a plane of *equal reality* — *unreal reality*, as Klein herself says — which does not permit conceiving the *dissociation of the different sets* of primitive objects at issue. Why? Because Klein has no theory of the *imaginary* and no theory of the *ego*.

But if we introduce this notion — in the measure that one part of this reality is imagined, the other real; but that inversely, in the measure where one is reality, the other becomes imaginary — we understand why the play of conjunction of the different "sets" can never be completed.

Here we are uniquely under the angle of the *mirror-relation*, generally called the plane of *projection*. Lacan does not say *introjection*; the term is badly chosen. One would need another word for the correlate of this projection — what concerns *introjection* as we use it in analysis. The word *introjection* is always practically employed (as one will notice, this will enlighten) at the moment when it is a question of *symbolic introjection*. The word *introjection* is always accompanied by a *symbolic denomination*. *Introjection is always the introjection of the speech of the other*.

This introduces a quite different dimension. Around this distinction one can make the parting between what is the function of the *ego* and what is of the order of the first dual register, *function of the superego*. It is no accident that analytic theory distinguishes them, nor that one admits the superego — the true, authentic superego — is a *secondary introjection* relative to the function of the *ideal ego*. These are lateral remarks. Lacan returns to Klein's case.

What can we think other than the different references Lacan is illustrating for them? If we see what happens, it is this: the child is there, has a certain number of significant registers — one in the imaginary domain, whose *extreme narrowness* Klein (whom we can follow here) underlines, the *poverty* of the possible play of imaginary transposition by which alone can be made that progressive valorization on the so-called "affective" plane of objects, by a kind of multiplication, fan-deployment of all the imaginary equations permitting human beings, among animals, to be the one with an almost infinite number of objects at disposal — marked with *Gestalt-value* in their *Umwelt*, isolated as forms.

Klein marks this both: the poverty of this imaginary world, and at the same time the impossibility for this child to enter into effective relation with these objects *as structures*. The important correlation is this.

What is the significant point if we take only the objective attitude of this child? If we resume all Klein describes of his attitude: *this child addresses no call*. Here is a notion Lacan asks them to retain, for they will see it return.

You will tell me: of course, with his call he reintroduces language. But Lacan said he had it already, his system of language. He has it sufficiently. The proof: he plays with it. He uses it even for something very particular: to conduct a play of *opposition* with the adults' intrusion attempts.

For example — well known — he behaves *negativistically*: when his mother proposes a name, he is capable of reproducing it correctly, but will reproduce it unintelligibly, deformed, *à propos de bottes* — that is, in a way that serves nothing.

We find here the distinction between *negativism* and *denegation*, as Hyppolite showed — a way that proves not only his culture, but that he has seen patients and knows what negativism and denegation are. They are not at all the same. It is properly *negativistically* that this child uses language.

By introducing the *call*, Lacan is not reintroducing *language*. More: not only is it not language, it is not a kind of level *superior* to language. Even more: it is *below* language, if one speaks of levels. You need only observe a domestic animal to see that a being deprived of language is quite capable of addressing calls — and up to a point, calls directed (all kinds of gestures to attract your attention) toward something precisely lacking to it.

The call at issue, the *human call*, is a call to which a possible, ulterior, richer development is reserved — precisely because it is produced inside a being who has already acquired the level of language. It is a phenomenon that *exceeds* language but takes its value as *articulation*, as a second time, in relation to language.

To be schematic: there is a Karl Bühler who made a theory of language. Not the only or most complete theory, but it contains something: the *three stages* of language. He unfortunately associated them with registers that do not make them very accessible. The three stages:

— The *énoncé* (statement), taken as such — a level having its value as a kind of *natural-given* level. We consider the énoncé when, for example, two people, I am saying the simplest thing — an imperative. At the énoncé level we recognize: all this concerns *the nature of the subject*. Clearly a man — an officer, a professor — will not give his order in the same style or language as someone else, a worker, a foreman. At the énoncé level, all we learn concerns the subject's nature, in his very style, his intonations.

— Another plane: in any imperative, that of the *call* — the tone in which the imperative is given. Also very important: with the same text, the same énoncé can have completely different values. The simple énoncé "stop" can have, in different circumstances, a completely different *call-value*.

— The third value is properly the *communication*: what is at issue and its reference to the ensemble of the situation.

We are at the level of the *call*. It is something whose value is within the *already-acquired* system of language. And what is at issue is precisely that *this child emits no call*. There the system is interrupted — the system by which the subject comes to situate himself in language, at the level of *speech*, which is not the same.

This child is master of language, properly speaking, up to a certain moment, a certain level. Quite clear. He does not *speak* — as Lacan said last time, this subject is there and literally *does not respond*. *Speech has not come*. What is happening? This is said at length, very clearly, throughout Klein's text. She has renounced any technique. She has minimum material. She does not even have games — this child does not play. Even when he takes the little train, he does not play — he does it as he crosses the atmosphere, not as an invisible person, but everything is in a way invisible to him.

What does she do? One would need to reread Klein's exact phrases to bring out what is at issue. She is keenly aware she is making *no interpretation*. She says: I start from my already-known preconceived ideas of this stage. So I go at it squarely: I tell him "Dick-train" and the big train is "Papa-train."

The child begins to play with his little train. He says the word *station*. There is the sketch, the *junction of language with this imaginary system* — his extremely short register composed of trains, the possibility of valorizing a dark place, doorknobs. To this are limited the faculties not of communication but of *expression*. For him everything is equivalent: the imaginary and the real. And what does Klein tell him at that moment? *The station is Mommy. Dick enter into Mommy.*

From this everything is triggered. She will only make him such interpretations, no others. Very quickly the child tries it. It is a fact — the object of the experience, in the same way the bouquet of flowers on the table. Another register: inside/outside. From this everything will start. Of course it will enrich itself — but she has done nothing other than to bring the *verbalization, symbolization* of an effective relation: that of a *named being* with another.

From this, the child — after a first ceremony of taking refuge in the dark space, having reconnected with the *container* for the first time from this kind of *plastered-on verbalization-symbolization* of the *situation-myth* (to call it by its name) brought by Klein — and this is what Klein notes perfectly: as the newness awakens, *the verbalization also by the child* — a call, but a *spoken call*.

There has been no call in the style of what we commonly call *psychic contact*; on the spoken plane there is now a first call. The child asks for his nurse immediately after — that nurse with whom he had entered, whom he had let depart as if nothing — he had not registered the blow of the separation.

And for the first time he produces a *call-reaction* — that is, something not simply an affective call, a thing mimed by the whole being, but in its first form a *verbalized call*, comprehending in some way a *response* — a first communication, in the proper and technical sense.

When Klein has pursued her experience further, things develop to the point that she brings in all the other elements of a situation thenceforth organized, much richer — including the father, who comes to play his role. Klein tells us: relations develop on the Oedipal plane, in an unambiguous way.

He *realizes*, *symbolizes reality* around him, from this kind of initial nucleus, this little palpitating cell of symbolism given him by Klein. What is this? It is what Klein will then call: *having opened the doors of his unconscious*. Lacan does not even ask them to reflect: in what has Klein done anything that manifests or signifies any apprehension whatever of any *recess* in the subject that would be his unconscious? She admits it, straightaway, by habit.

Lacan asks them simply to reread this observation. You will see the absolutely sensational formulation Lacan always gives: *the unconscious is the discourse of the Other*. Here is a case where it is absolutely manifest. There is no species of unconscious *in the subject*. It is *Klein's discourse* that grafts onto this *situation of egoic inertia* in the child his first symbolizations — absolutely brutal, appearing arbitrary in certain cases — of the Oedipal situation as Klein practices, always more or less implicitly, with her subjects. This engenders and determines, in this particularly dramatic case of a subject who has not acceded to human reality (in whom there is no call), the position from which he can literally *conquer* — for that is what is at stake — a series of developments, a series of *equivalences*, a system of substitution of objects; can realize the whole series of *equations* allowing him most visibly to pass from the interval between the two door-leaves where he took refuge in the absolute dark of the total container to a number of objects he will progressively substitute: the basin of water, around which he deploys and articulates his whole world; from the basin to a heater of some kind — something increasingly elaborated, increasingly rich, increasingly *pleated* as to its possibilities of content, and equally as to its possibilities of definition of *content* and *non-content*.

What does it mean, then, to speak in this case of *development of the ego*? This rests on the ultimate ambiguities in analysis: confusing *ego* and *subject*. It is in the measure that the subject is integrated into the symbolic system and exercises and affirms himself there by the exercise of a *veritable speech* — and (note) it is not necessary that this speech be his own — a veritable speech can be brought there, in the momentarily formed couple between therapist and subject, in its yet *least affectivized* form. Within this, a certain speech — surely not just any (here we see the virtue of what we call the symbolic situation of Oedipus, which is truly the *key*).

A key very reduced. Lacan has already indicated there is probably a whole keyring. He may one day give a lecture on what one of the primitive myths gives us here. He will not say "lesser primitive" — they are not lesser; they know much more than we do. When we look at a mythology (perhaps the one to emerge regarding a Sudanese population), we see that for them the Oedipus complex is only a *thin little joke*, a tiny detail of an immense myth allowing the collation of a whole series of inter-relations among subjects of a richness and complexity next to which Oedipus appears so abridged an edition that, in the end, one can say it is not always usable.

But never mind. For us analysts, up to now we have contented ourselves with it. We try to elaborate it a little, but timidly. We always feel horribly entangled because of insufficient distinction among *imaginary*, *symbolic*, and *real*. Klein broaches the Oedipal schema; the subject situates himself. At that moment, the already-constituted imaginary relation is complex; but the *excessively reduced, poor* relations he has with the external world allow him to introduce, within the world we call *real*, this *primitive real* which is for us literally *ineffable* — the world of the child in which, when he tells us nothing, we have no means to penetrate except by *symbolic extrapolations*.

This is the ambiguity of all systems like Klein's, when she tells us that within the empire of the body's interior he is there with all his brothers, not to mention the father's penis. This world — Lacan has shown its movement, in a first stage of structuration between *imaginary* and *real*: how to understand its movement, namely what we call the *successive cathexes* that delimit the variety of objects (human objects, summable) from this first *fresco* (so Lacan called it) of what is properly a *significant speech*, insofar as formulating a first fundamental structure of what in the law of speech *humanizes man*.

How to say this another way, to prepare further developments? What does Klein call *to him alone*? What does the *field of the call* represent inside speech? The *possibility of refusal*. Lacan says "the possibility": the call does not imply refusal, no dichotomy, no bi-partition. But it is at the moment when the *call* is produced that we see manifestly establishing in the subject the *relations of dependence*. For from this moment he will receive his nurse with open arms; he will manifest toward Klein, by going to hide behind the door deliberately, the need to *have a companion* in this small corner he had occupied — *dependence will establish itself after*.

You see thus play independently in this observation the series of relations *pre* and *post* language — preverbal and postverbal — in the child. And you see precisely that the *external world* — what we call the real world, which is only a humanized, symbolized world, made of the *transcendence introduced by the symbol* into primitive reality — can constitute itself only when there have been produced, *in the right place*, a series of encounters, of positions of the same order as those that in this schema make it that the eye must not be just anywhere for the situation to structure itself.

Lacan will use this schema again. He has introduced only one bouquet here, but one can introduce others — the Other. Before speaking of the Other, of identification to the other, he has wanted today simply to say \[this\] inside these relations among *real*, *imaginary*, and *symbolic*, and to show this significant observation.

A subject who has, in some way, all the elements — language, a number of possibilities of imaginary displacements that would allow him to structure his world — may not be \[...\] in the real, simply because *things have not come in a certain order*, because the *figure as a whole is deranged*.

There is no way for him to give this whole the slightest development — what is called in this case the *development of the ego*. This is a more technical sense.

If one re-reads Gélinier's text, one sees to what point — and still better in Klein's text — Klein says both: that the ego has been developed prematurely (in that the child has too *real* a relation to reality, in a certain sense too real, because the imaginary cannot introduce itself); and in a second part of her sentence she uses *ego* by saying it is *the ego that arrests development*. This means simply that the ego cannot, in a certain position, be validly used as the apparatus for structuring the external world. For a simple reason: because of the *eye's bad position*, it does not appear — purely and simply.

Let the vase be virtual: the vase does not appear, and the subject remains in a *reduced reality* with an equally reduced *imaginary baggage*. The spring of this observation is that you must comprehend, said in a particularly significant way, the *virtue of speech* — of the act of speech as *symbolic functioning*, coordinated to an entire symbolic system already established, typical and significant — the function of speech in the *development of the system real-imaginary-symbolic*, which is its base.

Perhaps this would merit your asking questions, rereading the text, also manipulating this little schema, seeing yourselves how, in reality, it can serve. What he has given them today has the value of a theoretical elaboration done right against the text of the problems raised last time by Mlle Gélinier. They will see how it serves not next Wednesday, but the following: *The Transference, at the distinct levels at which it must be studied*. There is another face of transference, better known — *transference in the imaginary*. They will see how today's considerations will serve.

---

## **Session of 10 March 1954**

Lacan opens by saying his audience has been able to grasp, through their dialogue, what presides over their commentary: their attempt to re-think and to re-understand always better the fundamental texts of analytic experience. They have grown familiar with the idea that is the soul of their deepening: *what is always best seen in an experience is what is at a certain distance*.

It is no surprise that it is now and here, in this conversation, that they are led to set out — to understand analytic experience — from what is implied in its most immediate datum: the *symbolic function*, which in their vocabulary is exactly the same thing as the *function of speech*. This central domain of analytic experience they find indicated everywhere, never named, but indicated at every step of Freud's work.

Lacan does not think he is forcing anything in saying that *to translate almost algebraically* what can be immediately translated in this register, in the margin of any Freudian text, gives already at least an important part of the *solutions to the antinomies* manifested there, with an openness, an honesty that makes a Freud text *always an open text* — never closed or sealed as if the whole system were there.

In this sense, Lacan indicates already that he would very much like — and they will see how it inserts into their progress — for someone to take charge for the next session of the commentary on a text not only exemplary but situating itself as the *essential theoretical correspondent* of the period defined by the field of the *Papers on Technique* (1908 to 1920). It is situated very exactly between the text translated as *Remembering, Repeating, and Working-Through* (*Erinnern, Wiederholen und Durcharbeiten*) and the text *Observations on Transference-Love*. Between these — the two most important texts in the collection — is *Zur Einführung des Narzißmus*: *On the Introduction of Narcissism*. It is one of the texts they cannot fail to integrate into their progress, for the simple reason that what is at stake now is precisely this. It is in function of the *analytic dialogue situation* (with the different phases and prolongations implied in the terms *situation* on one hand, *dialogue* — in scare-quotes — on the other) that they have progressed and tried to define in its proper field what is called *resistance*.

Then they were able to formulate a quite general, fundamental definition for this experience — that of *transference*. Yet you sense well that there is a *distance* between this something that separates the subject from the *full speech* analysis awaits of him — this something that they have manifested as *resistance*, where they showed it is the *function of anxiogenic inflection*, properly speaking in its most radical mode the *phenomenon of transference at the level of symbolic exchange* — and what we commonly designate, what we handle in the notion, of a fundamental phenomenon they are going to name. The phenomenon we handle technically in analysis, which appears as the fundamental *energetic spring* (as Freud himself says) of transference in analysis.

In other words, the transference in the sense Freud does not hesitate — in *Observations on Transference-Love* — to call by its name: *love*. They will see, in reading this text (which Lacan supposes they have all read), to what point Freud *distinguishes transference little from love*, how little he elides the amorous and passional phenomenon in its more concrete sense — to the point of saying that, in its ground, with what we know, what we call in life *love*, there is between transference and that *no truly essential distinction*: the structure of this *artificial phenomenon* that is transference and that of the *spontaneous phenomenon* called love (very precisely *love-passion*) are equivalent on the psychic plane.

There is no elision on Freud's part — no way of dissolving the scabrous into some "symbolism" understood as the unreal. The phenomenon is not elided. But this phenomenon is what is commonly called *love*. Around this will center, in the coming sessions, the conclusion of these *Papers on Technique* — hopefully not quite before Easter vacation, though Lacan would not want it to drag much beyond. It will turn on the nature of this *transference-love*, in the most precise, most affective sense. There we can use the term most properly. This will carry them to the heart of another notion Lacan is trying to introduce, without which one cannot make a proper distribution of what we handle in our experience: the *function of the imaginary*.

Do not believe that, any more than the symbolic function, this imaginary function is absent from Freud's texts. Simply, Freud has not put it in the first plane, has not brought it out everywhere it can be found. When they study *On the Introduction of Narcissism*, they will see that Freud himself, in his text, finds no other term to designate — and this may surprise some — the difference between *dementia praecox*, *schizophrenia*, *psychosis*, on one side, and *neurosis* on the other, than precisely this definition. He tells us: the patient who suffers from hysteria or obsessional neurosis has, like the psychotic and as far as the influence of his illness extends, *given up his relation to reality*. But analysis shows he has *in no way broken his erotic relations* with persons and things: he holds them still in *fantasy*. He has on one side substituted for the real objects *imaginary objects* founded on his memories — recall their schema from last time — or has mixed the two; while on the other side he has ceased to direct his motor activities toward attaining his aims in connection with real objects. *To this condition of the libido alone*, Freud says, can we legitimately apply the term *introversion of the libido* (which Jung used without discrimination). It is otherwise with the paraphrenic: he seems really to have withdrawn his libido from the persons and things of the external world *without replacing them by others in his fantasy*. When this then happens — that he *recreates an imaginative world*, his imaginative faculty — the process appears to be a *secondary process*, an effort at reconstruction whose goal is to redirect the libido toward an object.

Here they enter all that the analysis of *Schreber* (which Lacan hopes to begin before year's end) will permit them to deepen: the essential distinction between the *functioning of the imaginary in neurosis and psychosis*. This nevertheless cannot be placed only in the background of what Lacan will express, he thinks, next time under the announced general title *The Function of Transference in the Imaginary*.

That is why he found it auspicious to have learned last night that, at the sub-group on Child Psychoanalysis, Rosine Lefort (here at his right, his pupil) has brought an observation of which she has spoken to him for some time — of a child in an extremely particular situation. As with most observations of children in such grave cases, this one leaves them, certainly, in great embarrassment from the diagnostic and nosological point of view. But she has known how to see, with great depth, what they will hear.

Just as they set out, two conferences back, from Klein's observation as introduction to many things Lacan could then express in the next session, today they will use this alternating rhythm and yield the floor to Rosine Lefort, who will both present a particularly suggestive case as to the function of the imaginary in the formation of the child, and open — to the extent time permits — those questions, so that next time he can insert what could be brought as response in what he will have to expound under the rubric *Transference in the Imaginary*.

Dear Rosine, he yields the floor; expose the case of Robert.

### **Rosine Lefort**

Robert is a little boy, born 4 March 1948\. His history was reconstituted with difficulty; it is above all thanks to material brought in sessions that they were able to know the traumatisms he had undergone. Father unknown. Mother currently confined as paranoid. She had him with her until five months old, wandering from maternal home to maternal home. She neglected essential care to the point of forgetting to feed him; she had constantly to be reminded — especially the bottle. He was so neglected he really suffered from hunger. At five months he was hospitalized in a state of severe hypotrophy and denutrition. Hardly hospitalized, he developed bilateral otitis necessitating a double mastoidectomy. He was then sent to *Paul Parquet*, known for the strict character of its prophylaxis: isolated, not seeing other children, tube-fed because of anorexia. Then forcibly returned to his mother for two months — nothing is known of his life during that period.

At 11 months his mother left him at the Public Assistance depot; some months later he was registered, his mother not having seen him again. From this period (11 months) to the age of 3 years 9 months, he underwent *25 changes of residence* — institutions for children, hospitals — never a proper foster placement because of his condition. The hospitalizations were necessitated by childhood illnesses, adenoidectomy, and normal neurological examinations (ventriculography, electroencephalography). Sanitary and medical evaluations indicated profound somatic perturbations; then, somatic improvement, psychological deteriorations. The last evaluation at *Denfert* (at 3½) proposed *definitive internment* with an undefined parapsychotic state. Gesell test: DQ of 43\. He arrived at 3 years 9 months at the institution where Mme Lefort took him in treatment.

At that moment, he presented as follows. Statural and ponderal condition: very good, except chronic bilateral otorrhea. Motor: pendular gait, great motor incoordination, constant hyperagitation. Language: total absence of coordinated speech, frequent cries, guttural and discordant laughter. He knew only two words, which he shouted: "Madame" and "the wolf." This word, *the wolf*, he repeated all day, so much that Lefort nicknamed him "the wolf-child" — it was really the representation he had of himself.

Behavior: hyperactive, constantly agitated by brusque, disordered, aimless movements. Incoherent prehension: he threw his arm forward to take an object and, if he missed, could not rectify and had to start the movement from the beginning. Varied sleep disorders. Against this permanent background, *crises of convulsive agitation* (without true convulsions), with reddened face, piercing screams, on occasion of routine scenes: the potty (especially emptying the potty), undressing, food, open doors he could not bear, nor darkness, nor other children's cries, and (as they will see) *changes of rooms*.

More rarely, diametrically opposed crises: completely prostrated, looking aimlessly, depressive. With adults: hyperagitated, non-differentiated, no true contact. With children: he seemed to ignore them entirely, but when any of them cried or shouted, he entered a convulsive crisis. In these moments he became dangerous, strong; he strangled other children, so he had to be separated from them at night and meals. One sensed then no manifestation of anguish or felt emotion.

Diagnostically — they will return to it — they did not really know in what category to place him. They nonetheless attempted treatment, wondering if they would arrive at anything.

She will speak of the first year of treatment (afterwards interrupted for a year). It divides into phases. A *preliminary phase* with the behavior he had in life: guttural cries, entering the room running ceaselessly, screaming, jumping and landing crouched, taking his head between his hands, opening and closing the door, turning the light on and off. He took objects, rejected them, or piled them on Lefort. Marked prognathism.

The only thing she could draw from these first sessions: he did not dare approach the bottle on the table — he could approach it only if the table was empty, then would not touch it but blow on it. Another interest: the *basin*, which when full of water seemed to trigger panic.

At the end of this preliminary phase, in one session, after piling everything on her in great agitation, he fled and Lefort heard him at the top of the stairs (which he could not descend alone), saying in a pathetic tone, a very low intonation that was not his usual one: *Maman*, facing the void. This phase ended: outside treatment, one evening after bedtime, standing on his bed, with plastic scissors he tried to cut his penis before the terrified little girls.

In the second part he began to expose what *the wolf* was for him. He shouted it constantly. He began one day by trying to strangle a little girl Lefort had in treatment. They had to separate them and put him in another room. His reaction was violent — intense agitation. Lefort had to come and bring him back to the room he usually lived in. As soon as he was there, he screamed "the wolf\!" and threw everything across the room — food and plates (the refectory). The following days, each time he passed through the room where he had been put, he screamed "the wolf\!"

This theme struck Lefort. It also illuminates his behavior with doors, which he could not bear *open*. He spent his sessions opening them to make her close them and screaming "the wolf\!" If one recalls his history, *changes of place and of rooms were for him a destruction*, since he had changed places and adults ceaselessly. This had become for him a veritable *principle of destruction* that had intensely marked the foundation of the primordial manifestations of his life: *ingestion and excretion*. He expressed this mainly in two scenes — with the bottle, and with the potty.

He had ended by taking the bottle. One day he went to open the door and extended the bottle to someone imaginary (when alone in a room with an adult he continued to behave as if other children were around). He extended the bottle, returned tearing off the nipple, made her put it back, re-extended the bottle outside, left the door open, turned his back to her, swallowed two swigs of milk, and facing her tore off the nipple, threw his head back, doused himself with milk, poured the rest on her, and, taken by panic, departed *unconscious and blind*. She had to pick him up on the stairs where he was beginning to roll. She had the impression he had *swallowed destruction* at that moment, where the open door and the milk were linked.

The potty scene that followed bore the same character of destruction. He had felt obliged, at the start of treatment, to defecate in session, thinking that if he gave her something, he kept her. He could do it only pressed against her — sitting on the potty, holding her apron with one hand, the bottle or a pencil with the other, in great fear. He ate after, and especially before. And for urine he drank. The emotional intensity testified to great fear. The last of these scenes illuminated the relation between *defecation and destruction by changes*.

In this scene, he began by defecating beside her. Then, his feces beside him, he leafed through book-pages, turning them. He heard a noise outside. Mad with fear, he went out, took his potty, deposited it before the door of the person who had just entered the next room. Then he came back, pressed himself against the door, screaming "the wolf\! the wolf\!" She had the impression of a *propitiatory rite*.

This feces — he was incapable of *giving* it to her. He knew, to a degree, that she did not demand it. He went to put it outside; he knew it would be thrown out, *destroyed*. She explained this. He went to get the potty back, put it in the room beside her, hid it with paper as if not to have to give it.

Then he began to be *aggressive* against her — as if by giving him permission to *possess* himself, through this feces he could dispose of, she had given him the possibility of being aggressive. Until then, unable to possess, he had not the sense of aggression but of *self-destruction*, which moreover explained his behavior with other children.

From that day on, he no longer felt obliged to defecate in session; he used *symbolic substitutes*: sand. He showed the confused representation he had of himself. His anxiety, agitation grew greater in life — he became untenable. Lefort herself attended in session true *tourbillons* in which she had difficulty intervening.
