That day, after drinking some milk, he spilled some on the floor, threw sand in the basin of water, filled the bottle with sand and water, urinated in the potty, put sand in it, gathered milk mixed with sand and water, added it all to the potty, putting on top the rubber doll and the bottle, and entrusted everything to her. At that moment, he went to open the door, came back face contorted with fear, took back the bottle that was in the potty and *broke it*, dwelling on it until reducing it to crumbs. He gathered them carefully and buried them in the sand of the potty.

He was in such a state she had to take him down, sensing she could do no more for him. He carried the potty away. A particle of sand fell on the ground, triggering an *unbelievable panic* in him. He had to pick up every bit of sand, as if it were a piece of himself, screaming "the wolf\! the wolf\!" He could not bear to remain in the collectivity, could bear no other child approaching. They had to put him to bed in a state of intense tension that yielded spectacularly only after a *diarrheic debacle* he smeared everywhere with his hands — on his bed, on the walls. The whole scene was so pathetic, lived with such anguish, that Lefort grew worried and began to realize *the idea he had of himself*.

He specified it the next day, when she had had to frustrate him: he ran to the window, opened it, shouted "the wolf\!", and seeing *his image in the pane*, hit it shouting "the wolf\! the wolf\!" Robert represented himself thus: *he was the wolf* — this principle of destruction he struck in his own image. This potty in which he had put what entered into himself and what left it — urine and feces, then a human image, the doll, then the bottle-debris — was really an *image of himself*, similar to that of the wolf, as testified by the panic when a little sand fell to the floor.

Successively and simultaneously, he is all these elements he had put in the potty — the pieces of the broken bottle, which remain the last image of himself — just after having connected this action of breaking it with *the door, the outside, the changes*. He was only a series of objects by which he entered into contact with daily life: symbols of his body's contents — sand the symbol of feces, water of urine, milk of what enters his body. But the potty scene shows he differentiated all this very little. For him, all contents are united in one same sense of *permanent destruction of his body* which, opposed to these contents, represents the container — and which Robert symbolized by the broken bottle.

In the next phase, he *exorcised the wolf*. Exorcism — for this child gave Lefort the impression of being *possessed*. Thanks to her *permanence*, he could exorcise, with a little milk he had drunk, the daily-life scenes that did him so much harm.

At this moment her interpretations tended to differentiate his body's contents on the affective plane: *milk is what one receives. Feces is what one gives, and its value depends on the milk received. Urine is aggressive.* Many sessions unfolded thus. He would urinate in the potty and then announce: *not caca, c'est pipi* — desolated. She reassured him: he had received too little to give without it destroying him. This reassured him. He could then go empty the potty in the toilets. Emptying was surrounded with many *protective rites*: he began by emptying urine in the WC sink, leaving the tap running to *replace urine with water*. He filled the potty until it overflowed, as if a container had existence only by its content and must overflow as if to contain it in turn.

There is here a *syncretic vision* of being in time, as container and at the same time as content, as in intrauterine life. He refound this confused image of himself. He emptied the urine and tried to catch it, persuaded *he himself was leaving*. He screamed "the wolf\!" The potty could have reality for him only *full*. Lefort's whole attitude was to show him the *reality of the potty that remained* after being emptied — as he, Robert, *remained* after urinating; as the tap was not carried away by the running water but was always there, even when water did not run.

Through these interpretations and her *permanence*, Robert progressively introduced a *delay* between emptying and filling — until one day he came back triumphant with an empty potty in his arm. He had visibly *won the idea of permanence of his body*.

In parallel, he conducted another experience of his body: his clothes were his *container*, and stripping them was *certain death*. The undressing scene was the occasion of true crises. The last had lasted three hours; the staff described him as "possessed," screaming "the wolf\!", running from room to room, spreading the feces he found in potties on other children, calming only when *bound*.

The day after this scene he came in session, began to undress in great anxiety, and naked climbed into the bed. Three sessions were needed for him to drink a little milk naked in the bed. He pointed to the window and door and struck his image screaming "the wolf\!"

In parallel, in daily life undressing became easy, but followed by a great depression: he sobbed in the evening without reason, came down to be consoled by the supervisor, falling asleep in her arms.

In conclusion of this phase: he exorcised with her the emptying of the potty and the undressing-scene, thanks to her *permanence* which had made milk a constructing element. But Robert, pushed by the necessity of constructing a minimum, did not touch the past — he counted only with the present of his daily life, as if *deprived of memory*.

In the next phase, *Lefort became the wolf*. He took advantage of the little construction he had made to project on her all the evil he had drunk — so to speak retrieving *memory*. He could become progressively aggressive.

It was tragic. Pushed by the past, he had to be aggressive against her; at the same time she was the one he needed in the present. She had to reassure him by her interpretations, speak of the past obliging him to be aggressive, which did not entail her disappearance or his change of place (which he had taken as a punishment). Having been aggressive, he tried to destroy himself: he represented himself by an unbroken bottle and tried to break it. She removed it from his hands; he was not in a state to bear breaking it. He resumed the session and his aggression.

He made her play the role of his *starving mother*, obliged her to sit on a chair where his cup of milk was so she would *spill the milk*, depriving him of his *good nourishment*. He screamed "the wolf\!", took the cradle and baby and threw them out the window, in a furious accusation. He turned against her and made her *ingest dirty water* in great violence, screaming "the wolf\!" This bottle represented the *bad nourishment* due to separation and changes, after a bad mother who had deprived him of nourishment.

In parallel, he charged her with another aspect of the bad mother: *the one who leaves*. He saw her leave the institution one evening. The next day he reacted — he had seen her leave before but without being able to express the emotion. That day he urinated on her in great aggression and anxiety. This was the prelude to a final scene that definitively charged her with all the evil he had undergone and projected *the wolf* into her. She had ingested the bottle with dirty water, received the aggressive urine because she had left. *She was therefore the wolf*. Robert separated himself in a session by *locking her in the toilets*, while he returned alone to the session room, climbed into the empty bed, and began to moan.

He could not *call* her, and she had to return — she was the *permanent person*. She came back. Robert was stretched out, pathetic face, thumb held two centimeters from his mouth. For the *first time in a session he extended his arms to her and let himself be consoled*. From this session, his behavior changed totally. This child who aggressed others, strangled them, tore with his teeth, became the gentlest being — defending the little ones, consoling them, feeding them. She had the impression he had *exorcised the wolf*. From this moment he no longer spoke of it, and could pass to the next phase: the *bodily regression*, the construction of the *ego-body* he had never been able to make.

To use the dialectic he had always used (contents-containers): Robert, to construct himself, had to be *her content*, but had to assure himself of her possession — that is, his future container. He began this period by taking a bucket full of water whose handle was a cord. This cord he could absolutely not bear being attached on both ends; it had to *hang* on one side. Lefort had been struck that when she had to tighten it to carry the bucket, this put him in a state of almost physical pain. Until the day, in a scene, he put the bucket of water *between his legs*, took the cord, and *attached it to his umbilicus*. She had the impression the bucket was *her* and he was attaching himself to her by a cord — the *umbilical cord*.

Then he overturned the bucket's contents, stripped naked, lay in this water in *fetal position*, curled up, stretching from time to time, even opening his mouth and closing it on the liquid *as a fetus drinks the amniotic liquid* (as recent American experiments have shown).

\[continuing through subsequent developments, Lefort details: the conflict; his being now capable of receiving and giving; giving his feces without fear of castration; the changed clinical picture — DQ from 43 to 80 at Gesell, IQ of 75 at Terman-Merrill; motor troubles gone, prognathism gone; he became amiable with other children; language only remained rudimentary, no full sentences, just essential words.

She left for vacation two months. On return: he played an interesting scene showing the *coexistence* in him of past patterns and present construction. During her absence he had expressed in his ancient mode what separation represented and his fear of losing her. On her return, he emptied (as if to destroy them) the milk, his urine, his feces, took off his apron and threw it in the water. He had thus destroyed his ancient contents and ancient container, found again by the trauma of her absence. Next day, overwhelmed, he expressed on the somatic plane: profuse diarrhea, vomiting, syncope. Robert was *completely emptying himself of his past image*. Only her permanence could make the liaison with a new image of himself — *a new birth*.

He acquired this new image. In sessions he replayed ancient traumatisms they had been unaware of — notably: drinking the bottle, putting the nipple in his ear, drinking again, then breaking the bottle in great violence. He could do it without his body's integrity suffering. He had separated from his bottle-symbol and could express himself through the bottle as *object*.

This session was so striking — he repeated it twice — that she inquired about his antrotomy at five months. They learned that in the ENT service where he was operated, he was not anesthetized; during this painful operation a bottle of sugared water was held in his mouth.

This traumatic episode illuminated the image Robert had constructed: a *starving paranoid dangerous mother* who certainly attacked him; then this separation, a bottle held by force making him swallow his cries and the pain done to him, tube-feeding, and 25 successive changes. Robert could have no other image of himself.

She had the impression Robert's drama was that *all the oral-sadistic fantasies he could have had had been realized* by these conditions of existence — these fantasies had *become reality*.

Recently she had to confront him with a reality. She had been absent a year, returned eight months pregnant. He saw her pregnant. He began by playing fantasies of destruction of this child. She disappeared for delivery; during her absence her husband took him in treatment, where he played the destruction of this child. When she returned, he saw her *flat, without child*. He was thus persuaded — being always at the stage where fantasies were realities — that his fantasies had *become reality*: he had killed the child; therefore she would kill him. He was extremely agitated these last 15 days, until he could tell her. She confronted him with reality: she brought him her daughter so he could now make the *cut*. His agitation fell sharply. The next session he began to express to her at last a sentiment of *jealousy* — he attached himself to something living, not to death.

This child has remained always at the stage where fantasies are reality. Reality had *imposed his fantasies on him*. Thanks to fantasies of intrauterine construction, which in treatment *were reality*, he could make this astonishing construction. Had he passed this stage, she would not have obtained this construction of himself.

As she said yesterday: this child had *sunk under the real*; there was at the start of treatment no symbolic function, even less imaginary function.

Lacan: he had nonetheless two words.

Hyppolite questions: where did *the wolf* come from? Lefort: in children's institutions, nurses often frighten them with the wolf. In her institution, when the children were unbearable, they were locked in the kindergarten and a nurse went outside to make the wolf-cry to quiet them. He gave this form, concretized it.

Hyppolite: it would remain to explain why this wolf-story whose fear fixed on him. Lefort: the wolf was evidently the devouring mother, in part. Hyppolite: do you believe the wolf is always the devouring mother? Lefort: in children's stories one always says the wolf will eat. At the sadistic-oral stage, the child wants to eat his mother and thinks she will eat him; this wolf one threatens him with will eat him; so his mother will eat him — she *becomes* the wolf. Probably the genesis. Not sure.

There are in this child's history many unknown things. She thinks it is thanks to this he gave this *wolf-image*. When he wanted to be aggressive he did not used to go on all fours and bark; now he does. He knows he is human, but needs from time to time to identify with an animal, as an 18-month-old does. To be aggressive he goes on all fours and says "ouh, ouh," without anxiety. He gets up and continues. He can express aggression only at this stage.

Hyppolite: he thus overcomes — it is between *zwingen* and *bezwingen*. The whole difference between the word where there is *constraint* and the word where there is not. *Zwang* — constraint, the wolf giving him anxiety — and *Bezwingung*, the anxiety overcome, the moment he plays the wolf.

Lefort: I agree.

### **Lacan resumes**

*The wolf* naturally poses all the problems of symbolism, which is not at all limitable — they are forced to seek its origin in a *general symbolization*. Why the wolf? Not a personage that remains very familiar in our regions. That the wolf is chosen for these effects ties them directly with a broader function: on the mythic, folkloric, primitive-religious plane the wolf plays a role.

That it attaches to a whole filiation by which they arrive at *secret societies* — with their initiatic content in the adoption of a totem, or more precisely the organization of this style of community, identification with a personage. They cannot make these plane-distinctions on so elementary a phenomenon. But this *superego* —

Lacan wishes to draw their attention: questions that will pose themselves later involve the *reciprocal function*, the difference between what one must call *superego* (in the determinism of repression) and what one must call *ideal of the ego*.

Lacan does not know if they have noticed: there are two conceptions which, as soon as one makes them intervene in any dialectic explaining a patient's behavior, appear directed in *exactly contrary senses*: *the superego is simply constraining; the ideal of the ego is exalting*. These distinctions tend to be effaced; one passes from one to the other as if synonymous.

This question deserves to be posed apropos of the *transferential relation* to the analyst, depending on the angle from which one broaches *the foundation of therapeutic action*. One will say the subject identifies the analyst with his ideal of the ego, or on the contrary with his superego — substituting one for the other in the same text without explaining the difference.

Lacan will be led to examine — but not now, since the notion of superego had not been elaborated in the texts they are studying — what the superego is in the different registers. Anticipating: it is impossible to situate the superego except in a mythical way, as a key-word, a force-word, a mythical elaboration handled for the use one can make of it, without seeking further — *a new idol*. If we do not limit ourselves to such blind use, if we want to seek to understand what the superego is — its essential elements — surely the superego, in distinction from the ideal of the ego, *situates itself essentially and radically on the symbolic plane of speech*.

The superego is an *imperative*: "super-ego," as common sense and usage indicate, is coherent with the register and notion of *law* — that is, of the system of language as defining the situation of man as such, not only of the biological individual.

What we can also accentuate is the often-emphasized character of *blind imperative, pure tyranny* in the superego. This indicates where we can synthesize these notions. Very obviously: the superego has on one hand a certain relation with *law*, and on the other an exactly contrary relation: it is an *insane law*, a law reduced to something going so far as to be its *misrecognition*.

This is how we see the superego act in the neurotic — for which elaborating the notion in analysis was necessary, since the neurotic's morality is *insane, destructive, purely oppressive*, intervening in a function literally — relative to the register of law — almost *antilegal*.

*The superego is at once the law and its destruction, its negation*. The superego is essentially *speech itself*, the *commandment of law*, insofar as nothing remains of it but the *root*. The law is reduced entirely to something one cannot even express, like the *you must* — simply a *speech deprived of all its senses*.

In this sense the superego ends by identifying with what is most ravaging, most fascinating, in the primitive premature experiences of the subject — ends by identifying with what Lacan calls *the ferocious figure*, with the figures one can more or less directly link to the primitive traumatisms (whatever they be) the child has undergone.

What we see in this case is, in a privileged and incarnated way, *this function of language under its most reduced form*: in the end, under the form of a *single word* whose sense and import we ourselves cannot even define for the child, is reduced something that nonetheless connects him to *the human community*.

As Lefort pertinently noted, this is not simply a *wolf-child* who would have lived in pure savagery: he is nonetheless *a speaking child*. But it is by this wolf-word that she had from the beginning the possibility of *instituting the dialogue*. What is admirable: the moment when the use of the word *the wolf* disappears, after the scene Lefort described; how around this *pivot of language*, of this relation to the word *the wolf* (which is for him in some sense the summary of a *law*), turns the passage from first to second phase. Then an extraordinary elaboration whose termination will be that overwhelming *self-baptism* ending with the pronunciation of his own first name. They cannot fail to touch here something extraordinarily moving: *the fundamental relation, under its most reduced form, of man to language*.

What other questions? Lefort: only diagnosis. Lacan invites Dr Lang, who had spoken last night. Lang: *hallucinatory delirium* — by analogy with the magma of schizophrenia where one always tries to find an analogy between profound behavioral troubles in children and what we know in adults. Often one speaks of "infantile schizophrenia" when one does not really understand. But an essential element for schizophrenia is missing: *dissociation*. There is no dissociation because there is hardly any construction. It rather recalled certain forms of organization of hallucinatory delirium. Lang made great reserves: there is a step to traverse from direct observation of a child of such age to the habitual nosography.

Lacan: as he understood when it was reported. A *hallucinatory delirium* (in the sense of chronic hallucinatory psychosis) has only one thing in common with this subject — the dimension Lefort finely noted: *this child lives only the real*. To the extent that "hallucination" means something — namely that sentiment of reality insofar as it exceeds and detaches itself from all interpretation — something is *truly assumed as real* by the patient. We can say something similar. You know how problematic this remains even in hallucinatory psychosis. In adult Chronic Hallucinatory Psychosis there is a *synthesis of imaginary and real* — that is the whole problem of psychosis. There is a *secondary imaginary elaboration* — precisely what Lefort brought into relief here, as literally the *non-inexistence at the nascent state*.

Lacan had not reviewed this observation in a long time. Last time he had made them the great schema of the vase, the flowers, where the flowers are imaginary, virtual, illusory and the vase real, or inversely (one can dispose the apparatus contrarily): vase real and flowers imaginary (real image); flowers real and vase imaginary (real image).

He cannot fail to make them note the pertinence of this kind of *model of the relation between contained flowers and containing vase*. The container-content system, which he put in the first plane of the signification he gives to the *mirror stage*, plays here in full, bare. They truly see:

— the child behaving with a more-or-less mythic function — what he stuffs in and out, and his way of behaving toward this container, and as Lefort noted, *finally being able to bear it empty*, that is, properly speaking *as container*; — the *identification of this vase as such* with an entity for the first time a *properly human object* — an *instrumental object*, the instrument detached from its function, whereas until then he could bear it only as *function-nothingness*.

This is also extraordinarily expressive of the elements of construction not only of the human world but of the world insofar as in the human world there is not only *the useful* but the *tool* — instruments, things that are independent, that *exist as such*.

Hyppolite: *universal*.

Dr Lang notes the passage from the *vertical* position of the wolf to the *horizontal* — amusing, and significant. The wolf at the start is *lived*.

---

## **Session of 17 March 1954**

Lacan opens (for those who were not there last time) by situating the problem and utility of the intervention of Freud's article *Zur Einführung des Narzißmus*. They have arrived at a point in their exposé of technique, of their examination of the foundations of technique. Today, owing to the defection of Leclaire (Liebschutz in the stenotype), Lacan will be led — and perhaps this is no bad thing — to short-circuit certain notions he is usually led to repeat, almost to drum into them, in order to make them enter their categories, better still their habits of thought.

How to resume the point we have reached? Lacan has noticed this week, not without satisfaction, that some are beginning to be seriously concerned by the systematic use he has been suggesting of a fundamental reference to the categories of *the symbolic* and *the real*. They know it is by insisting on this notion of the symbolic, and by telling them it is fitting always and strictly to start from there to understand what we do in the whole positive part of our intervention in analysis from this category of the symbolic, that one must always ask what such-and-such an element of our intervention means and where it situates itself — especially the positive interventions, namely *interpretation*.

They have thus arrived at insisting much on that face of resistance situated at the very level of the *emission of speech* insofar as it can express the *being* of the subject — insofar as it succeeds in doing so. (And can one say it succeeds? Up to a certain point it will never succeed.) They have arrived at the moment of asking: what are, in relation to this fundamental element of communication of speech, what are all those affects, all those references — let us now call them by their name, *imaginary* — that are properly, commonly evoked when one wants to define the *inter-psychological action of transference* in analytic experience? You have sensed well that this is not self-evident.

It is in the *gift of speech* that, when speech is given, the two subjects — the locutor and the allocutary — for what concerns *full speech*, speech insofar as it aims at, forms that truth establishing itself in the *recognition of one by the other*, one of the subjects finds himself afterward *other than he was before*. The full signification has made a true act of speech: as essential speech this cannot be elided from analytic experience. We cannot think the whole analytic experience as a kind of game, lure, illusionary manipulation, suggestion as is said.

If analysis is effectively an experience and realizes an authentic progress, it is at this level that the *last term* must hold. In relation to this last term, Lacan would say that the whole of analysis holds *short of it*. And even the whole question of the \[imaginary\] extreme point is nonetheless something animating the whole movement of analysis.

From this point posed, you may have noticed, many things orient themselves and become clear, and many *paradoxes and contradictions* appear. The importance of this conception is precisely to make these paradoxes and contradictions appear — which does not mean opacities and obscurings. It is often, on the contrary, what appears too harmonious, too comprehensible, that conceals some opacity; and inversely it is in *antinomy*, in *gaping*, in *difficulty*, that we find chances of transparency. On this viewpoint rests our whole method, and hopefully also our progress here.

The first contradiction that appears, of course, is that it is assuredly singular that the analytic method — if we think it aims to attain full speech — proceeds in a way that appears, at maximum, a *detour*: it seems to start by the strictly opposite path.

Namely, that as it gives the subject as instruction the object of a speech as *unhitched* as possible from any supposition of *responsibility*, as it liberates the subject from any *exigence of authenticity* — telling him he must say everything passing through his head — it is certain that, by this very fact, the least one can say is that it facilitates by every means his return to that path in speech which is (let us say) *below the level of recognition* — that is, what concerns specifically *the third*, *the object*.

You have understood that we have situated at these levels speech *in its function of recognition*. What does that mean?

It means that we have discerned two planes in which is exercised this *exchange of human speech*: the plane of the *recognition of speech* insofar as it binds between subjects this *pact* by which the subjects themselves are transformed, established as human and communicating subjects; and the *order of the communicated*, situating itself at all sorts of levels, from the level of the *call*, of properly speaking *discussion*, of *knowledge*, even *information*, and which in the last term tends to realize something which is *agreement on the object*.

You sense the term *agreement* is still there, but the important thing is to know in what measure the accent is placed on an object — that is, something considered as exterior to the action of speech, which speech in sum *signifies* and even, in the last term, *expresses*.

Of course this term *object* — envisaged only in its reference to speech — is something already partially given by all sorts of hypotheses not all conforming to reality. In this *object-system* (objectal, objective) are integrated the whole sum of *prejudices* constituting a *cultural community*, up to and including psychological prejudices, from those most elaborated by scientific work to the most naïve and spontaneous, which certainly do not fail to communicate largely with the properly scientific references and even impregnate them.

Here is the subject invited, by the path on which the instruction engages him, precisely to deliver himself in total abandon to the *system* — that is, to what he holds and possesses, his scientific system on the plane of what he can imagine as such, on the plane of the knowledge he has taken of his state, his problem, his situation, and to the most naïve prejudices on which rest his illusions — including his *neurotic illusions*, insofar as this constitutes an important part of the neurosis.

It would seem that either this path is *badly chosen*, or, if for some reason no other can be chosen, one sees badly how *progress* would establish itself in this act of speech, except by way of an *intellectual conviction* arising from the properly educational, superior, teaching intervention coming from the analyst. In the end, that is what is aimed at when one speaks of an initial state, a first phase that would have been "the intellectualist phase" of analysis. As one imagines it.

You think naturally it never existed; but it could have existed in the *insufficiency* of conceptions analysis then made of itself. This does not mean that, really, at the start of analysis, intellectualist analyses were made — for the *forces* authentically at play were there from the origin. Had they not been there, analysis would not have had the occasion to prove itself, to introduce itself as evident method of psychotherapeutic intervention.

But to explain it thus is very important. What is called *intellectualization* on this occasion is something quite other than simply the connotation that it would be something *intellectual*. This "something intellectual" is present in our whole subsequent conception of analysis; it is, after all, only a matter of *theory*, of comprehension of what happens in analysis. And the better we comprehend and analyze the various subjects of what is at stake, the better we will distinguish what must be distinguished and *unite* what must be united — the more efficient our technique. That is what we are trying to do.

It must thus be about this *interval* between the ideal point of aim of this essential action of speech and the paths we pass by. There must be something explaining, in another way than by *endoctrination*, the *efficacy of analytic interventions*. We all know — something we do not \[doubt\] today — it is precisely what experience has demonstrated to be particularly efficacious in *the action of transference*.

Only, this is where opacity begins. What, finally, is this *transference*? You see the question is of different nature. We have understood that in its essence the efficacious transference at stake is simply *the act of speech*. Each time a man speaks to another in an authentic and full way, it is a *transference* in the sense that *something happens that literally changes the nature of the two beings in presence*.

It is a question here of *another* transference — of the transference of that function which presented itself as not only problematic but as *obstacle* in analysis — namely something happening on the *imaginary plane*, for which has been forged what you know: *repetition of ancient situations*, *unconscious repetition*, *putting into action* an action that can be considered a *historical reintegration, a reintegration of history* — but in the *contrary sense*: on the imaginary plane, the past situation being lived in the present, unbeknownst to the subject, insofar as the historical dimension is *negated* — that is, properly speaking *misrecognized* (Lacan did not say *unconscious*, note) — by the subject.

These are explanations, what is brought to define the situation, to define what we observe — something with all the worth of an assured empirical observation, which yet does not unveil its *reason*, its *function*, its *signification in the real*. Why is it thus?

You may tell me this is being demanding, manifesting a kind of appetite for theoretical satisfaction where brutal minds would perhaps wish to impose a barrier. But besides the fact that the analytic tradition here does not distinguish itself by a special absence of ambition (there must be reasons for that), justified or not, carried along or not by Freud's example, one can hardly say there are psychoanalysts who have not at some moment fallen back upon *mental evolution*.

This kind of *metapsychological enterprise* is truly impossible to do without, for reasons that will unveil themselves a bit later. To practice psychoanalysis even for a second without thinking in *metapsychological terms* is exactly like M. Jourdain, who was forced to speak prose, whether he wanted to or not, from the moment he expressed himself. Even were this not a structural fact of our activity, the question is constantly reopened — in the most practical way — of what we must consider transference to be.

Lacan alluded last time to Freud's article on transference-love. Given the strict economy of Freud's work — how he never addressed a subject not absolutely urgent — we cannot fail to see that the most important points include the *relation* between transference-bonds and the characteristics, positive and negative — *positive* especially: the *amorous relation*, a singular thing nonetheless. This poses a question regarding this amorous relation: insofar as Lacan has not concealed in Freud that this carries the whole question of the amorous relation.

Clinical experience, and the theoretical history of discussions on what is the *spring of therapeutic efficacy* — a subject on the order of the day since about 1920 (Berlin Congress, Salzburg Congress, Marienbad Congress) — has done nothing else: ask the *utility of the function of transference* in our handling of the patient's subjectivity.

We have realized too well that something even goes by the name not only of a *transference-neurosis* (nosological label for what affects the patient), but a *secondary neurosis*, so to speak artificial — the *actualization of this transference-neurosis in the transference itself*: the transference-neurosis in its second sense, namely the neurosis insofar as it has entangled in its threads the *imaginary person of the analyst*.

We know all this. Yet the question of the *spring* — not the means by which we act, but what is essentially the source of therapeutic efficacy — has remained obscure enough that the greatest diversity of opinion spreads over this in the analytic literature. To go back to old discussions: see the last chapter of Fenichel's little book. Lacan does not often recommend reading Fenichel, but here, for the historical data, he is an instructive witness. You will see the diversity of opinions: Sachs, Rado, Alexander, when this was broached at the Salzburg Congress.

Striking: a kind of *announcement* by Rado of the direction he intended to push the theorization — followed by non-execution. He never elaborated what he had promised. Some mysterious resistance seems to act so that this remains in relative shadow, which seems not solely due to the obscurity of the subject (sometimes brilliant lights appear in directions shown). One truly has the feeling that something effective in the actions present is closely approached, but as if before the elaboration into concepts of what is so well glimpsed, something seems to forbid the subject from going further — about which we could make various hypotheses.

It is certain that there is sometimes a kind of *suspension of the mind* above certain problems, and there, perhaps more than elsewhere, the dangers of a certain *precipitation*, a certain *prematuration* — even of the achievement of theory or only of its progress — could be sensed as a *danger*. Not excluded. Doubtless the most favorable hypothesis.

What is at stake — what we have seen manifested in the sequel of theories on the nature of the *imaginary place* established in transference — has the closest relation with the notion of *object-relation*. This notion has now come to the foreground of the elaboration of the analytic notion as fertile.

You know too how hesitant the theory is on this point, how — to take things at the present point — it is extremely difficult to know. Take for example the reading of an article on the *spring of therapeutic efficacy*: that of James Strachey in the *International Journal of Psychoanalysis*. A fundamental article, one of the best elaborated. It puts the whole accent on the role of the *superego*.

You will see to what difficulty this conception leads, and how, around this fundamental reference, to sustain it, to render it subsistent and viable, the number of *supplementary hypotheses* Strachey is led to introduce — for example, the distinction between the function of *superego* the analyst would occupy in relation to the subject, and the precision that it is a *parasitic superego*. Indeed, this cannot hold if we admit the analyst should be purely and simply the support — which he already is in the subject — of the *function of the superego* as one of the most decisive springs of the neurosis. There would be no way out of this circle without introducing this supplementary notion; and to introduce it one is forced to go too far.

One sees it in Strachey's article: for the superego to be a *parasitic superego*, there must have occurred between analyzed and analyst a series of *exchanges of introjections and projections* carrying us especially to the level of the mechanisms of constitution of *good and bad objects*, broached in the practice of the English school by Melanie Klein — which is not itself without presenting the danger of constantly making them be reborn.

It is on a quite other plane that the whole question of relations between analyzed and analyst is: not on the plane of the superego, but on the plane of the *ego and non-ego* — that is, essentially on the plane of the *narcissistic economy of the subject*. The question of what transference-love means has always been too closely linked with the whole *analytic elaboration of the notion of love itself*.

Love not in the sense of *Eros*, the universal presence, the power of binding between subjects underlying all reality in which analysis moves; but *love-passion*, as effectively and concretely lived by the subject as a kind of *catastrophe* in the psychological domain — posing quite other questions, very precisely (Lacan anticipates, you will have to confirm): how this love-passion is, in its foundation, equally *linked to the analytic relation*.

After having said some good about Fenichel's book, Lacan must say a little bad of it. It is as amusing as striking to note the kind of *revolt, even insurrection*, that seem to be provoked in Fenichel by the remarks of two authors extraordinarily pertinent in their analysis of the relations of love and transference, where they put precisely the maximum accent on the *narcissistic character of the imaginary love-relation*: how and how much the beloved object is *confused, by a whole face of its qualities, attributes, and action* in the psychic economy, with the *ideal of the ego of the subject*.

There one sees curiously conjoined this general *syncretism* of Fenichel's thought and the kind of *middle path* that is his — with a kind of *repugnance* before the paradox, the veritable *phobia* before this type of *imaginary love* which, in its ground, participates essentially of the *illusion*. One sees him stop almost with a kind of *horror*, indeed often *devalorizing* the very function of love, when it is precisely this that is at issue: what is this love, insofar as it arises as imaginary spring in analysis? Something marking a profound correlation in the subjective structure.

This is what is at stake — what we can mark out as notion, category, line of force, structure, between: — the *narcissistic relation* insofar as we use it in analytic theory; — the *function of love* in all its generality; — the *transference* in its practical efficacy.

To say something to allow you to find your way at each crossroads between renewed ambiguities: you have noticed, however little analytic literature you have read, that this ambiguity is renewed at every step. There are more than one method \[...\]. Lacan thinks to teach them thus the introduction of a new category permitting *essential distinctions* — not exterior, scholastic distinctions, multiplying bi-partitions to infinity. This mode of progress is always allowed (always introducing supplementary hypotheses), but on the contrary, *progress in comprehension* is bringing into value what *simple already-existing notions* imply.

There is no interest in indefinitely decomposing, as has been done in an excessively remarkable work on the notion of transference. There is interest, on the contrary, to leave the notion of *transference* in its whole empirical totality, but to understand it has several faces, is plurivalent, is exercised at once in several registers — and in this that *the symbolic, the imaginary, and the real* introduce. These are not three fields. Lacan has given several concrete examples: even in the animal kingdom you have been able to see that it is apropos of the same actions, the same behaviors, that one can distinguish, precisely because it is a question of something else, what can incarnate itself in what one can distinguish as functions of imaginary, symbolic, and real — for the reason they do not situate themselves in the same *order of relations*.

There are several ways of introducing this notion. It has its limits, like any dogmatic exposé; its utility is to be *critical*, to arise at the point where the concrete empirical effort of researchers meets a *difficulty of handling* of the already-existing theory. This is what makes it interesting to proceed by way of *commentary of texts*.

Lacan invites Leclaire to give what he has prepared, even incompletely. Leclaire: he has read only the first ten pages. He situates the article in its epoch — difficult. In these first pages one passes through several formulations of *narcissism*. Freud first *eliminates* narcissism as *perversion* — defined as love of one's own body, loved as the body of another. Lacan: that is to *exclude* it.

Leclaire: to exclude it. To consider that narcissism exists as a form of libido in many other behaviors. One arrives fairly quickly at the definition: *narcissism would be libido withdrawn from the external world and turned back upon the ego*. The base definition.

Lacan: why do you say "external world"? Leclaire: *Außenwelt*. Lacan: the *Außenwelt* you speak of is at the moment when Freud takes the comparison of *pseudopods*, of *protoplasm*. Leclaire: a little before. Lacan: that introduces it.

Leclaire: not exactly. What pushes Freud, if he has understood this first reading, to formulate a theory of narcissism, is that one has been led to study how the study of *dementia praecox* and *schizophrenia* can be integrated into the theory of the libido. That is what makes the elaboration of a theory of narcissism urgent for him. Freud sets out from these clinical considerations to differentiate the behavior of psychotics from the behavior of neurotics (principally hysterics). He arrives at the formulation: it seems the libido withdrawn from objects in the neurotic is invested or remains free to apply itself to *imaginary objects*. This is what he calls the *paraphrenic*. It is not so, he says: it seems the schizophrenic has withdrawn all his libido from the persons and things of the external world *without having invested it in fantasies, in fantasmatic objects*. From this comes Freud's definition of narcissism as libido withdrawn from objects and things of the external world and turned back on the ego. Whence the distinction (which Freud judges fundamental) of *libido of objects* and *libido of the ego* — though Freud says this is only a hypothesis.

Surely a hypothesis founded on the results of his clinical experience on the study of neurotics. Here Leclaire was stopped to pose his question. Lacan: pose it; that will suffice for today.

Leclaire wants to articulate it on the text. In sum, Freud distinguishes thereafter a *sexual energy* (libido) and an *energy of the ego*. But before going further, Freud poses two questions, the first being: *what becomes of narcissism in auto-eroticism* (which Freud has described as a primitive, primary manifestation of libido)? The second: *can one not limit this distinction*? Leclaire will not broach the second. To the first, Freud responds: it seems necessary to distinguish in the individual a *different unity* — different at least because the *ego* must be the result of a process of development; hence the ego already appears as something *secondary*. The auto-erotic drives, by contrast...

Lacan interrupts: permit me — I will cite what you do not cite. What you say is quite right. The question Leclaire is exposing is this: there is a relation between something *x* that has occurred on the libidinal plane and that *withdrawal of investment from the external world* characteristic of forms of dementia praecox (understood as broadly as you wish).

Here the problem is most acute. To pose it in these terms engenders extreme difficulties in relation to the already-existing analytic theory, already constituted at that moment. This relates to the *Three Essays on Sexuality*, to which refers the notion of *primordial auto-eroticism* — outside of which (by a kind of evasion, prolongation, *pseudopod*) this libido, as constitutive of objects of interest, *distributes itself*, constituted by a certain *emission by the subject of his libidinal cathexes* — the different forms and stages by which, according to his proper instinctual structure and a kind of *elaboration of the world*, the instinctual progress of the subject would constitute itself.

This seems to go alone, at a stage when Freud has given to the *libido* the definition he has given it — leaving outside the libidinal mechanism everything that could be related to a register other than the properly libidinal of *desire as such*, defined and situated by him as a kind of extension of all that manifests itself concretely as *sexual*, as a kind of absolutely essential *dyadic rapport* of the animal being with its *Umwelt*.

Everything is *bipolarized* in this conception. Freud has always sensed that this conception would not go if one neutralized in any way, or excessively generalized, this notion. It is admitted that nothing essential would be brought to the elaboration of neurotic facts if one considered this as more or less identical to what M. Janet, for example, could call the *function of the real*.

Within real or *realizing* relations, within a whole series of functions that have nothing to do with this *function of desire* (everything concerning the ego's relations to the external world, everything concerning instinctual registers other than the sexual — for example, all the domain of *nutrition*, *assimilation*, *hunger* insofar as it serves the *conservation of the individual* as such), it is within this — on the more extended, general ground of these very real relations — that the *libidinal relations* situate themselves, that the libido situates itself.

If the libido is not isolated from the ensemble of the *functions of conservation* of the individual, *it loses all sense*. It is precisely because something happens in *schizophrenia* that completely perturbs the subject's relations to the real, drowning ground with form, that suddenly poses the question whether the theory of the *withdrawal of libido* does not go much further than what was properly defined from this central organizing nucleus of *properly sexual relations*.

The question is so well posed that it has historically already been crossed. Lacan will show this when they analyze the commentary on Schreber. Freud, in commenting on Schreber, is led to realize the difficulties of the problem of *libidinal investment in the psychoses*. He there employs notions ambiguous enough that Jung could say Freud renounced the *properly libidinal and sexual* character of the fundamental function of all his theory of instinctual evolution — namely the single force called *libido* essentially of sexual nature.

Jung crosses this step and introduces the notion of *introversion*, which for him — as Freud says — presents itself (the critique Freud makes) as a notion *ohne Unterscheidung*, without distinction, ending in the vague general notion of *psychic interest* — drowning in one single register what is of the order of the *sexual polarization* of the individual in his objects (in a certain relation to himself, of libidinal order) — which Freud says is entirely centered and ordered around the *realization of the individual in possession of the genital functions*.

Historically the question has thus already been posed, introduced by Jung's notion of *introversion*. You see how psychoanalytic theory is at that moment opened to this kind of *neutralization* of the problem: on one side strongly affirming it is a matter of *libido*, on the other saying it is simply a matter of something that is of the *property of the soul* as creatrix of its own surroundings and world.

This is extremely difficult to distinguish from analytic theory, insofar as at this moment, with this index, the idea of the *auto-eroticism* from which the constitution of all objects would emerge is, at this stage of Freud's thought, something almost equivalent in its structure to Jung's theory.

It is at this moment, when he writes this article (one of its reasons), that Freud *returns to the necessity of strongly distinguishing* what is of *egoistic libido* and what is of *sexual libido*, and maintaining this distinction. The problem is extremely arduous for him to resolve, for, while maintaining the distinction, he turns throughout this article around the notion of their *equivalence*.

How can these be so closely, rigorously distinguished and at the same time how can we keep the notion of a kind of *energetic equivalence* of the two terms — which would make it precisely insofar as the libido is *de-invested from the object* that it returns to be turned upon the ego? This is the whole problem posed and developed, carried to a point of concrete elaboration across all the planes where Freud can find a *criterion of experience* throughout this article.

To pose at the start what Leclaire was just introducing: Freud is led to conceive narcissism as a *secondary process*, to underline that a *unity comparable to the ego does not exist at the origin* —

---

## **Session of 24 March 1954**

\[Continuing the Narcissism commentary\] — to underline that an *ego-comparable unity* does not exist from the origin, *nicht von Anfang*: it is not present from the beginning in the individual. The *Ich must develop*, *entwickelt werden*.

The auto-erotic tendencies and drives are on the contrary there from the beginning, *Urbild*. Freud introduces this — without otherwise resolving it — apropos of the notion of what this *Urbild* of the ego could be. Those familiar with what Lacan has brought concerning the *mirror stage* must understand the analogy with what the notion of a *unity* means. This is articulated in Freud.

This confirms the utility of a conception such as Lacan teaches by way of the mirror stage: it is at a defined, determined moment that this ego-comparable unity is constituted — the *Urbild* from which the ego begins to constitute its functions. From that moment the human ego is constituted by a certain relation — precisely this imaginary relation, this *imaginary function*. Lacan hopes they will see developed in the next two or three sessions some precisions on the precise, limited, distinct usage we must make of it. They will see it is *plural*. Lacan began with the schema given the other day apropos of the real image — the bouquet. He began to give them the indication of what this imaginary function is, at least as *container of the plurality of the lived*.

But this does not limit there. They will see why — precisely because of the need to *distinguish psychoses and neuroses*. There are *two registers* implied in the mirror stage. This Lacan has not yet taught them; in the light of Freud's article they will see why this is necessary, and how usable, simply usable.

Freud defines this *imaginary function of the ego* as having to have *eine neue psychische Gestalt* — something going precisely in the sense of what Lacan is explaining: in the function of *Gestalt*, of formation, of *imaginary formation*. That is what is designated by this *neue psychische Gestalt*: something new appearing at a moment of development, which is to *give form to narcissism*.

You will see throughout this very text all the impasses one arrives at. It is on this plane that the problem poses itself. What is important in the beginning of this text is: the difficulty Freud has in *defending the originality of analytic dynamics* against what one may call (Lacan would not even say *generalization* but) *the Jungian dissolution of the problem*.

Lacan very justly notes: if this general schema of *psychic interest*, going and coming, leaving and entering, coloring — reducing things along a very traditional perspective of thought — shows the difference of orthodox analytic thought that retrieves a *traditional thought* by drowning in a kind of universal illusion the *magma* at the base of all Jungian elaborations of the constitution of the world — it is nothing other than a kind of *alternating illumination* coming and going, projecting and withdrawing from reality at the will of *the pulsation of the psychic in the subject*. A pretty metaphor — but in truth, in practice, illuminates nothing.

As Freud underlines: the differences between the kind of *withdrawal of interest from the world* the anchorite can arrive at, and the structurally quite different result that is the schizophrenic — not at all directed, withdrawn, sublimated, but *perfectly stuck*. It is certain that much was brought clinically by Jungian investigation, interesting in its picturesque, its style — for example, the rapprochements between productions of mental or religious ascesis and those of schizophrenics. There may be something with the advantage of giving color and life to research; but it has elucidated nothing in the order of *mechanisms*. Freud underlines this cruelly in passing.

What Freud aims at is to give an idea of the different distinctions existing between this *withdrawal of reality* observed in special form and structure in the neuroses, and the other observed in psychoses. Surprisingly — at least for those not specially attached to these questions, grappling with these problems — what Freud tells us is that one of the major and fundamental distinctions is this: in this misrecognition, refusal, blocking-of-reality that is the neurotic's, we observe something Freud defines as a *recourse to Phantasie*.

There is here a function: in his vocabulary, this can only be in the order of all that is of the *imaginary register*. We return to this when we recall how the persons, things in the neurotic's surroundings are entirely *charged with value* in relation to a function which nothing prevents \[our qualifying\] as *imaginary* in the most immediate way — without seeking a more elaborate theory of what it means in language. *Imaginary* in the sense the word *image* has: first, relation with *formative identifications* for the subject (the full sense of *image*); second, in relation to the real, the *illusory character* often emphasized of the imaginary function.

But what is quite striking — rightly or wrongly, no matter for the moment — is that Freud underlines that *in psychosis there is nothing similar*. What does that mean, then, this subject who loses the *realization of the real*, the psychotic? Freud says he finds *no imaginary substitution* — that is what distinguishes the psychotic from the neurotic.

This may seem extraordinary at first aspect. You sense one must make a certain step in conceptualization to follow Freud's thought. For what do we call a *psychotic delirious subject*? Normally, one of the most widespread conceptions is: the psychotic dreams, is in the imaginary. So in Freud's conception, the *function of the imaginary* must not simply be the same thing as the *function of the unreal*. It is completely different. Otherwise one would not see why Freud would refuse the psychotic access to the imaginary.

And since Mr Freud generally knows what he says, we should at least seek to elaborate, understand, deepen what on this point he means. You will see it is precisely this that introduces us to a most coherent conception of the most fundamental relations of the *imaginary* and the *symbolic*.

This is one of the things on which Freud carries with greatest energy this difference of structures: in this kind of *need of "reconstruction of the world"* characteristic of the psychotic, what is first invested — and you will see in what unexpected path this engages us — *will be words*. There you cannot fail to recognize the *category of the symbolic*.

So we find ourselves before this result. We can push further what this critique already broaches: that it would be in an *unreal symbolic*, or a *symbolic marked by the unreal*, that the proper structure of the psychotic would situate itself; and that the *function of the imaginary* would be something *entirely elsewhere*.

Then you begin to see the difference in the apprehension of the position of psychoses between Jung and Freud. For Jung, the two domains, *symbolic and imaginary*, are completely confused. One of the first articulations permitting us to bring out Freud's article is a *strict distinction*.

Today is only a broaching. Lacan will ask Leclaire to continue, and to associate Granoff (who has a special propensity to interest himself in *Observations on Transference-Love*). He will entrust someone with another article from the metapsychology of the same epoch: *Metapsychological Supplement to the Theory of Dreams*. He proposes Perrier, which will give him occasion to intervene on the subject of schizophrenics.

### **\[Next session — incorporated here as the natural continuation given Lacan's framing\]**

The next session (24 March) opens with Leclaire continuing. Leclaire has read with Perrier and Lehmann; everyone is embarrassed. There are viewpoints, formulations that seem dated. The text is divided in three parts. The first introduces the fundamental distinction of *sexual energy* and *ego energy*. Freud says they cannot be distinguished from the stage when *object-investment* is produced. In the *narcissistic state* these two energies are indissociable. From this distinction Freud is led to the study of narcissism.

Freud is led — by observation of children's behavior — to the conclusion that *the original libidinal investment of the ego* is what is subsequently brought to bear on objects. He arrives at the fundamental distinction of sexual energy (libido) and energy of the ego-drives. The two questions Leclaire brings: the relations of *primary auto-eroticism* and *narcissism*. The phrase: *there must exist a preexisting unity, prior to the ego, since auto-eroticism is a primitive fact* (*Es ist eine notwendige Annahme, daß eine dem Ich vergleichbare Einheit nicht von Anfang an im Individuum vorhanden ist; das Ich muß entwickelt werden. Die autoerotischen Triebe sind aber uranfänglich; es muß also irgend etwas zum Autoerotismus hinzukommen, eine neue psychische Aktion, um den Narzißmus zu gestalten*).

Lacan: how does Freud justify it? Leclaire: by the popular distinction between hunger and love, by certain biological notions, by the *double simultaneous development of the individual* on biological and individual planes.

Lacan: do not summarize — this is precisely what is interesting. Last time he pointed out: this article, like most or almost all the articles of this period (beginning of the 1914 war), is moving to think Freud pursued this elaboration in that period. *Einführung des Narzißmus* is from 1914, and all that follows — the metapsychology in particular, all we classify under that rubric — develops between 1914 and 1918\. This succeeds immediately the 1912 appearance of Jung's work translated into French as *Metamorphoses and Symbols of the Libido*.

Here Freud alludes to the war, since he uses Jung's term *introversion*, giving the whole range of mental illnesses... Jung approached these illnesses differently from Freud: Jung's experience centered around schizophrenias, while Freud's was centered on neurotics. Jung brought a kind of *grandiose unitary conception of psychic energy*, fundamentally different in inspiration and definition from the notion Freud elaborated as *libido*. The theoretical difference is difficult to make at that moment, so Freud grapples with certain difficulties reflected throughout this article.

An attempt to maintain in delimited (today we would say "operational") use the notion of *libido* and the theoretical notion which, from the relief of the Freudian discovery, was founded on this apprehension that *in the neurotic's symptom* there is exercised *a detoured form of sexual satisfaction*.

Freud said it apropos of neurotic symptoms and demonstrated it concretely by a series of equivalences, the last being a *therapeutic sanction* — of price. Freud has always claimed not to bring a new totalitarian philosophy of the world, but something well-defined, founded on a certain limited field, and entirely new — the exploration of human realities specially psychopathological, *subnormal phenomena* not studied by normal psychology: dreams, troubles, ratés, slips, found in a number of so-called *superior functions* of the subject.

Freud senses well that the problem now posed is that of the *structure of psychoses*: how can the structure of psychoses be elaborated in relation to the general theory of libido?

Jung's solution: the profound transformation of reality manifested in psychoses is due to something Freud glimpsed in neuroses — this *change, metamorphosis of libido* (differentiated term), making the *inner world* (left in the greatest ontological vagueness) — the inner world of the subject in which this libido *introverts itself* — responsible for the fact that reality for this subject *sinks into a kind of crepuscule*, permitting us to conceive with *perfect continuity* the mechanism of psychoses with that of neuroses.

Freud, very attached to a series of extremely reduced, precise mechanisms from experience, extremely careful of his empirical reference, sees the danger of such an apprehension. He sees everything transforming into a kind of vast *psychic pantheism*, a series of imaginary spheres (also imaginary), enveloping one another — useful for a kind of classification of contents, events, *Erlebnisse* of individual life, up to what Jung calls *archetypes*. One clearly senses this is not the path on which a clinical and psychiatric conception of the objects of his research can pursue itself.

That is why Freud tries at this moment to critique the relation between drives — *sexual* drives, to which he had given so much importance because they were hidden, revealed by his analysis — and *ego-drives* he had left in the shadow but which now come into question.

Namely: are they such that one is the *shadow* of the other? Is all reality constituted by that kind of *universal libidinal projection* at the base of Jungian theory? Or is there that *difference, opposition* making all the value of Freudian conceptions of neurosis — a *conflictual relation* between ego-drives and libidinal drives?

Freud, with his habitual honesty, says: the fact that he holds to this distinction is *founded on his experience of neuroses*; after all this is only a limited experience and could well change value with broader facts. That is why he also says no less clearly that at the primitive stage (anterior to what our psychoanalytic investigation can penetrate), one must say *with the ununterscheidbar state* — which does not permit a sufficient analysis or dissection for our analytic experience.

We have a kind of *stage* where it is impossible in our current means to discern these two fundamental tendencies inextricably mingled, *beisammen*, confused in the state of narcissism and *not unterscheidbar*, not distinct, for our gross analysis: it is not possible to distinguish *a sexual libido* from the *Ich-Triebe*.

Freud centers the whole question at the level of psychoses. From the start he tells us why he attempts to maintain this distinction. The first question is founded on the experience of neuroses. The second: the current lack of clarity in the distinction between ego-drives and sexual drives is perhaps imputable only to the fact that what we have begun to elaborate in experience as ego-drives and sexual drives is something that is, in sum, *the last point of reference of our theory* — these famous drives.

It is not what is really at the *base* of our construction; it is what is at the very top. Freud himself underlines that the notion of *Triebe* is *eminently abstract* — what he will later call our *mythology*. With his mind oriented to the concrete, conforming or not to his personal tendencies, putting always in their proper place the speculative elaborations which were his, he knows well their *limited value*. He brings their reference to the highest notions of physics, of which he says: through the history of physics, notions like *matter, force, attraction* elaborated themselves through historical evolution, beginning by an uncertain — even confused — form before arriving at a *purification* permitting precise application.

Freud thus specifies that nothing else is at stake when maintaining the opposition between ego-drives and sexual drives. It is to the elaboration and deepening of these notions that he consecrates himself in the article.

Freud brings the reference to a fundamental notion: biology itself indicates (the evolution of biology to the point reached in his time) that what astonished us in "a theory of instincts" cannot fail to take account of a certain *diffusion, fundamental bipartition*, between the *aims of preservation of the individual* and the *aims of continuity of the species*.

This is nothing other than the *theories of Weismann*. The theory of Weismann is not yet definitively proven: an *immortal substance of sex cells* directly differentiated already in the nucleus of the sex cells of the previous individual of the lineage — constituting an absolutely continuous sexual lineage. While the somatic plasm presents itself as a kind of *individual parasite*, laterally pushed from the point of species-reproduction, with the sole end of conveying this eternal germinal plasm by the succession of individuals.

Freud's reference is immediately supported by the fact that he is not constructing a *biological theory*, and has no pretension to. Whatever the *provisional price* — Lacan develops, beyond Freud's text:

That what supports the sexual instinct, what makes it its concrete psychological spring, what determines its putting-into-function — the *enormous mechanic*, the *trigger* (as Tinbergen says after Lorenz) — is precisely *not a real individual*, not the reality of the living being, the sexual partner. It is something that has the greatest relation with what Lacan just called *type* — that is, *an image*.

What the ethologists' elaboration of the mechanisms of *parade-displacement* demonstrates is the prevalent importance of an *image* — so important that this image constitutes itself, forms itself, appears under the form of a *transitory phenotype*: by particular colorations in a great number of species, by modifications of external aspect, which by their *model* serve as a kind of *signal* — a *constructed signal*, a *Gestalt* (as we say today).

What we see appear is that from the standpoint of biology — a point on which some philosophers have already reflected — if one wants to distinguish different *planes* of the world of man, to recognize that what distinguishes his plane is precisely this: he belongs to a world dominated by *heredity*, where the *preformed* element, the element *of the past*, is what in the scansion of the three temporal times (past, present, future) is the past which determines absolutely everything — with the exception of that completely enigmatic element called *maturation*. Set aside provisionally.

In the *normal transmission of species*, the individual only reproduces the *type already realized by the whole lineage of his ancestors*. From the strictly animal point of view, this individual is after all not only mortal but *something already dead*, something without future properly speaking. From the species' point of view he is only the incarnation, the support of something that is not a horse — such-and-such a horse — but is *the horse*. It is moreover on this foundation that the *concept of the species* is founded. If natural history exists, it is because there are not only horses but *the horse*.

That this manifests itself on the psychological plane by the fact that what is proposed, what determines the putting-into-play of what is properly of the order of the trigger and engagement of the sexual instinct, is essentially *crystallized on a rapport of images* — Lacan arrives at the term they await: a *rapport imaginaire* — is sufficiently indicated. The natural introduction to the problem of the rapport of *Libidotriebe, Sexualtriebe* and *Ichtriebe*, as it will be developed throughout this article from this notion — not new, expressed in many places — gives you the *frame* in which the problem must pose itself for us.

The question is now posed at the plane of the relations of *libidinal drive* with the two domains we use ceaselessly (often wrongly): *imaginary* and *real*. If you do not yet see that the libido *poses itself, is centered around the function of the imaginary* — not as a kind of idealist and moralizing transposition of analytic doctrine (as some have wanted to make us believe, into a kind of progress of a certain ideal imaginary state of *genitality*, which would be the sanction and last spring of the establishment of the real) — you can understand *nothing*.

This is sufficiently indicated in the article, and you will see in the sequel — and it is from here we must start — that from this is truly posed the problem of the *real function* played by the ego in the psychic economy.

Leclaire continues. Freud poses two questions: the first he answers (necessity of an *intermediate psychic action* between primary auto-eroticism and narcissism); the second, he responds: the necessity to respond clearly to it must entail in every psychoanalyst a *notable malaise*. One defends oneself against this sense of absorbing one's attention on sterile difficulties, but one must not renounce the search for a solution. The second question: that of *fundamental psychic energy* — would it not be simpler to have only *one* fundamental psychic energy? At certain moments, reading this text, one has the impression of grasping notions that do not seem so fecund.

Leclaire's impression: this fundamental bi-partition of libido into *Ich-libido* and *Objekt-libido* incontestably has a *pragmatic value* (the only one Freud recognizes in this text). It has pragmatic value in the study of neuroses. But what was put in evidence in the study of neuroses essentially? *Sexual drives*, which were hidden. In a bi-partition one always needs two terms — the difficulty arises in the fact that the other term has been called "libido of the ego," "Ich-libido," or "ego-energy" primitively. This was not imposed.

This "ego-energy" is a kind of differentiation of that fundamental energy Freud evokes at every instant. Each time one speaks of this "ego-energy," one feels malaise from the moment one poses a systematic construction trying to articulate the relations between the two — since this *ego-libido* poses the question of *narcissism*. And we shall see — in the third part — that the problem begins to clarify when Freud brings into play the notion of *ideal of the ego*. To attack the second term of this bi-partition (ego-energy, ego-libido) relates a bit to the sterile effort we can make in attacking the study of *primitive energy*.

### **Mannoni intervenes**

Mannoni asks the floor. He has been embarrassed by a problem: the *investment of objects by the libido* is at bottom a *realistic metaphor*, since libido invests only the *image of objects*; while *investment of the ego* can be an *intrapsychic phenomenon* where *the ontological reality of the ego is invested*. If the libido has become libido-of-objects, it can no longer invest anything but something *symmetric to the image of the ego*.

So we will have *two narcissisms*: depending on whether it is a libido that *intrapsychically invests the ontological ego*, or an *object-libido* that comes to invest something which would be perhaps the *ideal of the ego*, in any case an *image of the ego*. So we have a *well-founded distinction* between *primary narcissism* and *secondary narcissism*.

### **Lacan resumes**

Mannoni, with an elegant jump, has quickly introduced us to where, as you sense — leading you step by step — Lacan wants to lead you. We are not going entirely by adventure, though Lacan is ready to welcome discoveries made along the way. In the end this is what is at stake. He is happy to see Mannoni leap into the subject from time to time.

Returning to his last step: you have understood what it tends to? To rejoin what is indicated by the reference to biology — that fundamental experience the current elaboration of the theory of instincts brings us. Namely: what is the *trigger*, the *objectal element* in the trigger of the libido of the sexual behavior-cycle, is something where one can say *the subject is essentially deceivable* (*leurrable*).

What ichthyologists' experiments show: on one side, the male stickleback must have taken on its colors on belly or back for this whole *display, dance* to begin (as Lacan has more or less exposed in other circumstances and will re-expose). Inversely: we can very well make any *cut-out* (a kind of poorly delineated rough cut), provided it bears certain traits, certain *Merkzeichen* (markers), will have exactly the same effect on the subject.

We must never lose sight of these fundamental elements at the heart of the processes we pursue — representing what originally distinguishes, by entirely special properties, what is the *instinctual cycle in the sexual order*. As Lacan insisted in his first exposé on *real, symbolic, and imaginary* (which was only a rough cut), especially: *sexual behaviors are especially deceivable*; and *displacement phenomena* hold the fundamental characteristic of all that develops as original in *perversions and neuroses*.

### **Granoff intervenes on hypnosis and the optical schema**

Granoff brings a passage from the *Metapsychology* still more explicit: "Thus the hereditary Self shelters innumerable individual resistances \[...\] in the ego and the self, the ego and the superego..." There are many formulations as explicit as what Mannoni said.

### **Lacan develops the optical schema further**

Since we are here, Lacan will introduce — before leaving — a complement to the schema he gave three seminars ago, in his little course on what he called *the topic of the imaginary*. He worked to make them feel what could be drawn from a certain model — which, he indicated, is in the line of Freud's own vows.

For Freud says formally somewhere, in several places (Lacan said especially in the *Traumdeutung* and the *Outline*) that the *agencies* he brings as fundamental psychic agencies must be conceived for the most part as representing what is produced in a *photographic apparatus*: namely those *images* (virtual or real) that flow from the functioning of the apparatus.

The organic apparatus representing precisely the *mechanism* of the apparatus, and what we apprehend being precisely those *images* whose functions are not homogeneous — a real and a virtual image are not the same thing. It is by a schema of this kind that what Freud elaborates as *agencies* must be interpreted — not as something substantial, *epiphenomenal* in relation to the modification of the apparatus itself. Something Freud indicated many times and never realized.

Recall the schema: the concave mirror by which the phenomenon of the *inverted bouquet* is produced. Lacan transformed it for convenience to the inverted vase. Here, a vase, which — by the play of the reflection of rays on a sufficiently large spherical surface — is reproduced here in a *real image* (not virtual) on which the eye can accommodate. Supposing we have arranged some flowers, if the eye accommodates exactly at the level of the flowers, the real image of the vase will surround them, give them this style, this *unity, this unification*, a *reflection of the unity of the body itself*.

This is a model. It serves to understand something. Lacan pointed out: for the image to have a certain consistency, it must truly be an *image*. What is the definition of the *image* in optics? That to each point of the object corresponds a point of the image, that all rays cross at a single point. An optical apparatus is defined this way: a univocal or bi-univocal convergence of rays from one point of an object to another precise point where the image is specifically constituted. For this image to be visible — supposing the concave apparatus is here and our prestidigitator's mounting in front — the image cannot be seen clearly enough to produce the *illusion of reality* (that very particular illusion called *real illusion* — not like the image in a mirror which is *not where you see it*; this image is where you see it) — unless you are placed in a certain *angle*, a certain prolongation defined by a *cone* in relation to the apparatus.

According to the different positions of the eye looking at the apparatus, we could distinguish a number of cases permitting us to understand certain distinctions of the *position of the subject in relation to reality*. A subject is not an eye, Lacan said, but if this model applies it is because we are in the *imaginary*, and the eye has much importance.

Since someone has introduced the question of the *two narcissisms*, you sense this is what is at stake: the relation between the *constitution of reality* and a certain relation (which Mannoni, more or less appropriately, called *ontological*) with the *form of the body*.

Suppose Lacan is between the object and the construction from which the illusory real image will form — adossed to the concave mirror, onto which we can probably project all sorts of things with *organic sense*: he has said it is very probably the *cortex*. But let us not substantialize too quickly: this is not a pure elaboration of the "*little man inside the man*" theory. If it were merely to remake that little man, why critique it all the time?
