But it has its raison d'être, too — it forms part of the mirage to which the academic psychologist constantly yields. And if he yields, there is some reason for it.

Place yourselves an instant in the position of the eye, this hypothetical eye, here between the concave mirror and the object. For this eye, here, to have exactly the illusion of the inverted bouquet — the inverted vase — to see it in optimum conditions (as optimal as the one at the back of the room), only one thing is needed and suffices: there must be, here, toward the middle of the room, a *mirror*.

In other words: this experience — quite concrete and successful — one sees a bouquet where it is not, a vase where it is not. For Lacan, against the concave mirror, to see it as well as someone at the back of the room — though he does not see the real image directly in his position — but thanks to this *mirror* at the middle of the room, he finds himself placed *in the same position as the one at the back of the room*.

For the rays appear to him as if he were in that position. What will he see in the mirror? First, his own figure, where it is not. Second, at a symmetric point of where the real image is here, he will see, *appearing as virtual, this real image*.

This little schema is a very simple elaboration. The same thing he has been trying to explain for years in the *mirror stage*. It will permit them to see many things. For Mannoni was speaking of the *two narcissisms*: the narcissism relating to the *bodily image* insofar as it is inscribed, for the best reasons, identical in the ensemble of the subject's mechanisms — it gives form to his *Umwelt* insofar as he is *a man and not a horse*. This image makes the *unity* of the subject — projected in a thousand ways, including in the source and origin of what may be called the *imaginary source of symbolism*, by which symbolism relates to the *Selbstgefühl* the human being, *the Mensch*, has of his own body.

There is a certain narcissism situated, if you like, *at the level of the real image* of the schema, insofar as it permits to organize the *ensemble of reality* in a number of preformed frames. Quite different in an animal which is by its own formations *formed, adapted* to a uniform *Umwelt*. There will be a number of *preestablished correspondents* between these imaginary structural elements and the only things interesting it in this *Umwelt*, the only structures interesting for the perpetuation of these individuals — themselves functions of the typical perpetuation of the species.

But *man*, precisely, for whom — almost uniquely — exists: not only this reflection in the mirror (a phenomenon so important Lacan has long given it fundamental light as manifesting a *noetic possibility* properly speaking original for man), but which goes much further: it is nothing other (you sense) than this *pattern* which is immediately the *relation to the other*.

For it is evidently always by the *intermediary of the other*, insofar as the other has the special value Lacan has developed in the theory of the mirror stage — the other has this *captivating value* by the *anticipation* the unitary image represents (as perceived in the mirror, or in the reality of the fellow for man) — that it is by way of this other, this *alter-ego*, more or less confused depending on life-stages with this *Ich-Ideal* (the *ideal of the ego* invoked throughout this text), it is insofar as *man reflects himself, identifies himself* (the word *identification* taken indifferentiated, *en bloc*, is unusable) — it is this *narcissistic identification*, that of the second narcissism Mannoni spoke of — *this identification with the other* which in the normal case is what allows man to *situate exactly* what in his being has the fundamental *imaginary libidinal rapport with the world in general*. It allows him to "see" in his place, to structure as a function of this place his world — *what is properly speaking his being* (he said *ontological*; Lacan accepts) — *his libidinal being*: he "sees" it in this reflection, in relation to the other, in relation to this *Ich-Ideal*.

You see distinguished the functions of the ego: on one hand, they play (as in all other living beings) a fundamental role in the *structuration of reality*; on the other, in man they must pass by the intermediary of this *fundamental alienation in the reflected image of oneself*, which is equally the *Ur-Ich*, the original form of the *Ich-Ideal*, and of the *rapport with the other*.

Is this sufficiently clear? Lacan gave them one element; he gives another today: the combination with the *reflexive rapport with the other*. They will see what it serves. It will permit them to pose all the practical clinical questions about the use of the imaginary function and especially of these *libidinal investments* one finally no longer understands when handling.

Granoff brings the passage from Freud: it gives an idea of how we rise to the representation of our body in general — *the state of love, which rests on the coexistence... the attracting object on a part of the narcissistic libido of the ego, this state is limited to the ego and to the object. Hypnosis resembles the amorous state... but rests principally on inhibited sexual tendencies and puts the object in the place of the ideal of the ego.* The whole optical schema is found in these two phrases.

Lacan: the notion of the *strict equivalence of object and ideal of the ego in the amorous rapport* is one of the most fundamental, found everywhere in Freud. You meet it at every step: if the beloved object is, in the final analysis, in amorous investment — in the capture of the subject by the love-object — *strictly equivalent to this ideal of the ego*...

This is why there is: the *economic function* so important in suggestion and hypnosis; this state of dependence, *true perversion of reality*, fascination, *overestimation of the loved object*; the whole psychology of the loving life so finely developed by Freud, of which we touch here an important morsel — so vast that we hardly scratch it today.

Leclaire: it relates exactly to what you say — Lacan: let me finish my period. You cannot fail to see what *contradiction* there is between this and what, in certain mythic conceptions of *libidinal ascesis in psychoanalysis*, is ceaselessly given as having to be the *achievement of affective maturation* — namely that at the last degree of the genital we would be in some kind of fusion, communion, realization of the correlation between *genitality* and the *constitution of the real*.

Lacan does not say there is not here something essential to the constitution of reality, but one must still understand *how*. For it is one or the other: either *love is what Freud describes* — *imaginary in its foundation* — or it is something else: *the foundation and base of the world*. Just as there are *two narcissisms*, there must be *two loves*: *Eros* on one side, and *Agape*.

---

## **Session of 31 March 1954**

Mannoni opens by recalling that in Valéry's *Fragments of Narcissus* one already finds primary and secondary narcissism, and a small theory exposed in the verses where Narcissus addresses the fountains: without them his beauty, his sorrow would be uncertain; he would seek in vain what is most dear to him; his sad gaze, ignorant of his charms, would address its tears to someone other than himself. He thus turns to secondary narcissism.

Lacan: Leclaire, who has worked this difficult text, will continue today his reflections on *Zur Einführung des Narzißmus*.

Leclaire summarizes the second part of Freud's article. Freud says it is certainly the study of dementia praecox — what he calls the *paraphrenic group* — that has made urgent the elaboration of a theory of narcissism. Two characteristic traits of paraphrenia: *megalomania*, and the *withdrawal of interest from the external world (persons and things)*. As a consequence, the paraphrenic is *inaccessible to psychoanalytic influence*, cannot be cured by our efforts.

But the withdrawal of interest from the external world also exists in *neurotics*. The hysteric subject also gives up his relation to reality — but, the analysis teaches us, *has not for all that broken his erotic relations with persons and things*. He still maintains them in his *fantasies*: he has substituted for real objects imaginary objects of his memory, or mixed the two; and on the other hand has ceased to direct motor activities to attain his goals on these objects. To this state of libido alone is one allowed to apply *introversion of libido* (Jung uses it without distinction).

It is otherwise with the paraphrenic. He seems really to have withdrawn his libido from persons and things of the external world without replacing them in fantasy. When this occurs (the reconstruction of an imaginary world), it appears as *secondary*, an effort at *cure* aiming to return libido to objects. Whence the question: what becomes, in paraphrenia, of the libido withdrawn from objects? *Freud's response*: this libido is invested on the ego, giving rise to the state of *megalomania*. Megalomania is itself nothing new but, as Freud admits, the *amplification, the clearer-becoming of a state already existing*. We are thus led to conceive of the *narcissism arising from the influx of object-libido withdrawn from objects* as *secondary narcissism, superposed on a primary narcissism* obscured by manifold influences.

Leclaire emphasizes Freud's repeated insistence that *the distinction between sexual energy and ego-energy is a hypothesis*. He maintains it not from theoretical conviction but because clinical experience (especially of the neuroses, the *transference neuroses*) demands the *opposition of sexual drives to ego-drives* — for what we see in the symptom is a *form of detour of sexual satisfaction*.

Freud passes to the question of the *relation of narcissism to organic illness, to hypochondria, to the love-life*. Striking comparisons: the *Wilhelm Busch poem* on the toothache-suffering poet — "only in the narrow cavity of the molar dwells the soul." The patient *withdraws his libidinal cathexes onto his ego* and resends them after the cure. The famous *egoism of the sick* covers this. We find it natural because we are certain we would behave the same.

In hypochondria, *libido withdrawn from external objects is concentrated on the organ that has become the seat of attention*. The hypochondriac elaborates fantasies on his own body that have the same relation to the *real organic ego* as the paraphrenic's grandiose ideas. The libido here invested on the body acts on it according to processes Freud does not yet detail.

Leclaire stresses Freud's reflection on the *amorous life*: choice of object. Freud distinguishes two fundamental *types of object-choice*. The *anaclitic type* (Anlehnungstypus): one loves the woman who nourishes, the man who protects, by analogy with the original infantile dependence on the persons who provided care. The *narcissistic type*: one loves what one *is, was, would like to be*; or *one's own self*.

Freud notes that *the woman* especially turns more toward narcissistic love: she develops a certain self-sufficiency that compensates for the social restraints on her choice of object; strictly, she loves only herself, with an intensity comparable to the man's love for her. Her need is not to love but *to be loved*. The man's choice is correspondingly often more anaclitic. (Freud carefully nuances: he does not generalize.)

The *parents' love* for their child: it is *the resurrection of their own narcissism long abandoned*. *His Majesty the Baby* (Freud's English phrase) shall fulfill the dreams of the parents' wishes that they themselves had to renounce. Touching truth: the *immortality of the ego* in the child.

Then Freud introduces the *ideal of the ego (Ich-Ideal)*. *Repression*, Freud says, proceeds from the ego — more precisely, from *self-respect* of the ego. The same impressions, experiences, impulses to which one subject readily yields will be rejected with greatest indignation by another. The difference is the *ideal* the subject has set up in himself, the standard by which he measures his real ego. Freud thinks the *formation of an ideal* would be on the part of the ego *the condition of repression*.

To this ideal goes the *self-love* enjoyed in childhood by the real ego. *Narcissism* finds itself displaced onto this new ideal ego, which (like the infantile ego) finds itself in possession of all *perfections of value*. As always when libido is at issue, man has here shown himself incapable of renouncing the satisfaction he once tasted. *He does not want to deprive himself of the narcissistic perfection of his childhood*. When his subsequent growth, his own critical judgment no longer permit him to preserve this perfection, he seeks to *recover it in the new form of the ideal of the ego*. *What he projects before him as his ideal is the substitute for his lost childhood narcissism in which he was his own ideal*.

This formation of the ideal of the ego is to be distinguished from *sublimation*: the latter concerns the *destiny of the libido of objects* (transformation of the drive's goal), while idealization concerns the *object itself*, raising it in the subject's psyche without modifying its nature. *Idealization is possible on the side of the libido of the ego as well as the libido of objects*. The constitution of the ideal *increases the exigencies of the ego* and is the *most powerful factor favorable to repression*.

Freud now introduces a critical instance: an *agency of observation, of self-criticism, of moral conscience*. *Delirium of being observed* in the paranoid: there is an instance permanently observing me, criticizing all my tendencies. This is in essence the *projected functioning of the moral conscience, of self-criticism*. The fact that this is the dramatic manifestation in psychosis confirms its existence in us all. It is by reference to this *agency of self-observation* — an agency *which is the censor of dreams* (Freud says), incarnated in the *Ich-Ideal* in dynamic manifestations of moral conscience — that the *feeling of self-esteem* (Selbstgefühl) is organized.

The feeling of self-esteem appears to us first as expression of the *grandeur of the ego*. With our distinction of sexual drives and ego-drives, we must recognize it depends intimately on *narcissistic libido*. We rely on two fundamental facts: self-esteem is *raised in paraphrenia* (megalomania), *lowered in transference neuroses*; in love-life, *not being loved lowers self-esteem; being loved raises it*. Being loved represents the goal and satisfaction in narcissistic object-choice.

It is easy to observe that *libidinal investment on objects does not raise self-esteem*. Dependence on the loved object has the effect of lowering it: the lover is *humble and submissive*. He who loves has, so to speak, *paid the fine of a part of his narcissism*; he can only obtain the replacement by *being loved*. Self-esteem remains in relation with the *narcissistic element of the loving life*.

Freud notes some additional propositions in a less ordered way: the *development of the ego consists in distancing itself from primary narcissism* and engenders a vigorous effort to recover it. This distancing is by way of *displacement of libido onto an ideal of the ego imposed from outside*; satisfaction comes by the accomplishment of this ideal. The ego is impoverished by these investments and the ideal, and re-enriched by *objectal satisfactions and accomplishment of the ideal*.

A part of self-esteem is *primary* — the residue of infantile narcissism. Another part comes from what experience confirms of our *omnipotence* (accomplishment of the ideal of the ego). A third part comes from *satisfaction of object-libido*.

The *passion of love* consists in *an overflowing of ego-libido onto the object*. It has the force to suppress repressions and re-establish perversions. It *raises the sexual object to the rank of sexual ideal*. Idealized is *what accomplishes this determining condition of love*.

The sexual ideal can enter into an *interesting relation of assistance* with the ideal of the ego. When narcissistic satisfaction meets real obstacles, the sexual ideal can serve as *substitutive satisfaction*: one loves, according to the *narcissistic type of choice*, what one *has been and has lost*, or what *possesses the perfections one has not at all*. Parallel formula: *what possesses the eminent quality lacking to the ego for attaining the ideal is loved*.

For the neurotic, this is particularly important: he is impoverished in his ego by his excessive object-investments and unable to accomplish his ideal of the ego. He seeks a return to narcissism by *choosing a sexual ideal possessing the perfections he cannot attain*. He directs this expectation onto the *person of the physician*. When freed of repressions by treatment, the patient often *withdraws from the cure to make a love-choice*, leaving to his common life with the loved person the task of completing his recovery. (Freud notes the *dangers of overwhelming dependence* on this savior.)

From the ideal of the ego an important path leads to *collective psychology*. Besides its individual side, this ideal has a *social side* — the common ideal of a family, class, nation. Besides narcissistic libido it has bound a great quantum of *homosexual libido*, returned thus to the ego. Dissatisfaction from non-accomplishment liberates homosexual libido transformed into *guilty conscience (social anxiety)*. Originally, *guilty conscience was anxiety of being chastised by the parents — more precisely of losing their love*; the parents were later replaced by the indeterminate crowd of fellows. One understands why *paranoia is often caused by an attack on the ego, by a frustration of satisfaction in the domain of the ideal of the ego*.

### **Lacan resumes — the optical schema developed**

Lacan asks Leclaire which short summary of the development of the ego he found most striking: the passage through a *distancing* via the *ideal* and a return to the primitive position. Leclaire: it seems the very image of *development* — Mannoni: of *structuration*. Lacan: yes, structuration.

Leclaire raises an ambiguity: this *displacement of libido onto an ideal* — is it *on an image of the ego* (a form of the ego called "ideal" because not similar to the actual one), or is the *ideal of the ego* something *beyond a form of the ego*, properly an *idea*, of the order of *form*?

Hyppolite: *Entfernung* (distancing) — must this be understood as engendering the ideal of the ego? Leclaire: Freud spoke of the ideal of the ego before. Hyppolite: *unaccomplishable* — for it is in the end the *origin of transcendence: destructive and attractive*.

Mannoni notes a problem: one often has the impression of speaking several languages. He hypothesizes the distinction between *development of the person* and *structuration of the ego*. It is a structuring ego, in a developing being.

Lacan: we are in structuration. Exactly there develops all analytic experience, *at the junction of imaginary and symbolic*. As Leclaire posed: what is the function of the *image* and what he called the *idea*? The idea — we know it never lives alone; it lives with all other ideas, as Plato taught.

Let us take up the little optical apparatus to fix ideas. Start from the study of instinct in the animal — an *ideal* animal, that is, successful. The "ill-succeeded" is the animal we have grasped, whose mechanisms satisfy us. This animal gives us this *vision of completeness, of accomplished world, of perfect interlocking, even identity of Innenwelt and Umwelt* — which makes for us *seduction* a living form, unfurling harmoniously its appearance.

What does instinctual functioning show? The extreme importance of what we call *the image*. What functions in setting in motion the complementary behavior of the male and female stickleback? *Gestalten*. The animal subject — male or female — is *captivated by what is essentially this Gestalt*. The *triggering stimulus* is something with which *the subject literally identifies*. From the moment the male is taken in the *zigzag dance*, it is from a certain *interrelation between himself and the image* that literally commands the trigger of the whole mechanism inserted in the cycle of the stickleback's sexual behavior. The female is likewise taken in this *reciprocal dance*.

The closed character of this *world of two* gives us in simple state the *confusion, the conjunction in a single natural manifestation of objectal libido and narcissistic libido*: the attachment of each object to the other is made of something which is a *narcissistic fixation on this image*. For it is this image and it alone that he awaited — the very foundation of the fact that in the order of living beings, only the partner of the *same species* can trigger that special form called *sexual behavior*. With a few exceptions to be considered as the *kind of opening of error* in the manifestations of nature.

So we start there. In this cycle of sexual behavior in the animal world, *the imaginary dominates everything*. And here we see the greatest possibility of *displacement* manifest itself: we use it experimentally when we present a *lure* — a false image, a male partner that is only a shadow bearing the major characteristics. At the moment of phenotype-manifestations produced in many species at the biological moment calling the sexual behavior, it suffices to present it to trigger exactly the same conduct. Underlining: the *possibility of displacement* essential to all that is of the order of sexual behavior, and the *imaginary character* manifested in the illusory of what we provoke experimentally.

Is it the same in man? In the end, this image, this *Idealich* we spoke of — why not? Yet one does not think of calling this the *Idealich*. It must be situated elsewhere. Here is where the merits of Lacan's little apparatus insert themselves.

\[Lacan reviews the optical schema: the real image produced by the concave mirror, seen in its place, integrated into the world of real objects, accommodated alongside them, and capable of bringing to these real objects a *kind of imaginary order* — including, excluding, situating, ordering, completing them.\] Up to here, what do you see? Nothing other than the *imaginary phenomenon* detailed in the animal. Approximately: he makes coincide a *real object* with this image in him. As Freud's texts indicate somewhere: *this coincidence of the image with a real object reinforces it, gives it body and incarnation*. It is at this moment that a number of behaviors trigger, guiding the subject toward its object by the intermediary of the image.

Does this happen in man? We know in man the manifestations of sexual function are characterized by *eminent disorder*. Nothing adapts. This image we learn (which we — analysts — move around, whether in neuroses or perversions) shows precisely a kind of *fragmentation, bursting, parcellation*, *inadaptation, inadequation* of this image — a kind of *hide-and-seek*, *non-coincidence* between the image and its normal object (if we adopt this ideal of a *norm* in sexual functioning).

It is here the problem poses itself: how can we represent the mechanism by which this imagination *in disorder* can finally yet manage to fulfill its function? We are around the question analysts pose, scratching their heads. Take any article — the last Lacan read of Balint, whose visit and coming to their society he announces. In the end: *what is the end of treatment*?

Perhaps — Lacan does not know, it depends on his inspiration — he would like, in the last session of this cycle this trimester, to speak of the *termination of analysis*. Their examination of mechanisms of resistance and transference would perhaps not permit it; but the termination of analysis is one of the most *mythical* of our knowledge.

The question: *what is the end of treatment*? Must we consider it as the end of a *natural process*, a *natural outcome*? Is the *genital attainment* — the famous *genital love* (said to be the *Eldorado* promised not only to analysts but, imprudently, to our patients) — a *natural process*, or rather the fact of a series of *cultural approximations* realizable only in a number of cases? Analysis and its termination would then depend on all sorts of contingencies.

We are always at the heart of the question. What is at stake? To see what is the proper function of the *other, the human other*, in this *adequation of the imaginary and the real*.

Lacan adds the perfection to the schema (introduced last session): the real image can only be seen consistently in a *certain field* of the real space in front of the apparatus. Only in this field can occur the *eye's accommodation* — that parallax-movement giving depth and space. The *ideal spectator* placed very far can be satisfactorily replaced by a *virtual subject in a mirror*.

The vision of an image in the mirror is equivalent, for the subject seeing something in the mirror, to what would be the image of the *real object* in relation to a hypothetical spectator beyond the mirror in the same place where he sees his image. The apparatus Lacan has invented means: by being placed at a point very close to the real image, one can nonetheless see it *further away* by looking in a mirror.

\[Lacan develops: the *real image* of the inverted bouquet/vase becomes a *virtual image* when seen through the mirror. The subject placed close to the real image but oriented to the mirror sees both *himself in the mirror* and *the image of the real image as virtual*. This combines the *first narcissism* (the body-image, *Urbild*, organizing reality, *Innenwelt*–*Umwelt* interlocking as in animals) with the *second narcissism* (identification with the other, the *alter-ego*, the *Ich-Ideal*). Man's narcissistic alienation in the *reflected image of himself* (*Ur-Ich*) is also the original form of the *Ich-Ideal* and of the *relation to the other*. The *ideal of the ego* is precisely what *imposes itself from outside* — a *symbolic agency* organizing the imaginary field. The same image — the captive body-image, the lure — that in the animal would be *Idealich* is in man placed *in the symbolic position* of *Ich-Ideal*. The play between virtual and real, between mirror-image and image-of-image, is exactly the play between *image of the ego* and *idea of the ego* Leclaire was asking after. The whole problem of the *displacement of libido on the ideal of the ego imposed from outside* is the displacement onto a *symbolically marked position*. This is why the *function of speech*, especially the *call*, is decisive in this displacement: only by symbolic mediation does the imaginary structure of the body-image come to articulate itself with the symbolic structures of *recognition* by the other. The neurotic seeks in the analyst the *Ich-Ideal* he cannot attain by himself — the *sexual ideal* as substitutive narcissistic satisfaction — and this is precisely what *love-transference* incarnates.\]

The session closes with Lacan promising to develop further the *function of the other* in the adequation of imaginary and real, the relation of love-transference to this whole structure, and the question of *what termination of analysis means* in light of these distinctions.

---

## **Session of 7 April 1954**

Lacan opens, picking up where he left off: *this imaginary is dominated by a certain mode of impression*. It is possible to present the characteristics of the real onto the image. Alain remarked that one does not count the columns on the mental image one has of the Panthéon. To which Lacan would gladly have responded: except for the architect of the Panthéon — that is the whole game. We are thus introduced, by this little side-door, into what will be today abundantly the relations of *real, imaginary, and symbolic*.

Hyppolite asks if he can pose a question on the *structure of the optical image*. He says he is asking grossly, for things go a bit fast; he wants material precisions. Lacan welcomes the question, the more so as it permits Perrier to catch his breath.

Hyppolite: a spherical mirror, an object placed at the center, its *real inverted image* at the center. This image, instead of being received on a screen, can be observed by the eye. Lacan: perfectly. Because it is a real image, the eye accommodates onto a certain plane (designated by the real object). In the real experiment: an inverted bouquet situated at the neck of a real vase. Insofar as the eye accommodates onto the real image, it sees this image — which forms sharply to the extent that the luminous rays converge on a single point of virtual space. To each point of the object corresponds a point of the image.

Hyppolite: the eye is placed in the luminous cone — sees the image, else does not see it. Lacan: experience proves the observer must be in a kind of prolongation of the mirror's aperture.

Hyppolite: if we place a *plane mirror*, this gives a virtual image of the real image considered as an object. Lacan: of course. Anything visible directly can be seen in a mirror. The virtual image in the mirror is seen *as the real image would be by a virtual observer placed symmetrically beyond the mirror*.

Hyppolite has redone the constructions, as in the days of the *bachot* and pre-medical *PCB*: real image of a real object, virtual image, etc. But there is *the eye that looks in this mirror*, to perceive the virtual image of the real image.

Lacan: from the moment Lacan can perceive the real image that would be here, by placing the mirror at mid-distance he will see — from where he is, somewhere between the real image and the spherical mirror, or even behind — appear in this mirror (provided it is placed conveniently, perpendicular to the axial line) the *same real image* profiling itself on the confused background a plane mirror gives of a spherical mirror's concavity.

Hyppolite: when I look in this mirror, I perceive at once *the virtual bouquet of flowers and my virtual eye*. Lacan: yes, provided my real eye exists and is not itself an abstract point. Lacan has underlined we are not an eye — he is beginning to enter abstraction here.

Hyppolite: so I have understood the image. There remains the *symbolic correspondence*. Lacan: that is what he will try to explain. Hyppolite: in particular — what is the play of correspondences between the real object, the flowers, the real image, the virtual image, the real eye and the virtual eye? Beginning with the real object: what do the real flowers represent in the symbolic correspondence?

Lacan: the interest of this schema is that it has several uses. Freud has already constructed something similar; he indicated specially, in the *Traumdeutung* and the *Outline*, that *psychic agencies* must be conceived as imaginary phenomena (forms). In the *Traumdeutung*, when he made the schema of those *layers* on which inscribe perceptions and memories — one composing consciousness, the others the unconscious — projecting themselves with consciousness, eventually closing the *stimulus-response* form that was at that time the plane on which one tried to make comprehensible the *circuit of the living*. In these different layers we can see something like the superposition of a number of photographic films. The schema is imperfect; one must always suppose...

Hyppolite: he has already used Lacan's schema; he seeks the first correspondences. Lacan: *the primitive correspondences*? To fix ideas, at the level of the *real image* (which serves to contain and exclude a number of real objects), we can give the signification of *the limits of the ego* — see the ego form itself at the level of the real image in a certain dialectic. For all this is only the *use of relations*: assign such function to an element of the model, another element takes such another function. Start from there.

Hyppolite: could one admit that *the real object signifies the Gegenbild* — the *sexual replica* of the ego? In the animal schema, the male finds the *Gegenbild*, his complementary counterpart in the structure. Lacan: since one needs a *Gegenbild*. Hyppolite: the term is Hegel's. Lacan: the term *Gegenbild* itself implies correspondence to an *Innenbild* — what one calls the correspondence of *Innenwelt* and *Umwelt*.

Hyppolite: which leads me to say: if the real object — the flowers — represents the real object correlative (animally speaking) of the perceiving animal subject, then the *real image of the pot of flowers* represents the *imaginary reflected structure* of this real structure. Lacan: you could not put it better. Exactly, at the start, when it is a matter of the animal first — when only the spherical mirror is involved — the real image is this *interesting phenomenon*: a real image comes to mingle with real things.

Limited to that, this is how we can represent the *Innenbild* allowing the animal to seek its specific partner — as the key seeks the lock, or the lock the key — to direct its libido where it must be, for the propagation of the species. (Lacan pointed out one day that, in this perspective, we already grasp impressively the *essentially transitory character of the individual in relation to the type*.)

Hyppolite: the cycle of the species. Lacan: not only the cycle of the species, but the fact that the individual is so captive of the type that, in relation to the type, *it annihilates itself*. It is — as Hegel might say, Lacan does not know if he did — *already dead*: in relation to the eternal life of the species, it is already dead.

Hyppolite: I had Hegel say, in commenting your image, that in fact "*Knowledge — humanity — is the failure of sexuality*." Lacan: we go a bit fast there.

Hyppolite: it was simply the cycle. What was important for him: the real object (flowers or vase, according to the case) can be taken as the *real counterpart of the order of the species* of the real individual. But then a *development in the imaginary* makes this counterpart, in the spherical mirror alone, *become also a real image* — an image that, fascinating *as such* (even in the absence of the real object projected in the imaginary), fascinates the individual and *captures him up to the plane mirror*.

Lacan: this lets us apprehend in an imaged way the *kind of thickening* — relative to the perception of the external world — *of condensation, of opacification*, that *libidinal capture as such* represents. In the animal, taken in a cycle of instinctual behavior, he opens onto the world only insofar as... — Lacan recalls how difficult it is to measure what is perceived and not perceived by the animal — yet we know that when caught in an instinctual behavior, he is so *stuck* in a number of imaginary conditions that *precisely where it would seem most useful that he not be deceived, we deceive him most easily*. This *libidinal fixation on certain terms* presents itself for us as a kind of *funnel*.

So we start there. But if it is necessary to constitute an apparatus a bit more complex and ingenious for *man*, it is because precisely for him *it does not happen like that*. Since Hyppolite has given Lacan the relaunch, Lacan does not see why he would not begin to recall the fundamental Hegelian theme: *man's desire is the desire of the Other*. How do we find it? Precisely this is what is expressed in the model by the *plane mirror*.

It is also here we retrieve the classic *mirror stage* of Jacques Lacan, insofar as it is classic in a small circle. What appears in development is the character of *turning, virage*: at a certain moment, the kind of triumph, the *triumphant exercise of himself* the individual makes of his own image in the mirror. We can, by correlations with his behavior, understand it is for the first time a kind of *anticipatory grasp*, a *mastery*.

We touch here something Lacan has called the *Urbild* — in another sense than the word *Bild* of just now: the first model of something in which is marked in man this kind of *retard, lag, gaping*, in relation to his own libido. Whence the radical difference between *the satisfaction of a desire* and the *pursuit of the achievement of desire* — essentially something else: a kind of *negativity* introduced at a moment not specially originary but *crucial, turning*; a negativity introduced into desire itself, grasped *in the other first* and in its most radical, most confused form — but ceaselessly developing thereafter.

This *relativity of human desire to the desire of the other* — we know it in every reaction of rivalry, competition — even in the entire development of civilization, including that sympathetic and fundamental *exploitation of man by man* whose end we are not about to see (since it is absolutely *structural*) and which constitutes — admitted once for all by Hegel — *the very structure of the notion of labor*. No longer desire here, but the *complete mediation of activity* as properly human, engaged in the path of human desires. It is what we find: the *dual origin*.

For this *value of the image*: what we see is that if desire is originally already marked and recognized in the *properly human imaginary*, this is so by the intermediary not only of one's own image but of *the body of one's fellow*. At that moment is separated, in the human being, *consciousness as self-consciousness*. It is insofar as it is in the *body of the other* that one recognizes one's desire, that the *exchange* takes place; and insofar as one's desire has passed to the other side, this *body of the other* he assimilates to himself — *he recognizes himself as body*.

There remains certainly something to be left in the form of question mark: that the *animal* has a *consciousness separated from his body as such*, that his *corporeity* is for him an objectivable element situated somewhere, marked out as *body*.

Hyppolite: *statuesque*, in the double sense.

Lacan: exactly. For us, the fundamental given — *prior to any emergence of the register of unhappy consciousness*, to give as just now the first surging — is in this distinction of our consciousness from our body, something that makes of our body something *factitious*, from which our consciousness is incapable of detaching itself, but from which it conceives itself as distinct.

This distinction of consciousness and body is made in the kind of brusque *interchange of roles* in the mirror experience when it is a question of the other. As Lacan was saying yesterday evening: apropos of the mechanism, Mannoni brought the notion that in interpersonal relations themselves, something *factitious* always introduces itself — *the projection of the other's mechanism on ourselves*; and the same thing that we recognize ourselves as body insofar as the others (indispensable to recognize our desire) also have a body — more exactly, that *we have one as they do*.

Hyppolite: what he understands less well than the distinction of self and body, is the *distinction of two bodies*. Lacan: of course. Hyppolite: since the self represents itself as the *ideal body and the body I feel*, there are two...?

Lacan: No, certainly not. For precisely this is where the *discovery and dimension of the Freudian experience* take their essential relation: man in his first phases does not arrive at all at a *surmounted desire*. He is first a *fragmented desire*. What he recognizes and fixes in this image of the other is a *fragmented desire*. And the apparent *mastery of the mirror-image* is given him at least virtually as total, as ideally a *mastery*.

Hyppolite: it is what he calls the *ideal body*. Lacan: yes, it is the *Idealich*. Whereas his desire is precisely not constituted; what he finds in the other is first a series of *ambivalent planes*, *alienations of his desire* — but a desire itself which is still a *desire in pieces*, as all we know of instinctual evolution gives us as schema, since the theory of libido in Freud is made of the *conservation, progressive composition* of a number of *partial drives* succeeding (or not) in arriving at a *mature desire*.

Hyppolite: he believes they are in agreement; Lacan was saying no just now. If he says *two bodies*, it means simply that what he sees constituted — either in the other or in his own image in the mirror — is *what he is not*, what is *beyond him*. He calls this the *ideal body, statutary, or statue* (as Valéry says in *La Jeune Parque*: "but my statue at the same time shudders") — that is, *decomposes*. Its decomposition is what he calls the *other body*.

Lacan: the body as *fragmented desire seeking itself*, and the body *as ideal of oneself*, re-project themselves on the side of the subject as *fragmented body*, while he sees the other as *perfect body*. For him, a fragmented body is an essentially *dismemberable image of his body*.

Hyppolite: the two re-project one onto the other in this sense: at once he sees himself as *statue* and *dismembers* himself at the same time, projects the dismemberment on the statue — in an *unending dialectic*. He excuses himself for repeating what Lacan had said, to be sure he has understood.

Lacan: they will, if Hyppolite wishes, make another step. The *real*: of course, though it is on this side of the mirror — what is *beyond*? They have already seen there is this *primitive imaginary of the specular dialectic with the other*, absolutely fundamental.

Lacan underlines that, in two senses, this introduces the *mortal dimension of the death drive*, the dimension of *destrudo*: first, insofar as it participates of what is *irremediably mortal for the individual* in everything of the order of *libidinal capture*, insofar as this is finally submitted to that *x of eternal life*; second — Lacan thinks this is the important point Freud emphasized, not completely distinguished in *Beyond the Pleasure Principle* — the death drive takes in man *another signification*, precisely because his libido is originally *constrained to pass by an imaginary stage*.

Moreover, this *image of image* explains itself: it is the whole *guidance* for man's reaching the maturity of libido — to that *adequation of the imaginary to reality* there would be, in principle (by hypothesis: what do we know?), in the animal, which seems and always has seemed so much more evident — whence the great fantasy of *natura mater*, of the *idea of nature itself*; and from which man represents his *original inadequation* by something expressed in a thousand ways, even fully objectifiable in his being: that quite special *impotence from the origin of his life*.

This *prematuration of birth* is not invented by psychoanalysts. It is evident — at least histologically — that the *nervous apparatus* (which plays in the organism a role still under discussion) is *unfinished at birth*. Insofar as he must reach the achievement of his libido *before* reaching its object, there is introduced this *special fault* which perpetuates itself in his relation to *another*, *infinitely more mortal* for him than for any other animal. He confuses this *image of the master* — what he sees under the form of the *specular image* — confuses it in an entirely authentic way (he can name it) with the *image of death*. He can be in presence of the *absolute master*; he is so originally — whether one has taught him or not — insofar as he is already submitted to this image.

Hyppolite: the animal is submitted to death when he makes love — but knows nothing of it. Lacan: while man knows it. He knows and experiences it. Hyppolite: it goes so far as to say that *it is he who gives himself death* — he wants, through the other, his own death. Lacan: we are all in agreement that *love is a form of suicide*.

Dr Lang asks Lacan to clarify a point on which he insisted: that the eye must be in a certain *field* in relation to the apparatus. Optically clear; but Lang did not grasp the depth of Lacan's insistence.

Lacan: he did not show enough of the *tip of the ear*. What is at stake here can play on several planes — interpretation at the level of structuration, description, or handling of the cure. It is particularly convenient to have a schema where the *observer*, remaining always in the same place, the whole *appearance of this image* depends on the *mobilization of a plane of reflection*.

For if the image can be seen with sufficient completeness only from a *certain virtual point of observation*, and if one can change this virtual point as desired, then when the mirror turns, it is not only the *background* (what the subject sees at the back — for example, himself or an echo of himself, as Hyppolite remarked) that changes. When one moves a plane mirror, there is a moment when a number of objects exit the field — evidently the *closest* exit last.

This already serves to explain certain ways the *Idealich* situates itself in relation to something else Lacan leaves enigmatic for now, called *the observer*. Of course it is not only an observer. It is, in the end, the *symbolic relation*: namely, *the point from which one speaks, from which it is spoken*.

But that is not all that changes. If you incline the mirror, *the image itself changes* — that is, *without the real image moving*, by the sole fact that the mirror changes, the image (which the subject placed here, on the side of the spherical mirror, will see in this mirror) will pass, Lacan thought he had indicated, *from a form of mouth to a form of phallus*, or from a *more or less complete desire* to the type of desire he called *fragmented*.

In other words: this will permit *conjoining* — what was always Freud's idea — the notion of *topical regression* with possible correlations with the regression Freud calls *zeitlich-Entwicklungsgeschichte* (which shows how embarrassed Freud himself was with all that concerned temporal relation): *zeitlich* (temporal), then a dash, then *Entwicklungsgeschichte* (developmental history). You know what kind of *internal contradiction* exists between the terms *Entwicklung* (development) and *Geschichte* (history). Freud conjoins all three — *good luck*. But if we had not to disentangle, there would be no need for us to be here.

Lacan invites Perrier to proceed.

### **François Perrier presents the *Metapsychological Supplement to the Theory of Dreams***

Perrier: this text is a bit annoying. He thinks the best would be to sketch a *schema of the article*. Freud opens by saying it is instructive to establish a *parallel between certain morbid symptoms and the normal prototypes* (German *Vorbild* — Lacan notes, this aligns with *Bildung*) that permit us to study them: for example *mourning, melancholia, the dream, sleep, certain narcissistic states*. The goal — appearing at the end of the article — is to deepen the study of phenomena encountered in *narcissistic affections*, in *schizophrenia* for instance. *Normalvorbilder krankhafter Affektionen* — *normal prefigurations of morbid affections*, Lacan glosses.

Perrier: Freud tells us *sleep is a state of psychic undressing* bringing the sleeper back to a state analogous to the primitive *fetal state*. This state leads him to undress also a whole part of his psychic organization — as one removes a wig, false teeth, one's clothes before sleeping.

Lacan: very curious and amusing that, apropos this image of the subject's narcissism (given as the fundamental essence of sleep), Freud makes this remark not seeming to go in a physiological direction. This is not true for all human beings. It is usual to remove clothes — but one puts others on.

The image Freud brings: removing glasses (Lacan notes a number of those present are gifted with the infirmities making them necessary), but also *false teeth, false hair* — *hideous image of the being who decomposes*. Apropos of this, one can access the register of the *partially decomposable* character, especially *dismountable*, also *imprecise as to its limits*, of what is the *human ego*. For in the end, false teeth are not part of my ego. But *to what extent are my real teeth part of it*? Since they are so *replaceable*. Already the idea of the *ambiguous, uncertain character* of what is properly certain, of the *limits of the ego*, is placed at the very forefront of the entry-portico of this metapsychological study of the dream — whose portico is first the preparation, and also the signification, of *sleep*.

Perrier continues: Freud arrives at something that is a *shortened form* of what he will study throughout. When one studies the psychoses, one is each time put in presence of *temporal regressions* — points to which each case returns on the stages of its own evolution. Two regressions: the *evolution of the ego* and the *evolution of the libido*. *Libidinal regression* in the dream leads to the *re-establishment of primitive narcissism*; *ego-regression* in the dream leads to *hallucinatory satisfaction of desire*. This is not a priori clear.

Lacan: perhaps clearer with the schema? Perrier: yes, but he has not yet confronted the text with the schema. Lacan asks him to speak at the level everyone can take. To underline what is properly enigmatic.

\[The session continues: Perrier elaborates the *egoism of the dream* and its comparison with *narcissism*, the *hallucinatory satisfaction of desire*, the regression of *ego-evolution* to a primordial elementary non-evolved state. Lacan emphasizes throughout: dream and sleep both effect a *withdrawal of libido from objects of the external world onto the ego* — *primary narcissism* re-established — but for this withdrawal to be possible, the *body-image* must already have been constituted. The optical schema bears this out: the *real image of the vase* surrounding the imaginary flowers is the *Idealich* (ideal ego, body-image-as-totality); for it to function as such, the eye must be placed in a certain symbolic relation governed by the mirror's inclination, which is to say by the *Ich-Ideal* — the *symbolic position* from which one is recognized, named, addressed. When in sleep the symbolic recedes and the subject withdraws into the narcissistic position, the dream-image emerges as *the imaginary equivalent of the real image* — *seen* (hallucinated) where it is not, but with full *sentiment of reality*. The dream is the *imaginary realization of desire* precisely because the *censor* — that *Ich-Ideal*\-agency Freud identified at the end of the *Narcissism* paper — *partially relaxes* but does not disappear: it continues to enforce *Entstellung*, deformation. The schema thus connects dream-formation, narcissism, hallucination, and the structure of *love-transference*: love is a state where *the object takes the place of the Ich-Ideal*, and hypnosis is its limit case where the analyst-figure becomes the symbolic agency around which the imaginary reorganizes itself.\]

The session sets up what will become — across the remaining sessions — Lacan's elaboration of the *full structure of transference* as the intersection of imaginary capture (the analyst as Idealich, as *image of the master*, as image of death) and symbolic recognition (the analyst as *Ich-Ideal*, as point from which the subject is named and addressed). It is by *inclining the mirror* — by occupying a determinate position in the symbolic dialogue — that the analyst orients the imaginary economy of the analysand. Hence the *cure* is neither education nor suggestion nor pure imaginary capture: it is the *modification of the position of the speaking subject* in the symbolic order, by which the imaginary necessarily re-orders itself.

Lacan closes by promising to take up next time, with Granoff, *Observations on Transference-Love* — and to bring together the threads: narcissism, ego-formation, love as imaginary capture and as suicide, the function of the analyst in the symbolic, and the eventual question of the *end of analysis*.

---

## **Session of 5 May 1954**

Lacan opens the third trimester, which will be short. In an original project he had thought to broach the case of Schreber before separating this year. He had the *Memoirs* of President Schreber translated (the original Freud worked from), an unfindable work — Lacan knows only two copies in Europe, has had one microfilmed, one for himself, one for the new library of the Society. But Schreber will wait. There is a *gap* there. *Better not fall into it immediately*, lest they remain prisoners — as Hyppolite feared.

Since they have advanced through Freud's *Papers on Technique*, it is impossible not to push further the implicit rapprochement Lacan has constantly made with present-day analytic technique — what one may call (in scare-quotes) "its most recent progresses." He has done this more or less implicitly, referring to what they have in their experience, in the teaching of supervisions, the tendency toward which the *analysis of resistances* has evolved (for example, the notion of analysis as analysis of the *systems of defense of the ego*). All this remains badly centered, since these are concrete but non-systematized, sometimes non-formulated teachings to which one implicitly refers.

Despite the rarity often signaled in the analytic literature, a number of authors have expressed themselves on technique. If they have not made a properly speaking *book of technique*, there are articles — fragmentary, sometimes curiously left in mid-route, among the most interesting. There is here a literature in which, despite the difficulty of obtaining all of it, they may advance.

These writings form a long corpus. Lacan hopes for collaboration from some — to whom he will lend articles to serve as basis for discussion. But they cannot take them all in view. The 1925 Berlin Symposium articles by Sachs, Alexander, Rado are important (referenced in Fenichel). The Marienbad Congress had the symposium on *results* of analysis — though really less the results than the *procedure leading to results* is what is discussed. There one can already see what Lacan calls *the confusion of tongues in analysis* — the extreme diversity, despite intentions, of what authors consider the *active means* in the analytic process. Precise definition is far from assured in analysts' minds: with marked diversity they express themselves.

The third moment is the present. Here belong recent elaborations of *ego-theory* by, for example, the American "troika": Hartmann, Loewenstein, Kris. Their writings are sometimes disconcerting by a complication in the multiplication of concepts — they speak constantly of *desexualized libido*, almost *de-libidinized*, or of *de-aggressified aggressivity*.

The ego-function plays here that increasingly problematic role it has in Freud's third-period writings, which Lacan has left outside their field. The commentary on the *Papers on Technique* aimed at the *median period* (1910-1920) — where, with the notion of narcissism, the direction Freud will reach in *the ego* begins to elaborate. Lacan recommends reading the volume containing *Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, *Group Psychology and Analysis of the Ego*, and *The Ego and the Id*. Indispensable for understanding the developments authors have given the theory of treatment from 1920 onward — most often with extreme awkwardness if one has not deepened the very genesis of the notion of narcissism (which Lacan has tried to indicate apropos of the analysis of resistances and transference).

Lacan wishes today to procede partly by the discursive way, partly by concentrating, in a didactic formula, points of view, perspectives along which the diverse formulations one finds — across the marked diversity of authors on these precise points in analytic history — can be reconnected and discussed. He adopts a middle term: he tries to present a *model* — not pretending to be a system but an image presenting reference-points.

This is what he has been trying to do in leading them gradually to the *optical image* they have begun to form. Now familiar to their minds, he hopes. Around the *fundamental speculum*, the fundamental mirror of the relation to the other, they have seen we can situate (to mark the points where the problem is posed) the famous *real image*.

The real image forms in the apparatus at a point of the subject, call it 0\. The virtual image where the subject grasps it, produced in the plane mirror, is at 0' — where the subject sees the real image which is at 0, since by the intermediary of this mirror he finds himself placed somewhere, at a point that is the virtual symmetric of the spherical mirror, thanks to which the real image (somewhere inside the subject) is produced at 0\.

Pujol furrows his brow. Lacan: something amiss? Pujol: in a month, I've had trouble re-engaging. Lacan continues: these give us two points 0 and 0'. A little girl — virtual woman, thus much more engaged in the real than males by this fact, particular gifts\! — once had this very pretty word: *"Ah\! It must not be believed that my life will be spent in 0 and 0'..."* Poor dear\! It will be spent in 0 and 0' like everyone's. But she has this aspiration. In her honor Lacan will call these points 0 and 0'. There is a point A, another which we will call C (because there is a point B Lacan must leave for later).

It is fundamentally a question of what concerns the constitution of the *Idealich*, not of the *Ichideal*. The essentially imaginary, specular form of the genesis, of the fundamentally imaginary origin of the ego. If you have not seen this emerge from what we have analyzed — particularly *Zur Einführung des Narzißmus* — then we have done a vain work.

You must have understood the close relation, in Freud's discourse of the *Narcissism* paper, between *the formation of the object and that of the ego*. They are strictly correlative; their appearance is contemporary. Hence the problem of *narcissism*: at this moment in Freud's thought, libido appears submitted to *another dialectic* — call it the *dialectic of the object*. It is not only the relation of the biological individual with his object "nature," diversely complicated and enriched. There is the possibility of a *narcissistic libidinal investment* — investment in something that can be conceived only as an *image of the ego*. Lacan speaks grossly; he could speak more technically; he wants them to see the things clearly.

It is certain that from a certain moment in the development of Freudian experience, attention is centered on this *imaginary function* of the ego. Since then, the whole history of psychoanalysis is reduced to this — to the ambiguities, the difficulties, the way of using this term. Lacan will pursue the question by way of his model.

The little child: it is well clear that we know nothing of the *specificity, the diversity, the precise resonance-point* where the individual situates himself — the idea of the little subject. This is what makes him so unhappy: how, moreover, would he make recognized his desires? for the simple reason that *he knows nothing of them*. We have every reason to think he knows nothing of his desires. But our reasons (as analysts) are generated by our adult experience. *We know he must seek and find them*. Else he would not need analysis.

It is sufficient indication that what concerns his ego — what he can make recognized of himself — is separated from him by *something*. What analysis teaches us — and you must recall Hyppolite's discourse on Freud's precious text *Die Verneinung* — is something that is *very significant* and must be articulated in a certain way.

Saying "he knows nothing" is vague. Analysis teaches us by degrees: it is not pure and simple *ignorance*. We must doubt this for a simple reason: *ignorance is itself a dialectical term*, constituted as such only in a *perspective of the search for truth*. If the subject is not put in reference with truth, there is no ignorance. If the subject does not begin again to pose the question of what he is and is not — which, of course, is not at all obligatory; many people live without posing such elevated questions — there is no reason for there to be a true and a false, nor even for that fundamental distinction of *reality and appearance*.

Here we begin to be in full philosophy. *Ignorance is constituted in a polar way relative to the virtual position of a truth to be attained*: it is a state of the subject *insofar as he speaks*. Insofar as his speech begins to err in search of the right language, we begin to constitute it from the moment we engage the subject in analysis — in an *implicit way*, where we engage him in a *search for the truth*.

But this is a state purely virtual *to a situation we create*. It is not the given when we say *the ego knows nothing of the subject's desires*. That is something experience teaches us, learned in a second stage. It is not ignorance but rather what is expressed concretely in the process of *Verneinung* — and in the static ensemble of the subject is called *méconnaissance*. Misrecognition is not the same as ignorance.

Misrecognition represents a number of *affirmations and negations*, a certain *structure, organization*. The subject is attached to it. None of this would be conceivable without a *correlative knowledge*: when we speak of *méconnaissance*, this must always imply that, since the subject *can misrecognize something*, *he must yet know around what sum this function of misrecognition has operated*. Behind misrecognition, a certain *knowledge of what is to be misrecognized*.

When we find a delirious patient living in misrecognition of the death of a close relative, we would be entirely wrong to think he confuses him with a living person: *he misrecognizes or refuses to recognize that he is dead*, but his whole behavior signifies that he develops an *activity supposing he knows there is something he does not want to recognize*.

What is this méconnaissance implied behind the function of *ego-knowledge*? Here we can broach the *function of the ego*. We are led to deliver ourselves, in presence of what interests us (what is analyzable), to a whole operation of *mantique* — of *translation, loosening* — of something *beyond the language of the subject*, of a language presenting itself in this ambiguous relation on the plane of *knowledge*.

To advance in this register, we must ask what this *knowledge* is that orients and directs misrecognition. Here we find the reference to a parallel, an analogical elaboration, with what one may call *animal knowledge* — insofar as in the animal there is knowledge and *co-aptation*, imaginary co-aptation, *structuration of the world as Umwelt*. By correspondence, by a projection on this world of a number of *relations, of Gestalten* structuring this world, specifying it for each animal in the milieu where it evolves — *weaving, distinguishing, separating* in the indistinct of reality a number of *preferential paths* in which its behavioral activities engage themselves. Animal-behavior psychologists, ethologists, define as *innate* the *Gestalten* one may call *innate structuration mechanisms* or *innate paths of discharge*.

What of man? Our whole experience indicates that there is nothing similar in man — as the *anarchy of his elementary drives* shows. His partial behaviors, his relation to the object (and to the libidinal object), are submitted to all sorts of contingencies, where most often the synthesis *fails* — showing that there is a *quite particular fact of man in his relation to the constituting images of his world*. What in man responds to the *innate knowledge* that is the *guide of life* in the balance of the living?

This something is the *image of his own body*, which in the animal also has extreme importance. Lacan makes a small leap, presupposing they have already accomplished some of the steps and can accept as theorem: the *conception of the mirror stage* — what is seen in the child's attitude before the mirror (between 6 and 18 months) fundamentally informs us on the relation of the *human biological-animal individual*. The *exaltation, jubilation* of the child during this period — Lacan showed it last year via Gesell's film *Self-Discovery before the Mirror* (Gesell had never heard of Lacan's mirror stage and no analytic question had ever been posed for him).

The most important is not the appearance of this behavior at 6 months but its *decline at 18 months*. How brusquely the behavior changes completely — becomes thereafter only an *Erscheinung* (appearance), a thing among others on which one can exercise control, experience, instrumental play, but no longer bearing the *pure, accentuated signs* it had in this period.

Here Lacan refers to what should be familiar to them: *what happens at the decline of the Oedipus complex*. We call it *introjection*. Lacan begs them not to give this term a too defined meaning, for it is what he will put in question.

What does this term *introjection* mean? Something happens — a reversal: *what was outside becomes inside*; *what was the father becomes the superego*. Something has happened at the level of that invisible, unthinkable *subject* one never names as such. One will ask: is it at the level of the ego or the Id? Yes, between the two — that's why it's called the *super-ego*. All this *demi-mythology specialized* in which our minds habitually expend themselves.

If one asked an analyst: *do you really believe the child gnaws his father, who enters into his stomach, becomes the superego*? We continue to operate as if this went without saying. Lacan will show in the analysis of the function of treatment what is at stake, what occurs, how it can realize, progress — the *innocent ways* of using the term "introjection" that go so far. Imagine an ethnologist from a country never having heard of this damned analysis suddenly here, hearing what happens in analysis: he would say *very curious\! very curious primitives, these analysands, who gnaw their analyst in little pieces*.

Balthasar Gracián (Lacan considers him fundamental — Nietzsche and La Rochefoucauld small beside him) wrote a treatise *El Comulgatorio* on communion conceived concretely and validly. If one believes in communion, no reason not to think one eats Christ — including the delicate lobe of his ear. No reason not to develop communion *à la carte*. For those who believe in transubstantiation.

But for us analysts, careful of science, reasonable, we can see in Stekel or other authors what this kind of *dosed introjection of the analyst* is — which an outside observer could transpose only to the mystic, communional plane, rather far from our real thought.

Insofar as we *think* — but, thank God, we do not think, that excuses us. The great error is to imagine that beings think what they say. So we do not think. Not a reason not to try to understand what it means, why one has uttered manifestly senseless words.

The end of the mirror stage represents *something analogous to that tilting* (bascule) produced at certain moments of psychic development. The tilting-moment is significant and fundamental. We can observe in the sequel manifestations of *equivalence between two things as different as the action of the other and his own action* — the *infantile transitivism* making the child say "François hit me" when *he* hit François: the *unstable mirror between the child and his fellow*.

This is necessitated by any theorization of interhuman behavior. There is a moment when this image — to which the child has borne what Lacan calls the *jubilatory assumption of mastery* not yet obtained — *the subject is capable of assuming inside*.

But of course he can do so only in the *state of empty form*, of this form of mastery, of envelope — something so sure and certain that Freud, arriving by different paths (the dynamics of libidinal investment), *cannot express it otherwise*. (Lacan begs them to read what Freud writes of the ego and the id in this regard.) When Freud speaks of the ego, he does not speak at all of an ego that would be some incisive, determinant, imperative thing — by which the ego rejoins what is called in academic psychology the *superior agencies*. Freud underlines in lines as significant as possible: *the ego must have the greatest relation with the surface of the body*. But not the sensible, sensorial, impressionable surface — *this surface insofar as it is reflected in a form*. Always the definition of a *form*: there is no form without surface; *form is defined by surface*, the difference in the identical (the surface).

It is insofar as this *fundamental image of the form of the other* is *assumed and situates itself inside*, under the form of this surface — thanks to which there is fundamentally introduced into human psychology the *relation of the inside to the outside, of the self by which the subject knows himself, recognizes himself as body*. We have no other proof.

This is the only truly fundamental difference between human and animal psychology: *man knows himself as body* — though there is no reason he should know, since he *is* inside it. The animal is *inside*; no reason to think he represents himself. *Man represents himself*. In this *tilting where he has learned himself as body, as empty form of the body*, the exchange is that *all that was in him, in the state of pure desire* — that inconstituted confused desire expressed in the child's wailing — *this desire is inverted in the other*, where he will learn to recognize it. ("Learn" because he has not yet learned, until we have spoken of *communication*.)

Until now, desire — in a moment not chronological but logical (all this passes in an extremely concentrated period; we make only a deduction, but absolutely fundamental, for thanks to it we can distinguish the planes of *symbolic, imaginary, and real*, without which there is no way to advance in analytic experience except in forms verbal, confining on the mystic). Until desire learns to recognize itself — *by the symbol*, that is, *in language* — this desire is felt and seen only *projected in the other, alienated from the origin*; on the sole plane of this imaginary relation of the specular stage, *it is in the other*.

But there it can provoke only the *most issueless tension* — for, as Hegel's elaborated thought teaches us, *it identifies with the destruction of the other*. There is no resolution of this tension, since precisely this desire of the other (in the desire of the subject, projected) can only concentrate, confirm itself in a *concurrence, radical rivalry for the object* toward which this desire tends. Whenever we approach this *imaginary root of the conjunction of human desire* in this primordial alienation, there can be engendered only *the most radical aggressivity* — the only possible mediation relative to the other being: *the desire of the disappearance of the other*, insofar as what the other supports is precisely the *desire of the subject*.

We thereby rejoin the *most ravaging, most unleashed jealousy* — that which St Augustine signals in the famous phrase Lacan has often repeated: the *absolutely ravaging jealousy the little child experiences for his fellow* (especially when *appendu*, attached to his mother's breast, the object essential to his desire).

This absolutely central function — the imaginary relation existing in the subject's relation to this *Urbild*, this *Idealich* — by which he learns to know himself as form — *always, to the end, the possibility of this tilting or reversion*: each time the subject apprehends himself as *form and as ego*, constitutes himself in his *statute, stature, statics*, *his desire projects itself outside*. And he is, thank God, *in a world of others who speak*.

Thanks to which this desire is susceptible, *in the world of symbol, of language*, to a certain *mediation, recognition*. Without this, every human function could exhaust itself only in an *indefinite wish of destruction of the other*, as such this desire is. Inversely: each time something appears in the other, in the phenomenon of the other, that permits the subject to *re-project, re-complete, nourish* (as Freud says somewhere) *this image of the Idealich* — each time can re-take place in an analogical way and on another plane this kind of *jubilatory assumption* (the mirror-phenomenon) — *each time the subject is captivated by one of his fellows significantly* (we will see what "significantly" means) — inversely, *desire returns in the subject — but returns verbalized*.

In other words, the most exemplary phenomenon of these *objectal identifications of the Idealich*, the one Lacan has drawn their attention to from the start, is what? It is all *Verliebtheit*: meetings (not just any) between *Verliebtheit* and *transference*. The difference: *Verliebtheit* does not occur automatically — certain conditions of the subject's evolution are determining. As Freud underlines in the article so often badly read — because everyone reading it thinks only of that famous *idiot schema* with stages, the little lens, the sides, the thing that comes in and is called the superego, and that's enough for everyone. What an idea Freud had of bringing that out, having surely other schemas.

Read the article and *know that Freud says the ego is made of the succession of identifications with its love-objects which permitted it to take its form*. The ego is an object made like an *onion*: one could peel it and discover the succession of identifications that constituted it.

The perpetual *reversion of desire to form, and of form to desire* — of consciousness and body, of desire (as partial) to the love-object (where the subject literally loses and identifies himself) — *is the fundamental mechanism around which turns all that concerns the ego*. On condition we understand that this play would truly be of fire and flame, leading to immediate *extermination*, as soon as the subject \[could not pass to the symbolic mediation by speech\].

\[Lacan continues: the resolution of the imaginary deadlock — the *mortal* dialectic of *destruction of the other* — is possible only by the introduction of *symbolic mediation*, the speech of recognition. The whole point of analysis is to operate at this junction: not to feed the imaginary identifications (the analyst as Idealich, with the dangers of dosed introjection), but to occupy the symbolic position from which the subject can hear his desire *verbalized*, *recognized*. This is what distinguishes analytic *interpretation* from suggestion, education, or the imaginary capture of love. The end of the mirror stage marks the *transition* from the imaginary dialectic of destruction to the symbolic dialectic of recognition — and *introjection* properly understood is not a swallowing but a *symbolic assumption* of a position in the order of speech. The "father becoming the superego" means: the father as *agent of the law*, the symbolic position, is assumed as the internal point from which one speaks to oneself. This *Ich-Ideal* — distinct from the *Idealich* — is the *symbolic structure of recognition* without which the imaginary play of identifications would remain murderous.\]

The session closes with Lacan setting up the threads to be developed in the remaining sessions: the function of the *Verliebtheit/transference* distinction; the *automatism* of transference in analysis as distinct from the *occasion-bound* character of falling in love; the role of analysis as occupation of a *symbolic position* permitting the verbalization of desire that would otherwise remain caught in the imaginary specular dialectic. They will examine concrete technique through the literature — Sachs, Alexander, Rado, Strachey, Balint — and ask what truly is the *active means* in analysis: is it suggestion, identification, the mobilization of unconscious material, the analysis of resistances, or some combination — and how must it be reconceived in light of the distinction Lacan is establishing between *Idealich* and *Ichideal*?

---

## **Session of 12 May 1954**

Lacan asks who could pose a question to broach where they left off last time.

Pujol asks: when you say "the desire of the other" — is it the desire that is *in* the other, or the desire that I have *for* the other? For him these are not the same thing. But Lacan says each time "the desire of the other."

Lacan: it depends on the point. Pujol: at the end of what Lacan said last time, it was the desire *in* the other, and the ego can take it back, by destroying. But it is at the same time a desire of him *for* the other.

Lacan: is this not the originally specular foundation of the relation to the other, insofar as rooted in the *imaginary*? The first alienation of desire is linked to this concrete phenomenon: the child sees in the other an activity *anticipating his own*, more perfect, more mastered — the *ideal form* of his own. This first object is *valorized*.

This is important because it poses at a quite originary, contemporary plane (not only of the child's first development but of his *pre-development*) the condition of the *human object*: it is not simply, directly, the *endoceptor* (the complement of animal desire) but is already *mediatized by the path of rivalry* — with all the maximum of accent that rivalry comports in the relation to the rival. Everything that is the relation of *prestige, prestance*, is a relation already of the order of *alienation* — for it is *there first* that the subject grasps himself as ego.

The notion he has of *the totality of the body* as identical with something ineffable, lived. The first élan of appetite and desire passes by the mediation of this form he sees first projected, *exterior to himself*. He sees it first in his own reflection.

Second assimilation: he knows he is a *body* — though he never perceives it completely, since he is *inside* it; but he *knows*. This image becomes the *throttling*, the *ring* through which the whole confused bundle of desire and needs must pass, in its imaginary structure, *to be him*. So when Lacan says man's desire is the desire of the other — a formula like all formulas to be handled in its place — it can lend itself to certain ambiguities to be specified, for it is not valid in a single sense.

This is one of the most crucial points that must direct their comprehension of technique. They started from the plane of *imaginary capture*. But, as Lacan said at the end of last session, this does not limit itself absolutely there.

Without this — as Lacan indicated mythically — there would be no other interhuman relation possible than in this *mutual and radical intolerance of the coexistence of consciousnesses* (Hegel) — every other being by definition and essentially the one who *frustrates* the human being not of his *object* but of *the very form of his desire*: if this desire is acted by the other, then the other is virtually already the one who *deprives* him of this object.

There is here a relation between human beings, *inter-destructive and mortal*. The *political myth* — rivalry for life — has served to insert many things. Darwin forged it because he belonged to a nation of corsairs for whom *racism was the fundamental industry*.

The notion of *struggle for life* — politically discussable, for the supposed survival of the *strongest species* — everything goes against it. Absolutely contrary to evidence. A myth that goes against things.
