All one can establish about *zones and areas of expansion* of different forms proves on the contrary that there are species, points of *constancy and equilibrium* proper to each, not even conceivable without knowledge of the same points and areas of equilibrium of other species with which the first live, in a *coordination* — even between "eaters" and "eaten." But it never goes to this kind of *destructive radicalism*, for a simple reason: it would lead to *the annihilation of the eating species* having nothing more to eat.

On the human plane, this is extremely important because we operate, implying in the notion of *aggressivity* (handled brutally without deepening) that aggressivity is *aggression*. It has nothing to do with that. Virtually at the limit it resolves itself in an aggression, but in an aggression having nothing to do with vital reality — an *existential aggression*, linked to an *imaginary rapport*. A truth absolutely essential — a key permitting one to rethink in a completely different register all sorts of problems, not only ours.

This desire realized in the human subject *in the other* — as Pujol says, *in* the other, *by* the other — in what we may call the *second time* (the specular type, when the subject has integrated the form of the ego): he could only integrate it by a first *tilting or reversal*, having exchanged this ego against this desire *seen in the other*. *This desire of the other, which is man's desire, enters the mediatization of language*. It is *in the other and by the other* that this desire will be *named, recognized*, will enter the *symbolic relation of "I" and "you"* — with all that comports of *recognition, reciprocal, transcendence* — simply because it has been *named*, because it enters the order — already prepared to include the history of each individual — the order *of a faith*.

Lacan recalls the *Fort/Da* example: how the child enters naturally into this play. He begins to play with the object, precisely on the sole fact of its *presence or absence* — an object already transformed, an object of *symbolic function*, already a *sign*, already *devitalized*. When it is there he chases it; when not there he calls it.

The object passes already, by the first plays, into the plane of *language, of symbol*, which becomes more important than the object. If you do not get this in your head — Lacan has repeated it so many times — the *word or the concept is nothing other than the word in its materiality*; for the human being it is *the thing itself*. Not simply a kind of shadow, breath, virtual illusion of the thing: *the thing itself*.

If you reflect for a moment: in the real, *it is much more decisive for everything that has happened to elephants — living elephants — that the word "elephant" exists in human language*. Thanks to which, however narrow the doors, we really bring the elephant here into our deliberations. Men, by the existence of the word *elephant*, have taken regarding elephants — even before touching them — *resolutions much more decisive for everything that has happened to elephants in the world* than anything that has happened in their history (crossing a river, lake, natural sterilization of a forest, etc.). With just the word *elephant* and the way men speak of them, it suffices for things favorable or unfavorable, faste or néfaste — in any case catastrophic — to befall elephants, *before anyone has raised a gun or arrow toward them*.

Let us leave the elephants. It is clear that *I have only to speak of them; they need not be there*. They are here thanks to the word *elephant* — *in a greater reality than the contingent reality of the individual elephant*.

Hyppolite: that is Hegelian logic. Lacan: is it then attackable? Hyppolite: no. Mannoni was saying just now: *it's politics*.

Mannoni: it's the side by which human politics inserts itself, in the broad sense — for if men do not act like animals, it is precisely because they exchange knowledge by language. Politics is thus possible. Politics regarding elephants is possible thanks to the *word*.

Hyppolite: but not only — *the elephant itself is reached*; it is Hegelian logic.

Lacan: it is *pre-political*. Simply the way to make them touch the importance of *the name*. We are at the plane of *nomination*. No syntax yet. But syntax is born at the same time — and especially the child articulates elements of *axioms* before *phonemes*. The "*si des fois*" (if perhaps...) appears sometimes alone. This does not let us decide a logical anteriority — it is a phenomenal emergence. But it is already on the *symbolic plane* that this essential articulation places itself, by which from the origin the desires of the child — as they will be reintegrated by this *play of tilting* — are reintegrated. This *mirror play* makes the projection of the image succeed projection of desire, with a correlative reintrojection.

\[Lacan continues: at the climax of imaginary maturation, of the *Verliebtheit* arising in the libido's relation to the narcissistic image, the narcissistic image becomes the *invested image of the love-relation* — which is the phenomenological evidence of *love*. The libido's *pre-genital* phase is the point where man plays between his weakness, his natural weak point, and a certain natural realization. There is the *mirage-point between Eros and Thanatos*, between love and hate. This is the simplest way to express the role the ego plays in our conception of the role played by the so-called *desexualized libido of the ego* in the possibility of *reversion, instantaneous turning of hate into love and love into hate* — the problem Freud said in *The Ego and the Id* posed him the greatest difficulties.\]

The *aggressive reaction* of *Oedipal rivalry* is linked precisely to one of these changes of level. At the *decline of the Oedipus complex*, the father — who has first realized, on the imaginary plane, one of the most manifest figures of the *Idealich*, invested by *Verliebtheit* in this position — is, precisely at this moment of a certain *regression of the libidinal position* (the Oedipal phase, between 3 and 5), at this regression of libidinal level that the *sentiment of aggression or rivalry toward the father — hatred* — appears. Something that, at a *very small change in libidinal level near a certain threshold*, makes what was *love become hate*, and inversely — and can oscillate during a certain time between the two phenomena.

\[Lacan now returns to the analytic situation.\] *The plane on which plays the imaginary relation* gives definitively the *frames* in which all properly libidinal fluctuations will take place. Last session he left the question open on the plane of *symbolic functions*. He must start from *what happens in treatment* to say something organized and solid. What does it mean? *The use we make of language and speech in treatment*.

If memory serves, he defined this use of language as *a language that is speech* — since there are two subjects linked by a *pact* establishing itself at very diverse, even very confused levels at the origin, but no less essentially a pact, to which we do everything to establish this character from the start.

This justifies the various preliminary *rules* we give to the analytic relation. Within it, we do everything to *untie a whole series of moorings of speech* in the mode of speaking, in style, in the way of addressing one's allocutary — the subject is liberated from politeness, courtesy, even coherence — *we let go a certain number of moorings of speech*.

If we consider that a tight permanent link remains between *the way a subject can express himself, make himself recognized*, and the dynamics — *lived* — of his desire-relations, we must see that this alone introduces what we see produced: a *certain disinsertion, a certain floating*, *possibility of oscillations* in what is precisely the *mirror-relation to the other*.

This is why Lacan's little model exists. They have managed to conceive how the *oscillation of the incidence of his rapport with the other* varies, makes shimmer, completes and de-completes, oscillates in all ways the image that is to be glimpsed precisely in *that completeness to which the subject has never access* — for the simple reason the model lets them imagine: *the apparatus is badly made*.

For the subject truly to recognize *all the stages of his desire*, all the objects that have come to bring this image consistency, nourishment, sufficient incarnation — for the subject to constitute, by a series of *re-takings and successive identifications, the history of his ego*.

In this *floating spoken rapport with the analyst*, something tends to reproduce what is not produced in any other experience: variations sufficiently repeated though *infinitesimal*, sufficiently ample though sometimes limited, for the subject to perceive much more than he perceives in other circumstances — *this series of re-takings of possession of those captatory images at the foundation of the constitution of his ego*.

Lacan spoke of *small oscillations*, of *limitation in these oscillations*. He need not extend on what constitutes their smallness and limitation. There is *braking, arrests*. All that technique teaches us *to cross, fill, even sometimes reconstruct* (as Freud indicated).

But what you must begin to glimpse is *why is produced, by such a technique, something that — to whatever degree, however little — is in the subject to be reconstructed*: this *relation of imaginary mirage with himself, beyond all the limits daily life permits him to obtain*, tends to create artificially and as mirage what is precisely the fundamental condition of all *Verliebtheit*.

In other words: precisely because the *real image* — perceivable from where the subject is only in the mirror, always more or less blurred, sharp only at certain points — but where precisely the *rupture of the moorings of speech* lets him see at least successively the diverse parts of this image — to obtain what may be called what is at stake.

\[Lacan returns to the function of the *Verliebtheit* in transference. In ordinary love, the love-object meets us by *encounter* — the chance encounter that matches certain determining conditions of the subject's history. In analytic transference, the analyst is *automatically* invested as Idealich — as *image of mastery* — by the structural conditions of the analytic situation itself. The subject, set adrift from the social moorings of speech, addresses himself to one whose silence and *attention from the symbolic position* invites the projection. The analyst becomes the *empty form* in which the subject's repressed identifications, his *captive images*, can come to be visualized successively. The mirror-apparatus tilts and reorients, and the subject sees fragments of his *Idealich* he had never seen before. The labor of analysis is to ensure that these glimpses, instead of fixing themselves in a new imaginary capture (the analyst as new *idol*), become *verbalized* — assumed in symbolic speech, named, integrated into the *history of the subject*. This is what Lacan means by "full speech" — speech in which the subject's desire, *previously caught in the mute imaginary*, becomes *recognized and named*. The end of analysis would be the moment when the subject can speak this desire in his own name — having dissolved the imaginary capture by passing through *symbolic recognition*.\]

The session is thus a transitional one: between the elaboration of the imaginary structure (Sessions 8–14) and the elaboration of *what specifically the analytic technique does* with this structure (the sessions to come). The schema with its two points 0 and 0' has done its work; the eye, the mirror's inclination, the cone of vision — all serve now to bring out what is *operative* in the analyst's *position* — namely, the symbolic place from which the mirror is *inclined* in such a way that the real image can be seen.

---

## **Session of 19 May 1954**

As we advance in this year, taking the slope of its decline, it is a satisfaction to have heard — by echoes and questions posed — that a number of you begin to understand that what Lacan is teaching concerns the *Whole* of psychoanalysis: the very sense of your action.

Those of whom Lacan speaks have understood that the conception of *the sense of analysis* is the point from which alone every technical rule can depart. Every application depends on the dimension in which you apprehend it, *the dimension in which you yourself move*, so as to understand the *subject* of your action.

In what Lacan is spelling out, all does not yet appear absolutely clear, transparent. But it seems a question of nothing less than a *fundamental taking of position* on certain points of view that will animate their action, their intervention — in their comprehension of the *existential place of analytic experience* and equally of its *ends* — what one seeks to obtain by this action.

Last time, though he had not the feeling of making them take a great step, he placed them at a certain central point, to make them understand at least *a sort of play*. Lacan must give them an *imaged materialization* of something that remains enigmatic in the way it is made to intervene in analysis — what one calls in English *working through*, difficultly translated into French as *élaboration*, *travail*. That dimension which can appear at first mysterious, making us return *cent fois sur le métier* with our patient before a certain progress, *passage* (essential, subjective) is accomplished.

If something can express itself in the *mill-movement* expressed by the two arrows — from 0 to 0' and from 0' to 0 — manifesting the play of *going-and-coming*, *miroitement* by which there passes successively from this side to beyond the mirror an image of the subject as regards (a) his *completion in the course of analysis* and (b) his *desire* — the subject reintegrates it, sees it manifest, surge in himself in the form of *tension*, particularly acute each time a new step is made in the completion of this image. The movement does not stop at a single revolution but at as many revolutions as necessary for the different phases of *imaginary, narcissistic, specular identification* (these three words equivalent in representing things theoretically) — for the image to be realized, well seen, detached.

Lacan has not said the phenomenon exhausts itself there. Nothing is conceivable without the intervention of that *third element* introduced at the end of last session: the *conjunction of the subject's speech*. Not just any conjunction, but the conjunction at this *significant moment of emergence of desire* in confrontation with the image — that is, the emergence of desire *in general*, in a particularly *anguishing* form in those moments of *completion of the image*. Not without reason the image had been *de-completed*, the imaginary face *non-integrated*, *repressed*, or *suppressed*.

It is in the *conjunction of the speech with this desire* — at the moment desire is felt by the subject (it cannot be felt without this conjunction of speech, *and is pure anguish and nothing else*) — that is found the *fecund moment, the fecund point* of all that authors like Strachey have tried to specify.

Strachey tried to specify, name, delimit with precision what he calls *the transference interpretation*, more precisely *the mutative interpretation*. Strachey emphasizes that it is at a particularly defined, *limited moment* that interpretation can have the value of *progress, change* — that it is something *decisive in analysis*. Occasions present themselves neither frequently nor approximately. Not around, nor before, nor after — but *precisely at a moment when what is about to hatch, to surge in the imaginary, is at the same time there in the analysis, in the verbal relation with the analyst*. It is on this precise moment that, the interpretation being given, its decisive value, its *mutative function* can exercise itself.

What does this mean if not what Lacan has been explaining: at the moment when, in presence of a situation where *the imaginary and the real of the analytic situation conjoin themselves*, the desire of the subject is there — at once *present and inexpressible*.

A certain support of the analysis by *nomination, the denominating intervention* of what is in the situation itself, brings — according to Strachey — the *articulation, the essential pivot* by which and to which the analyst's intervention must limit itself. The only *truly fecund point* where his speech has to be added to that fomented by the patient in this long monologue, this kind of *mill of words*, where the kind of *turning presentation* (see the arrows of the schema) would well justify the metaphor.

To illustrate, Lacan recalled last time the function of Freud's interpretations in the case of *Dora*, including the *inadequate character* and the resulting *stoppage*, in a mental wall corresponding to a first time only of the analysis.

Have any of you, two years ago, attended Lacan's commentary on *The Wolf Man*? He hopes so. Few. He would like one of them — Père Beirnaert — to amuse himself rereading *The Wolf Man* for next time. You would see how Freud's whole discussion of this observation centers on the elements of *infantile neurosis* (the title in the German edition); you would see how this schema is truly explanatory, fundamental.

*The Wolf Man* is what would today be called a *character neurosis*, or *narcissistic neurosis*. As such it offers great resistance to treatment. Freud chose deliberately to present a part: the man is twenty-five at the time of analysis. Freud chose to express the *infantile neurosis* because it was of great utility for posing certain questions axial to Freud's exposé: the *value of trauma*. The theory of the function of trauma.

This is 1913 — at the heart of the period of Freud's thought, the *1910–1920 ensemble* their commentary on the *Papers on Technique* must inscribe itself in. *The Wolf Man* is indispensable to understanding what Freud elaborates of technique at this moment: the *theory of trauma*, put into question, shaken by Jung's obstinacy and remarks.

What do we see in this observation? Lacan recalls a salient trait: Freud here brings *complements* of his theory — parts of the theoretical conception of repression absolutely essential. In this text it is expressly formulated, repeated, in the most precise way that *the repression* (in *The Wolf Man* linked to the traumatic experience of *the vision, the spectacle of a copulation* — reconstructed by Freud, since never directly evoked, remembered by the patient — between his parents, in a position reconstituted from consequences in his behavior to have been a *coitus a tergo*) — the history of the subject (and it is of *history* it is a question, even of *patient historical reconstruction*) approaches monuments, archive-documents, all elements of the *critique and exegesis of texts*. The principle: if an element appears at some point in more elaborated form, the less elaborated giving an element is *anterior*.

Freud situates without equivocation, with absolutely rigorous conviction, at a date defined by *n \+ 1/2 year* the event. *n* cannot exceed 1, since at two and a half it cannot be (for certain reasons that follow from the consequences of this *spectacular revelation* on the young subject). Freud excludes 6 months — though Lacan would rather lean toward 6 months than 1 1/2 years; he may say later why.

What Freud specifies: the *traumatic value of the imaginary effraction* produced by this spectacle is *not at all to be situated immediately after the event*. It is at the moment when, between 3 years 3 months (where something acts as *capital turning* in the subject's history) and 4 years (a date we have because he was born — decisive coincidence — on Christmas Day, since it is in the *expectation of Christmas events*, accompanied for him as for all children by the bringing of gifts supposedly from a descending being) that the subject first has the *anguish-dream* which is the *pivot, the center* of the whole analysis.

This anguish-dream is for us the first manifestation of the traumatic value of what Lacan called the *imaginary effraction*. To borrow from instinct-theory (today more developed than in Freud's time, especially for birds, cf. Lorenz), the *Prägung* — stamp, impression, imprint, with resonances of the *stamping of a coin* — the *Prägung* of the originative traumatic event.

It is insofar as this *Prägung* — first situated in something not yet theoretically nameable, an unrepressed unconscious (not integrated in any way into the verbalized system of the subject, not even risen to verbalization, not even to signification) — insofar as this *Prägung*, *strictly limited to the imaginary*, *resurges by and during the progress of the subject in a more and more organized symbolic world*.

Freud explains by recounting the subject's history between this *moment x*, originary, and the moment of 4 years where he situates the repression. The repression can only have occasion to take place insofar as the events of these precocious years are historically sufficiently *eventful*: — *seduction by the elder sister*, more virile than him, manifest object of rivalry and identification; — *his retreat and refusal* before this seduction, of which at this precocious age the subject has neither the springs nor the elements; — the attempt of *active seduction* on his part, in the sense of a primary Oedipal genital evolution (normatively directed), followed by the *refusal, the rejection-movement of the governess Nania*, a drama for him; — appearance of the *first castration-threat* at the same time.

So an entry into Oedipal dialectic, but *falsified by the first captivating seduction of the sister*. He is pushed back from the terrain he engages toward *sado-masochistic positions*, whose register Freud gives.

It is *insofar as the subject, waiting to integrate himself into a symbolic world* (which never ceases exercising its directing attraction throughout his subsequent development — later there will be moments of happy solution, precisely insofar as *educational figures* will intervene: such-and-such a professor or, earlier, the introduction of *the whole religious register*, will have an influence Freud shows is properly insofar as his drama is *integrated into a myth with extended human or universal value*). *It is by introduction into symbolic dialectic that all favorable issues can be hoped for*.

What happens at this moment between 3 years 1 month and 4 years can be assimilated to this schema, and to the *process of analysis*. Namely: — insofar as the subject *learns to integrate the events of his life into a law, a field of symbolic significations, a universalizing human field of significations*, what makes an *infantile neurosis* at its beginnings is *exactly the same thing as a psychoanalysis* (at least at this date); — insofar as it plays the same role as analysis — namely *reintegration of the past*, *putting into function in the play of symbols of the Prägung itself* (only reached at the limit by a *retroactive play* — *nachträglich*, Freud writes strictly) — insofar as the Prägung is *by the play of events integrated in symbol-form, in history*, by the subject, it comes very close to surging; — from the very fact of the particularly shaking form of this first symbolic integration, it does surge — taking on *après coup, nachträglich, exactly* (according to Freud's theory) 2 1/2 years after (or perhaps 3 1/2 years, according to what Lacan said about the 6-month hypothesis) its intervention in the subject's imaginary life — *its value of trauma, in the sense that trauma has a repressive action*.

At this moment something *detaches itself* from the subject in the very symbolic world he is integrating, becoming: — *something that is no longer of the subject*; — something the subject no longer speaks, no longer integrates, but which nevertheless *remains there, somewhere*; — something *that will remain spoken — spoken by something the subject no longer has integration or mastery of* — and which will be the *first nucleus* of what we will call *his symptoms*.

In other words: between the moment of analysis Lacan has described and the intermediate moment between the *stamp* and the *symbolic repression*, *there is no essential difference*. There is only one: at the intermediate moment, *no one is there to give the word*. Hence *repression begins*, having constituted its first nucleus — and so a central point around which successive symptoms and repressions can organize themselves. And, since *repression and return of the repressed are the same thing*, also the *return of the repressed*.

Does it not astonish you, Perrier, that *repression and return of the repressed are the same thing*?

Perrier: *Oh — nothing astonishes me any more*. Lacan: there are people whom it does astonish.

Mannoni: this eliminates the notion sometimes found of *successful repression*?

Lacan: no, it does not eliminate it. To explain, one would have to enter the whole *dialectic of forgetting*. *Every successful symbolic integration involves* — though this would take us far from Freudian dialectic — *a kind of normal forgetting*.

Mannoni: but without return of the repressed?

Lacan: yes, without return of the repressed. *Integration into history involves the forgetting of a whole world of shadows that are not carried to symbolic existence*. And if this symbolic existence is fully successful, *assumable* by the subject, *without leaving any weight behind* — we fall into Heideggerian notions: in every passage, every entry of being into its habitation of speech, there is a *margin of forgetting, a λήθη complementary to all ἀλήθεια*.

Hyppolite: *forgetting is not nothing. It is itself contained in symbolic expression*. Lacan: exactly.

Hyppolite: it is the word *successful* in Mannoni's formula he does not understand. What does *successful* mean?

Lacan: it is a *therapist's expression*. It is an *absolutely essential lêthê*.

Hyppolite: because *successful* could mean precisely *the most fundamental forgetting*. Lacan: that is what he speaks of, on condition we give *fundamental* the sense Hyppolite says.

Hyppolite: *successful* means then, in certain respects, *what is most missed*: you have managed to *integrate being*. For this, the essential had to be forgotten. *This success is a failure*.

Lacan: he is not sure this is what Heidegger means by the *Irre* (errance) fundamental to all *temporal incarnation* of being.

Hyppolite: this is another question for Heidegger. He would not accept the word *successful*: it can only be a therapist's view.

Lacan: yes — but this *margin of error* in every realization of being is reserved by Heidegger to a kind of fundamental *Verborgenheit* (concealment), *shadow of truth*.

Hyppolite: *the therapist's success is for Heidegger the worst — the forgetting of forgetting*. Heideggerian authenticity is precisely not to *sink into the forgetting of forgetting*.

Lacan: Heidegger has made a kind of *philosophical law* of this *return to the sources of being*. They leave the question in suspense. If Lacan introduces it here — and if he did not pass by Mannoni's intervention — it is because they will have to ask: — in what measure can a *forgetting of forgetting* be *successful*? — in what measure must every analysis lead to this *return into being* — or onto a certain *retreat in being*, taken by the subject relative to his own destiny?

In other words — Lacan catches the ball in flight — does the subject, starting from 0 (the *point of confusion and innocence at the start*), through the dialectic of *symbolic reintegration of desire* coming from C, *where is this going to lead?* Or does it suffice that the subject *nominate his desires*, simply *have permission to name them*, for the analysis to be terminated and finished?

This is the question Lacan will pose at the end of the session. He does not stop there. At the end, after a certain number of completed circuits permitting the *complete reintegration of his history*, will the subject still be at 0, or rather toward A? *Does something of the subject remain at the level of the engluement-point we call his ego*?

Has the analysis purely and simply to do with what one *seems to consider* a kind of given — the subject's *ego* — as if it were a purely internal structure one could *perfect by exercise*? Lacan will show this is what Balint and a whole tendency in analysis come to think — *either the ego is strong, or it is weak*, and this ambiguity persists. *If weak*, they are led by an internal logic to think *it must be reinforced*.

From the moment one thinks the ego, without further complement, is purely and simply this *exercise of mastery by the subject of himself*, somewhere in his interior — from the moment one maintains the notion of the ego as a *power of mastery given, somewhere at the summit of the hierarchy of nervous functions* — one engages straight in this path: *what is at stake is teaching it to be strong*. One enters the notion of an *education by exercise*, *learning*, even — as Balint writes — *performance*.

Apropos of this *reinforcement of the ego*, Balint goes so far as to remark how *perfectible* the ego is: years ago what was a world record in some sport is now just necessary to qualify as average athlete. Something happens around which the human ego, in self-competition, attains *more and more extraordinary performances*. From which one is led to deduce... — *no proof at all, and for cause*. In what way would an exercise like analysis structure the ego, introduce ego-functions? An apprenticeship would be only — when one speaks of *strength or weakness* of the ego in analysis — *to make it capable of tolerating a greater sum of excitation*?

In what way could analysis (in itself a *verbal play*) serve any apprenticeship of this kind? *If we do not see this*, if we blind ourselves to the fundamental fact analysis brings — that *the ego is an imaginary function* — this is the whole difference between the path almost all analysis engages in today and what Lacan teaches: the *radical difference* between a certain conception of the ego and the conception of the ego as *imaginary function*, whose form, springs, faces, and stages Lacan shows.

This is why, from the moment we consider the ego as imaginary function, *it is far from being confused with the subject*; *it is not confused with the subject from the start*. What do we call a *subject*? Precisely what, in the development of *objectivation*, is *outside the object*. The ideal of all science (within limits) is to reduce the object to something that can close in a *system of interactions of forces*, where the object is only *an object for science*.

There is only one subject: *the scientist who looks at the ensemble* and hopes one day to reduce all to a determined play of *symbols* enveloping all interactions between objects. He is forced, in a certain domain, to imply there is *something that escapes*, namely *action* — when it is a question of an *organized being*, one can consider it from both angles, but as long as one speaks of it and maintains its *organism-value*, one *introduces the notion that it is a subject*.

One can, during the whole development of analysis, eliminate from an instinctual behavior the subjective position. But there is a domain where it is absolutely *not negligible*: the domain of the *speaking subject*. Why? *Because the speaking subject as such we must admit as subject for one simple reason: he is capable of lying* — *he is distinct from what he says*. This dimension of the speaking subject — and of the *speaking subject as deceiver* — *is what Freud uncovers for us in the unconscious*.

\[Lacan now returns to the *Wolf Man* and the structure of trauma to deepen the point.\] The whole point is that the *infantile neurosis* and *the analysis* perform structurally homologous operations — both function as *symbolic reintegrations* of the *Prägung*. The difference between the spontaneous infantile neurosis (in which, no one being there to *give the word*, the *Prägung* becomes the *nucleus of repression*) and analysis (in which the analyst gives the word at the right moment, allowing the *Prägung* to integrate fully into the *symbolic history of the subject*) is the entire difference between the *forming of a symptom* and *its dissolution*.

This is why Strachey's *mutative interpretation* operates at exactly the moment when the *imaginary and the real of the analytic situation conjoin*: when the *Prägung* — having traveled its circuit, having been re-actualized in the imaginary transference relation — is *present and inexpressible*, on the brink of integrating into the symbolic. The analyst's *naming* at this moment is what permits the *passage* from the imaginary to the symbolic — *from the engluement of the ego at 0 to the reintegration in A*, the symbolic position.

The analyst, having occupied the position of *Idealich* in the imaginary capture, must — *at the right moment* — take the position of *Ich-Ideal*, the *symbolic position of recognition*. Strachey's *parasitic superego* (developed in the analytic relation in opposition to the original repressive superego) becomes, in Lacan's reading, the *symbolic Ich-Ideal* substituted at the right moment for the imaginary capture.

This is also why the *end of analysis* cannot be the simple permission to *nominate desires* — for then the subject would remain at the level of the ego, *engluement* in the imaginary. The end must rather be a movement of *retreat in being* — what Hyppolite, with Heidegger, identifies as the *forgetting of forgetting* — but assumed differently from the therapist's perspective. *The end of analysis must include the assumption of the subject's own destination in the symbolic*.

Lacan promises to develop these themes — through Balint, through Strachey, through *The Wolf Man* — in the sessions to come.

---

## **Session of 26 May 1954**

\[Session 17 picks up with the discussion of the subject, the ego, and the *Wolf Man* schema; introduces the *superego* as scission in the symbolic order; presents Lacan's Islamic patient as illustration of the Law and the symbolic; turns to Granoff's presentation of Balint's *Primary love and psychoanalytic technique*.\]

In the science as currently practiced, the subject ends up retained only on the plane of *consciousness*: at bottom, the subject is *the scientist who possesses in himself the system of science*, and there the subject maintains the *dimension* — he is subject insofar as he is the *reflection, the mirror, the support* of all that is of the objectal world.

But from the moment Freud shows that in the human subject not only is there *something that speaks*, but speaks in the full sense of the word *to speak* — *there is something that lies, knowingly and outside the contribution of consciousness* — then there is, in the evident, imposed, experimental sense of the term, the *reintegration of the dimension of the subject*.

But this dimension of the subject *is no longer confused with the ego*. The ego is *fallen from its absolute position in the subject*: the ego is a *mirage, like the rest*, an element of *the objectal relations of the subject*.

It is precisely for this reason that Lacan emphasized Mannoni's introduction: the question is whether what is at stake in analysis is only an *enlargement of objectivations correlative to an ego considered as something given* — a "more or less restricted center" (Anna Freud's term, the exact sense of *eingeengt*) — which would be a question of *enlarging*?

When Freud writes *Wo Es war, soll Ich werden* ("Where it was, the ego must be"), must we take this sentence in the sense of this *enlargement of the field of consciousness*? Or is it a *displacement*? Where the It was — for it was in many places. In Lacan's schema, the subject looks at the play of the mirror at A; for a moment let us identify the subject with the It, and say the It *was at A*, that *where the It was, at A, the ego must be*.

The ego has *displaced itself*. At the end of an ideal analysis, *it must no longer be there at all* — which is quite conceivable, since *all that is there must be realized there*, in what the subject *recognizes of himself*.

You follow Mannoni? Mannoni who posed the question follows — already something.

In any case, with the *Wolf Man* remark, you see the utility of such a schema: it *unifies*, conforming to the best analytic tradition, the *originary formation of the symptom*, the *signification of repression itself*, with *what happens in the analytic movement* — itself considered as *dialectical process*.

Lacan leaves to *R. P. Beirnaert* the task of taking his time to reread the *Wolf Man* observation and one day make a small summary, also bringing to value certain questions. What Lacan wants for the moment is to advance on certain questions linked to what the schema essentially aims at: *the comprehension of what is the therapeutic procedure*, the *spring of therapeutic action*. Precisely where Lacan has situated the question: *what does it mean, this nomination, this recognition of desire, at the point where it has arrived, at 0?*

Must everything stop here? Or is a step beyond demandable?

It is clear that everyone has long remarked that the analyst occupies a certain position relative to an *absolutely essential function*: the *symbolic integration by the subject of his history*. This function we have called *the superego*. It appeared first in Freudian theory under the form of the *censorship*. From the origin we are — apropos symptom, apropos all unconscious functions in everyday life — in the dimension of *speech*. If the censorship is exercised, it is precisely in the absolutely essential end of *lying*, of *deceiving by mission*. Freud did not for nothing choose the term *censorship*: this notion of an agency that *splits, cuts in two* — into an accessible, recognized part, and an inaccessible, forbidden part — *the symbolic world of the subject*.

This same notion we retrieve, scarcely evolved, transformed, with changed accent, under the register of the *superego*. Impossible to understand the notion of superego without referring to its origins. Lacan emphasizes immediately the opposition between this notion of superego (one of its faces) and the common one.

Commonly the superego is always thought in the register of *tension*, even reduced to purely instinctual references (primordial masochism, for example). Freud goes further, in *The Ego and the Id*: striking — *the more the subject represses his instincts, the more in some register one could consider his conduct "moral," the more the superego exaggerates its pressure*, becomes severe, imperious, demanding. A clinical observation not universally true.

If Freud is carried away by his object (neurosis) and goes to consider the superego as something like *toxic products* produced by vital activity, putting an end to the cycle of reproduction in given conditions — it is interesting because it is implicit in a whole *latent conception* reigning in analysis on the superego — there is yet *something else* to formulate in opposition: *the superego is properly of the domain of the unconscious, a scission of the symbolic system integrated by the subject as the formation of the totality defining the subject's history*.

The unconscious is a *scission, limitation, alienation by the symbolic system for the subject* insofar as it has value for him; the *superego is something analogous*. Where? *Also in the symbolic world, but not limited solely to the subject*. The subject's symbolic world is realized in a *language* — the common language, the *universal symbolic system* in its empire on a certain community to which the subject belongs.

The superego is precisely this *scission* insofar as it is produced — *not only for the subject in his relations* with what we will call *the Law*. Lacan will illustrate with a patient.

This patient — already analyzed by another before referring to Lacan — had singular symptoms in the *domain of activities of the hand* (significant organ for diverting activities on which analysis has thrown vivid light). An analysis along classical line had striven without success to organize the symptoms around the history of *infantile masturbation* and the *interdictive, repressive activities* this would have entailed. These existed, of course — they always do. Unhappily it had explained, resolved, understood nothing.

The subject was of *Islamic religion* (delicate to report particulars, but inseparable here). A striking element of his subjective development was the *aversion, distance, indifference* manifested toward what is, in this culture, an essential register of individuals: *Quranic Law*, something *infinitely more total* than we can suppose in our cultural area defined by *Render unto Caesar what is Caesar's, and unto God what is God's*. Absolutely not on these bases that things are instituted in the Islamic area, where on the contrary the Law has a *totalitarian character* not permitting one to define, discern, isolate juridical from religious plane.

Whence a kind of *méconnaissance of Quranic Law* in this subject belonging by ancestry, functions, future to this cultural area. Striking — in function of the idea (Lacan thinks sound) that *one cannot misrecognize the symbolic belongings of a subject*.

Quranic Law states: *concerning one guilty of theft, the hand shall be cut*. In a particularity of this patient's history, the subject had been caught during his childhood in a private and public whirlwind — his father a civil servant who had lost his place. The subject heard say that *his father was a thief, that he must have his hand cut*. Of course this Quranic prescription is no longer executed (no more than the Manu Code's "He who has committed incest with his mother shall tear out his testicles and, carrying them in his hand, go off toward the West"). But it remains in the *order of symbolic foundation of interhuman relations* called *the Law*.

Precisely insofar as for this subject this part of the Law was *isolated from the rest in a privileged, fundamental way*, passing into his symptoms — and for him too the world of his symbolic references, the *primitive arcana* around which a defined subject's most fundamental relations to the universe of the symbol organize themselves, the rest having been struck with this *kind of degradation* by reason of the wholly individual prevalence this prescription took on for him — this constitutes the *unconscious symptomatic expressions in him*, linked to the originally *conflictual* character of this childhood experience.

What does this mean? Just as Lacan represents in the progress of analysis that the revolution of symptoms is around approaches to these *traumatic elements* — founded in an image *never integrated* (the points, holes, fracture-points in the *unification, synthesis of the subject's history*, that by which he can regroup himself in the symbolic determinations making of him a *subject with a history*) — likewise *also in the relation to something vaster*, absolutely fundamental for every human being's existence: *the Law to which he attaches*, in which is situated all that can happen to him personally — *which unifies his history insofar as he says himself such-and-such of these backgrounds structuring and founding a determined symbolic universe*. Not the same for everyone.

Here intervene, by the intermediary of *tradition and language*, *symbolic diversifications* in the subject's reference. Insofar as *something in the Law is discordant, ignored, must be abolished* — or *on the contrary promoted to the first plane by a traumatic event* in the subject's history — *the Law simplifies itself into a kind of point that becomes inadmissible, unintegrable* — *the blind, repetitive something we usually define by the term superego*.

This small observation should be striking enough to give the idea of a *dimension in which our reflection does not often go* but which is indispensable to understanding something not ignored in analysis: this *dimension of the Law*. We cannot suppress it: all analysts witness, no resolution of analysis is possible, whatever the diversity of *archaic events* it brings into play, *if all does not finally come to be knotted around a grasp essentially dominated by this legal, legalizing coordinate called the Oedipus complex*.

This is so essential to analytic experience that it appears from the start of Freud's work — the preeminence in the edifice, as system of coordinates, of *Oedipus*. Maintained to the end of his work. Which is to say: the Oedipus complex occupies a *privileged position in the current state of our culture*, in the extremely complex state of Western civilization where man is placed in presence of an evolution of tradition, a situation of the individual relative to *plural*.

Lacan has alluded to the *division in several planes* of the register of the Law in our cultural area. The multiplicity of planes does not make life easier for the individual: we find ourselves ceaselessly in presence of *conflicts between these different registers*. What is now most frequent, most dominant, is the strictly Freudian theory, rooted in the most ancient, fundamental form.

As a civilization evolves in the complexity of its different languages, its *point of attachment with the more primitive forms of the Law* becomes this *essential but extremely reduced* point: the *Oedipus complex*. What is put forward by the expression of neuroses is this *resonance in individual life of the register of the Law*.

This is not to say that, because it is the most constant point of intersection (the minimum requirable), it is the only one and outside the field of psychoanalysis. That we should be discharged from all that concerns the *recognition of the subject's desire* produced at 0 with the ensemble of the symbolic system in which the subject is *called*, in the full sense, *to take his place*.

If we forget this, we may encounter — as in this clinical case — what one may call *a pure and simple misrecognition* of what is at stake in the subject's history. The fact that the Oedipus complex is always demandable does not dispense from realizing that *other things of the same level — on the plane of the Law — may play in a determined case a role just as decisive*.

Once the requisite number of revolutions in the subject's *imaginary history* is made — the successive nomination of what is, in presence of this image, the *reintegration of equally successive desires, tensional, suspended, anguishing* — *this is not yet accomplished*.

What has been first at 0, then here at 0', then returns to 0, must be carried back where it came from — *speech emerged from the silence of the analyst* — into the *completed system of symbols*, insofar as the issue of analysis demands it. Where must it stop? Must we push our analytic intervention into *fundamental dialogues on justice and courage* of the great dialectical tradition?

A question not easy to resolve, for contemporary man has become *singularly incapable* of approaching such great themes. He prefers to resolve things in terms of *conduct, adaptation, group-morality* and similar bilks. *But this poses a grave question — that of the human formation of the analyst*.

\[The session ends. The following session, dated 26 May 1954, opens with Granoff's promised exposition of Balint's *Primary Love and Psychoanalytic Technique*, which Lacan asked him to take in hand. Lacan introduces it:\] We will orient ourselves under the form already announced for two seminars: trying to understand, within the theoretical comprehension this or that analyst has formulated of his experience, certain points of view that give us an idea of the *way he directs his experience*. For if it is fine to say *theory and technique are the same thing*, let us profit from this. Let us try to understand each one's *technique*, when his theoretical ideas are sufficiently articulated to permit us at least to *presume something*.

Of course it is not applicable to everyone — many of those who push forward theoretical ideas, even "fine spirits," are not for that usable, for *those who manipulate theoretical concepts do not always know what they say*. But in some cases one feels vividly that *they express something quite direct in the experience*.

This is the case of *Balint*, Lacan thinks. He preferred to choose the support of someone *close, even sympathetic* in many sides, *manifestly oriented toward convergences with our requirements* of what intersubjective relation in analysis must be — but at the same time the way he expresses it gives the feeling he *undergoes* something. Interesting precisely *not where the deviations are gross, apparent, even delirious*, but *where they are subtle, where they mask less a radical aberration than a certain way of missing the goal*. Lacan wished to test the import of teaching — that one follow it. He trusts Granoff, one of those most interested, oriented by the path Lacan tries to lead them. Granoff has been asked to communicate what he gathered from Balint's *Primary love and psychoanalytic technique* — a collection of articles extending approximately \[over the past two decades\].

\[Granoff begins; Lacan punctuates. The Balint book — its central theses on *primary love* as the patient's persistent need to be the object of another's love, care, affection without consideration for the object's needs; the analyst as ideal love-object; the analytic situation as recreating the conditions of primary love; the *new beginning* at the end of analysis; the satisfaction-frustration dialectic. Lacan's intervention emphasizes: Balint articulates clearly what others muddle, and in doing so he reveals the *deviation*: placing the whole play of analysis on a single tendency — primary love — without correctives, without other elements, is to *miss precisely the symbolic dimension*. Balint represents the *terminal point of a certain evolution of analytic technique* — placing more and more accent on *relations of dependence*, on *instinctual satisfactions, frustration* (the same thing). The end of analysis described as a state of *unbridled exaltation of desires* in the subject, a kind of *intoxicated sensation of mastery of reality* — *still illusory*, from which the subject must free himself in the *post-terminal period* by *progressive replacement in the nature of things*. The last session at which both partners cry. *Honest testimony — but precious in showing the dead-end of an approach that treats the ego as a power of mastery to be reinforced rather than an imaginary function to be dissolved by symbolic integration*.\]

\[Lacan develops: the question of *primary love* presupposes precisely what is in question — that the *love-relation is between a subject and an object*. But the love-relation is structured by *imaginary capture* (Idealich) and *symbolic recognition* (Ich-Ideal). To reduce it to *primary love* is to remain in the imaginary, in the *captation-relation*, missing the *symbolic mediation* by which alone the love-relation can be properly *recognized* and the subject can take his place in his own history. Balint's *new beginning* is then nothing but a *new imaginary fixation* on a new ideal object — the analyst — instead of a *re-entry of the subject into the symbolic order* and a *recognition* of his desire. The crying at the last session is *the symptom of this failure*: the partners weep because the imaginary capture is being broken, but no symbolic resolution has been attained. This is why such analyses, *correctly conducted by Balint's criteria*, are nevertheless *incomplete*. The Wolf Man and Dora cases — well or badly handled by Freud — point in a different direction: the analyst's intervention must be a *symbolic naming* at the right moment, not the cultivation of a love-relation toward the analyst.\]

---

## **Session of 2 June 1954**

### **Granoff continues presenting Balint**

Granoff continues his exposition of Balint. He reads a passage where Balint, noting the *vocabulary-shift* in analytic writing since around 1938-1940, observes that the term *sadistic* is almost no longer used: its connotation was *too libidinal*, too tied to instinctual aims and gratifications. In its place one finds the *aggressive, destructive*\-style terms — *unmistakable affinities with object-relations*.

Lacan: very just. You bring out the *enlisement* (bogging-down) of the terms in usage in articles from this period, orienting the analytic situation toward *object-relations*. Balint detects signs of it — including the disappearance of the whole vocabulary of *source, direction, satisfaction of the instinct*. The fact that *sadistic* is almost no longer used, with the admission that its connotation was *too libidinal*: a kind of *puritanization of the analytic atmosphere* — striking, worth highlighting as a convergent sign of a certain evolution. The phrase is significant.

Granoff: somewhat insolently — what Balint suffers from is a *trouble of the imaginary function*. Lacan: not him — his theory.

Granoff: Balint is caught in a kind of capture. Not astonishing that he puts forward propositions which become, in the next paragraph, somewhat astounding if one situates oneself in what is for him an *object-relation*. Balint says: now, attention — one must not forget what the analyst's *behavior* in the psychoanalytic situation consists of. He dispenses justice to what is taught in the well-thinking seminars, namely *that the analyst is there totally outside the coup* — not as he must really be, but as he still thinks he is. Balint shows him *empêtré* (entangled) in a *dual relation*, and *denying it, denying he is in it*.

Balint: *all these questions of friendly detachment, comprehension, well-rhythmed interpretations — must not make us forget that if the patient's relation to his analyst is libidinal, the analyst's relation to the patient is libidinal in the same manner*. But Balint does not stop there — does not even seem tempted to speak of *subject-to-subject relation*, even toward the end of his exposé. One of the *tours de force* he realizes incidentally, in *méconnaissance*.

Lacan: in truth, he does not succeed in avoiding it. He does not accede to it. We return to our starting point: there must be *something existing between two subjects*, since two subjects are there. Lacking completely the *conceptual apparatus* — even broadly elaborated elsewhere, the *true function of language* as mediation — to introduce the *intersubjective relation*, he is led to speak of *two bodies' psychology*. This is not a slip but corresponds to the idea he has of it. He thinks he is leaving *one body's psychology* by saying: we will do a *two bodies' psychology*. But *two bodies' psychology is still an opposition — still an object-to-object relation*.

This is the *ambiguity of the term object-relation*. Not grave theoretically if it did not have *technical consequences* in concrete therapeutic exchange. *It is not an object-to-object relation*. As Granoff so happily expressed: *entangled in a dual relation, and denying it*. Lacan congratulates the formula — it expresses how one usually expresses oneself in trying to explain analytic relation.

Granoff: an extremely promising phrase: *we do not realize what we miss by describing experiences with two bodies, two characters, analytic technique, in a language belonging to situations with one character*. Lacan: exactly what I said. And he doesn't realize he continues...

Granoff: not only continues but reinforces. *What is to be done*? Having no key out of the impasse, Balint says — and it becomes a *forced objectivation* of the patient: first, create an *atmosphere*, do not close, do not forget — oral avidity is connected only to the mouth but that is not true. It is *skin, epidermis, warmth, friction* — an enumeration. He literally goes around the individual to expand his position in the frame of *object-relations*. And, contrary to what he had said, he finally says: *and if this doesn't work, as we do, well — we put a double dose and it will end up working*. That is where he arrives.

Lacan: I would not qualify it in the same sense as you — in the *objectivating* sense. Granoff: he does not qualify it thus. Lacan: nor would I. I consider it as something *moving* — a kind of *recourse in appeal*, properly speaking, not at all to the *objectivating register* Lacan defends.

Every advance in knowledge and technique has interest in *objectivating what is objectivable*. But this is an *objectivating tendency in the relation to the subject*: pushing, by interventions, by the technique itself, *the subject to objectivate himself, to take himself for an object, to believe progress is made by an objectivation of what he is*. Not what is at stake.

This *recourse in appeal* is *a recourse to the real*, insofar as it is a question of an *effacement by méconnaissance* (as you said) of *the symbolic register*. Insofar as the symbolic disappears completely in the *object-relation* — it is nowhere — that is why the *objects take this absolute value*. He no longer has as third term *the imaginary function*.

He says: what is the *sense of our operative function* now in analysis? He says *create an atmosphere — a proper atmosphere*. That is all he has to say — extraordinarily uncertain, hesitates in the *unspeakable*, brings in then *all of reality*, what he calls *the event* — for analysis is precisely not made for us to throw ourselves on our patient's neck and vice versa.

The *limitation of the means* he has poses the problem of *in what plane it takes place*. But lacking conception (relative to these means) of *where his experience defines and limits itself*, he is led to make this great *appeal to the awakening of all registers of the real*. *This plane of the real* — not for nothing always in the background, never directly designated in what we comment here — *not for nothing precisely excluded*. Balint will not make it re-enter any more than another.

But that is *where his recourse in appeal carries*. The *failure of theory* corresponds to this *inclination, this deviation of technique*. Next time Lacan will try to permit them to mark exactly its direction and precise sense.

Granoff: this appeal to the real — Balint is so engaged in it that at another moment of his career, as if he had been a moment sensitive to certain ecueils (pitfalls) of his thought, he wants at least to say what, in his opinion, does *not* belong to it — and gives a unique description-example in the literature: the only time express mention is made of *little paper handkerchiefs, cushions, divans*, etc. At a much earlier moment.

Lacan: yes, in the article "Transference and Counter-Transference."

Granoff: perhaps not for nothing he thought of it. He ends on *pessimistic considerations on the termination of analysis*. He makes (rather pertinently) the *trial of our societies* — bringing in that at a historical moment (around 1930\) there entered a number of analysts whose analyses had evidently not been terminated. We have arrived at two *standards* — A and B — uncertainty of the judicious moment to launch an analyst in practice. Pessimistic since a treatment is terminated, in his estimate, in *one or two cases out of ten*. With some thousands of treatments per year terminating, that makes some hundreds truly terminating — one could take the pain to look more closely.

But what Granoff finds more pessimistic than this is his *theory*. He says: *I terminate rather rarely treatments — one or two cases out of ten*. Not in itself so pessimistic. But what is anguishing: *in the other cases, après coup, I have understood where the error was*. Yet not the most afflicting — but: *once I have understood, however well I understood, there is nothing more to do. It's fouled, once and for all*.

This seems the *culmination of his perspective*: the *inevitability of failure once a certain type of error has been made*. (No reversibility in his model — testimony to the fundamentally imaginary structure of his conception, in which a fixed object-relation, once badly cathected, cannot be re-symbolized.)

### **Lacan resumes**

Lacan: it is late. He will not exceed quarter-to-two. He believes Granoff can be given *a good mark* — he has realized the appeal, presented the ensemble of problems posed by Balint's book (which is in sum his unique book, resulting from his meditations and career). If a number of questions emerge for them, that is what is to be expected. Lacan will take them up next time, completing in the order Granoff introduced.

What Lacan wants now to highlight is from an article Granoff did not speak of: *Transference of Emotions*. Already our whole problem: *are it emotions that are transferred*? A title like this seems to scandalize no one. It is an article of 1933 — remarkable things, including how Balint introduces transference to non-analytic readers (since the article addresses also non-analysts).

Lacan will start there. Starting from this *hole left at the center of the exposé* on Balint, to re-illuminate the rest, to make non-prepared readers feel *what the phenomenon of transference is*. You will see to what extent — *from the fact that a just definition of the symbol is lacking* — *it (the symbol) is forced to be everywhere*. *An analyst cannot avail himself of it, from the fact that it is lacking*. Balint, in his exoteric expression, goes further than he thinks: he gives a *definition that has nothing to do with transference*. To introduce us to transference, he speaks of *displacement*. All examples he gives are examples of *displacement*.

In this same article, Balint says: the result of the work by which analysts interpret most often their experience and action is naturally a *psychology, or characterology, of the psychoanalyst himself*. So it is not Lacan saying it, but Balint himself. The author brings the testimony of analyzing under this angle, *to make a psychoanalysis of the analyst as theorist*, situating with rigor certain current tendencies of theory and technique.

This implies knowing *where we can anchor a theory of technique* permitting us to *escape this kind of relativization to object-relations* — to have a *system of references* leaving the *strictly individual interpsychology* of analyst and analysand.

Not new — exactly the sense of what they have been doing here since October and before. Lacan may take the occasion (and surely will) to re-specify the *fundamental points* of the theory he gives here of analysis — showing how it permits one to *escape this issueless relativization*.

\[The session marks the pivot: from the diagnostic phase (Balint as symptom of the entire deviation toward object-relations and the imaginary capture between analyst and patient as "two bodies") to the constructive phase that will occupy the remaining sessions: defining the *symbolic anchor* of analytic theory, the proper *function of speech and language* in technique, and the relation of the *Idealich/Ich-Ideal distinction* to the practice of interpretation. Granoff has correctly identified — without Balint himself being able to formulate it — that his theory suffers from a *trouble of the imaginary function*: it has effaced the symbolic and so cannot conceive transference except as a libidinal object-relation between two bodies. The whole structural framework Lacan has built — the optical schema with its two mirrors, points 0, 0', A, C — has been precisely to enable the *third term* (the symbolic) without which the imaginary capture between analyst and patient is inescapable. The lesson is methodological: *to read an analyst's theory is to read his technique* — for what is missing in the theory is missing in the practice. The whole evolution of analytic technique since 1920 toward *object-relations*, *ego-strength*, *primary love*, *frustration-gratification*, is the *symptom of a theoretical lacuna* — the lacuna of the symbolic. To remedy it is the entire project of Lacan's teaching this year and beyond.\]

---

## **Session of 9 June 1954**

\[Session 19 develops the *phenomenology of the perverse relation* (sadism, scoptophilia) drawing on Sartre's *Being and Nothingness* (Part II, the analysis of the *look*), to demonstrate that even what appears most purely libidinal — the perverse relation between two bodies — is structured by *intersubjectivity*, by the *consent* and *recognition* of the other. The session uses Sartre to show what is missing in Balint's *two bodies' psychology*: the *symbolic mediation* at the level of *intersubjectivity*.\]

Lacan resumes from last time: *primary love, dual relation, the two bodies' psychology* as Balint rigorously expresses it. He hopes they have understood the impasse one arrives at by making this *imaginary relation* — as essentially saturating harmonic of natural desire, a relation to an object of satisfaction — *the central notion of dual relation*.

To demonstrate, Lacan turned to the phenomenology of the perverse relation as such, accenting *sadism* and *scoptophilia*. He left aside (for the moment) the homosexual relation, which would warrant infinitely nuanced study in the same light.

The structure of *sadism* implies — and this is what is most often missed — that *what is handled in the relation between the two subjects is something that hooks the consent of the partner*. *Consent in the largest sense of acceptance*. It is a question of *consent, of freedom, of the avowal of humiliation* of the partner.

Proof: most clinical manifestations of sadism remain rather at the threshold of execution than push themselves to the extreme. In all sorts of elements one may qualify as *the waiting of the other, the fear of the other, the pressure, threat exercised, observation of the more or less secret forms* of what Lacan will call *the consent of the other, his participation in the play* — it is here it plays out.

You know how much, under the form of perversion, remains the largest part of the clinical sum — a *ludic execution*, involving in the subject *a correspondence not of a subject submitted to a need, but of a subject who participates in the mirage of the play by identification with the subject* (just as the subject identifies with the other in this play). *Intersubjectivity is the essential dimension*.

Lacan cannot fail to refer to the author who most magisterially described it: in pages forming part of a work that can philosophically fall under all sorts of critiques, but which (at least in this part of *phenomenology*) attains by talent and brio something specially convincing — *Sartre's Being and Nothingness*, second part, *the phenomenology of the apprehension of the other-as-knowledge*. Admirably brought out.

Sartre turns the demonstration around the fundamental phenomenon called *the look (le regard)*. The *human object*, originating particularly in the field of my human experience, distinguishes itself absolutely, originally, *ab initio*, from all my field — *not assimilable to any other perceivable object* — in that it is *an object that looks at me*.

Sartre places extremely fine accents: this *look* must not be confused with the fact of *seeing his eyes*. I can feel looked at by someone whose eyes I do not even see, *and not even his appearance*, but where something signifies he *might be there*. For example, this window — if it is a bit dark and I have reason to think someone is behind — *there is already a look*. As subject, I model myself; from the moment this look exists, *I am already something else*: in this relation to the other, I feel myself becoming, *for the look of the other, an object*.

But in this reciprocal position, *he too knows that I am an object that knows itself seen*. The whole phenomenology of *shame, modesty, prestige*, the particular *fear* engendered by the look of the other — admirably described. Lacan recommends Sartre's work — *an absolutely essential reading for an analyst*.

Especially at the point we have come to of forgetting this register of intersubjectivity, here in a form *literally woven into* a certain register of experience you must immediately recognize as the plane Lacan always teaches them to distinguish — the *plane of the imaginary*. Suspending ourselves on the plane of perverse manifestations, we obtain nuances *far from being confused* with what Lacan teaches them to put at the *pivot of symbolic relation*: namely *the plane of recognition*.

Lacan insists on certain characters of this relational zone made around the pivot of *the other as look*. Extremely ambiguous forms — Lacan did not for nothing speak of *shame*. If we went in the sense of *prestige*, finer analysis would show, if we maintain strictly the plane of the *sole action of the look*, *derisory forms* of prestige: the style manifested in children, this kind of *form of excitation*...

\[Lacan tells the bullfighting anecdote.\] A friend recounted an anecdote of the *joke* preceding the bull-runs in Spain, in which awkward people are made to participate. A scene extraordinarily beautiful: the crowd seized by a kind of *collective sadism* showing how far ambiguity goes.

A *half-idiot* was paraded — clothed in the most beautiful matador ornaments — before the entry of the little beasts (vacquillas, young cows) that participate in such plays. The crowd cries out: *but he is so handsome*\! The character enters a kind of *panic*, beginning to demur. His comrades say: *go on — see, everyone wants you*. The whole world takes part. Panic increases — he refuses, wants to dodge. They push him out of the barriers.

Finally the *swing, the tilt* (bascule) produces itself, absolutely totally. The character suddenly *disengages* from those who push him into play, and according to this *crushing insistence of the people's clamors*, he pushes everyone away and substitutes himself in this kind of *buffoon-hero* — implicated in the structure of the situation, going with all characteristics of the *sacrificial attitude* (though still on the plane of buffoonery) toward the beast, and is immediately and radically *spread out on the ground*. He is carried away.

A sensational scene defining the *ambiguous zone* of this relation where *intersubjectivity is essential*. The *symbolic element* — the pressure of the clamor — plays an essential role, in some sense *quasi-annulled* by the mass-phenomenon character. The ensemble is brought back to the level of *intersubjectivity* Lacan defines as the plane of manifestations *provisionally connoted as perverse*.

But we can go further. Sartre — Lacan advanced his author not for nothing, for Sartre's analysis goes far — gives a structuration of the *phenomenology of the amorous relation* that seems absolutely irrefutable. Lacan cannot redo it entirely (one would have to pass through all the phases of the dialectic of *for-itself* and *in-itself*). One must take some pain and refer to the author.

Sartre's level, where he tries to grip the dialectic of love in its acute, concrete form — he very justly notes: *what we demand from the object of whom we desire to be loved is not simply that he yield to us, that he give in to us, that he accept our domination, but on the contrary that, by an act of his absolute freedom, he should freely choose us, freely yield to us*.

\[Lacan develops: the *fundamental ambiguity* of love. The lover demands not just possession but the *recognition* of the lover by the loved as the *chosen one*; he wants the other's freedom *as freedom* to be turned toward him alone. This produces the *impasse* of love: to be loved by the other-as-freedom, one must reduce the other to *object* (since freedom by definition cannot be possessed); but to reduce the other to object is to lose precisely what one demanded. This is *Sartre's analysis* of love as *project doomed to failure* — the fundamental *bad faith* of love. From which arise *sadism* (the attempt to seize the other's freedom *through their body*, fixing freedom in the *flesh*) and *masochism* (the attempt to reduce *oneself* to object before the other's freedom, to be reabsorbed in the other's gaze).\]

\[Lacan now returns to *Balint* and the *child*.\] Are we, in studying perverse manifestations, to conclude: — that *perversion is a mode of privileged exploration of a certain existential possibility of human nature*, of a certain *internal tearing* — this *gap (béance)* by which the whole *supernatural world of the symbolic* has also entered; — that all the *qualitative value of perversion lived in the adult* must, as is said, *be projected onto the child*?

Does this mean that? *Of course not*. The question Lacan poses: *must we seek in the child this fundamental intersubjectivity*, if it is what we see constitutive of perversion in the adult? *No*.

What strikes authors speaking of the child — what they lean on to speak of *primary love* (which takes no account of the *selfishness* of the other) — are phrases like: even the child who best loves his mother says to her coolly *when you are dead, mommy, I will take your hats*, or anything — *when grandpa is dead*...

This *easy adulation of the other in the child's discourse*, which appears to us adults, in the *misunderstanding* making of the child this *divine being scarcely conceivable* whose sentiments escape us — when we fall on such paradoxical phenomena, we try to resolve it by *projecting* it (in the good or eternal way): when men have to resolve the question of the *transcendent*, when they no longer understand, they think *it is a god or an animal*. We take children too much for gods to admit it, so we take them in *terms of animality*.

This is what Balint does: thinking the child has no kind of recognition of the other object except in relation to *his need*. *An absolutely total error*. This simple example of *when you are dead*, which the child says so easily, shows where *fundamental intersubjectivity* — which we must rediscover from the origin — *effectively manifests itself in the child*: precisely *the fact that he can serve himself of language*.

This is just what Granoff said: the child with his *first plays of occultation of the object* manifests this kind of capacity — Lacan does not say *to call presence in absence* but *to reject the object in the presence*. *This is what Balint misrecognizes*: that this is *a language phenomenon*. He sees one thing — that the child takes no account of the object. What is important is not that he takes no account of the object but *that he is capable, as little human animal, to serve himself of this symbolic function* by which (as Lacan explained) we can bring the *elephants* into this room, whatever the narrowness of the door.

*Intersubjectivity is first given with maximum accent in the register of the handling of the symbol*. And this *from the origin*. It is from there that produces itself, in increasingly complicated way, this *incarnation of the symbolic in lived imaginary*, modeling all the inflections that, in adult lived experience, this *imaginary engagement of capture, fixation* can take — *all that first departed from this possibility of naming* which is at once: — *destruction of the thing*; — *passage of the thing to the symbolic plane* — thanks to which the properly human register installs itself.

The relation is *intersubjective* essentially. Neglecting this dimension, one falls into the register of the *object-relation* from which there is no way out, leading to *theoretical and technical impasses*.

Has Lacan at least closed a loop sufficiently this morning? This summarizes for those who want a gross schema: *for the child there is first the symbolic and the real, contrary to all that is believed*. All we see composing, enriching, diversifying in the *register of the imaginary* must first depart from an *essential predominance of these two poles*.

If this seems astonishing — if you believe the child is *more captive of the imaginary than the rest* — in a certain sense you are right, *because the imaginary is naturally there*. But *it is absolutely inaccessible to us*. It is accessible only from its much later realizations in the adult.

When we seek to evoke, to attain analytically the past, the lived, the history of our subject — what we have to see as *infantile, childish behavior in analysis* is not what someone (as in last night's audience) confusedly represents as the *behavior, dozings, fingerings of the subject during analysis*. *What we draw out — knowingly or not — is the childish language in the adult*.

\[Lacan promises to demonstrate next time with reference to Ferenczi's article — which magisterially saw the importance of these questions: what makes the *child within the adult* participate in analysis? What part to give? What usable emerges? *That which is irruptively verbalized in analysis*.\]

The session has thus completed the diagnostic work: through Balint, through the phenomenology of perversion (sadism, scoptophilia), through Sartre's analysis of *the look* and *love*, Lacan has shown how missing the symbolic in the theory of the *object-relation* and the *two bodies' psychology* produces the entire deviation of contemporary analytic technique. The constructive work — the proper articulation of *the function of speech, the symbolic recognition of the subject in his history, the analyst as occupying the symbolic position* — will continue in the final sessions, including the eventual return to Augustine's *De Magistro* and the dialectic of teaching by speech.

---

## **Session of 16 June 1954**

Lacan recalls the pivot of last session: *the look* — not simply something situated at the eyes-level, someone looking at you, but *a constitutive dimension of a relation as such* not necessarily presupposing the apparition of eyes (which can be masked, supposed by the look). In the look appears precisely *that X we see* — not necessarily the face of our fellow, but also the window behind which we suppose he lies in wait. *In the object he is, beyond the object he is not, appears on the contrary the object before which he becomes object*.

The relation Lacan chose for demonstration was *sadism*. He showed: *in the look of the being I torment, I must sustain my desire by a challenge each instant*. If I am not *above the situation*, if not *glorious*, this desire *falls into shame*. Same of the scoptophilic relation. Lacan recalls Sartre's analysis of *the one surprised in the act of looking*, for whom the whole color of the situation changes by the surprise. At that moment of total *reversal of the situation*, hinging on nothing — that I do not sustain by a kind of triumph imposing a situation in which I am surprised — I *become a pure thing, a maniac*.

Perversion is not defined merely as *atypicality, aberrance, anomaly* relative to social criteria, contrary to good morals — though that register exists too — nor merely as deviation from the *reproductive finality* of sexual conjunction. *It is something else in its nature*. Not for nothing said of certain perverse penchants that they are *a desire that dares not say its name*.

One touches an essential register. In fact, already at the limit of the register of *recognition* that fixes, situates, *stigmatizes* perversion.
