Structurally, intimately, perversion as such (in the imaginary register) can only be exercised in a *precarious status* at each instant contested from within — *unsustainable, fragile*, at the mercy of that *reversal, subversion* — like the change of sign added in certain mathematical functions when passing from one value of the variable to the next, the correlate passing from *plus to minus infinity from one moment to the next*.

This *fundamental uncertainty of the perverse relation* — not settling in any satisfying action — makes the drama of *homosexuality*. In the triad of these three fundamentally grouped registers in the *dialectic of narcissism*: scoptophilia, sadism, homosexuality. This structure also gives perversion its value of *deepening experience* of human passion (in the Spinozian sense) — where man is *open* not in the fecund sense of openness to the world of truth but in this kind of *division from himself* structuring the imaginary that aims, between 0 and 0', at the specular relation.

It is *deepening* in that, in this *gap of human desire*, all the nuances appear — from shame to prestige, buffoonery to heroism — making this human desire *entirely exposed* in the deepest sense *to the desire of the other*. Lacan recalls Proust's prodigious analysis of homosexuality on the plane of the myth of *Albertine* — no matter that the character is feminine, the structure of the relation is eminently homosexual: how far the demand of this *style of desire* goes, satisfied only by the *inexhaustible capture of the other's desire*, pursued even in dreams — implying *the entire abdication of the subject's own desire*.

In this *miroitement* (in the sense of the *miroir aux alouettes*, the lark-mirror making the full turn on itself) — this *reversal sustaining itself, exhausting pursuit of a desire of the other that can never be seized as the subject's own desire* (the subject's own desire is never anything but *the desire of the other*) — resides the *drama of jealous passion*, also a form of imaginary intersubjective relation.

What is at bottom of this relation, seizable only *at the limit* and in those reversals whose sense is seen in a *flash*? A relation sustainable only *subject to subject*, supposing at each instant *extreme instability*, sustained only by *the annihilation either of the other or of oneself as desire*. In the other and in oneself, this relation *dissolves the being of the subject* — both other and self — into the form where, in the other, his *reduction to being an instrument* of the sole remaining subject (oneself); on the side of oneself: *as an idol offered to the desire of the other*.

*Perverse desire has the property of having at its limit the ideal of an inanimate object*. But not only can it not content itself with this realized ideal; *as soon as it realizes it, it loses this object at the very moment it reaches this ideal*. Its quenching is thus condemned by its structure to realize itself *before the embrace* by either *extinction of desire* or *disappearance of the object*.

Lacan underlines *disappearance* because in such analyses lies the secret *key* to what certain analysts (not without value, not without rigor) need to complete with their *vocabulary*: this *disappearance of the object*. The *aphanisis* Jones speaks of when trying to see beyond the castration complex something he touches in the experience of certain *infantile traumas*. Here one loses oneself in mystery. We do not find elsewhere this fundamental structural element defining a zone, a plane of *intersubjective relations* which is properly the *plane of the imaginary*.

A whole part of analytic experience is nothing other than *the exploration of these dead-ends of imaginary experience* with prolongations not innumerable but limited in number — relying on a certain number of *dimensions of the somatic structure* itself, *of the body insofar as it appears as body* and defines as such a *concrete topography*.

It is not for nothing Lacan has specially evoked three registers today (corresponding to three dimensions). Inversely it is *in history* — at the limit of the term "history," in his *development* — that appear certain *fecund moments, temporalized*, the different possible stages, *different styles of frustration* defining a kind of *negative of development* — also the hollows, faults, gaps appearing in these developments.

*Frustration*: in the analytic discourse a certain naturalistic slope of language always lets it escape. *The bad object is felt itself as frustration; frustration is felt in the other*. The notion of what we may call *the mortal relation* — structured by two slopes, two abysses, where either *desire extinguishes* or *the object disappears*. Structured by a *reciprocal relation of annihilation*. This makes useful the reference to *Hegel's dialectic of master and slave*.

Lacan is obliged at many turns to refer back to this dialectic, re-explain it, reintroduce it. That he has not been able to take it up and develop it before them does not exhaust it. Some wishing to have it developed someday — comprehensible\! It can be of great enrichment. But it is *at the limit* of what Lacan exposes here. Lacan does not say *this imaginary relation* and Hegel's master-slave dialectic are identical — *Hegel begins from other problems, other faces of the concrete structure*. The *imaginary register* appears only at the *limit of our experience*; our experience is *defined on another plane* — and this is what Lacan brings today.

*This plane is the symbolic plane*. In what sense is intersubjectivity structured on the symbolic plane? Lacan stops a moment at the master-slave dialectic. What does Hegel account for? *A certain face, a certain mode of the interhuman bond* fundamental in his ensemble: he must answer not only of *society* but of *history*; he cannot neglect any face; he knows well that one of these essential faces is not simply the *collaboration between men*, the *pact*, the *bond of love*, or all that exists; nor can it be put at the *center of the deduction*.

In the edification unfolding in *passion, seriousness, the negative of struggle and labor* — that is where Hegel centers. To structure himself in an *originary myth*, this fundamental relation on the plane he defines as *negative, marked by negativity*. The necessity of his development pushes him to what we might call *a real relation of utilization of man by man*. Animal societies (insects) have such forms — *why not? — the term societies does not frighten me*. Here marks itself the difference between animal and human societies. *Human societies are not marked nor conceivable by any analysis of objectifiable interindividual bond*. Precisely the *intersubjective dimension* as such must enter.

It is not a question of *domestication of man by man*. Hegel must introduce something he calls — not *fear of death* — for that does not found the relation. The pact of master and slave is not that the slave avows himself vanquished and cries for grace. What founds is *that the master has engaged in this struggle for reasons of pure prestige* — *that he has opted for reasons of pure prestige*, *risked his life*. *That is his superiority*. *In the name of this — not of his force — is he recognized as master by the slave*. In the name of this also, the situation — exactly like the imaginary situation — *begins by an impasse*. For if in the name of *risk assumed for reasons of pure prestige* the slave recognizes the master as master, *this recognition is worth nothing for the master*, since he is recognized by the slave (whom, in the name of the same register of risk, *he* does not recognize as man).

The situation would be issueless, remaining on the imaginary plane. Note the *affinity* of the master-slave dialectic's beginning with this imaginary situation. Developed as such. This impasse aboutits to — this is its imaginary affinity-face. *The other face permits the dialectic of history to constitute itself by something else, introducing the symbolic plane* — namely *all the prolongations of this situation*.

The prolongations: this is why one speaks of master and slave. From this initial *mythic* moment — imaginary — establishes itself the *relation of jouissance and labor*. An action organizes itself. The rules are precisely the *law imposed on the slave* of a function — to *satisfy the desire and jouissance of the other* — conceivable only as *organized and defined*. For it does not suffice for the slave to demand grace; he must then *go to work*. And to work there are *rules, hours*. *We enter the symbolic*.

This symbolic is not in simple relation of succession to the *imaginary pivot* of the situation constituted by the *mortal relation*. We do not pass by a leap from anterior to posterior, following pact and symbol. Deepening the myth: it is conceivable only as *absolutely encircled by the symbolic register*. The situation cannot be founded on biological panic at the approach of death — death *never experienced, never real*. *Man is only ever afraid of an imaginary fear*; the death at stake in *risk of death* is an *imaginary death*. But not only this: it is structured not as *fear of death* but as *risk of death*, as *enjeu* (stake). *There is already in the originary constitution of the master-slave myth, from the start, a rule of the game*.

The intersubjective dimension bathes throughout, even where we maintain it in any development of the imaginary. Every intersubjective relation *insofar as it structures a human action is always more or less implicitly involved in a rule of the game*. Analyzable only as such.

Lacan retakes, from another face, his relation to *the look* — the look allied to the window or corner of the wood, and Lacan advancing in the plain supposing himself under a look. What concerns him *in concrete dialectic* — if he supposes a look watching him — is not so much that he fears some immediate action, revelation of an enemy, his attack-manifestation (else the situation détends and he knows whom he deals with). *As long as he sustains being supposed observed*, what matters most is *to know what he detects, what he supposes, what he imagines of my intentions* — since I am supposed in a *war-situation*, it is *to conceal my movements from the enemy* — it is *ruse*.

It is on this plane that the *intersubjective dialectic of the look* sustains itself. What is important is not that he sees *where I am* but *where I go* — i.e. exactly *where I am not*. *In every analysis of reciprocal intersubjective relation, what is escamoted is that it is not what is there, what is seen, that structures the situation, but what is not there*.

The *theory of games* — fundamental study-mode of this subjective relation — illustrates this: by the sole fact of being a *mathematical theory*, *we are already on the symbolic plane*. Open any modern book on game-theory (improper label, not limited to conventional games — monopolies, bimonopolies on a market, draughts, chess). The analysis of these structures is *absolutely essential for any development of intersubjectivity*. This analysis always supposes at the start a number — however simple — of *numerical givens, and as such symbolic*.

If you read the Sartre alluded to the other day, you will see something extremely troubling: after defining the *imaginary intersubjective relation*, he seems to imply that if there is a *plurality* of inter-relations, *this plurality is not numeral*, since each subject would be *by definition the unique, at the center of references*. Maintained in his analysis of *en-soi/pour-soi*: strictly *phenomenological* — he does not realize the *intersubjective field cannot fail to debouch on a numerical structuration*, on this *3* and this *4* Lacan teaches them to mark, from when we tried to define analytic experience.

The relation of this *symbolism* — however primitive, structured in the situation by a number of marks, numbers — puts us *exactly and immediately on the plane of language*. No conceivable numeration otherwise. In looking for examples, Lacan read three days ago an old work from the start of the century: *History of New World called America*. The problem of *the origin of language* — vexing many linguists.

Every discussion of language's origin is tainted by *irremediable puerility*. Each time one tries to make language emerge from some *progress of thought* — a circle cretinized by secondary formation. Some progress of thought would *isolate, encircle the detail, the particularity, the combinatory element* in the entire situation. How suppose that thought seizes precisely the combinatory element, passing beyond the *detour-stage* of animal intelligence to the *symbol-stage*? How not realize one has first the *symbol* — *the structure of human thought* — substituting for elephants the *word "elephant"*, and even for the sun the *sign of a roundness*?

Do you realize what an abyss this represents? *The sun, by essence and phenomenologically center of what runs across the world of appearances, the unity of light* — and one suddenly makes of it *a round, by seizing one of its particular aspects, makes a round*. An abyss to cross. Is it then that the sun is *an aspect of plate or dish*? What would be the progress over animal intelligence? Nothing. The sun is worth nothing *as designated by a round on occasion*, but is worth as this round *is put in relation to a series of other formalizations* constituting with it that *symbolic Whole* in which it holds its place *at the center of the world*, for example, or the periphery — it organizes in *a world of symbols*.

Apropos those speculating on language's origin and trying transitions between *appreciation of the total situation* and *symbolic fragmentation*, what always strikes is *holophrases*: in certain peoples' usage (and we wouldn't have to look far in our common usage), things *not decomposable*, referring to *a situation taken in its ensemble*. One seems to seize a line between the *animal world* (passing without structuring situations) and the *human symbolic world*.

Lacan recalls Silberer's *auto-symbolism*: in fatigue or half-sleep, thought finds figuration in first hypnagogic images. Analysts say there is more: figuration always has *sense* — conflict, superego, history. They are right; but Silberer is not contradicted — he shows a *virage*, the apparition of the figurative phenomenon.

Lacan encountered an analogous phenomenon in Fijian holophrases: the Fijians pronounce *a number of situations* with the phrase *ma mi la pa ni pa ta pa*. Reading the ethnographer's gloss — note the *direct relation* with what Lacan teaches: the holophrase designates the *state or events of two persons looking at each other, each hoping that the other will offer to do something that both parties desire, but are unwilling to do*.

Defined here, with entire innocence of an ethnographer no theoretician of intersubjectivity — defined with exemplary precision: this *state of inter-regard*, where *each awaits from the other that he decide on something to be done by both, between the two, but where each refuses to enter, to wet himself*.

Reverse the central point of the perspective. *It is not a question of genesis, of intermediate between some primitive assumption of the total situation* (the animal mode of action) *and symbolization through some first engluement of the situation in verbal mode*. On the contrary: *what is of the order of symbolic composition is defined at the limit, at the periphery*.

\[Lacan now turns to Balint's article on *Transference of Emotions*.\] The *domain of the symbol* is introduced — not in its general structuration as it should be — but uniquely *as displacement*. By definition and from the start one places in parallel — in pendant — the so-called *emotion*, the phenomenon of psychological surging that would be *the real*, and the *something* in which it is to find its expression, its realization — under the angle of *displacement*.

The symbol does function in displacement. But the question is: *is that its function*? Must we define it in this *vertical register, as displacement*? A *false road, false way* — *taken in the transversal sense instead of the sense in which it must advance*: instead it is taken in the sense where everything *stops*.

The analysis continues with metaphor — *front of a mountain, foot of a table*. We continue in language phenomena. *Conclusion*: the type of *transfer-operation* is — *you are angry, it is to the table that you punch*. As if I really gave a punch to the table — a *fundamental error*. Nevertheless, it is a question: — *how the act displaces itself in its goal*; — *how emotion displaces itself in its object*; — how this *ambiguous relation between real structure and symbolic structure* makes itself in the vertical sense, *point-to-point relation between universes* — except the *notion of universe is not there*, no way to introduce it.

This is sustained to the end of the article. The notion of transference as *transfer of emotions* — to which emotion transfers itself, in all his examples, is *an inanimate object*. Balint humorously asks: *I do not ask what the inanimate object thinks of it*. Intentionally chosen inanimate, because there *the situation is clear, simpler*. Otherwise — Lacan notes the word *inanimate* appears at the limit of the imaginary dialectical relation — *if we think the other is also a subject, we enter a complication from which, says Balint, there is no way out*.

If there is no way out, *there is no way to do analysis*. Here we arrive recently: such a fuss, promised with an *air of bravado* revealing some unease (manifested by the very style of the *fanfaronnade* about *counter-transference*). *In the end, there is no way out*. We are before the famous *two bodies problem*, unresolved in physics. On this plane of two bodies, *no satisfactory symbolization*.

\[Lacan now tells the story of Balint's clinical experience.\] Balint passes from blackboard to clinic — telling a beautiful story. A gentleman comes to see him, at the edge of analysis. We know this situation; he does not decide. He has seen several analysts. He comes to Balint, telling a long, rich, complicated story with details, feelings, sufferings. *Here Balint — whose theoretical positions Lacan defames with regret, but it is essential to know where we go — reveals the marvelous personage living in this story*. Not the *counter-transference* (the *key-language in which we croup*, which consists in calling *ambivalence* the fact of hating someone, or *counter-transference* the fact of being an imbecile).

*Balint is not an imbecile*. He listens. He has heard many things, is mature, *is someone, is a man, a being*. He *does not understand*. Stories like that: one does not understand. When you do not understand a story, *do not accuse yourself immediately* — say to yourself: *it must have a sense I do not understand*. Balint not only does not understand but *acts as one who does not understand*, considering that from the moment he does not understand, *he is entitled not to understand*. *There must be a cause*. He says nothing and has the type come back. The type returns, continuing — *adds to it*. Balint still does not understand. Things plausible as others, *but they do not go together*.

A clinical experience that happens to us — to be greatly taken into account. Sometimes it projects us toward a *diagnosis*: something organic in certain cases — that's how Freud teaches us to diagnose. But not here. The complicated story — nothing appears on the horizon that is not strange. Balint says: *curious, you tell me many interesting things, but I must tell you — your story: I understand nothing of it*.

The type *blooms*, with a large smile on his face: *you are the first sincere man I meet. All those things I have just told you, I have told to a number of your colleagues who immediately saw the index of an interesting, complex, refined structure. But I told them all this as a test, to see if yes or no you were like all the others — a charlatan and a liar*.

The interesting thing is the example — *what range there is between the two registers*: Balint at the blackboard, exposing emotion-displacements onto the British lion and unicorns; and Balint *in function*, when he speaks intelligently of things he experiences. The patient, by telling a tissue of *plausible but disjointed* lies, was *testing* whether the analyst could sustain the *not-understanding* — could remain in the *symbolic position of the one to whom one speaks the truth in lies* — rather than fall into imaginary fabrication of meaning where there is none. Balint's *honest not-understanding* opens the symbolic dimension: it is a *speech-act of the analyst* refusing imaginary capture. The patient's relief — *finding a sincere man* — *is the conversion of the lie into truth* by the analyst's withholding of false interpretation.

The session has thus brought together: the structure of *intersubjectivity* both imaginary (the look, perversion, master-slave) and symbolic (the rule of the game, holophrase, numerical structure, language); the failure of Balint's *transference of emotions* concept (transference as *displacement* onto an inanimate object, missing the symbolic dimension); and a positive demonstration through Balint's own clinical example of *what the analyst can do* — refuse imaginary meaning, sustain the not-understanding, occupy the symbolic position from which the patient's truth can emerge.

---

## **Session of 23 June 1954**

\[Session 21 takes up Nunberg's article on transference, the analysis of *what transference is* — not displacement of emotion, not projection of imagery, but *modulation of speech, parenthesis of time* — and arrives at Freud's actual definition of *Übertragung* in Chapter 7 of the *Traumdeutung*. This is the *positive constructive* counterpart to the diagnostic critique of Balint.\]

We find ourselves with all sorts of contradictions, for we spend our time realizing that *transference is not at all something illusory*. It is no analysis at all to tell the subject *but, my poor friend, the feeling you have for me is only transference\!* That has never arranged anything. The situation is not truly illuminated by this *simplistic point of view*.

There, as always, when authors are well-oriented, they have a certain sentiment of truth, *contradicted at the theoretical plane by their examples*. This is Nunberg's case. The example he gives as typical of the experience of transference is particularly instructive.

\[Lacan paraphrases Nunberg's case.\] *I had a patient who really brought the maximum of material — who spoke and expressed authentically, with care for detail, for being complete — the avowal, the abandonment of tone. All he could bring was truly without limits. And yet nothing moved. Until we realized: the analytic situation reproduced for him a childhood situation in which he gave himself to confidences, equally pushed, as entire as possible, founded on total confidence in his interlocutress — who was none other than his mother, who came every evening to sit at the foot of his bed. The patient complacent, like Scheherazade, to give her a report as extensive as possible — not only of his day but of his acts, desires, tendencies, scruples, remorse. Never hiding anything. Except: this whole play was vividly appreciated because the warm presence of his mother in nightclothes was for him the source and occasion of a pleasure sustained as such — structuring the situation lived. Namely, to divine beneath her gown the contour of her breasts, the presence of her body, and to give himself to the first specially sexual investigations of his loved partner.*

How shall we analyze this? Let us try to be coherent. There are *two things*. The *primary situation* where the subject lives a certain mode of satisfaction by the means of this *spoken exchange* — where we can distinguish two planes of *symbolic relations subordinated, subverted by the relation we may call imaginary*. The subject has the *prehension, revelation* of something attached to his self-reference — *the objects of his desire are entirely impregnated with that fundamental narcissism* Lacan has shown essential in the constitution of the *object of desire*.

But what happens? How understand the situation *in the analysis at the moment it produces itself* — when the subject behaves with this kind of *entire abandon, total good will, submission to the rule realized to its last terms*? Shall we say that *something resembling the primitively felt satisfaction is there, present*?

For many, the step is easily taken: *yes — behind this speech the subject seeks a similar satisfaction*. One would speak without hesitation of *repetition-automatism*, anything you like. The analyst would have made proof of some *detection* of some sentiment or emotion *present behind this speech*, mark of a *psychological beyond* constituted and conceived as *present*. One would speak of *analysis of transference* as having been a *reality beyond this speech*.

But reflect\! Can we in any way admit that *in this position which is exactly the inverse of the primitive position* — namely, *the analyst is not at the foot of the bed but behind*, and is *far from presenting the charms of the primitive object* nor lending himself to the same *concupiscences* — *anything permits us to cross such a step*?

These are silly things. But it is precisely by *spelling out* the structure and saying simple things that we must teach ourselves *to count on our fingers* the elements amid which we act and intervene.

You will tell me: *if one does not give this explanation, the whole issue is unthinkable*. Why would tout d'un coup having given and revealed it entail a *complete transformation of the analytic situation* — namely that the *same words* will become *efficacious*, will mark a real *progress in the general situation of the subject*?

Let us try to understand. By the very nature of *what speech is*, of *the bringing to day of a group, a world of significations* — *we are in language and in the operation of speech*. Namely: — *speech as such* — because it is speech — *institutes itself in the structure of that semantic world that is language*'s, which never has but one sense at a time among all senses; — *speech always has a beyond*: behind what it says (as behind what the swine, suddenly inspired companions of Ulysses, were saying) — *speech has several functions, several senses*. Behind what a discourse says, *what it wants to say*; behind what it wants to say, *yet another vouloir-dire*. Nothing is ever exhausted, except in arriving at the final point which is precisely the *creative function of speech*.

Namely: it makes surge something which is *its essence* — *what is called the concept* — that *evocation from always of the concept, of something that is the thing itself, present there* (as Hegel teaches).

Here we must make a leap (not easy in Hegel). Recall what Hegel says of the concept relative to the thing: *the concept is the time of the thing*. He arrives by rigorous way: if the concept (contrary to classical theory) *is the thing itself*, what must it be of the thing?

Certainly not the thing in what it is, for a simple reason: *the concept is always there where the thing is not*. Where it arrives to replace the thing — as the elephant Lacan brought in by the word *elephant*, which struck some of them: the elephant was there from the moment we named it, *much more present (at least for the consequences for the destiny of elephants) than if the elephant had entered the room* — what can be there *of the thing*? Not its form or reality, *for in the actual all places are taken*. Hegel says rigorously: *that is what makes the thing be there while not being there*.

This *identity in difference* characterizing the relation of concept to thing is the same that makes the thing be a thing, makes *the fact be symbolized*. *We speak of a thing, not of that unidentifiable something* — each instant succeeding each instant, *never twice does the current of the world pass by the same situation*. Heraclitus: *if we instaurate the existence of things in this absolutely infinite mouvance, never reproducing itself, of the always-changing world, it is precisely because already in the thing this identity-in-difference is saturated*. *One does not descend twice in the same river*.

Whence Hegel deduces: *the concept is the time of the thing*. Important to promote — it places us at the heart of the problems Freud advances when he says *the important takes place outside of time*. True and not true: it places outside time *exactly as the concept does*. Precisely *because the concept is the time of the thing itself, it can place itself outside time*, because it is the *pure time of the thing*. As such this time can reproduce the thing in a certain *modulation* whose material support can be *exactly anything*.

What is at stake in *repetition-automatism* — insofar as it intervenes *in the speech itself* — *is exactly nothing else than this*.

This must take us far — including into *the problems of time* in our analytic practice. We can literally conceive the *transformation* that operates when the transferential situation is analyzed *by evocation of this ancient situation*: where the subject was in presence of a quite different object, no assimilation with the present object. The point: *this present speech, exactly like the ancient speech, is put in a parenthesis of time, in a form of time*.

This *modulation of time* makes this present speech *have the same value as the ancient speech*. But *this value is nothing but a value of speech*. Currently *no sentiment, no kind of imaginary projection* (as Nunberg exhausts himself to construct, in an inextricable situation). Loewenstein will say *not a projection, a displacement* — speaking of a mythology that has all the aspects of a labyrinth. What is at stake is: *the element is an essential, primordial dimension of all that is of the order of speech*.

This *modulation of time* is that the *last word* (Lacan says exactly *last word, last sense*) of the speech the subject is uttering before the analyst is *nothing other, identifiable with nothing other than this temporary form Lacan speaks of, which is already by itself a speech*.

If indeed (as Hegel says) the concept is time, then the last sense of this speech we can analyze by stages — finding all sorts of things between the lines of what the subject says — what we find in the end is something, but something that is *also a speech*. ("Tu es celui qui...")

This something that is also a speech is exactly that fundamental *existential rapport of man suspended before the object of his desire as such*, in that *narcissistic mirage*, needing not to take in this occasion any particular form — *nothing other than that sweat of this rapport*. At once in what we call *preliminary pleasure*, *suspended in this isolation of man relative to the object of his desire in the essentially specular rapport*, putting all speech in a kind of *suspension* relative to this purely imaginary situation.

But this has nothing present, nothing emotional, nothing real, nothing sentimental. Comprehensible only insofar that *once reached, it changes the whole sense of this speech* — *showing the subject that his speech is what Lacan called* (introducing the term in his Rome Report) *empty speech* — and that as such it is, for a certain time, *without effect*.

All this is not easy. *Are you with me?* If at all — you must understand that *the beyond* to which we are referred is always to a *deeper speech*, going to that limit where there is an *ineffable limit* in speech: namely *speech creates in sum the whole resonance of all its senses*. In the end *we are referred to the very act of speech as such*. *Of the value of this actual act* — *whether the speech is empty or full*, *to what point of its presence it is full* — this is what is at stake *in the analysis of transference*.

If you find this somewhat speculative, even unorthodox, Lacan will still bring you in conclusion for today — at one of those crossroads worth your stopping at — *a reference*.

Lacan is here to commentate Freud's texts. *Not inopportune to point out that this interpretation is strictly orthodox*. Seek in all Freud's work where the word *Übertragung, transference* appears: — *not in the Papers on Technique*, regarding real, imaginary, even symbolic relations with the subject; — *not regarding Dora*, nor the miseries she caused him (supposedly because he could not tell her in time that she was beginning to engage in a tender feeling toward him); — *it is in Part VII of the Traumdeutung*: *Psychology of dream-processes*, regarding *wish-fulfillment in dreams*.

Freud — in a whole book Lacan may commentate someday — concerned with demonstrating in the *function of the dream* essentially that *plurality, superposition of semantic orderings* going from a *signifying material* to the whole depth and superposition of significations — shows us how *speech, the transmission of desire that can make itself recognized through anything, provided this be organized into a symbolic system* — that is the source of the *long-time indecipherable character of the dream*.

For a certain time, as one did not know how to compose hieroglyphs in their symbolic system — one realized too late that the little human silhouette in hieroglyphs could mean a *man* but could also represent the *sound* "Homme" composed as syllable in a word — *so is made the dream*.

What does Freud call *Übertragung*? *The phenomenon constituted by the fact that a certain desire repressed by the subject, for which there is no possible direct translation* — Granoff has signaled the comparison with the *Rosetta Stone* in Bennitt's article; Freud himself cited the stone on which Champollion made his discovery — *this desire cannot make itself recognized*. Why? Among elements of repression, there is something participating of the *ineffable* — essential relations no discourse can sufficiently express *except in what Lacan called the "between the lines"*.

Next time, Lacan will make the comparison with the way any *esoteric author* organizes his discourse — for example the *Guide of the Perplexed* (Maimonides), an esoteric work. The way it presents itself, *how the author deliberately organizes his discourse such that what he wants to say* — which is *not sayable* — *can yet reveal itself*. To whom? Lines composed not only of what he says but of certain *intentional disorders, ruptures, discordances*. By these he wants to say what either *cannot or must not* be said.

Lacan will show: the way this writer expresses himself is the *inverse* of what *we* call the *reading of unconscious tendencies in slips, holes, contentions, repetitions*. What we read is also something in the subject expressing itself — *quite spontaneously and innocently*, as the writer organizes his discourse *deliberately*. To be brought together.

What does Freud tell us in the first definition of *Übertragung*? What do the *Tagesreste* (day-residues) serve? *They are the signifying material*. Not exactly how Freud expresses it: he says they are *de-invested from the standpoint of desire* — for the dream and in the dream-state, *kinds of errant forms* having become for the subject of *lesser importance, emptied of their proper sense*. This is what happens each time we deal with *a signifying material*.

Signifying material — phonematic, hieroglyphic, or other — are *forms fallen from their proper sense*, and as such *retaken in an organization through which a new sense finds to express itself*. Exactly what Freud calls *Übertragung*. *Insofar as unconscious desire (i.e. impossible to express) finds the means to express itself through the alphabet, the phonematics of these day-residues themselves de-invested of desire*. *A language-phenomenon as such*. The signification of the sign is *neutralized by the realizing signification*.

\[Lacan summarizes: *transference* in its original Freudian sense — the place it first appears in Freud's work — is *the transfer of meaning across signifying materials, the use of de-invested signifiers to express what cannot be directly said*. This is *not* the displacement of emotion onto an object (Balint), *not* the projection of childhood imagery onto the analyst (Nunberg). It is *a structural property of speech as such*, by which the unconscious desire — refused direct translation — *finds form in the materiality of signifiers*. The *Rosetta Stone* analogy is decisive: *the unconscious is a script*, not an emotion; *the analyst's task is decipherment*, not reception of affect. *Transference in the analytic situation* is then nothing but *the manifestation in concrete speech-interchange of this same structural property* — the patient's speech is at once *empty* (filled with day-residues, semantic surfaces) and *full* (carrying within its modulations of time, repetitions, suspensions, the trace of the unconscious desire). The analyst's intervention is *decipherment* — bringing the *modulation of time*, the *parenthesis*, to articulation in the symbolic. The *transformation* that occurs when the transferential situation is analyzed by *evocation of an ancient situation* is not the recognition of an imaginary identification but *the recognition that the present speech and the ancient speech share the same form of time, the same modulation, the same parenthesis* — i.e., the *concept* of which both are *temporalizations*. Hence Lacan's claim: *this interpretation is strictly orthodox*. To return transference to its rightful place — to the *Traumdeutung*, to the *dream-work*, to the *language-phenomenon* — is to return analysis to Freud's discovery: *the unconscious is structured like a language*.\]

---

## **Session of 30 June 1954**

\[Session 22 takes up Augustine's *De Magistro* as commented by Père Louis Beirnaert, with Lacan's interventions. The dialogue between Augustine and his son Adeodatus on the function of signs is taken — section by section through fourteen chapters — as the systematic phenomenology of the symbolic, providing the philosophical foundation Lacan has built toward all year. Lacan ends with the *Freudian discovery*: truth catches error by the collar in the *méprise*.\]

Beirnaert opens by reading the text. Lacan punctuates. The session moves chapter by chapter through Augustine's dialogue.

### **I. LANGUAGE IS INSTITUTED TO TEACH OR RECALL MEMORIES.**

Augustine and Adeodatus open by asking: *what do we do when we speak*? Augustine establishes: when we speak, we have one of two ends — *to teach* or *to recall memories* (our own or another's). Speech does not first refer to *things* but to the *symbolic operation* of teaching and recall. The very institution of language is bound to the *intersubjective dimension* of recognition and memory.

Lacan: this is precisely what Lacan teaches under the term *speech*. *Language is instituted to teach or to recall memories* — to teach (*docere*) and to recall (*commemorare*) are *the same operation* at the level of the symbolic. *Discere is docere*, as Augustine will later articulate. To learn and to teach are the *same act*: that of *symbolic transmission*. The recall of memory is itself a *teaching* — for what is recalled is the *structure of the symbolic*, not merely the *content*. *Memory* in Augustine, as in psychoanalysis, is *symbolic memory* — the inscription of the subject in a *symbolic order* by which past, present, and future are temporalized.

### **II. SPEECH IS NECESSARY TO SHOW THE SIGNIFICATION OF SPEECH.**

Adeodatus asks: must one speak to teach the *signification* of speech? Augustine: yes — only by *speaking* can one *show* what speech is. *Speech alone is self-referential* — the only sign whose signification is shown *only by itself*.

Lacan: this is a *crucial point*. Speech is the *only sign* whose *signification can be demonstrated equivalently by itself*. *It is the action by essence that demonstrates itself by signs*. *Only signification is retrieved in our appeal: signification always refers to signification*. This is what makes speech *exceptional* in the order of signs — what places it in the position of *master-sign*, the sign by which other signs are recognized. The symbolic order is articulated *from within itself*: it does not need an external ground.

### **III. IS IT POSSIBLE TO SHOW ANYTHING WITHOUT USING SIGNS?**

Adeodatus answers: yes — by *doing the thing*, one can show it without sign. Walking, sitting, lying down — these can be shown by performing them. Augustine challenges: *but is this not itself a sign*? When you walk to show me what walking is, *the walking itself becomes a sign of walking* — *every showing is a signifying*.

Mannoni recalls the Sunday game at Guitrancourt — actors developing pieces *without speech, by dance*. Lacan: very instructive. The game of dividing into camps and making partners guess a word given secretly by the leader makes evident exactly what Augustine demonstrates. *What is at stake is not so much the dialectic of gesture as the dialectic of indication*. *Even indication is still a sign* — perfectly ambiguous. Designating *the wall* — how know whether it is the wall or the quality of being *rough, green, gray*? In the other day's game, someone having to express *ivy* went to get ivy. *You cheated*, they told her. *Error*. What did she bring? *Three leaves of ivy* — could designate form, green color, or the *Holy Trinity*.

The mediation by language is unavoidable. Even showing *the thing itself* is a *signifying operation*. *Every act of demonstration is already symbolic*.

### **IV. ARE SIGNS NEEDED TO INDICATE SIGNS?**

Augustine: by *signs* alone we usually show *signs*. The hand-gesture (pointing) — *deixis* — is itself a sign indicating another sign. *Signs refer to signs in a network*.

Lacan: *signa facimus* — when we speak, *we make signs*; whence *significare*: to signify, to speak — *signa facere*. The symbolic order is a *system of signs referring to signs*. There is no *primary outside-sign* by which signs would be grounded. The system is *autonomous, internal to itself*.

### **V. RECIPROCAL SIGNS.**

Augustine explores how signs can refer to each other in a *closed circulation*. The word *nomen* refers to the word *verbum*, and *verbum* to *nomen*. *Reciprocity of signs* in the *intersubjective dimension*: every sign awaits a *recognition*, presupposing another sign.

Lacan: this *reciprocity* is exactly what Lacan teaches as *the dialectic of recognition*. The sign is not a *one-way arrow* from signifier to signified; it is *a structure of intersubjective recognition*, a *pact*. *Nomen* — Lacan notes — has linguistic ambiguity: it bears the *double relation*: with *numen* (the sacred) in its original form, and with *noscere* (to know) by linguistic evolution. *Agnomen* comes from a *captation* of *nomen* by *cognoscere*. *Juridical usages* confirm: *nomen* in its function of *recognition, pact, interhuman symbol* — the *symbol in the sense of pact*. Hugo's pun *nomen-numen-lumen* (Lacan does not believe Hugo was a madman in this) opens the entire dimension of the *symbolic as sacred bond*.

### **VI. SIGNS THAT DESIGNATE THEMSELVES.**

Some signs designate themselves: *the word "word" is a word*. *The word "speech" is itself a speech*. *Auto-reference* opens the *self-grounding character of the symbolic*. The signifier, in being also a *signified of itself*, escapes the *infinite regress* of grounding outside itself.

Lacan: this is what allows speech to *demonstrate itself* — and what makes speech the *master-sign*. The auto-reference of speech is what makes possible the *cogito*, the *I think therefore I am*, but already in Augustine's framework — *si fallor sum*: if I am deceived, I am.

### **VII. SUMMARY OF THE PRECEDING CHAPTERS.**

Augustine summarizes: signs are needed for signs, but not for everything; some things may be shown by the thing itself. The dialogue has reached a first plateau. Augustine and Adeodatus have established *the autonomy of the symbolic order* internally, but have not yet shown how this order *relates to the thing* and to *knowledge*.

### **VIII. UTILITY OF THIS DISCUSSION; TO RESPOND, ONE MUST APPLY THE MIND TO WHAT THE SIGN RECALLS.**

What is the point of all this? Augustine answers: *to apply the mind* — to *recall* what the sign points to. *Memory and attention* are the indispensable operations of *understanding signs*. Without the *application of mind* to what the sign *re-calls*, the sign remains *dead sound*.

Lacan: this is the *symbolic act* — *the application of mind, the recollection, the act of recognition*. Without this, signs are *empty speech*. With this, they become *full speech*: speech in which the subject *recognizes himself*.

### **IX. SHOULD ONE PREFER THE THING OR ITS KNOWLEDGE TO THE SIGNS THAT EXPRESS IT?**

Augustine asks: between *the thing*, its *knowledge*, and the *sign*, which is preferable? He establishes: *knowledge of things is preferable to the signs that express them*. *The sign serves knowledge of the thing*; the sign is *instrumental*.

Lacan: but Lacan immediately notes (we will return to this) that *this hierarchy will be reversed*. For the *thing itself*, in our experience, is *constituted by the symbolic*. Knowledge of the thing is not *prior* to the sign but *constituted by the symbolic operation* of the sign. *We know things only through their inscription in the symbolic order*.

### **X. CAN ONE TEACH WITHOUT SIGNS? — WORDS DO NOT GIVE KNOWLEDGE.**

Here Augustine produces his most radical thesis: *words do not give knowledge*. *Nothing is learned through words*. When I hear the word *head*, I learn nothing — for either I knew already what *head* meant (in which case the word *recalls* but does not *teach*), or I did not know (in which case the word teaches me *nothing about the head* but only that the sound is a *sign* of something I do not yet know).

Augustine: *to know the signification of a word, one must know the thing it signifies*; thus *the knowledge of the thing precedes and grounds the knowledge of the word*, not vice versa. *In a sign there are two things: the sound and the signification*. The perception of sound comes not from the sign but from the sound striking the ear. The signification, we know *by seeing its object*.

Lacan: a paradoxical reversal. From the *autonomy of the symbolic order* internally, Augustine moves to *the limit*: *signs do not teach*. *Words resound; truth teaches*. The signifier as such is *empty*. *The signifier signifies signification only by virtue of a knowledge already there, illuminating from elsewhere*. Augustine prepares the move to the *interior Master* — the *Christ who teaches within*.

But this is a *partial truth*. Augustine *misses* one thing: that *the very dimension of truth* in which his demonstration unfolds is *itself constituted by speech*. The dialogue between Augustine and Adeodatus, *by being a dialogue*, presupposes and *creates* the dimension of truth. *The very question of truth is included within the discussion*. *As soon as Augustine puts speech in question, he puts it in question with speech*. *It is with speech that he creates the dimension of truth*. Every emitted, formulated, communicated speech *introduces into the world this new* — this *affirmation*, this *emergence of sense, posing itself, affirming itself*, less as truth than as *disengaging from the real the dimension of truth*.

### **XI. WORDS RESOUND IN THE EAR; TRUTH TEACHES THE MIND.**

Augustine pushes the thesis: *words have force only to excite us to study without making us know anything*. Words are *occasions, invitations*, not *transmissions of knowledge*. *Knowledge comes from the thing seen, not from the word heard*. The famous formula: *for me to be instructed, what I want to know must be placed before my eyes, before some other corporeal sense, or even before the mind*. *Words teach nothing but words — or rather the sound and noise they produce*.

Lacan: this thesis, taken alone, would conduct to an *aporia*. The word would be *radically separated from truth*. But this is *not Augustine's whole thought*. The thesis prepares the move to the *Truth that teaches within* — *Christ as interior Master*. Truth, for Augustine, is *not in the sign*; it is in the *interior illumination* by which the sign is understood. The *autonomy of the symbolic order* — its internal articulation — does not suffice to *generate* truth from within. Truth must come from *the interior light* — Augustine's theological move.

But what is the *philosophical* (and analytic) translation of this? The signifier as such does not *teach*; but the signifier *organizes* the field in which the *recognition of truth* becomes possible. The truth is not *outside* the signifier; it is *the very dimension* that the signifier *opens up*. Without speech, there is no question of truth; with speech, the question of truth becomes *unavoidable*. *Truth is the dimension constituted by the very emission of speech*.

### **XII. CHRIST IS THE TRUTH, HE TEACHES WITHIN.**

Augustine arrives at his theological thesis: it is *Christ as interior Truth* who *teaches within*. Every sign is interpretable only by *appeal to this interior Master*. The whole *De Magistro* leads to this conclusion: *the true Magister is interior*.

Lacan: from a philosophical-analytical standpoint, what is this *interior Master*? Lacan will reverse the perspective. Truth is not *outside* the signifier in some *interior light*; truth is *constituted by the very operation of the signifier* — by the *intersubjective recognition* in which the subject is *named, called, recognized*. The *interior Master*, demystified, *is the Other* — the *place of speech*, the *symbolic order* in which the subject inscribes himself. Augustine's *interior Master* is precisely the *Ich-Ideal*, the *symbolic position* from which the subject hears himself addressed — *the agency of recognition*.

### **XIII. SPEECH DOES NOT EVEN MANIFEST THE INTERIOR FEELINGS.**

A radical chapter: Augustine claims that speech does *not even* reliably manifest the speaker's interior states. Words can *lie*. *The speaker may say something other than what he thinks*. Speech is not even a reliable index of the *speaker's interiority*.

Lacan: precisely the *opening* through which the *Freudian discovery* enters. *Speech can lie* — this is what Augustine takes as objection to the symbolic determination of truth. But Lacan reverses it: *the fact that speech can lie* is precisely what *gives it the dimension of truth*. *A speech that could not lie would not be speech*; it would be a *natural reaction, an animal cry*. The capacity to lie *defines* the speaking subject. *The speaking subject as such we must admit as subject for one simple reason: he is capable of lying — he is distinct from what he says*. This is what Freud uncovers in the *unconscious*: the dimension of the *speaking subject as deceiver, as divided from his own discourse*.

For *to deceive presupposes the dimension of truth*: *the lie affirms itself as true to the one who listens; for the one who lies, the very deception imposes the support of the truth to be hidden, and as the lie develops, it requires a real deepening of the truth*. So *the truth is constituted as a counter-image of the lie* — but also, more importantly: *the lie is constituted by truth*. The dimension of truth is *not a result* of the symbolic operation but is *included from the start* in every speech-act.

*Error* — the more important phenomenon than the lie — is similarly *defined only in terms of truth*. *Error is the common and habitual incarnation of truth*. *The paths of truth are paths of error by essence*. Until truth is entirely revealed (i.e. probably *until the end of time*), *it is of its nature to propagate itself under the form of error*. This is a *constitutive structure of the revelation of being as such*.

### **XIV. MAN SPEAKS OUTSIDE, CHRIST TEACHES WITHIN.**

Augustine's conclusion: man's exterior speech is *occasion only*; the *true teaching* is from *Christ within*. The whole dialogue resolves in *theological transcendence*: the symbolic order is *grounded* in the *interior light*, which is *Christ*.

Lacan: here is where Lacan parts ways with Augustine — *not in rejection but in transposition*. Augustine's *Christ within* is, for the analytic experience, *the Other* — the place of the symbolic, the *Ich-Ideal*, the agency of recognition. The *interior teaching* is not from a *supernatural light* but from the *symbolic order itself*, in which the subject is *constituted as speaking subject*. Augustine's theology *correctly perceives* the *gap* between the *natural and the symbolic*; but it fills the gap with God. *Lacan fills it with the structure of the symbolic itself* — the *Other*, the *intersubjectivity from which the subject is called*.

\[Lacan now develops the bridge to the Freudian discovery.\]

The Augustinian dialectic, having led us through a *double reversal* of the position — from *signs grounded in things* to *truth grounded in interior light* — *deprives speech of all reference to the dimension of truth in which it nevertheless develops*. The dialectic *implicitly* moves in truth — by its very motion it *cannot fail to refer to truth at every instant*.

Here we cannot fail to be struck by *the Freudian discovery*. Freud brings something which, although of the *empirical domain*, brings to this question — *which seems beyond all experience and literally metaphysical* — *a contribution* of such striking character that it *blinds*, or one thinks only to *close one's eyes to its existence*.

Observe: if the Freudian discovery is what Lacan says — that in a whole series of human manifestations *not properly of the order of discourse* (what generally characterizes them, why they are called, from a certain angle, *irrational*) — *what is expressed is literally a speech*.

Observe that *the essential spring of all that is of the analytic field* is this — and supposes — that *the discourse of the subject develops normally in the order of error, méconnaissance, even denegation* (not quite the lie, between error and lie). *Truths of common good sense*, but worth recalling.

Our human discourse — especially the one held during analytic session — developing in the *register of error*, *something happens by which truth bursts into this system*. This is what *Freud says*; we must become *conscious that this is what Freud says*. This *something brings the message of truth*.

For us analysts — those introduced, who know to see this field of phenomena — *something happens that is not the contradiction of discourse*. We do not have to push subjects as far as possible in the way of *absolute knowledge*, to make their *education* on every plane. Not psychology alone — *the whole system of sciences*. We do that here because we are analysts; *but if we had to do that for the patients, you see where it would lead*.

Nor is it the *encounter with the real*: we take them *between four walls*; we do not guide them by the hand in life — into the consequences of their *bêtise*. *Something by which truth catches error from behind*. This *something* is what we may call essentially *the most manifest representative of méprise*: it is in the *lapsus*, in the action improperly called *failed* (*manquée*) — for *all our failed acts*, like all our *speech that stumbles, are speech that confesses and acts that succeed* — *succeeding precisely in the sense of revealing a truth that is there, essentially behind*.

This is what Freudian thought implies. *If Freud's discovery has a sense, it is this: truth catches error by the collar in the méprise*.

In Freudian thought, a *truer, purer sense manifests itself* within something more or less confused — call it *free associations, dream images, symptoms*. *Which is a speech, in the sense that it is what brings the truth*. *It is in the symptom, in the succession of dream-images*. Expressed in Freud — reread the beginning of the chapter *Dream-Work*: *a dream is a sentence, a rebus*.

*Ein solches Bilderrätsel ist nun der Traum* (such a picture-puzzle is the dream). Fifty pages of *The Interpretation of Dreams* would lead us to this consideration. *Condensation*: do not believe condensation means simply *term-to-term correspondence of a symbol with something*. Freud says: in a given dream, *the ensemble of dream-thoughts, i.e. the ensemble of things signified, of senses of the dream*, is taken *as a network in its ensemble* and is represented *not at all term-to-term but by a series of crossings (entrecroisements)*. The ensemble of senses is represented by the *ensemble of what is signifying*. In each *signifying element of the dream, each image, there is reference to a whole series of points, things to signify*; inversely, *each thing to signify is represented in several signifiers*. We retrieve the structure — the relation of *what signifies and what is to signify*.

Here is where the Freudian discovery brings us: *to see manifest itself this something that is speech, and that speaks, in some way through — even in spite of — the subject*. This *something*: Freud shows it not only by his speech but by *all sorts of other subjective manifestations*. Going as far as one could dream: *by his body itself the subject emits a speech which is as such a speech of truth* — *a speech he does not even know he is emitting as signifying*. *The subject says always more than he wants to say, always more than he knows how to say*.

The principal objection Augustine makes to the *inclusion of the domain of truth in the domain of signs* is: *very often subjects say things that go much further than what they think — they are even capable of confessing the truth without adhering to it*. The reference to the *Epicurean* who sustains that the soul is mortal but, to sustain this, cites his adversaries' arguments — for those who have eyes open, says Augustine, *they thus prove the fact of truth*. Even citing the adversaries' arguments as refuted, *those who see, see that here is the true speech; they recognize the value of the doctrine that the soul is immortal*. Such is the Augustinian perspective.

By something whose *structure and function of speech* we have recognized — *the subject testifies of a sense truer than all he expresses by his discourse of error*. If this is not how *our analytic experience* is structured, *it has strictly no sense*.

Lacan emphasizes: if for one instant you do not think this is the perspective of our experience — *that the speech the subject emits without knowing it goes beyond his limits as discoursing subject but within his limits as speaking subject* — if you do not conceive what we do in this perspective, *the objection appears immediately*: *why does this discourse, which you detect in the register of méprise, not fall under the same objection as the rest*? In other words, *why is the lapsus more truthful than the deliberate utterance*?

The answer can only be: *because the symbolic order is structured in such a way that the lapsus, by being a "failure" of conscious intention, is precisely the place where the unconscious — which is the discourse of the Other — emerges*. The truth manifests itself *where the ego fails*, where the *deliberate intention* breaks down. This is *Freud's discovery in its full implication*: *the unconscious is structured like a language; it is a discourse — the discourse of the Other*.

Augustine prepared this. *De Magistro* is the philosophical document where the *autonomy of the symbolic, its self-reference, its function of truth, and the gap between word and interior knowledge* are all articulated. Freud completes it by *secularizing* the interior Master — making him the *Other*, the *symbolic order itself, in which the subject is named, called, recognized*. The whole of analytic technique consists in *occupying the position of this Other for the subject* — in being the *symbolic agency of recognition* — and in *deciphering the discourse of the Other* in the lapsus, the dream, the symptom.

The session — and with it the year's work — has thus arrived at its proper anchor: the *symbolic order* as the *constitutive ground of analytic experience*; the *Other* as the *secularized name* of Augustine's *interior Master*; the *lapsus, the dream, the symptom* as the *places where the discourse of the Other emerges*; and the *analyst's position* as that of the *Other* in the *symbolic recognition* of the subject.

---

## **Session of 7 July 1954**

\[The final session. Lacan opens to questions. Mme Aubry asks the structuring question on the *three fundamental passions*. Leclaire returns to *transference*. The session synthesizes the year's work: the *imaginary structure of love* (Verliebtheit), the *symbolic position of analyst as Idealich/Ich-Ideal*, the *three passions at the junctions of imaginary, real, symbolic*, the *temporal dimension of working-through (Durcharbeiten)*, and the conclusive equation: *transference is the very concept of analysis because it is also the time of analysis*. The session — and the year — closes with the case of the obsessional and the master-slave dialectic, and the famous distribution of *elephant figurines* to the audience.\]

Lacan opens: *Who has questions*?

Mme Aubry: she understands that *at the conjunction of imaginary and real one finds hatred* — on condition of taking *conjunction* in the sense of *rupture*. What she understands less is that *at the conjunction of symbolic and imaginary one finds love*.

Lacan: an excellent question. This will perhaps permit him to give this last meeting the atmosphere he prefers — *familial rather than magisterial*. To Leclaire: you too surely have things to ask. Last time you said something resembling a question: *I would have liked you to speak about transference*. They are hard. Lacan only talks of that — and they are still not satisfied.

There are *profound reasons* why you will always remain hungry on the subject of transference. But Lacan will try today. He would like first that you take into account where you are: *what is realized in analysis* is *something of the order of the symbolic* — and what is *introduced as new* relative to all preceding human experience is *the dimension of being*. There is *literally realization of the human being as such*, and that is *what we deal with in analysis, in the dimension of speech*. Clearly not something *intellectual*. The intellectual is situated in the *imaginary* (pseudo-neutralized) projection of the *ego*.

It is at the level of *ego-phenomena* that we encounter the intellectual — which analysis has denounced under the name *defenses, resistances*. The great debate, the crucial point, is precisely around the situation, the function of the *ego*. This defines the foundation of psychoanalysis as Lacan tries to restore it.

Across history, we assist in *progress* — but not progress of the cerebral convolutions: *progress of the symbolic order*. Observe a science like *mathematics*: stagnant for centuries around problems now clear to ten-year-olds. *Powerful minds* were around. *The second-degree equation* was held up for ten centuries too long — the Greeks could have found it; they found cleverer things in problems of maximum and minimum. Simply, *from the day one invented a number of things much more symbolic on the mathematical plane*, one could resolve these problems. *Mathematical progress is not progress of the power of human thought*: it is from the moment a gentleman invents a sign like √ or ∫ that he *does good work*. *Mathematics is that*.

We are — happily — in a position of different nature, more difficult. The symbol at stake is *extremely polyvalent*. Insofar as we manage to formulate the symbols of our action and understand them adequately, we will make a step forward — which is also a *retroactive step*. What we are elaborating is *a psychoanalysis A* (a *first time*) — at once principles and applications — *also a return to the aspiration of its origin*. A *more authentic comprehension of the phenomenon of transference*.

\[Lacan responds to Aubry on the *three passions*.\] In speaking of transference last time, Lacan introduced *three fundamental passions*, into which he had *ignorance* enter. The sense of bringing in the three was to introduce as *third essential dimension* the *space* — or more exactly the *volume* — of human relations, *in the symbolic relation itself*.

*Love as human passion* — distinguished from desire considered in the limit, radical, established relation of the human being to his object, of every organism to its instinctual aim — is something else *precisely insofar as human reality is a reality of speech*. *No love is instituted, one can speak of love, only from the moment the symbolic relation exists as such, where the aim is not satisfaction but being*.

Lacan takes love (he could take any of the three). It is intentionally that only at the last session did he speak of these *passional ridge-edges* (as Aubry well underlined): *points of junction, points of rupture between the different domains where the interhuman relation extends — real, symbolic, imaginary*. *Crests situated between each domain*.

We have underlined that the *amorous relation in its phenomenon* (recall the *Einführung des Narzißmus* commentary) is — *love-passion, Verliebtheit*, distinct from *Liebe* (two words used not without reason) — *captivated, captured* essentially in the human being *by a narcissistic relation*.

The deep ambiguity human beings have toward this *essential, illuminating, profoundly disturbing passion* lies precisely around this. The all-acute problem of *love* turns precisely around this.

Without redoing the whole dialectic of narcissistic investment: let us speak of *the function of the relation to the other implied in what Lacan calls the mirage of Verliebtheit*. Analytic experience and Freud's teaching — and the most lucid view of those analysts who best understood Freud — *make love as passion something essentially of the imaginary plane*. Even in his passion, *the subject deliberately assumes by a kind of choice* (in the sense of *temptation* essentially) the development of his whole dialectic of relations to the other.

According as *the other saturates the image* — i.e. *fills it* — he becomes positively the object of *narcissistic investment* (that of Verliebtheit). Recall Werther: meeting Charlotte when she has the child in her arms — *he falls precisely (pile) into the narcissistic imago of the young hero*. Or on the contrary (exactly the same slope), *as frustrating the subject of his ideal and his own image*, engendering *maximal destructive tension*.

It is around this — a nothing turning in one direction or the other — that *Freud's questions* on the *possible and sudden transformation in Verliebtheit between love and hate* turn. *A nothing suffices*. This is what the *phenomenon of imaginary investment* turns around, insofar as we see it play a role *in transference*.

How call this role? *A pivotal role*. *Transference*, if it is true (as Lacan says) that it establishes itself *in and by the dimension of speech*, includes the revelation of this *imaginary rapport* only when *certain crucial points of the spoken meeting with the other (the analyst) are reached*. To the extent that the discourse — *unbound* from a number of its conventions by the *fundamental rule* — *plays more or less freely* (relative to ordinary discourse) in a way that permits the subject to be *maximally open to the fecund méprise* by which *truer speech rejoins the discourse of error*.

But also insofar as *this speech flees this revelation* — the fecund méprise — and *develops in deception*. Lacan has always underlined this *essential dimension* preventing us from eliminating the subject as such from experience. This is what reveals the points *not integrated, refused, or repressed* in the *assumption by the subject of his history*.

We meet here the *holes in this history* — points where occurred something *not assumed, refused* — *verworfen* or *verdrängt*. (*Verdrängt* — came once to discourse and was rejected. *Verworfen* — essentially original rejection. The distinction Lacan indicated apropos *The Wolf Man*.)

The *phenomenon of transference*, insofar as it meets *imaginary crystallization*, means: the subject finds, *in the other* (at 0'), the *totalization of various accidents that occurred at 0 in his history on the imaginary plane* — *captivations, fixations* — *imaginary*, *unassimilable to the action of speech*, *to the law of discourse*, to the *symbolic development of his history*.

This is why transference (as Freud says) *becomes essentially an obstacle when excessive*. In the erotic sense, or in the aggressive sense: *the echoes of the discourse* (distributed in the zone between 0 and mirror B) *approached too quickly, in anticipated fashion, came too quickly too near point 0'*. At that moment, between 0' and mirror B, occurs *something critical* evoking *resistance at its most acute* — *silence*: one of the correlatives of *too-intense transference*.

But if this moment comes *opportunely, at the right time*, this silence is also a silence that *takes all its value of silence* — not merely negative but *of a beyond-speech*. Lacan has emphasized the *positive* of certain moments of silence in transference — *literally the most acute apprehension of the presence of the other as such*.

\[Lacan urges the audience to reread Freud's *Papers on Technique* in light of these reflections.\] You will see how the texts will take on for you *a new, more living sense*. You will see how the apparent contradictions on transference (*at once resistance of transference and motor of analysis*) are *understood only in the report of this dialectic of imaginary and symbolic*.

What is uttered is *there at mirror A*, on the subject's side. What is uttered *is heard at B* on the analyst's side — and *makes heard not only to the analyst, but to the subject*. The *echo-rapport of discourse* (subject → analyst → subject) is symmetric to the *rapport of image to mirror*. What happens on the imaginary plane is grounded (by the apparatus) on *small displacements of the mirror*. These permit completing *in the other, projected on the other (0'), what the subject, by definition, essentially misrecognizes of his structuring image, the image of the ego, which is at 0*.

The *passage from 0 to 0'* is the realization, by the subject, of the *completion of the imaginary elements insofar as they fixed, pointed, "capitonné" his imaginary development* — and *capitonné* refers to his *expression in the symbolic order*. At certain points the symbol could not assimilate these imaginary elements — i.e. *that means they were traumatic*.

What happens: *what comes from C to 0* is *what the subject assumes in his discourse insofar as he makes it heard to the other*. (Correction: not exactly to 0 — somewhere between A and 0, much closer to 0 — i.e. *closer to the unconscious notion of his ego* than any other point.) *The whole question is: where will the spoken assumption of this ego take place*? *In 0'* — as he realizes himself in his imaginary 0'.

\[The schema thus serves to model: the analyst's position, like a mirror inclined at a particular angle, organizes the field in which the subject's imaginary elements can come to *visualization in 0'*. The analyst, by his *symbolic silence* and *interpretation*, permits the *imaginary elements to be brought to symbolic articulation*.\]

\[Lacan critiques the *analysis of resistances*.\] Some analysts (not without merit) have exposed the most modern technique — *analysis of resistances* — as consisting in *single-out* (Bergler's term) in the subject's ego *a number of patterns insofar as they present themselves as mechanisms of defense*. This *enlarges, completes* the ego on the side of 0'. But — *the question is precisely*: *what relation does this have to the symbolic realization of the subject*? *It has none, except by accident*. To attempt the *enlargement of 0' alone* is to remain *strictly in the imaginary* — the very fault Lacan has diagnosed all year. *True progress* is *in the order of symbolic relations* — *resolution of the arrests, inhibitions constituting the superego* — *fuller reintegration of the law of the ensemble of symbolic systems* in which the subject is situated.

\[Lacan now develops the temporal dimension.\] In what has always been said somewhat confusedly: *a certain extension of time to understand* — those who attended Lacan's *Wolf Man* seminar will recall the *temps pour comprendre*. You retrieve it in the *Papers on Technique* apropos *Durcharbeiten* (working-through). Is this *psychological wearing-down*? Is it (as Lacan said in his text on empty and full speech) *of the order of discourse as labor*? Yes — for it is insofar as *this discourse pursues itself long enough to appear taken entirely in the parenthesis of a construction of the ego* that it can come *suddenly* to resolve itself *for the one for whom it has come* — namely effectively *for the master*. That is, *to fall into its own internal value*, appearing only *as labor*.

But what is *further and behind*? Essentially this — Lacan can only approach, broach this year: *the concept is time*. In this sense: *transference is the very concept of analysis, because it is also the time of analysis*.

Mannoni: *not quite at the end*.

Lacan: it is exactly *the measure of time* necessary for what so-called analysis of resistances gives the subject *anticipatedly, prematurely* — what we called, citing a good author, *the patterns of the ego, the defenses, the caches*. Insofar as experience shows that such an analysis (as Freud reports at a precise passage of the *Papers on Technique*) *does not, for that, advance the subject one more step*. Freud says: in that case, *one must wait*. *If one must wait*, it is *exactly the measure of time* necessary for the subject *to realize the dimension at stake on the symbolic plane* — to *make of the thing lived in analysis*, in the *kind of pursuit, scuffle, embrace* that the analysis of resistances realizes, *the proper duration* of certain of these *repetition-automatisms* — *which gives them symbolic value*, gives them their *temporal dimension*, because this is the *conceptual dimension*.

Mannoni: a concrete problem. Obsessives whose life is *waiting* — they make of analysis *another waiting*. Why does this waiting of analysis *reproduce* the waiting in life and *change* it?

Lacan: same as the case of Dora — and as Lacan developed in last year's *Rat Man* seminar around the *master-slave relation*. *What does the obsessional wait for? The death of the master*. What does this waiting serve him? *The interposition between him and death*. *This waiting is what will go beyond the master's death*.

You retrieve this structure in all forms: *when the master is dead, all will begin*. He is right: *the slave can play justly on this waiting*. As Tristan Bernard said when arrested for the Dancig camp: *until now we lived in anguish; now we will live in hope*. The master, properly speaking, has a *much closer rapport to death*. The master in pure state can be in a *desperate position*: he has nothing to wait for but death, since he has nothing to expect from the death of his slave (except inconveniences). But the slave has *much to wait for* from the master's death — *which he believes, because it has not yet arrived*. For *beyond the master's death he will have to confront, like every fully realized being in the Heideggerian sense, his being-toward-death*. *But precisely the obsessional does not assume his being-toward-death*: *he is in reprieve (en sursis)*. That is what must be shown him. This is exactly *the function of the image of the master as such*.

Mannoni: who is the analyst.

Lacan: incarnated in the analyst. This is the proper structure of *obsessional analysis*. It is precisely in the *sketches of movement that must develop in a temporal dimension* — *sketches of movement toward the exit* — insofar as a certain *scansion, a certain timing* in the imaginary sketches *toward the exit, out of this prison of the master*, has been realized *a certain number of times*, that *the obsessional can realize the concept of his obsessions* — i.e. *what they mean*.

There is the strict necessity of a certain number of *scansions* of this kind. Were we to study these cases properly, *there are numerical signs in each case of obsession*. Exactly the same as the *famous example of the three discs* — insofar as *the subject, in thinking the thought of the other, sees in the other the image and sketch of the same movements as he*, this permits him *to reduce the obstacle to the unity of a single movement*, the *signification of what is at stake* each time the other is exactly *the same as him*: *there is no master other than the absolute master, death*.

But he needs *a certain time to see this*. For *he is much too content to be a slave, as everyone is*.

### **The closing diagram**

Lacan posted at the board his closing schema — *the little diamond, a kind of six-faced dihedron*. All faces alike, one above and one below a plane. Not a regular polyhedron, though all faces equal — but the polyhedral *vertices* are not all the same: two are *trihedral* (three faces), three are *tetrahedral* (four faces).

The *median plane* (containing the triangle dividing the pyramid in two) is the *surface of the real*. In this surface, *nothing can cross it*; *all places are taken*. *At each instant all places are taken; at the next instant all is changed*. With our world of words and symbols, *we introduce a second dimension*: *a hollow, a hole*, *something thanks to which all sorts of crossings and interchanges are possible*. This *kind of hole in the real* is called, according as we envisage it, *being or nothingness*. Being and nothingness are *essentially linked to the phenomenon of speech*.

It is in this *dimension of being* that situates the *tripartition* Lacan always insists on: *symbolic, imaginary, real*. Not for nothing they are three. There must be a *minimal law* the geometry incarnates: *if in this plane of the real you detach some flap that introduces itself into a third dimension, you can never make a solid except with at least two other flaps*.

The *three fundamental passions* inscribe themselves only *in the dimension of being, not of the real*: — at the *junction of symbolic and imaginary*: **love**; — at the *junction of imaginary and real*: **hate**; — at the *junction of real and symbolic*: **ignorance**.

What we call the *institution* — *from the outset, before any beginning of analysis* — of something already of the order of transference: the *triggered, lightning side* of the existence of this dimension *before anything has bound itself*. Already *virtually present these two possibilities, in their extreme form of love and hate*, can only be conceived in this register *with the accompaniment of that something that goes so without saying that one does not name it in the primitive components of transference*: namely *ignorance as passion*. Insofar as it is *instituted as such at the foundation of the situation*.
